If he learns but does not act, he has committed one act of disobedience by neglecting to act. And whoever learns and acts has attained salvation.] (3). The commentator said: [Because the principle of the Sharia indicates that any ignorance a legally responsible person can dispel is no proof for the ignorant person, especially when a long time has passed and the days have continued, for what is not known today will be known tomorrow.] (4). His words, ["especially when a long time has passed"], are supported by the words of Allah, Exalted is He: (Did We not grant you a life long enough for whoever would take heed to take heed, and did the warner not come to you? So taste the punishment, for the wrongdoers will have no helper.) Fatir: 37.


[Page 34]

5 - Abu Hamid al-Ghazali, may Allah have mercy on him, said:
[What is meant is that one who intends good by committing an act of disobedience out of ignorance is not excused, unless he has only recently embraced Islam and has not yet had sufficient time to learn. Allah, Exalted is He, has said: {So ask the people of knowledge if you do not know} (al-Nahl: 43] (5).
(Punishment for the act, not merely for negligence) Ibn Qudama said: "If a fornicator claims ignorance of the prohibition, and it is plausible that he could have been ignorant of it, as in the case of someone who has only recently embraced Islam or was raised in a remote desert, his claim is accepted, since he may be telling the truth. But if he is someone from whom this would not be unknown, such as a Muslim raised among Muslims and people of knowledge, his claim is not accepted, because the prohibition of fornication is not unknown to someone like him, and so his lie is known. If he claims ignorance that an invalid marriage is unlawful, his claim is accepted, because Umar accepted the claim of someone who said he was ignorant of the prohibition on marrying during the waiting period, and because this is something many people may be ignorant of and which is unknown to those who are not people of knowledge." (1).
7 (Punishment for the act) - Ibn Rajab al-Hanbali, may Allah have mercy on him, said:
[If someone raised in the abode of Islam among Muslims commits fornication and claims ignorance of its prohibition, his claim is not accepted, because what is apparent belies him, even though the presumption is that he did not know of it] (2). By "what is apparent," he means that he was raised among Muslims, which makes it possible for him to know of the prohibition. His statement, "the presumption is that he did not know," is because the presumption regarding a person is ignorance, as I mentioned in the first chapter of this book.
8 - Al-Suyuti, may Allah have mercy on him, said:
[(Who is accepted as claiming ignorance, and who is not) Anyone ignorant of the prohibition of something that most people have in common will not have his claim accepted, unless he has only recently embraced Islam,


[Page 35]

Or if he grew up in a remote desert region where such matters are unknown, such as the prohibition of adultery, murder, theft, wine, speaking during prayer, and eating while fasting] (3).
9 - Ibn Hajar al-Haytami, may Allah have mercy on him, said:
[According to us, if someone lives far from the Muslims, such that his failure to come to their lands to learn is not attributable to negligence, or if he has only recently embraced Islam, he is excused for his ignorance and should be taught what is correct. If he returns to what he said after that, he is an unbeliever. The same is said of one who considers that good or approves of it.] (4).
10 - Shaykh Muhammad ibn Abd al-Wahhab, may Allah have mercy on him.
He said: [This is astonishing: how can you doubt this when I have explained it to you repeatedly? The person against whom proof has not been established is one who has only recently embraced Islam, or who grew up in a remote desert region, or where the matter concerns a subtle issue such as currency exchange or love magic. Such a person is not declared an unbeliever until he has been informed. As for the fundamentals of religion, which Allah has made clear and firmly established in His Book, Allah's proof is the Quran; whoever has received the Quran has received the proof.] (5). His statements in which he grants an excuse on account of ignorance are qualified by these words of his.
11 -
12 - Shaykh al-Shinqiti, Professor Abd al-Qadir Awda, may Allah have mercy on him.
(Punishment for the act) He discussed in detail the criterion for being able to acquire knowledge when he said: [One of the basic principles of Islamic law is that an offender is not held accountable for a prohibited act unless he has full knowledge that it is prohibited. If he is ignorant of the prohibition, liability is lifted from him. The possibility of knowing that it is prohibited is sufficient for knowledge; whenever a person has reached


[Page 36]

A rational person for whom it was possible to learn what was forbidden to him, either by consulting the texts that establish its prohibition or by asking the people of knowledge, is deemed to know the prohibited acts. He may not plead ignorance or claim that he did not know. Hence the jurists say: "In the abode of Islam, an excuse based on ignorance of the rulings is not accepted." A legally accountable person is deemed to know the rulings when it is possible for him to know them, not only when he has actually acquired that knowledge. Accordingly, a text establishing a prohibition is considered known to everyone, even if most people have not encountered it or know anything about it, so long as it was possible for them to learn of it. The Sharia did not require actual knowledge, because that would cause hardship, throw the door wide open to claims of ignorance, and obstruct the implementation of the texts. This is the general rule in Islamic law, and it has no exceptions. If the jurists consider it acceptable to plead ignorance of the rulings for someone who lived in a remote desert region without contact with Muslims, or for someone who had only recently embraced Islam and did not live among Muslims, this is not, in reality, an exception. Rather, it is an application of the original rule, which prevents a person from being held accountable for not knowing that something is forbidden until knowledge becomes accessible to him. Such people had no access to that knowledge and are not deemed to know the rulings of the Sharia. But if someone claiming ignorance grew up among Muslims or people of knowledge, his claim of ignorance is not accepted.] (1).
May Allah have mercy on him.
He said: [As for one who is able to learn but neglects to do so, and gives precedence to the opinions of men over what he knows from revelation, he is not excused] (2)


[Page 37]

A group has now emerged, a new group whose theory, as the researcher has distilled it by examining the books of one of their shaykhs, called Abu Ubayda al-Jazairi, may be summarized as follows. First: They say, "We take our creed from the early generations." According to the statements of theirs that I have traced, they mean by this: "What is meant by knowledge is the Book, the Sunnah, and the reports of the Companions. The statements of the Successors follow the reports of the Companions, as a second rank. As for the words of those who came after them, they are not truly called knowledge among the hadith scholars and the early generations in general." (The Book of Knowledge.) They said, "The statements of the Companions are authoritative in everything, in creed and jurisprudence; likewise, the statement of a Successor is authoritative in matters of creed, reports, divine attributes, and jurisprudence," and they attributed this to the early scholars!!! Second: Even weakly transmitted statements of the Companions are authoritative. He said, "The early generations relied on weak statements of the Companions"??!!! Third: They also considered weak statements of the Successors authoritative if they appear in one of these books. Their leading theorist said, "If you find a report in the books of Ibn al-Mubarak and Ahmad, namely their books on asceticism, or in Ibn Abi Hatim's Tafsir, or in Abdullah's al-Sunna, or in Ibn Abi Shayba's Musannaf, these authors, even when they transmit through a weak chain, do not transmit texts containing objectionable material. The fact that a hadith is mentioned by the well-known authorities I have named is of benefit, even if its chain is weak: it indicates that it has a basis, and its being well known among them means it is sound"??!!! Third: This led them to accept statements attributed to the early generations declaring anyone who denies certain attributes or beliefs to be an unbeliever, and declaring him an unbeliever in absolute terms, as well as declaring anyone who does not declare him an unbeliever to be an unbeliever in absolute terms. These statements are either weakly transmitted from them, God willing, or are general and do not identify anyone in particular, such as, "Whoever says that is an unbeliever." They interpreted these texts to mean that the early scholars who declared those who denied certain attributes to be unbelievers intended every person who said such a thing, with no excuse for anyone who said it, even though most of their statements, as will be shown, are either weak or general. On the basis of the foregoing principles, they said that this was the doctrine of the early generations; so they declared those who interpret the attributes to be unbelievers, and declared those who excuse them to be unbelievers. On this basis, they declared anyone who excuses someone on grounds of ignorance to be an unbeliever, categorically, despite the existence of explicit statements in the very same books on which they relied, transmitted from other early scholars, which stipulate that a person must possess knowledge for the proof to be established against him. There are also other statements from them excusing those who interpret the texts, and consequently excusing those who excuse such interpreters. But they have ignored these, as we shall explain, God willing. On the basis of this chain of principles, they declared all Ash'aris to be unbelievers, including their scholars, even well-known figures such as al-Nawawi, Ibn Hajar, and others. Then they declared as unbelievers those who did not declare these individuals to be unbelievers and excused them, such as Ibn Taymiyya, Ibn al-Qayyim, and Ibn Kathir. After that, they moved on to another issue: they made certain questions of interpretation, such as whether the earth is spherical or flat, matters of definitive doctrine, relying on some earlier statements that it is flat, while also ignoring statements attributed to the early generations that it is spherical, in a highly arbitrary manner. They ruled that the earth is flat, not spherical, and made this one of the definitive doctrines that cannot be interpreted otherwise. Consequently, anyone who disagrees is an unbeliever. Thus they declared most Muslims today to be unbelievers, since they say that the earth is spherical, as do the majority of scholars, and


[Page 38]

of the earlier Muslims, because they say that the Earth is spherical. Consequently, no one remains a true Muslim except them. Regardless of the intention of the person who devised and established this way of thinking, was his aim to spread division, discord, excommunication, and killing among Muslims in order to weaken them? Is he malicious and cunning in this, with evil intentions, so that no scholar remains respected by Muslims and scarcely any Muslim remains on Earth, leaving no one whose reports can be trusted and no history that is valued? Or was he well-intentioned, with his independent reasoning leading him to this conclusion? I speak only of the person who established this way of thinking, not its followers, since I have found many of its followers whom I consider well-intentioned, but who misunderstand the matter and are excused on that account. To discuss these people, it is necessary to examine some of the important issues on which this way of thinking is based.

First: their claim that the opinion of a Companion or Successor is authoritative. As for the opinion of a Companion, yes, some scholars held that it could be relied upon, but only when there was no explicit evidence to the contrary in the Book and the Sunnah, and when it was not known that another Companion had disagreed with him. In that case, the matter is akin to tacit consensus. Others rejected it outright. But as for holding it authoritative when another Companion disagrees with it, I know of no one who held that view.

Ibn Taymiyya said: "Those scholars who held that a Companion's opinion is authoritative only said so when no other Companion disagreed with him and no text was known to contradict it." End quote from Majmu al-Fatawa (1/283).

Indeed, al-Hazimi, their shaykh, said in the thirtieth recording of Mufid al-Mustafid: "Scholars have differed over whether a Companion's interpretation is authoritative or not. This is a separate issue from whether a Companion's opinion in matters of legal verdicts is authoritative. Some of them even considered a Companion's interpretation to be a Prophetic report, as did al-Hakim and others, but the correct view is that it is not a Prophetic report." Thus, the issue is disputed. It is astonishing and suspicious of them to present it as one of the definitive matters, especially when they add the Successors to the Companions in claiming authority for their opinions; particularly when they rely on the opinions of some of the early Muslims despite the fact that other early Muslims disagreed with them, and especially when they rely on those opinions even if they are weak, as their own proponent has said, and use them in a grave matter such as disbelief and faith. Does all of this not give cause for suspicion?

This is the issue of whether a Companion's opinion is authoritative or not. Someone has summarized it as follows: "The fourth issue: The correct view is that a Companion's opinion is not authoritative. Some have said that it is authoritative without qualification, while others have made distinctions and mentioned several positions: (First) that it is authoritative if it contradicts analogy. (Second) that only the opinions of Abu Bakr and Umar (may Allah be pleased with them) are authoritative. (Third) that the opinions of the four caliphs are authoritative. Our evidence is the text, consensus, and analogy. As for …"


[Page 39]

The text is His saying, Exalted is He: {So take a lesson, O people of insight} [Surat al-Hashr, part of verse 2]. This is incompatible with the permissibility of following another's opinion without evidence. As for consensus, the Companions agreed that it was permissible to disagree with any individual Companion. Abu Bakr and Umar did not object to those who disagreed with them, nor did either of them object to the other in matters over which they differed. As for analogy, a person is able to ascertain the ruling by a method of his own, and therefore following another's opinion without evidence must be forbidden to him, just as it is in matters of the fundamentals of religion."(1)
Al-Shawkani added the following evidence from those who deny that a Companion's opinion is authoritative: "The truth is that it is not authoritative. For Allah, Glorified is He, sent to this community no one but our Prophet Muhammad (peace and blessings be upon him). We have only one Messenger and one Book, and all of this community are commanded to follow His Book and the Sunnah of His Prophet. In this respect, there is no difference between the Companions and those who came after them. Whoever says that proof in the religion of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, is established by anything other than the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger, and what refers back to them, has spoken about Allah's religion without proof and established as part of this Sharia a law that Allah did not command. This is a grave matter." He attributed this position to the majority.(2)
In general, those who hold the opposing view have cited several proofs that might suggest that a Companion's opinion or action is authoritative. Those who hold this view maintain that, in this case, one acts on the Companion's opinion in the specific matter where it differs from the apparent meaning of the text, because the Companion has reported the Prophet's intent (peace and blessings be upon him) concerning that apparent meaning.(3)
Evidence cited by those who regard the opinions and actions of the Companions as authoritative:
First: What was narrated from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), that he said: "My Companions are like stars; whichever of them you follow, you will be guided." Ibn Hazm said: "As for the hadith mentioned, it is false and fabricated."(4)
Al-Shawkani said: "This has never been established."(5) Al-Albani said: "Fabricated."(6)
Second: What is reported from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), that he said: "You must follow my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs after me."(7) Abu Dawud narrated it. Al-Albani said: Al-Tirmidhi called it "a hasan sahih hadith"; al-Harawi said, "This is among the finest hadiths of the people of Syria"; al-Bazzar said, "It is an established, sahih hadith"; and al-Hakim said, "It is sahih and has no defect."(8)
(Response): First, the Companions themselves did not understand this hadith to mean that it was obligatory to follow the practices of the four caliphs even when they contradicted the text. Al-Razi said: "They agreed that it was permissible to disagree with any individual Companion. Abu Bakr and Umar did not object to those who disagreed with them, nor did either of them object to the other in matters over which they differed."(9) Moreover, at most, the hadith calls for following the Sunnah of the caliphs, and no one else's, if we grant that this is what the evidence establishes.
Accordingly, we say: Whoever wishes to follow the Sunnah of the caliphs, which they agreed upon, should not object to someone who departs from their Sunnah and method because the text has become clear to him to the contrary. More than that, the four caliphs agreed that the text takes precedence over the opinion of any one of them. Following their Sunnah collectively means giving precedence to the text over the opinion of each of them. This is made clear by the following examples.
1- Abu Bakr's disagreement with Umar over the text:
__________

(2) Al-Shawkani (Muhammad ibn Ali ibn Muhammad), Irshad al-Fuhul ila Tahqiq al-Haqq min Ilm al-Usul, Dar al-Kitab al-Arabi, 1st ed., 1319 AH–1999 CE (2/182–188).
(3) Ibid. (1/162).

[Page 40]

Abu Hurayra (may Allah be pleased with him) reported: When the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) died, and Abu Bakr succeeded him, some of the Arabs apostatized. Umar said, "Abu Bakr, how can you fight the people when the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, 'I have been commanded to fight the people until they say, "There is no god but Allah." Whoever says, "There is no god but Allah" has protected his property and his life from me, except by its due right, and his reckoning is with Allah'?" Abu Bakr replied, "By Allah, I will fight whoever makes a distinction between prayer and zakat, for zakat is the due right of property."(10) Here, Umar opposed the caliph by citing the text, and the caliph argued against him by interpreting the text as well. If Umar's opposition had been impermissible, and he had been giving precedence to his own opinion over the text, Abu Bakr would have rebuked him. We therefore know that Abu Bakr's practice was to give precedence to the text over Umar's statement.
2- A woman's disagreement with Umar on the basis of the text:
In the presence of the Companions, no one rebuked her—not even Umar himself—when he prohibited excessive dowries. She countered him with the verse, "And you have given one of them a great amount" [Surat al-Nisa, part of verse 20].(11) Had Umar believed that his opinion could not be opposed by citing the text, he would have rebuked her. Rather, Umar accepted what she said. We therefore know that Umar's practice (may Allah be pleased with him) was to give precedence to the text over the statement of the caliphs, one of whom was himself.
3- Ali's disagreement with Uthman on the basis of the text:
Sa'id ibn al-Musayyib reported: Ali and Uthman (may Allah be pleased with them both) met at Usfan. Uthman was prohibiting tamattu or combining umrah with the pilgrimage, so Ali said, "Why do you prohibit something that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) did?" Uthman said, "Leave us alone." Ali replied, "I cannot leave you alone." When Ali saw that, he entered ihram for both rites together.(12) Al-Qasim ibn Sallam added: "Uthman said, 'Do you see me prohibiting the people while you do it?' He replied, 'I would not abandon the practice of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) for the statement of anyone.'"(13) We therefore know that Ali's practice was to give precedence to the text over the statement or action of the caliphs. Al-San'ani says: "Sixth: Among the proofs cited by those who follow others blindly is their saying: 'You must follow my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs after me, Abu Bakr and Umar; follow the guidance of Ammar and hold fast to the covenant of Ibn Umm Abd.' The answer is that being guided by them means following the Book and the Sunnah, accepting what they contain, calling people to them, and prohibiting blind imitation, for no such practice is reported from them. It is authentically reported from Ibn Mas'ud, who was Ibn Umm Abd, that he prohibited blind imitation and said, 'A man should not be an unthinking follower with no insight.' Moreover, it is well known that none of them would abandon the Sunnah for the statement of anyone. The practice and way of the rightly guided caliphs was to follow the Book and the Sunnah; thus, following their practice is following the Prophetic Sunnah and the Quran."(14)
Third: They said that the Companions (may Allah be pleased with them) witnessed the revelation, so they knew best what they had witnessed, and praise of them has been given.
(Response): Ibn Hazm said: "The obligation to praise them does not entail that they be followed blindly, since the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) stated that Abu Bakr and Umar were the best of their men after the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), yet they made mistakes, as ... Abdullah ibn al-Zubayr reported to them that a delegation from Banu Tamim came to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him). Abu Bakr said, 'Appoint al-Qa'qa ibn Ma'bad ibn Zurarah,' while Umar said, 'Rather, appoint al-Aqra ibn Habis.' Then this was revealed: ﴾O you who believe, do not raise your voices above the voice of the Prophet, nor speak loudly to him as you speak loudly to one another, lest your deeds come to nothing while you are unaware...﴿

[Page 41]

"...you do not perceive." [Surat al-Hujurat, verse 2] Ibn al-Zubayr said: "After that, whenever Umar related a hadith from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), he would relate it to him as one whispering a secret." Abu Muhammad said: "Whoever makes a mistake, it is not permissible to accept his opinion without proof that establishes its validity. As for his (peace and blessings be upon him) saying, 'You must adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs,' we know that he (peace and blessings be upon him) does not command what is beyond one's capacity. We found that the rightly guided caliphs after him (peace and blessings be upon him) differed greatly. There must therefore be one of three possibilities, with no fourth: either we accept everything over which they differed—which is impossible and beyond our capacity, since it would entail accepting a thing and its opposite—so this possibility is invalid; or it is permissible for us to accept whichever of their views we choose. This would be a departure from Islam, because it would mean that the religion of Allah, Exalted is He, is left to our discretion. Moreover, if this were so, then by accepting the view of one of them, we would necessarily have abandoned the view of another. In that case, we would not be following their Sunnah, and would thus have contradicted the hadith cited. Only the third possibility remains: to accept what they unanimously agreed upon. This applies only to what all the other Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, agreed upon along with them in following the Sunnah of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and affirming it."(15)
(("Al-Ihkam fi Usul al-Ahkam by Ibn Hazm" (2/12):
"We found that a Companion among the Companions (may Allah be pleased with them) would hear a hadith and interpret it in a way that took it beyond its apparent meaning. We also found that they (may Allah be pleased with them) acknowledged and admitted that many of the Prophetic practices had not reached them. Such is the well-known report from Abu Hurayra: 'My brothers among the Emigrants were occupied with trading in the markets, while my brothers among the Helpers were occupied with tending to their property.' Al-Bara also said: Muhammad ibn Sa'id ibn Nabat told us, saying: Ahmad ibn Awn told us, saying: Qasim ibn Asbagh told us, saying: Muhammad ibn Abd al-Salam al-Khushani told us, saying: Muhammad ibn al-Muthanna al-Anazi told us, saying: Abu Ahmad al-Zubayri and Sufyan al-Thawri told us, from Abu Ishaq al-Sabi'i, from al-Bara ibn Azib, who said: 'Not everything you relate did we hear from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him); rather, our companions related it to us, for we were occupied with tending the camels.' Abu Bakr (may Allah be pleased with him) did not know the prescribed share of inheritance for a grandmother, whereas Muhammad ibn Maslama and al-Mughira ibn Shu'ba knew it. Abu Bakr (may Allah be pleased with him) also asked Aisha how many pieces of cloth the Messenger of Allah had been shrouded in. Umar (may Allah be pleased with him) said regarding the hadith about seeking permission: 'This matter from the Messenger of Allah was hidden from me; trading in the markets distracted me.' He was also unaware of the ruling concerning a woman who miscarries, while others knew it. He became angry with Uyayna ibn Hisn until al-Hurr ibn Qays ibn Hisn reminded him of the words of the Exalted: {Accept what is readily offered, enjoin what is right, and turn away from the ignorant.} The Messenger of Allah's command to expel the Jews and Christians from the Arabian Peninsula remained unknown to him until the end of his caliphate, and it had also remained unknown to Abu Bakr (may Allah be pleased with him) before him throughout his caliphate. When Umar learned of it, he ordered them expelled, and left none of them there. Umar was also unaware of the Prophet's command not to enter a plague-stricken area, while others knew that..."
Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf. Umar asked Abu Waqid al-Laythi what the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) used to recite in the prayers of Eid al-Fitr and Eid al-Adha, even though the Messenger of Allah had performed them for many years. He did not know what to do about the Magians until Abd al-Rahman reminded him of the Messenger of Allah's instruction concerning them. He had forgotten that the Prophet had accepted the jizya from the Magians of Bahrain, a well-known matter; perhaps he had taken a share of that money, as others had. He forgot the Prophet's instruction that a person in a state of major ritual impurity should perform dry ablution (tayammum), and said that he should never perform dry ablution or pray unless he found water, until Ammar reminded him. He intended to divide the wealth of the Kaaba, but Ubayy ibn Ka'b argued against him that the Prophet had not done so, and he desisted. He used to send back women who had menstruated and departed before bidding farewell to the House, until he was informed that the Messenger of Allah had permitted this, whereupon he stopped sending them back. He used to assign different amounts of blood money for the fingers, until he learned from the Prophet that they were to be treated equally; he abandoned his view and adopted equality. He held that blood money belonged only to the agnatic heirs, until al-Dahhak ibn Sufyan informed him that the Prophet had granted a woman a share of the blood money, whereupon Umar adopted that ruling. He prohibited excessive dowries, citing the dowries of the Prophet, until a woman reminded him, and he withdrew his prohibition. He intended to stone a woman who was insane, until he was informed of the Messenger of Allah's saying, "The pen has been lifted from three"; he then ordered that she not be stoned. He ordered that Hatib's freedwoman be stoned, until Uthman reminded him that an ignorant person is not subject to the prescribed punishment, and he desisted from stoning her. He objected to Hassan reciting poetry in the mosque, but Hassan and Abu Hurayra informed him that Hassan had recited there in the presence of the Messenger of Allah, so Umar fell silent. It had escaped the Ansar and the Emigrants, including Uthman, Ali, Talha, al-Zubayr, and Hafsa, Mother of the Believers, that ritual bathing is obligatory after penetration even if there has been no ejaculation, although this is a matter that frequently arises. It had also escaped Aisha and Umm Habiba
__________
(15) Ibn Hazm, Al-Ihkam fi Usul al-Ahkam, cited above (6/79).


[Page 42]

The Mothers of the Believers, Ibn Umar, Abu Hurayra, Abu Musa, Zayd ibn Thabit, Sa'id ibn al-Musayyib, and the rest of the eminent jurists of Medina and others held that the ruling requiring ablution after eating food touched by fire had been abrogated.
All of this is a matter of widespread affliction and general occurrence. All of this, and what follows, refutes the claim made by those among the Hanafis and Malikis who give no weight to their words: that when a matter is one of widespread affliction, a solitary report is not accepted.
It is astonishing that both groups accepted reports that others contradicted, even though the matter was one of widespread concern: the Hanafis accepted that laughter invalidates ablution, while others were unaware of it; the Malikis accepted a ruling allowing an oath to be accompanied by one witness, while others were unaware of it. There are very many examples of this kind. Muhammad ibn Sa'id narrated to us: Ahmad ibn Abd al-Nasir narrated to us: Qasim ibn Asbagh narrated to us: Muhammad ibn Abd al-Salam al-Khushani narrated to us: Muhammad ibn al-Muthanna narrated to us: Abd al-Rahman ibn Mahdi narrated to us: Sakhr ibn Juwayriya narrated to us: Amir ibn Abdullah ibn al-Zubayr narrated to us, that Abd al-Rahman ibn al-Aswad informed him, saying: I was sitting with my father at Arafat, while Ibn al-Zubayr was addressing the people. Ibn al-Zubayr said, "This is a day for magnifying Allah, praising Him, and proclaiming His oneness, so magnify Allah, praise Him, and proclaim His oneness." My father got up and made his way through the crowd until he reached him, then leaned close and said, "I testify that I heard Umar ibn al-Khattab on this very pulpit reciting the talbiya." Ibn al-Zubayr then recited, "Here I am, O Allah, here I am," in a loud voice. Abu Muhammad said: As you can see, this report was unknown to Ibn al-Zubayr and others, although it is well known from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him). Umar had forbidden people to be named after the prophets, yet he saw Muhammad ibn Maslama coming and going to visit him, though he was one of the eminent Companions. He also saw Abu Ayyub al-Ansari and Abu Musa al-Ash'ari, who were known among the Companions only by their kunyas. He saw Muhammad ibn Abi Bakr al-Siddiq, who was born in the presence of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), during the Farewell Pilgrimage. His mother asked the Prophet for a ruling when she gave birth to him, asking what she should do about her state of consecration while in postpartum bleeding. Umar knew with certainty that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) knew the names and kunyas of those we have mentioned, approved them, and called them by them, without changing any of them. When Talha and Suhayb informed him that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) had permitted this, he refrained from forbidding it. He had intended to abandon jogging during the pilgrimage, but then recalled that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) had done it, and said, "We have no right to abandon it." Here is Uthman (may Allah be pleased with him): they have reported that he sent to al-Furay'a, the sister of Abu Sa'id al-Khudri, to ask her what the Messenger of Allah had ruled concerning her waiting period, and that he acted on that ruling. He also ordered a woman who had given birth after six months of pregnancy to be stoned, but Ali cited the Quran to him, pointing out that a pregnancy could last only six months, whereupon he withdrew the order to stone her. And here is Ali (may Allah be pleased with him): he acknowledged that many of the Companions had related to him things from the Prophet that he did not know, and that he would make them swear to it, except for Abu Bakr, whom he would not make swear. Allah, Exalted is He, would thereby benefit him with whatever He wished of what he heard, which he had not known before. Here is Talha, who permitted gold to be exchanged for silver on deferred terms until Umar reminded him. And here are Ibn Umar and Ibn Abbas, who sold one dirham for two until they were reminded, whereupon they stopped. Ibn Umar then related it from Abu Sa'id al-Khudri, from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him); Muslim recorded it. Ibn Umar then returned to that ruling and abandoned his own view. Ibn Umar later related it and said, "This is the instruction our Prophet gave us." Malik recorded it from Humayd, from Mujahid, from Ibn Umar. Ibn Umar spoke truly, and we say of a hadith from the Prophet, when it reaches us, "This is the instruction our Prophet gave us." In this way we understand all cases in which a Companion narrated a hadith and then acted contrary to it: he had given a ruling contrary to the hadith before it reached him, and once he was informed of it, it is not permissible to think anything else of the Companion. This is precisely what we have explained about Ibn Umar, in a way that admits no obscurity: they interpreted what they had heard of the hadith. Anyone who understands this otherwise necessarily places the Companion under one of two possibilities, from both of which Allah, Exalted is He, has absolved them; both are misguidance and sin. Either they openly opposed the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him)—which is impermissible for anyone, and it is impermissible to think this of them—or they possessed knowledge that required them to act contrary to what they had narrated, in which case they would not be justified in concealing it from us, narrating what had been abrogated and concealing the abrogating ruling from us. Such conduct would be disbelief on the part of whoever did it, and deception in religion. No one attributes this to them except someone whose heart has deviated or whose heart is blind. Thus their false assumption is disproved, and our position is established. Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds. There is absolutely no third possibility, except that they may have forgotten at the time some of what they had previously narrated. That too is possible. If they interpreted the reports, then their interpretation, may Allah be pleased with them, was conjecture, whereas their narration from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) was certain. It is not permissible for a Muslim to abandon certainty for conjecture. Thus the difficulty in this matter is entirely resolved. Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds. As for them, may Allah be pleased with them, they are excused, since this was their own exercise of judgment, and such cases on their part are also very few. The same does not apply to someone who follows them after being alerted to what we have explained. Here are Aisha and Abu Hurayra (may Allah be pleased with them): the wiping over leather socks was unknown to them, and to Ibn Umar as well, while Jarir knew of it, though he had only embraced Islam a few months before the Prophet's death. Aisha acknowledged
She had no knowledge of it and was instructed to ask someone who was expected to know about it, namely Ali (may Allah be pleased with him). This is Hafsa, Mother of the Believers, who was asked about intercourse that leaves the man in a state requiring ritual bathing: is bathing required or not? She said, "I have no knowledge of that." And this is Ibn Umar, who thought that a prohibition from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) against leasing land might have been issued, more than forty years after the Prophet's death. So he refrained from it, while acknowledging that they had leased it during the time of Abu Bakr, Umar, and Uthman. He did not say that it was impossible for something known to Rafi, Jabir, and Abu Hurayra to have escaped the notice of these men. Yet these are our brothers, who say whatever they wish: "If this were true, it would not have escaped Umar." But the permissibility granted by the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) for a menstruating woman to depart without performing the farewell circumambulation escaped Zayd ibn Thabit, Ibn Umar, and most of the people of Medina, until Ibn Abbas and Umm Sulaym informed them of it, whereupon they retracted their position. And Ibn Umar did not know that one should remain until the deceased had been buried, until Abu Hurayra and Aisha informed him of it. He then said, "We have missed out on many qirats."


[Page 43]

Numerous. It was said to Ibn Umar, regarding his preference for the tamattu' pilgrimage over performing Hajj alone: "You are going against your father." He replied, "Is the Book of Allah more deserving of being followed, or Umar?" We have related this from him through Abd al-Razzaq, from Ma'mar, from al-Zuhri, from Salim, from Ibn Umar. Ibn Umar was unaware that touching the penis invalidates ablution until Busra bint Safwan instructed him about it on the authority of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), and he accepted that. Ibn Abbas was unaware of the prohibition of tamattu' and of the prohibition on eating domestic donkeys until Ali (may Allah be pleased with him) informed him of it. Ibn Abbas said, "Do you not fear that Allah will cause the earth to swallow you up? I tell you that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said something, and you say, 'Abu Bakr and Umar said something.'" The Ansar had forgotten his statement, "The leaders are from Quraysh." Anas related it, and Ubadah ibn al-Samit related something that indicates the same. They would only abandon their own reasoning when something from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) had reached them. Abu Hurayra relates that they, may Allah be pleased with them, were occupied with their wealth and trade, whereas he himself kept close to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) and witnessed what they did not. We have mentioned this hadith in the chapter on consensus in this book of ours, in a section entitled "Refuting the claim that when the majority agree on an opinion and one person disagrees with them, his opinion is disregarded." Our having mentioned it there makes it unnecessary to repeat it here.
If we find that a Companion was unaware of the Sunnah, or that it reached him and he interpreted it in various ways—as they did regarding the prohibition of domestic donkeys—al-Bukhari related that they differed: one said they were forbidden because they ate dung; another, because they had not been subjected to the fifth; another, because he feared the riding animals would perish. Some said, rather, that they were categorically forbidden at that time. Ali said: All of this is false except the view that they were categorically forbidden. A text has come prohibiting them in themselves, because they are impure; Anas related this. Since all that we have mentioned is sound, and blind imitation is entirely invalid, what reason is there to follow a Companion's opinion when it does not conflict with a report—let alone when it is accompanied by a conflict with a report? In the chapter on invalidating blind imitation in this book, we have recorded what they issued as legal opinions, though the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) informed them that it was not so. Ali said: Whenever those who evade the truth cling to things of this sort and the like, when confusion overwhelms them, they are the furthest of all people from it.
And it is stated in "Al-Ihkam fi Usul al-Ahkam by Ibn Hazm" (2/127):
"Knowledge of tayammum (dry ablution) was held by Ammar and others, while Umar and Ibn Masud did not know it, and said that a person in a state of major ritual impurity must not perform tayammum, even if he went two months without finding water. The ruling on wiping over footwear was known to Ali, Hudhayfa (may Allah be pleased with them), and others, but Aisha, Ibn Umar, and Abu Hurayra did not know it, though they were Medinans. The rule of inheritance whereby a son's son's daughter inherits alongside a daughter was known to Ibn Masud, but Abu Musa did not know it. The ruling on seeking permission was known to Abu Musa and Abu Said, but Umar did not know it. The ruling permitting a menstruating woman to depart..."
Before she circumambulated, Ibn Abbas and Umm Sulaym knew of it, while Umar and Zayd ibn Thabit did not; and the ruling prohibiting temporary marriage was
The ruling concerning domestic donkeys was known to Ali and others, but Ibn Abbas did not know it. The ruling on currency exchange was known to Umar, Abu Sa'id, and others, but Talha, Ibn Abbas, and Ibn Umar did not know it. The ruling on expelling the People of the Covenant from the lands of Arabia was known to Ibn Abbas and Umar, but Umar forgot it for years and left them there until he was reminded; then he remembered and expelled them. Knowledge of the inheritance case of kalala was held by some, but Umar did not know it. The prohibition on selling wine was known to Umar, but Samura did not know it. The ruling concerning a grandmother's inheritance was known to al-Mughira and Muhammad ibn Maslama, but Abu Bakr and Umar did not know it. The ruling on taking jizya from the Magians, and on not entering a land afflicted by plague, was known to Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, but Umar, Abu Ubayda, and the majority of the Companions (may Allah be pleased with them) did not know it. The ruling on a grandfather's inheritance was known to Ma'qil ibn Sinan, but Umar did not know it. There are very many examples of this kind. The Companions proceeded as we have described; after them came the Successors, who learned from them, and each generation of the Successors in the regions we have mentioned ))
Ibn Hazm said:
"Al-Ihkam fi Usul al-Ahkam by Ibn Hazm" (6/76):
"As for what they rely on, namely the report that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said to Abu Bakr and Umar, 'Were it not for your disagreement with me, I would not have disagreed with you,' the first point is that this report is unsound. Even if it were sound, it would be proof against following their opinions, because what it says is that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) prevented them from acting on their opinions in worldly matters. It is therefore incumbent upon us to follow him (peace and blessings be upon him) and not to adopt their opinions in matters of the Sharia. This is clear. As for his saying (peace and blessings be upon him), 'You must adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs,' we know that he (peace and blessings be upon him) would not command what is beyond one's ability. We find that the rightly guided caliphs after him (peace and blessings be upon him) differed greatly. Thus, there must be one of three possibilities, with no fourth: either we adopt everything over which they differed—and this is impossible and beyond our ability, since it entails accepting a thing and its opposite. It is not possible, for example, to give the grandfather a share to the exclusion of the siblings, following the view of Abu Bakr and Aisha, while giving him only one-third and the remainder to the siblings, following Umar's view, or giving him one-sixth and the remainder to the siblings, following Ali's school of thought, and likewise in every matter over which they differed. This possibility is therefore invalid.


[Page 44]

because people are unable to do it. This is one possibility; or it may be permissible for us to take any of these things. But this would be a departure from Islam, because it would mean that the religion of Allah, Exalted is He, was left to our choice, so that each of us could forbid whatever he wished and permit whatever he wished, and one of us could forbid what another permits. Allah, Exalted is He, says: {Forbidden to you are carrion, blood, the flesh of swine, and that over which any name other than Allah's has been invoked, and the strangled, the beaten to death, the fallen to death, the gored to death, and that which wild beasts have eaten—unless you have slaughtered it—and what has been slaughtered
...over the stone altars, and that you seek to determine your fate by means of divining arrows. That is wickedness. Today those who disbelieve have despaired of your religion, so do not fear them; fear Me. Today I have perfected your religion for you, completed My favor upon you, and chosen Islam as your religion. But whoever is compelled by severe hunger, without inclining toward sin—then Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful} And His saying, Exalted is He: {Divorce is twice; then either retain her honorably or release her kindly. It is not lawful for you to take anything of what you have given them, unless both fear that they cannot maintain the limits set by Allah. If you fear that they cannot maintain the limits set by Allah, then there is no blame upon either of them for what she gives up to secure her release. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not transgress them. Whoever transgresses the limits set by Allah—it is they who are the wrongdoers} And His saying, Exalted is He: {Obey Allah and His Messenger, and do not dispute, lest you lose heart and your strength depart. Be patient; indeed, Allah is with the patient} refutes this corrupt view and establishes that whatever was forbidden then remains forbidden until the Day of Resurrection; whatever was obligatory then remains obligatory until the Day of Resurrection; and whatever was lawful then remains lawful until the Day of Resurrection.
Also, if this were so, then when we followed the opinion of one of them, we would necessarily have abandoned the opinion of the other. In that case, we would not be following their practice; rather, we would have fallen into opposition to the hadith mentioned, as they themselves did, whether they wished it or not. I recall that this happened with a mufti we had in al-Andalus, who was ignorant. His custom was to be preceded by two men upon whom the issuing of fatwas in that period depended. Beneath their fatwas, he would write, "I hold the same opinion as the two shaykhs." It so happened that those two shaykhs disagreed. When he wrote beneath their fatwas what we have mentioned, someone present said to him, "The two shaykhs have disagreed." He replied, "And I disagree whenever they disagree." Abu Muhammad said: Since these two possibilities have been disproved, only the third remains: to follow what they unanimously agreed upon. That can only be what all the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, agreed upon together, and their following the practices of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and adhering to them. Furthermore, when the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) commanded that the practices of the rightly guided caliphs be followed, there must necessarily be one of two possibilities: either he (peace and blessings be upon him) permitted them to establish practices other than his own—and no Muslim would say this; whoever permits it has disbelieved and apostatized, and his life and property are lawful—or else the matter is otherwise. For the entire religion consists either of what is obligatory or what is not obligatory, and either what is forbidden or what is lawful; there is absolutely no other category in religion. Whoever permits the rightly guided caliphs to have a practice that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) did not establish has permitted them to forbid something that was lawful during his lifetime until he died, or to make lawful something the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) had forbidden, or to make obligatory a duty the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) had not made obligatory, or to waive a duty the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) had imposed.
And he did not abandon it until he died. Anyone who permits any of these possibilities is an unbeliever and an idolater, by the consensus of the entire Muslim community, without any disagreement. And Allah Most High is the source of success. This possibility has thus been disproved, praise be to Allah. As for the possibility that he commanded us to follow them in their adherence to his Sunnah (peace and blessings be upon him), this is precisely what we say: this hadith admits no other interpretation at all. Some have said, "We follow them only in matters for which there is no Sunnah." Abu Muhammad said: And since he did not
Nothing remains except this, and their contention has fallen away. There is nothing in the world without a textually established Sunnah concerning it. We have explained this in the chapter on invalidating analogy in this book. And success is from Allah, Exalted is He.
They argued on the basis of what was reported to us by Abd Allah ibn Rabi, who said: Muhammad ibn Muawiya related to us, saying: Ahmad ibn Shuaib related to us, saying: Muhammad ibn Bashshar related to us, saying: Abu Umar related to us, from Sufyan—that is, al-Thawri—from al-Shaybani—that is, Abu Ishaq—from al-Shabi, from Shurayh, that he wrote to Umar asking him a question. Umar wrote back to him: "Judge according to what is in the Book of Allah. If it is not in the Book of Allah, then according to the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). If it is in neither the Book of Allah nor the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), then judge according to what the righteous have judged. If it is in neither the Book of Allah nor the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), and the righteous have not judged concerning it, then, if you wish, go ahead, and if you wish, hold back. I consider holding back to be better for you. Peace be upon you." Abu Muhammad said: This report is against them, not in their favor, because Umar did not say, "according to what some of the righteous have judged"; rather, he said, "according to what the righteous have judged." That means the consensus of all the righteous. This report also shows that Umar permitted refraining from judgment by analogy and preferred doing so. They may also be told, regarding their argument based on the report commanding adherence to the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs, that this is evidence against them: the Sunnah of all the rightly guided caliphs, without any disagreement among them, was not to imitate anyone, nor to imitate one another, but to seek out the Sunnahs of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) wherever they found them, then turn to them and act upon them. Umar (may Allah be pleased with him) strongly rebuked a man who asked him a question about the pilgrimage. When Umar gave him a ruling, the man said, "The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) gave me this very ruling." Umar struck him with his whip and said, "You asked me about something on which the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) had already given a ruling, and I might have contradicted him." We have transmitted this through Abd al-Razzaq. Umar (may Allah be pleased with him) also said: "Opinion from us is a burden we take upon ourselves, whereas opinion from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) was truth." Abu Muhammad said: Whoever follows them should follow them in this matter on which they agreed: abandoning imitation; in what they unanimously agreed upon: following the Sunnahs of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him); and in what they forbade concerning the…


[Page 45]

Straining, for by doing so he agrees with the truth and with the words of Allah, Exalted is He, and the words of His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him). These caliphs were opposed by people of their own time: Zayd, Ali, and others opposed Umar; Umar opposed Uthman; and Umar opposed Abu Bakr in many cases. Yet none of them said to anyone who opposed him, "Why have you opposed me, when I am the imam?" Had following them by imitation been obligatory, they would not have allowed anyone to act otherwise than as required. As for the sophistry of those who cite the words of Allah, Exalted is He: {O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and fairest in outcome}, this verse invalidates imitation beyond any doubt. It is the greatest proof against them, because He, Exalted is He, commanded obedience to those in authority only in what they transmitted to us from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), and not in anything else. If they say, "Rather, it is in what they say by their own reasoning," we have already shown that this assumption is invalid. And even if we conceded that, it would only make obedience obligatory to all of them, not to some of them, because Allah, Mighty and Majestic, did not say, "some of those in authority among you." Rather, He commanded us to follow those in authority among us, meaning all the people of knowledge. When they reach consensus on a matter, there is no disagreement that following them is obligatory. Allah, Exalted is He, made this clear in the very same verse and left us in no ambiguity, saying: {O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and fairest in outcome}. Thus, when disagreement arises, Allah, Exalted is He, omitted referral to those in authority and required referral only to the Quran and the Sunnah. He commanded obedience to those in authority among us only so long as there is no disagreement. This is our position, and praise be to Allah. As for the report, "Muadh established this practice for you," we have said that it is a hadith whose chain of transmission is unsound. Even if it were sound, it would not constitute proof for them, because joining the imam in prayer is an established practice not because Muadh did it, but because the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) approved it and commanded it. His words were: "Whatever you catch, pray; and whatever you miss, make up." Otherwise, Muadh did something in prolonging the prayer that angered the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), who forbade him from doing it again. If what Muadh did were a Sunnah, then prolonging the prayer when leading people would be a Sunnah, and that is wrong. It is therefore established that neither Muadh's actions nor anyone else's are a Sunnah unless the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) commands and approves them. This is our position, not theirs. As for the report, "Follow the two who come after me," it is an unsound hadith, because it is narrated from an unknown client of Rib'i and from al-Mufaddal al-Dabbi, who is not a reliable authority. As Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn al-Jasur related to us: Ahmad ibn al-Fadl al-Dinawari related to us: Muhammad ibn Jubayr related to us: Abd al-Rahman ibn al-Aswad al-Tafawi related to us: Muhammad ibn Kathir al-Mulla'i related to us: al-Mufaddal al-Dabbi, from Dirar ibn Murra, from Abd Allah ibn Abi al-Hudhayl al-Atri, from his grandmother, from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), who said: "Follow"
by the two who will come after me, Abu Bakr and Umar; follow the guidance of Ammar and hold fast to the covenant of Ibn Umm Abd.
And as Ahmad ibn Qasim related to us, he said: My father, Qasim ibn Muhammad ibn Qasim ibn Asbagh, told us: Qasim ibn Asbagh told me: Ismail ibn Ishaq the Judge told us: Muhammad ibn Kathir told us: Sufyan al-Thawri, from Abd al-Malik ibn Umayr, from the freedman of al-Rabi, from Hudhayfa, who said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, "Follow the example of the two who come after me, Abu Bakr and Umar; follow the guidance of Ammar, and hold fast to the covenant of Ibn Umm Abd." We also received it from one of our companions, from the Judge Abu al-Walid ibn al-Faradi, from Ibn al-Dakhil, from al-Uqayli: Muhammad ibn Ismail told us: Muhammad ibn Fudayl told us: Waki told us: Salim al-Muradi, from Amr ibn Hurm, from Rabi ibn Hirash and Abu Abd Allah, a man among Hudhayfa's companions, from Hudhayfa. Abu Muhammad said: Salim is weak. Some have named the freedman, saying he was Hilal, the freedman of Rabi, but he is unknown; no one knows who he actually was. Even if it were sound, it would count against them, not in their favor, for they—that is, the followers of Malik, Abu Hanifa, and al-Shafii—are the people who depart most from Abu Bakr and Umar. We have shown that the followers of Malik contradicted Abu Bakr in five instances, specifically in what they transmitted in the Muwatta, and contradicted Umar in about thirty cases, specifically in what they transmitted in the Muwatta. We have also mentioned that Umar and Abu Bakr differed, and that following both of them in matters on which they differed is impossible and unattainable; no one can do it. What is sound in this chapter is what one of our companions gave me and what Yusuf ibn Abd Allah ibn Abd al-Barr al-Namari also related to me. Both of them received it from Abu al-Walid Abd Allah ibn Yusuf the Judge, from Ibn al-Dakhil, from al-Uqayli: Muhammad ibn Ismail told us: Ismail ibn Abi Uways, from Abd Allah ibn Abi Abd Allah al-Basri and Thawr ibn Yazid al-Dayli, from Ikrima, from Ibn Abbas, who said: The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said, "Understand my words, O people, for I have conveyed the message. I have left among you, O people, that which, if you hold fast to it, you will never go astray: the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Prophet."))
And through him, al-Uqayli narrated to us: Musa ibn Ishaq narrated to us; Muhammad ibn Ubayd al-Muharibi narrated to us; Salih ibn Musa al-Talhi narrated to us, from Abd al-Aziz ibn Rafi, from Abu Salih, from Abu Hurayra, who said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said:
"I have left among you two things; you will never go astray after them so long as you hold fast to them or act upon them: the Book of Allah and my Sunnah. They will not part company until they come to me at the Pool."
As for the report, "My Companions are like the stars," it is a spurious narration. This is a hadith that was related to me by

Abu al-Abbas Ahmad ibn Umar ibn Anas al-Udhri, who said: Abu Dharr Abd ibn Ahmad ibn Muhammad al-Harawi al-Ansari related to us; he said: Ali ibn Umar ibn Ahmad al-Daraqutni related to us; he said: Judge Ahmad Kamil ibn Kamil Khalaf narrated to us; he said: Abdullah ibn Ruh narrated to us; he said: Salam ibn Sulayman narrated to us; he said: al-Harith ibn Ghussayn narrated to us, from al-A'mash, from Abu


[Page 46]

Sufyan, from Jabir, said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, "My Companions are like stars; whichever of them you follow, you will be guided." Abu Muhammad said: Abu Sufyan is weak, and al-Harith ibn Ghussayn is Abu Wahb al-Thaqafi. Salam ibn Sulayman narrates fabricated hadiths, and this is certainly one of them. Thus, this is a rejected report transmitted through a weak chain of narration. Abu Umar Yusuf ibn Abd Allah ibn Abd al-Barr al-Namari wrote to me that this hadith was also narrated through Abd al-Rahman ibn Zayd al-Ammi, from his father, from Said ibn al-Musayyib, from Ibn Umar; and through Hamzah al-Jazari, from Nafi, from Ibn Umar. He said: Abd al-Rahim ibn Zayd and his father are both abandoned narrators, and Hamzah al-Jazari is unknown. Al-Namari wrote to me: Muhammad ibn Ibrahim ibn Said told us that Abu Abd al-Rahman ibn Mufarrij related to them, saying: Muhammad ibn Ayyub al-Samut told us that al-Bazzar said, "As for what is narrated from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), 'My Companions are like stars; whichever of them you follow, you will be guided,' this is a statement that is not soundly established as coming from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him)." Abu Muhammad said: It has thus become clear that this report is not established at all; there is no doubt that it is fabricated, because Allah, Exalted is He, says of His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), {Nor does he speak from his own desire. It is only a revelation sent down to him.} Since everything he (peace and blessings be upon him) says concerning the Sharia is true, it is all from Allah, Exalted is He, without doubt; and what is from Allah, Exalted is He, contains no contradiction, as He says: {Do they not reflect upon the Quran? Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have found much contradiction in it.} Allah, Exalted is He, has forbidden division and disagreement, saying: {Obey Allah and His Messenger, and do not dispute, lest you lose heart and your strength depart.}
"And be patient; indeed, Allah is with the patient." It is impossible that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) would command people to follow every speaker among the Companions (may Allah be pleased with them), when some of them deem something lawful and others deem it unlawful. If that were so, selling wine would be lawful, in imitation of Samura ibn
Jundub. Eating hail would be lawful for a fasting person, following the example of Abu Talha, and unlawful, following that of others among them. Omitting the ritual bath after intercourse without ejaculation would be obligatory, following the example of Ali, Uthman, Talha, Abu Ayyub, and Ubayy ibn Ka'b, and unlawful, following that of Aisha and Ibn Umar. Selling fruit before signs of ripeness appear on it would be lawful, following the example of Umar, and unlawful, following that of others among them. All of this is transmitted in our sources with chains of transmission. End quote.
The upshot of what we have mentioned is that there is no explicit evidence establishing the authority of the opinion of any of the Companions, let alone those of lesser rank. If they say that their opinion is authoritative in matters of belief, then whoever disagrees with them is deemed an unbeliever.
The answer is: Ibn Taymiyya said: "He also said in Mukhtasar al-Fatawa al-Misriyya: 'The division of issues into fundamental issues, denial of which constitutes disbelief, and subsidiary issues, denial of which does not constitute disbelief, has no basis among the Companions, the Successors, or the imams of Islam. Rather, it was adopted from the Mu'tazila and similar imams of innovation, who are inconsistent. For when they are asked, "What defines the fundamentals of religion?" and they answer, "Issues of belief," they may be told: People have disagreed over whether Muhammad saw his Lord, whether Uthman or Ali was superior, many meanings of the Quran, and the authentication of some hadiths. These are matters of belief, yet there is no disbelief in them, by the agreement of the Muslims. The obligation of prayer, zakat, and pilgrimage, and the prohibition of indecencies and wine, are practical matters, yet one who denies them is unanimously judged to be an unbeliever ...'"
If it is said that the foundational principles are those that are definitive, it may be replied that many matters of inquiry are not definitive. Whether a matter is definitive or speculative varies from person to person: what is definitive for one may not be definitive for another, as in the case of someone who hears the wording of a text and is certain of its intended meaning, while another text does not carry the same force for him; for him, it is not speculative, let alone definitive...
The point is that the positions of the leading scholars distinguish between the category and the individual case, and whoever has reported a disagreement has not grasped the depth of their statement ...
One group relates two reports from Ahmad concerning the unrestricted excommunication of the people of innovation. But this is not the position of Ahmad or of any of the other imams. Likewise, al-Shafi'i declared Hafs al-Fard an unbeliever when he said, "The Quran is created." Al-Shafi'i said to him, "You have disbelieved"—that is, what you said is disbelief. For this reason, he did not seek to have him killed; had he considered him an unbeliever, he would have sought to have him killed.
It is clear from what has preceded that the Companions differed over some matters of belief on the basis of interpretation, yet they did not declare one another unbelievers. It will be shown explicitly later that Ibn Abbas did not declare the Kharijites unbelievers, but regarded them as people who interpreted the texts and excused them on that basis. If their statements are authoritative, then they must follow them in this matter of excusing people on the basis of interpretation: they must not declare an interpreter an unbeliever, even if his interpretation falls under what they have included among matters of belief. Otherwise, they will have contradicted themselves. God willing, we will devote a separate discussion to the matter of excusing people on the basis of interpretation.


[Page 47]

Everything stated above concerning the lack of probative authority in a Companion's opinion applies all the more to what has been transmitted from the Successors and those after them. However, what I have observed, after examining the statements of their theorists, is that they have not produced a single text from the Companions declaring an interpreter among the people of heretical sects to be an unbeliever, or declaring one who excuses him to be an unbeliever. I cite here two reports, one from Qadi Iyad and the other from Ibn Hazm, showing that the Companions' position was to excuse people on the basis of interpretation.
(What has been transmitted from the Companions concerning declaring those who interpret texts to be unbelievers)(1)((Qadi Iyad said: "People have differed over declaring those who interpret texts to be unbelievers. Once you understand this, the reason for their disagreement will become clear to you, and the correct position is to refrain from declaring them unbelievers.
And refraining from definitively condemning them to perdition, while applying the rulings of Islam to them in matters of retaliation, inheritance, marriage, blood money, funeral prayers, burial in Muslim cemeteries, and all other dealings with them. However, they are dealt with sternly through painful discipline, severe rebuke, and social boycott until they abandon their innovation. This was the practice of the earliest generation toward them. In the time of the Companions and afterward, among the Successors, there arose people who held these views concerning predestination, as well as the Khawarij and the Mu'tazila. Yet they did not disinter their graves or deprive any of them of inheritance; rather, they boycotted them and disciplined them with beatings, exile, or execution, according to their circumstances, because they were disobedient, misguided sinners who committed major sins. (I say: See here the account of Ali and Ibn Abbas with the Khawarij, which I will mention later: Ibn Abbas said to the Khawarij, regarding Aisha, "Are you insulting your mother?" This clearly shows that Ibn Abbas did not declare them unbelievers, since he called Aisha the Mother of the Believers and their mother. Al-Ash'ari has quoted the statement, "All the Khawarij say that the Quran was created" (Maqalat al-Islamiyyin, 108). This is evidence that Ibn Abbas excused those among them who held their view through interpretation; nevertheless, the Companions did not declare them unbelievers on that account.)


[Page 48]

He said: "Among those who transmitted the other view—that they should not be declared unbelievers—were Ali ibn Abi Talib, Ibn Umar, and al-Hasan al-Basri. This was also the view of a group of jurists, speculative theologians, and scholars of kalam. They cited as evidence the fact that the Companions and Successors granted inheritance to the heirs of the people of Harura and to those known for holding the doctrine of predestination who had died, buried them in Muslim cemeteries, and applied the rulings of Islam to them."

Ibn Umar (may Allah be pleased with him) used to pray behind Najda the Kharijite.

It is stated in Usul al-Sunna by Ibn Abi Zamanin (p. 283):

"Asad said: al-Rabi ibn Zayd told me, from Sawwar ibn Shabib, who said: Najda al-Haruri made the pilgrimage with his companions and made a truce with Ibn al-Zubayr. One of them led the people in prayer for a day and a night, and the other led them for a day and a night. Ibn Umar prayed behind them both. A man confronted him and said, 'Abu Abd al-Rahman, do you pray behind Najda al-Haruri?'

Ibn Umar replied, 'When they call, "Come to the best of deeds," we answer; and when they call, "Come to kill a person," we say, "No."' He raised his voice as he said this."

Shaykh al-Islam Ibn Taymiyya (may Allah have mercy on him) said: "Abd Allah ibn Umar and other Companions used to pray behind al-Hajjaj ibn Yusuf. The Companions and Successors also used to pray behind Ibn Abi Ubayd, who was accused of heresy and called people to misguidance." (7) Majmu al-Fatawa, 3/281


[Page 49]

Ibn Hazm said: "A group held that no Muslim is declared an unbeliever or a sinner on account of something he says concerning belief or a legal opinion, and that anyone who exercises independent reasoning in any of these matters and adheres to what he considers to be the truth is rewarded in all cases: if he is right, he receives two rewards, and if he is mistaken, one reward. This was the view of Ibn Abi Layla, Abu Hanifa, al-Shafi'i, Sufyan al-Thawri, and Dawud ibn Ali, may Allah be pleased with them all. It is also the view of everyone among the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, whom we know to have expressed an opinion on this issue. We know of no disagreement among them at all, except for the disagreement we have mentioned concerning declaring an unbeliever anyone who deliberately leaves the prayer until its time has passed, or fails to pay zakat, perform Hajj, or fast Ramadan, or drinks wine. Those who declared people unbelievers on account of disagreements in matters of belief cited various arguments, which we shall present, if Allah, Exalted is He, wills."

But they are not satisfied with Ibn Hazm or al-Qadi Iyad, because, in their view, they are Ash'aris or Jahmis. Yet they often contradict themselves: their books are full of quotations from those they accuse of heresy, such as Ibn Taymiyya, Ibn al-Qayyim, Ibn Kathir, and others, whenever they find in them something that supports their position. I do not know how to make sense of this contradiction: at one moment these men are unbelievers whose statements are unacceptable, and at another their views and arguments are cited. Therefore, I will discuss the issue in detail later, with regard to the statements of the Companions on this matter, if Allah wills.

The second issue: their reliance on the statements of the Companions and Successors. As noted above, they have not relied on a single statement of the Companions on this issue at all. Yet they have laid down the principle that even reports with weak chains of transmission may be acted upon. Although rejecting weak reports in matters of belief and rulings—even when attributed to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him)—is now almost universally accepted, some groups have added another condition alongside soundness: if the matter concerns belief, the report must also have been transmitted by mass transmission. How, then, can these people deem it permissible to accept weak reports in matters of belief, especially reports from someone other than the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), as a result of this dubious doctrine of theirs?


[Page 50]

They relied on reports transmitted from the early generations that had been judged weak by hadith scholars, and which contain disparagement of the prophets and angels, such as the story of Harut and Marut, the story of Joseph's passion, the story of David's sin, the story of Solomon's ring, and the story attributing shirk to Adam and naming his son Abd al-Harith so that he might live, as they claim. I do not know what their leading scholar intended by this. Moreover, he criticized Ibn Kathir for rejecting these hadiths, and I do not know what he intended by defending this disparagement of the prophets found in these weak hadiths. This is very suspicious. Likewise, they rely on statements attributed to some early scholars that the earth is flat, while disregarding other reports and the scholarly consensus transmitted from scholars that it is spherical. They then made this one of the matters of belief, declaring anyone who disagrees an unbeliever, and consequently declaring most Muslims, past and present, unbelievers on that basis. Also, for example, it is astonishing and suspicious that they deliberately weaken reports attributed to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) that are authentic according to some hadith scholars, but which contradict their school of thought, and refuse to rely on them because, in their view, they are weak. For example, their leading scholar did this with the report about the man who scattered his own ashes, in which he says, "Perhaps I will escape Allah." This report explicitly shows that the man was ignorant of the attribute of power, and it was transmitted through a chain of narrators that many scholars deem sound. I think he himself deems it sound, but he ignored this chain, produced another chain that is weak, and rejected the report on that basis, saying it was weak. As you can see, sometimes reports from people below the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) are treated as proof, even when weak, while at other times reports from the Prophet himself (peace and blessings be upon him) are not treated as proof because they contradict their school of thought. There is no might or power except through Allah.
Although it is clear that acting upon weak reports in matters of creed and rulings is impermissible, especially when the view in question is that of a Companion, there is no harm in briefly addressing this issue so that the discussion may be complete, God willing.
Ibn Taymiyya said: "What the scholars do in acting upon a weak hadith does not mean establishing that something is recommended on the basis of a hadith that cannot be used as proof, for recommendation is a legal ruling and can only be established by evidence.


[Page 51]

It is a matter of the Sharia. Whoever claims on God's authority that He loves a particular deed without Sharia evidence has prescribed as part of the religion what God has not authorized, just as if he were to establish an obligation or a prohibition... What they mean by this is that the deed must be one that has been established, by a text or consensus, as something God loves or dislikes, such as reciting the Quran, glorifying God, supplication, charity, freeing slaves, treating people well, and disliking lies, treachery, and the like.
If a hadith is narrated concerning the merit and reward of some recommended deeds, or the disapproval and punishment of some deeds, then, when a hadith is narrated about the amounts and kinds of reward and punishment and we do not know it to be fabricated, it is permissible to narrate it and act upon it. This means that a person may hope for that reward or fear that punishment. It is like someone who knows that trade is profitable but has heard that it yields a great profit: if that is true, it benefits him, and if it is false, it does him no harm. An example of this is exhortation and warning through Israelite reports, dreams, sayings of the early generations and scholars, accounts of scholars, and the like. Such things, on their own, cannot establish a Sharia ruling, whether recommendation or anything else, but they may be mentioned for exhortation and warning, hope and fear.
If weak hadiths about merits specify and delimit something—for example, a prayer at a particular time, with a particular recitation, or in a particular manner—then this is not permissible, because the recommendation of that specific form has not been established by Sharia evidence. This differs from a report that whoever enters the marketplace and says, "There is no god but Allah..." will receive such-and-such, since remembering God in the marketplace is recommended because it involves remembering God among the heedless...
As for the specified reward reported in it, it does no harm whether or not it is established.
The upshot is that this material may be narrated and acted upon for exhortation and warning, not to establish recommendation. Belief in what it entails—that is, the amounts of reward and punishment—depends on Sharia evidence. ))End quote

(Evidence for the impermissibility of acting on a weak hadith)


[Page 52]

There is no doubt that weak hadiths do not yield knowledge or preponderant probability; rather, they yield probability in the sense of doubt. This kind of probability is forbidden to follow in the Book and the Sunnah, and its adherent is blameworthy: he speaks about Allah and His Messenger without knowledge, and this is among the gravest sins.
Ibn Hazm said: "Ibn Hazm (may Allah have mercy on him) also said in al-Ihkam (1/133):
'Among the errors made by some hadith scholars is their saying: "So-and-so may be relied upon in matters of exhortation, but not in legal rulings."
Abu Muhammad (that is, Ibn Hazm) said: This is false, because it is an invalid division for which there is no proof; indeed, the proof refutes it. That is because every person on earth must be either sinful or not sinful. If he is not sinful, then he is upright, and there is no third category.
The upright person falls into two categories: a jurist and a non-jurist. The upright jurist is accepted in every matter, while the sinful person is not relied upon in anything.
The upright person who is not a memorizer is not accepted in any matter whatsoever, because the condition for acceptance specified by Allah Most High is not present in him (that is, His saying, "so that they may gain understanding of the religion and warn their people"). Whoever is upright in some of what he transmits is upright in the rest of it.
