It is impossible to deem some of his reports acceptable and the rest unacceptable unless there is a text from Allah Most High or a consensus distinguishing between them. Otherwise, this is arbitrary judgment without proof and speaking without knowledge, which is impermissible.' End quote."
Ibn Hazm said: "Al-Ihkam fi Usul al-Ahkam by Ibn Hazm (1/22):
'And Allah Most High said: {Rather, those who do wrong follow their desires without knowledge. So who can guide one whom Allah has led astray? They will have no helpers.} Thus, as you hear, Allah Most High has informed us that whoever follows a statement that accords with his view without knowing that it is sound is a wrongdoer, even if


[Page 53]

Whoever does not return to the truth he hears is among the people of the Fire. Allah, Exalted is He, says: {If they do not respond to you, then know that they are only following their desires. And who is more astray than one who follows his desire without guidance from Allah? Indeed, Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.} Allah, Exalted is He, also condemned a person for denying what he does not know, saying: {Rather, they denied what they had no knowledge of, and whose interpretation had not yet come to them. Thus did those before them deny. So see what was the end of the wrongdoers.} Thus, all that we have mentioned establishes the duty to refrain from what we do not know and to return to what the proof requires once it has been established.

Allah, Exalted is He, says: {And who is more unjust than one who fabricates a lie against Allah or denies the truth when it comes to him? Is there not a dwelling in Hell for the disbelievers?} Abu Muhammad said: This verse suffices to establish that no one should affirm anything unless proof has been established for it, and that no one should do anything when proof has been established against it. Who is more unjust than one who knows what we have mentioned, yet follows a whisper arising within himself, or a report for which no proof has been established that it must be believed, or imitates another person like himself, who may, in Allah's sight, be mistaken in what he thinks? In any case, that person is not infallible; he may be right or wrong. Allah, Exalted is He, says: {And they say, "None will enter Paradise unless he is a Jew or a Christian." Those are their wishes. Say, "Produce your proof, if you are truthful."}
Thus, He, Exalted is He, required whoever is truthful in his claim to produce proof; if he does not produce proof,
then he is a liar, one who asserts falsehood, or an ignorant person. Allah, Exalted is He, says: {Here you are, disputing about that of which you have knowledge; why, then, do you dispute about that of which you have no knowledge? Allah knows, and you do not know.} Thus, He, Exalted is He, required disputation only on the basis of knowledge and prohibited it without knowledge.

Allah, Exalted is He, says: {They will say, "They were three, the fourth of them being their dog," and they will say, "They were five, the sixth of them being their dog," guessing at the unseen. And they will say, "They were seven, and the eighth of them was their dog." Say, "My Lord knows best their number; none knows them except a few." So do not argue about them except with an evident argument, and do not ask any of them about them.} Abu Muhammad said: Since we found that Allah, Exalted is He, commanded argument and debate in the verses we have mentioned, and did not require acceptance of anything except on the basis of proof, we were obliged to seek out arguments.


[Page 54]

The blameworthy kind, as we have explained. We find that He said: {We send the messengers only as bearers of good news and warners. Those who disbelieve argue with falsehood in order to refute the truth by it, and they take My signs and the warnings given to them in mockery.} **
Thus, as you see, Exalted is He condemned arguing without proof and arguing for falsehood. In this way, Exalted is He refuted the claim of the deluded: that every person led astray is furnished with an argument. Exalted is He made clear that the deluded person is one who is not furnished with an argument, whereas the one in the right is truly furnished with an argument—and they are the people of truth. Exalted is He also said: {Those who argue about the signs of Allah without any authority having come to them—it is greatly hateful in the sight of Allah and in the sight of those who believe. Thus does Allah seal the heart of every arrogant tyrant.} These verses together explain both blameworthy argumentation and praiseworthy, obligatory argumentation. The obligatory kind is that in which the person arguing seeks to make the truth manifest. The blameworthy kind has two forms, as stated in the verses we have cited: first, arguing without knowledge; and second, arguing in support of falsehood through contentiousness and deception after the truth has become clear to him. This makes clear that the truth is one, and that nothing is valid except what is established by rational proof. These blameworthy people are those of whom Exalted is He said: {Have you not seen those who argue about the signs of Allah—how they are turned away?} And His saying: {Among the people is one who argues about Allah without knowledge and follows every rebellious devil.} And His saying: {Among the people is one who argues about Allah without knowledge, guidance, or an illuminating Book, turning his neck aside to lead others astray from the path of Allah. For him there is disgrace in this world, and on the Day of Resurrection We shall make him taste the punishment of the Fire.} And His saying:
Exalted is He: {None argue about the signs of Allah except those who disbelieve, so let not their movement through the lands deceive you. Before them, the people of Noah and the factions after them denied the truth,

and every nation plotted against its messenger to seize him, and they argued with falsehood in order to refute the truth by it. So I seized them; and how was My punishment?} Thus, as you see, Exalted is He made clear that forbidden argumentation is argumentation used to support falsehood and refute the truth without knowledge.))
«He said in al-Ihkam fi Usul al-Ahkam by Ibn Hazm» (1/65):


[Page 55]

"As for one to whom an unsound report from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) has reached, and an interpreter, an ignorant person, or a sinner whom he did not know to be sinful has deemed it authentic for him, that is the extent of his knowledge. If he acts on the falsehood that has reached him, he is excused on account of his ignorance and bears no sin, for he did not deliberately incline toward sin, and deeds are judged by intentions. He is a mujtahid, rewarded once for his intention and purpose of seeking good and obedience to Allah and His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him). If he acts contrary to what has reached him, then the sin is that of one who takes lightly his opposition to the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him): either he does so knowingly, in which case he is a sinner, or he does so with that intention, in which case he is an unbeliever. And success is from Allah, Exalted is He."

He said: "The correct view is that we have found Allah, Exalted is He, to condemn those who affirm something without knowledge, and to condemn those who deny something without knowledge. He says, Exalted is He: {Say: My Lord has only forbidden indecencies, both outward and inward, sin, wrongful aggression, associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and saying about Allah what you do not know.} Allah, Exalted is He, has forbidden, in the explicit wording of this verse, anyone to say anything about Allah, Mighty and Majestic, whose truth he does not know. The truth of anything beyond the first principles of reason and the immediate evidence of the senses can be known only through proof. Thus, this verse obliges anyone who claims to establish something to produce proof for it; otherwise, he has done what is forbidden to him. And He says, Exalted is He: {Rather, they have denied what they did not comprehend in knowledge, and whose interpretation had not yet come to them. In the same way, those before them denied. So see what was the end of the wrongdoers.} Thus, He, Exalted is He, condemned a person for denying something without knowing that it is false. And He says, Exalted is He: {They say, 'None shall enter Paradise unless he is a Jew or a Christian.' Those are their wishes. Say: 'Bring your proof, if you are truthful.'} Thus, Allah, Exalted is He, required everyone who claims to be truthful to produce proof; otherwise, his statement is invalid. We have found that everyone who denies something claims to be truthful in denying it, and everyone who affirms something claims to be truthful in affirming it. Therefore, both groups are obliged to produce proof for their claims, if they are truthful. Ali said: As for those among our companions who argue that proof is not required of one who denies something, citing the Messenger of Allah's (peace and blessings be upon him) ruling that the claimant must produce evidence and the one who denies must swear an oath, this applies only in...


[Page 56]

As for legal rulings, there is no disagreement among the people of the faith that no oath is required of someone who denies something in a debate concerning matters other than legal rulings. Ali said: When two disputants disagree, one affirming something and the other denying it, each must provide evidence for the truth of his claim, as we explained above, in accordance with the ruling of the words of Allah, Exalted is He. Whichever of them establishes proof, his statement is correct. They cannot both establish proof, because the truth cannot lie in two opposites, and it is impossible for something to be both false and true at the same time and in the same respect. If both are unable to establish evidence—and this is possible—the ruling on that matter is to suspend judgment: neither affirming nor denying it, but leaving it within the realm of possibility. For if the one affirming it established proof, the matter would indeed be affirmed as true; and if the one denying it established proof, it would be false and denied. If neither of them establishes proof, it is said of that matter: "It is possible that it is true, and possible that it is false; but we neither affirm it nor rule by it, nor do we assert categorically that it is false." This is precisely what the words of Allah, Exalted is He, state: {Do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge. Indeed, the hearing, the sight, and the heart—all of these will be questioned.} It has also been narrated from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), in a hadith concerning the People of the Book: "We neither affirm nor deny; rather, we say: Allah knows best."
((Allah, Exalted is He, says: {Do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge. Indeed, the hearing, the sight, and the heart—all of these will be questioned.} And He says, censuring a people who said: {And when it was said, 'Allah's promise is true, and there is no doubt about the Hour,' you said, 'We do not know what the Hour is. We only suppose, and we are not certain.'} And He says: {They are nothing but names that you and your forefathers have given them; Allah has sent down no authority for them. They follow nothing but conjecture and what their souls desire, though guidance has certainly come to them from their Lord.} And He says: {They have no knowledge of it; they follow nothing but conjecture, and conjecture avails nothing against the truth.} Thus, by the explicit words of Allah, Exalted is He, from which no Muslim turns away, it is established that conjecture is other than the truth; and since it is other than the truth, it is falsehood and a lie, without doubt, for there is no third possibility. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said: 'Beware of conjecture, for conjecture is the most false of speech.' Abu Muhammad said: This is the truth, and no one who hears it can turn away from it.))
Thus, all the evidence we have cited, including that cited by the scholars, establishes that it is forbidden to speak about Allah without knowledge. They do not disagree with us that a weak hadith yields neither knowledge nor, as some put it, predominant conjecture; rather,


[Page 57]

It conveys only conjecture, in the sense of doubt. So how can they rely on it after that, even if it comes from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him)? What, then, of something coming from someone below him, whose statements are not authoritative in the first place? It is noticeable that this group often say and repeat, "We transmit from the early generations, we follow the early generations, and our school is the school of the early generations," yet you will find no precise definition of whom they mean by this. So I would like here to touch on the question of who the early generations are.

(Definition of the early generations)
In al-Nihaya fi Gharib al-Athar (4/81), it says:
In the report, "[The best of you are my generation, then those who come after them]," this means the Companions, then the Successors. A generation (qarn) is the people of each era, and it is the approximate span of the lifespans of the people of each era. The word derives from iqtiran, as though it were the span during which the people of that era are close to one another in their lifespans and circumstances.
It is also said that a generation is forty years; it is also said, eighty; and it is also said, one hundred. It is also said that the term is unrestricted as to time. It is the verbal noun of qarana, yaqrinu. End quote. On the basis of this definition, if by "the early generations" they mean those whose views are authoritative on the issue, according to their position—even though I have explained that the statement of a Companion himself is not authoritative—then, since this is the position of some, I have met them on their own terms. Ibn Muflih said in Usul al-Fiqh: "The position of a Successor is not authoritative according to Ahmad and the scholars, because that would lead to an infinite regress. Some Hanafis have reported two narrations from him." End quote.


[Page 58]

Al-Zarkashi said in al-Bahr al-Muhit: "If a Successor states an opinion on a matter in which there is no room for analogical reasoning, it is not accorded the status of a Companion in our view, contrary to al-Sam'ani, as mentioned earlier. The author of al-Ghaya, among the Hanbalis, said: 'If someone gets up from a night's sleep and dips his hand into a vessel before washing it, al-Hasan al-Basri held that its purifying quality is lost. This contradicts analogical reasoning. When a Successor says something like this, it is authoritative, since it is most likely that he reported it on the authority of the Companions, or on the basis of a text established for him.' The author of al-Musawwadah said: 'The apparent meaning of Ahmad's words and those of our companions is that this is not taken into consideration; rather, it is treated like his own independent judgments.' End quote." At the very most, and by way of conceding as much as possible, if the Successors' opinions are authoritative, then they must stop at the Successors. Why, then, do they not cite those who came after the Successors in support of their position? I know of no one who holds that the opinions of the Successors of the Successors are authoritative. And if their argument is that matters of creed leave no room for personal opinion, then this must lead to an infinite regress here; they would have to regard the opinion of every scholar who has addressed a matter of creed, right up to our own time, as authoritative, since such matters are not based on personal opinion. Yet they would never accept this.
The era of the Successors of the Successors is the era following that of the Successors (may Allah be pleased with them). It began around the year 170 AH.
Al-Suyuti said: "The sounder view is that a generation—that is, a century—is not defined by a fixed period. The generation of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) comprised the Companions, and their period extended from the beginning of his mission until the death of the last of the Companions, a span of one hundred and twenty years. The generation of the Successors lasted from one hundred years to around seventy, and the generation of the Successors of the Successors extended from then until around the year 220." End quote.
On the basis of this division, they would have to refrain from citing the statements of scholars who came after the year 170. I have said all this only by way of concession.


[Page 59]

This is Ibn Hajar's account of his concise presentation and division of the generations, may Allah have mercy on him.

The generations: The first: the Companions, with their varying ranks, distinguishing those among them who had nothing more than merely seeing the Prophet from others.
The second: the generation of the senior Successors, such as Ibn al-Musayyib. If someone was a mukhadram, I stated that explicitly.
The third: the middle generation of the Successors, such as al-Hasan and Ibn Sirin.
The fourth: the generation that follows them, most of whose narrations are from senior Successors, such as al-Zuhri and Qatada.
The fifth: the junior generation among them, those who saw one or two Companions, but for some of whom hearing from the Companions is not established, such as al-A'mash.
The sixth: a generation contemporary with the fifth, but for whom meeting any of the Companions is not established, such as Ibn Jurayj.
These people must therefore stop at this generation and not cite those below it as evidence. Yet their books are replete with citations of those below that generation, namely the following, as Ibn Hajar said:
The seventh: the generation of the Successors of the Successors, such as Malik and al-Thawri.
The eighth: the middle generation among them, such as Ibn Uyayna and Ibn Ulayya.


[Page 60]

The ninth: the junior generation of the Successors to the Successors, such as Yazid ibn Harun, al-Shafi'i, Abu Dawud al-Tayalisi, and Abd al-Razzaq.
The tenth: the senior scholars who took knowledge from the Successors to the Successors, but did not meet the Successors, such as Ahmad ibn Hanbal.
The eleventh: the middle generation of these scholars, such as al-Dhuhli and al-Bukhari.
The twelfth generation: the junior scholars who took knowledge from the Successors to the Successors, such as al-Tirmidhi.
End quote.
Al-Shafi'i died in 204, and Abd al-Razzaq in 211. Note that, according to this classification, Ibn Hanbal does not even belong among the Successors to the Successors, and so falls outside the hadith about the best generations—if that is your proof for citing these scholars. But we will meet you partway. Before examining the evidence concerning the issue of declaring someone an unbeliever on account of an erroneous interpretation, we will here mention the statements of some of the early scholars who held that one who interprets erroneously is not to be declared an unbeliever, and report the disagreement on this matter. Allah is the one whose help is sought. We do this before discussing the evidence. If you appeal to the early scholars, then here are some of their statements, explicit in excusing the ignorant person who has not been negligent, and the one who interprets erroneously. So refer the matter to Allah and the Messenger, and do not claim consensus on this issue. According to your own position, you are obliged to rely on their statements, whether or not their attribution to them is authentic, just as you did previously. And from


[Page 61]

What is remarkable here is that, after citing weak statements from some scholars concerning the excommunication of anyone who denies some of the attributes—categorically, without discussing the case of a particular individual—when they came across a report from al-Shafi'i excusing someone ignorant of an attribute, they rejected it and said that al-Hakkari's report from al-Shafi'i is weak. So sometimes a weak report is proof when it agrees with their views, and sometimes it is not proof when it contradicts them. Can there be any religion left after that? Let the reader of this book know that we rely only on the Book or an authentic Sunnah. Nothing else constitutes proof for us. But we will meet the proponent of the view on his own terms in order to demonstrate the corruption of his doctrine.

(The ruling on the one who interprets) There is a difference between this issue and that of the heedless ignorant person. The view that seems strongest to the researcher is that someone who turns away from learning is not excused for affirming or denying of Allah what he does not know. But if he holds the general belief and is ignorant of something that he is not required to know in detail, then he is excused so long as he remains ignorant, does not affirm or deny anything in religion without knowledge, entrusts knowledge of it to Allah, and refrains from judgment. The present issue, however, is a different one: the discussion here concerns the one who interprets, by whom I mean a man who has exerted his utmost effort to understand the words of Allah


[Page 62]

and the words of His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), to the best of his ability, but he erred in the conclusion he reached. This is what I mean here by the person interpreting the texts: the researcher considers him excused, because he did not turn away from the truth but exerted his utmost effort, yet was not guided to it. He misunderstood; he did not act with bad intent or neglect the pursuit of knowledge. Rather, he exerted his utmost effort to know the truth, but failed to reach it. I will begin by citing the scholars' statements, then discussing their evidence, Allah Most High willing.
(Interpretation and the punishment of one who interprets the texts) Ibn Taymiyya said: "What I have mentioned concerning a Muslim's abandoning an obligation or committing a prohibition on the basis of an interpretation arising from independent reasoning or following another's opinion is clear to me, and his situation in this regard is better than that of an unbeliever who interprets the texts. This does not preclude my fighting a rebellious person who interprets the texts, flogging a drinker who interprets the texts, and the like; for interpretation does not altogether remove worldly punishment, since the purpose of punishment is to prevent the harm of transgression." End quote. (Majmu al-Fatawa 22/14)
He also said: "There is no one among these who belongs to the early generations of the community or its leading scholars; rather, each of them is a Muslim who is criticized for an innovation, even if he interprets the texts in support of it, and his error is forgiven." End quote. (Bayan Talbis al-Jahmiyya 2/358)
He said: "This is the position of the jurists among the hadith scholars, such as Ahmad and others: whoever calls people to an innovation deserves punishment to protect people from his harm, even if inwardly he is exercising independent reasoning." End quote. (Majmu al-Fatawa 7/385)


[Page 63]

He said: "Whoever adopts singing and clapping as worship and a means of drawing near to Allah has thereby imitated the polytheists and resembled them in something that is not the practice of the believers—the Emigrants and the Helpers. If he does this in the houses of Allah, his resemblance to them is all the greater, and he is occupied by it instead of prayer, remembrance of Allah, and supplication to Him. His resemblance to them has thus become grave, and he has a great share of the blame indicated by His saying, Exalted is He: {Their prayer at the House was nothing but whistling and clapping.} Yet this may be forgiven him because of his sincere effort, or because of good deeds that erase sins, or for some other reason. This is among the matters in which a distinction is made between a Muslim and a disbeliever. But his being distinct from the polytheists in other respects does not prevent him from being blameworthy, outside the bounds of the Sharia, and engaged in innovation." End quote (Majmu al-Fatawa 11/596).
He said: "The ignorant person must turn back and not persist in his ignorance or oppose what the Muslim scholars hold; for by doing so he becomes an ignorant, misguided innovator." End quote (Majmu al-Fatawa 7/682).

Error in interpretation: Interpretation (ta'wil) is placing a legal proof in a context other than its proper one, through independent reasoning or a doubt arising from failure to understand the indication of the text. A legally responsible person may therefore proceed to commit an act of disbelief without considering it disbelief, citing as proof a text whose meaning he has misunderstood. This error means that the condition of deliberate intent is absent, and error in interpretation prevents him from being declared a disbeliever. But if proof is established against him and his error is explained to him, and he persists in his act, he then becomes a disbeliever. The evidence for this is the incident of Qudama ibn Maz'un. Qudama deemed drinking wine permissible—and deeming it permissible is disbelief—citing the saying of Allah, Exalted is He: {There is no blame on those who believe and do righteous deeds for what they have consumed} (al-Ma'ida: 93). He cited this verse to Umar when Umar intended to impose the prescribed punishment on him. Umar explained his error to him and imposed the punishment. Regarding this, Ibn Taymiyya said: "Or he was mistaken and thought that those who believe and do righteous deeds...


[Page 64]

The righteous are exempted from the prohibition of wine. Those whom Umar required to repent made this same mistake, and there are other examples of this kind. They are to be called upon to repent and the proof is to be established against them. If they persist, they then become unbelievers; they are not judged to be unbelievers before that. The Companions likewise did not judge Qudama ibn Maz'un and his companions to be unbelievers when they made the mistake they made in their interpretation.(1)
This incident shows that, by the consensus of the Companions, error in interpretation prevents a person from being declared an unbeliever. It also falls under the general meaning of the words of Allah, Exalted is He: {There is no blame upon you for what you do by mistake} (al-Ahzab: 5).
Nevertheless, not every error in interpretation is considered an acceptable excuse that prevents a person from being declared an unbeliever:
An error in interpretation that is excusable is one that arises from examining a legal proof, but misunderstanding it.
An error in interpretation that is not excusable is one that arises from mere opinion and caprice, without relying on a legal proof. An example is Iblis's refusal to prostrate to Adam, arguing that {I am better than him: You created me from fire and created him from clay} (al-A'raf: 12). This was mere opinion. Another example is the esoteric interpretations by which the Batinites set aside religious obligations; these arose from mere caprice.
This error in interpretation is what prevented him from being declared an unbeliever, just as error in interpretation prevented Qudama ibn Maz'un (may Allah be pleased with him), who was also one of the people of Badr, from being declared an unbeliever when he drank wine, believing it to be permissible and interpreting the words of Allah in this way: {There is no blame upon those who believe and do righteous deeds for what they have consumed, so long as they are mindful of Allah and believe and do}


[Page 65]

righteous deeds) [al-Ma'idah: 93], until Umar (may Allah be pleased with him) said to him, "You have hit the wrong mark," and told him, "If you had been mindful of Allah, you would not have drunk it." Just as error in interpretation prevented Qudamah from being declared an unbeliever, it also prevented Hatib from being declared an unbeliever; both had fought at Badr. Allah, Exalted is He, said: {There is no blame upon you for what you do by mistake} [al-Ahzab: 5].
In explaining Hatib's excuse, Ibn Hajar said: "Hatib's excuse is what he himself mentioned: he did this on the basis of an interpretation that it would cause no harm." [(2)
Ibn Taymiyyah also said: "As for the four obligations, if someone denies the obligation of any one of them after the proof has reached him, he is an unbeliever. The same applies to anyone who denies the prohibition of an outwardly evident matter whose prohibition is widely transmitted, such as indecencies, injustice, lying, wine, and the like. But as for someone to whom the proof has not been established—for example, someone who has only recently entered Islam, or grew up in a remote desert where the laws of Islam did not reach him, or someone who mistakenly thought that those who believe and do righteous deeds are exempt from the prohibition of wine, as did those whom Umar ordered to repent, and others like them—they are to be asked to repent and the proof is to be established against them. If they persist, they then become unbelievers; they are not judged to be unbelievers before that, just as the Companions did not judge Qudamah ibn Maz'un and his companions to be unbelievers when they erred in their interpretation." [(3)
Both Qudamah ibn Maz'un and Hatib ibn Abi Balta'ah did something that constitutes unbelief, interpreting it as they did, yet neither was declared an unbeliever. Qudamah deemed drinking wine permissible, interpreting the verse in al-Ma'idah: {There is no blame upon those who believe and do righteous deeds for what they have consumed} [al-Ma'idah: 93],


[Page 66]

And Hatib allied himself with the unbelievers and supported them against the Muslims, interpreting the matter on the grounds that he had an excuse for doing so and that it would not harm the Muslims.
Qadi Iyad said: "As for anyone who knowingly denies or rejects one of Allah's essential attributes, Exalted is He, such as saying, 'He is not knowing, powerful, willing, or speaking,' or the like of the attributes of perfection that are necessarily His, Exalted is He, our imams have explicitly stated that there is consensus that whoever denies these attributes of Him, Exalted is He, and strips Him of them is an unbeliever. This is how the statement of Sahnun is to be understood: 'Whoever says that Allah has no speech is an unbeliever.' He does not declare those who interpret the texts to be unbelievers, as we have already explained. As for someone who is ignorant of one of these attributes, the scholars differed on this point. Some declared him an unbeliever, and this was reported from Abu Ja'far al-Tabari (the statement of al-Tabari was quoted earlier: he declares ignorant of the attributes that are known only through innate disposition to be unbelievers, but not of other attributes, such as hearing and sight, in his view), and others. Abu al-Hasan al-Ashari held this view at one point. Another group held that this does not remove a person from the designation of faith; al-Ashari later returned to this position. He said: 'For he did not believe this as something whose correctness he was certain of and which he regarded as religion and law. Rather, only someone who believes his statement to be true is an unbeliever.' They cited as evidence the hadith of the black woman, and that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) only…
He asked her for nothing but affirmation of God's oneness, and cited the hadith about the man who said, "If Allah should have power over me"—in one version, "He will surely punish me"—and then said, "So Allah forgave him." They said: Even if most people were examined about the divine attributes and they were disclosed to them, only a few would be found who knew them. The other scholar answered this hadith in several ways, including that qadara means qaddara, and that his doubt was not about...


[Page 67]

the ability to bring him back to life, but rather in the very resurrection, which can only be known through revelation. Perhaps no revelation had come to them that established it decisively, in which case doubting it would be disbelief. As for what no revelation has addressed, it is among the things reason deems possible. Or perhaps "decreed" means "made narrow," and what he did to himself was an act of self-reproach and anger at his disobedience.
It was also said that he said what he said while not understanding his own words or controlling his speech, because of the anguish and fear that had overwhelmed him and taken away his reason; therefore, he was not held accountable for it. It was also said that this occurred during a period without revelation, when mere affirmation of divine oneness sufficed. Another view is that this is a figure of speech in Arabic whose form suggests doubt but whose meaning is certainty. This is called "feigning ignorance," and examples of it occur in their speech, such as the words of the Exalted: {Perhaps he will take heed or fear}, and His words: {And indeed, either we or you are upon guidance or in clear error}.
As for those who affirm the description but deny the attribute, saying, "I say He is Knowing, but He has no knowledge; and Speaking, but He has no speech," and likewise with the other attributes, following the doctrine of the Mu'tazila: whoever judges them by the implication of their statement and the conclusion to which their doctrine leads, declares them disbelievers. For if they deny knowledge, then the description "Knowing" is negated, since only one who possesses knowledge can be described as knowing. It is as though, in his view, they had explicitly stated what their words entail. The same applies, in his view, to the other groups of those who interpret the attributes figuratively, including the anthropomorphists, the Qadariyya, and others.
Whoever does not consider them bound by the implications of their words, nor hold them to what their doctrine entails, does not consider them disbelievers. He says: "For when this is pointed out to them, they say, 'We do not say that He is not Knowing. We disavow the implication you have attributed to us, which we believe, as do you, to be disbelief. Rather, we say that our statement does not lead to that conclusion, according to the principles we have laid down.'"


[Page 68]

On these two grounds, people differed over declaring the proponents of figurative interpretation to be unbelievers. Once you understand this, the reason for their disagreement will become clear to you, and the correct position is to refrain from declaring them unbelievers.
And refraining from definitively condemning them to perdition, while applying the rulings of Islam to them in matters of retaliation, inheritance, marriage, blood money, funeral prayers, burial in Muslim cemeteries, and all their other dealings. But they are to be dealt with sternly through painful discipline, severe rebuke, and ostracism, until they abandon their innovation. This was the practice of the earliest generation regarding them. In the time of the Companions and afterward, among the Successors, there arose people who held these views concerning predestination, as well as the Khawarij and the Mu'tazila. Yet they did not disinter anyone's grave or deprive anyone of an inheritance; rather, they ostracized them and disciplined them with beatings, exile, and execution, according to their circumstances, because they were disobedient, misguided sinners who committed major sins. (I say: See here the account of Ali's dealings with the Khawarij. Al-Ash'ari transmitted the statement, "All the Khawarij say that the Quran was created." [Maqalat al-Islamiyyin: 108])
Among the scholars who have investigated the matter and the Sunnis who have not declared them unbelievers, there is disagreement, contrary to those who held otherwise. And Allah is the One who grants success in attaining what is correct.
Qadi Abu Bakr said: "As for questions concerning promise and threat, the vision of Allah, the createdness of the Qur'an, the creation of acts, the permanence of accidents, generation, and similar subtle matters, the prohibition against declaring those who interpret them to be unbelievers is all the clearer, since ignorance of any of them does not constitute ignorance of Allah, Exalted is He, nor have the Muslims unanimously agreed to declare anyone who is ignorant of any of them an unbeliever. In the preceding section, we have already presented the discussion and the nature of the disagreement on this matter, which makes it unnecessary to repeat it, by the help of Allah, Exalted is He." He said in al-Shifa bi-Ta'rif Huquq al-Mustafa (2/272).


[Page 69]

Chapter: As for one who attributes to Allah, Exalted is He, something unbecoming of Him, not by way of insult or apostasy, nor with the intention of disbelief, but by way of interpretation, independent reasoning, and error leading to caprice and innovation—such as likening Him to creation, describing Him as having a limb, or denying an attribute of perfection—this is a matter concerning which the early and later scholars differed over whether to declare its speaker and adherent an unbeliever. Malik and his companions also differed in their opinions on this, though they did not differ that such people are to be fought if they join a faction, and that they are to be asked to repent; if they repent, then well and good, but otherwise they are to be killed. Their disagreement concerned those acting alone. Most of Malik and his companions held that they should not be declared unbelievers or killed, but that their punishment should be severe and their imprisonment prolonged until they manifestly desist and their repentance becomes clear, as Umar (may Allah be pleased with him) did with Sabigh. This was the view of Muhammad ibn al-Mawwaz regarding the Kharijites, and of Abd al-Malik ibn al-Majishun; it was also the view of Sahnun regarding all people of heretical views. He interpreted Malik's statement in the Muwatta in this way, as well as what he related from Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz, his grandfather, and his uncle, concerning the Qadariyya: "They are to be asked to repent; if they repent, then well and good, but otherwise they are to be killed." Isa ibn al-Qasim said of people of heretical views among the Ibadiyya, the Qadariyya, and their like, who oppose the community through innovation and distortion in interpreting the Book of Allah: "They are to be asked to repent, whether they have made this manifest or kept it concealed. If they repent, then well and good, but otherwise they are to be killed, and their inheritance goes to their heirs." Ibn al-Qasim said the same in Muhammad's book concerning the Qadariyya and others. He said: "To ask them to repent means to tell them: 'Abandon what you are following.'" The same is stated in al-Mabsut concerning the Ibadiyya, the Qadariyya, and all other people of innovation: "They are Muslims; it is only their opinion that is evil." Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz acted accordingly. Ibn al-Qasim said: "Whoever says that Allah did not speak to Moses with speech is to be asked to repent; if he repents, then well and good, but otherwise he is to be killed." Ibn Habib and others among our companions held that they are unbelievers.


[Page 70]

And the declaring of their likes among the Kharijites, Qadaris, and Murji'is to be unbelievers. It was also related from Sahnun that he held the same view regarding one who says that Allah has no speech: he is an unbeliever. Reports from Malik differ. In a report from the Syrians, Abu Mushir and Marwan ibn Muhammad al-Tatari, he stated categorically: "Unbelief applies to them." He was consulted about marrying a Qadari and said, "Do not marry him." Allah, Exalted is He, says: {A believing slave is better than a polytheist}. It was also related from him (perhaps: from him) that all the people of heretical doctrines are unbelievers. He said that whoever describes any aspect of Allah's essence, or points to some part of his own body—a hand, hearing, or sight—should have that part cut off, because he has likened Allah to himself. Regarding one who says that the Quran is created, he said: "He is an unbeliever, so kill him." He also said, in a report from Ibn Nafi, that he should be flogged, beaten severely, and imprisoned until he repents. In a report from Bishr ibn Bakr al-Tinnisi, Malik said that he should be killed and his repentance not accepted. Qadi Abu Abd Allah al-Barnakani and Qadi Abu Abd Allah al-Tustari, among the leading Iraqi scholars, said that Malik's response varied: the one who is firmly convinced and calls others to the doctrine is to be killed. On this same point, his statements differed regarding repeating the prayer performed behind them. Ibn al-Mundhir related from al-Shafi'i that a Qadari is not asked to repent. Most of the statements of the early scholars declare them unbelievers. Among those who held this view were al-Layth, Ibn Uyayna, and Ibn Lahi'a; this was related from them concerning one who says that the Quran is created. The same view was held by Ibn al-Mubarak, al-Awdi, Waki, Hafs ibn Ghiyath, Abu Ishaq al-Fazari, and Ali ibn Asim, among others. It is the view of most hadith scholars, jurists, and theologians concerning them, the Kharijites, the Qadaris, those who follow deviant doctrines, and the proponents of interpretive innovations. This was also the view of Ahmad ibn Hanbal. They said the same of those who suspend judgment and those who doubt these foundational doctrines.


[Page 71]

Among those from whom the other view was transmitted—that they should not be declared unbelievers—were Ali ibn Abi Talib, Ibn Umar, and al-Hasan al-Basri. This was also the view of a group of jurists, legal theorists, and theologians. They cited the fact that the Companions and Successors granted inheritance to the heirs of the people of Harura and to those known for holding the doctrine of predestination who had died, buried them in Muslim cemeteries, and applied the rulings of Islam to them. Malik said of the Qadariyya and other people of innovation that they should be asked to repent; if they repent, then well and good, but otherwise they should be killed, because they spread corruption in the land. He said the same of the brigand: if the ruler sees fit to kill him, he may do so, even if he has not killed anyone. The brigand's corruption concerns only property and worldly interests, though it may also extend to religious matters, such as obstructing the pilgrimage and jihad. The corruption caused by people of innovation, however, is mostly against religion, though it may also affect worldly affairs through the enmity they sow among Muslims.

(Ali and Ibn Abbas's position on the interpretation of the Kharijites)
I will present this hadith in full here to clarify Ali and Ibn Abbas's position regarding the Kharijites who interpreted the texts, and to show that they excused them rather than declaring them unbelievers. This is so even though the researcher considers the Kharijites (those meant by the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him) to be unbelievers—not because they interpreted the texts and were mistaken, but because there is an established text stating that they would leave the religion, that they are the worst of creation, and that they are the dogs of Hell, describing and characterizing them as such. Nevertheless, I present this hadith to clarify two points: first, that some of the early generations excused those who interpreted the texts; and second, that no report from any of the Companions declares someone who excuses such a person to be an unbeliever. Even if some considered that interpreter an unbeliever, there is no report from any of the early generations declaring Ibn Abbas an unbeliever for excusing the Kharijites.


[Page 72]

Despite the fame of this hadith, their transmitting and narrating it, and its having reached them as well, al-Ashari explicitly stated, "All the Kharijites say that the Quran is created" [Maqalat al-Islamiyyin, 108]. I will cite the hadith in full; it is a hasan hadith, to clarify their doctrine.
The Imam, hadith master Abu Abd al-Rahman al-Nasa'i, said: Amr ibn Ali informed us, saying: Abd al-Rahman ibn Mahdi narrated to us, saying: Ikrima ibn Ammar narrated to us, saying: Abu Zumayl told me, saying: Abdullah ibn Abbas told me, saying:
"When the Haruriyya went out, they withdrew to a house. They numbered six thousand. I said to Ali, 'Commander of the Faithful, delay the prayer until it is cooler; perhaps I can speak with these people.'
He said, 'I fear they may harm you.'
I said, 'No.' So I put on my clothes, combed my hair, and went in to them at the house at midday, while they were resting. They said, 'Welcome, Ibn Abbas! What brings you here?'
I said to them, 'I have come to you from the Companions of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him)—the Emigrants and the Helpers—and from the Prophet's cousin and son-in-law. The Quran was revealed among them, so they know its interpretation better than you do, and there is not one of them among you. I have come to convey to you what they say and to convey to them what you say.'
A group of them stepped aside to speak with me.
I said, 'Tell me what you object to in the Companions of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) and his cousin.'
They said, 'Three things.'


[Page 73]

I said, "What are they?"
They said: As for one of them, he appointed men to judge in a matter belonging to Allah, while Allah says, {Judgment belongs to Allah alone} (1). What have men to do with judgment?!
I said, "This is one point."
They said, "As for the second: he fought, but took no captives and seized no spoils. If they were unbelievers, taking them captive would have been lawful; but if they were believers, neither taking them captive nor fighting them would have been lawful."
I said, "Those are two; what is the third?"
They said, "He erased from himself the words 'Commander of the Faithful'; if he is not the Commander of the Faithful, then he is the Commander of the Unbelievers!!"
I said, "Do you have anything besides this?"
They said, "This is enough for us."
I said to them, "What do you think: if I recite to you from the Book of Allah, Glorious is His praise, and the Sunnah of His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) something that refutes your position, will you recant?"
They said, "Yes."
‌‌_________
(1) Surah Yusuf: 40, 67. I said, "As for your claim that men judged in a matter belonging to Allah, I shall recite to you from the Book of Allah that He has entrusted His judgment to men concerning the value of a quarter of a dirham, and Allah, Blessed and Exalted is He, commanded them to judge in that matter. Have you considered the words of Allah, Blessed and Exalted is He: {O you who believe, do not kill game while you are in a state of consecration. Whoever among you kills it deliberately, the compensation is an equivalent from livestock, as judged by two just men among you}


[Page 74]

(1) It was part of Allah's decree that He referred the matter to men to judge, though, had He willed, He could have judged it Himself. Thus, the judgment of men was permissible.
"I adjure you by Allah: Is the judgment of men to restore harmony and spare their blood better, or judgment concerning a hare?"
They said, "Yes; that is better."
"And concerning a woman and her husband: {If you fear a breach between them, appoint an arbiter from his family and an arbiter from her family} (2). I adjure you by Allah: Is the judgment of men to restore harmony between them better than their judgment concerning a woman's private parts?! Have you withdrawn from this?"
They said, "Yes."
I said, "As for your claim that he fought but neither took captives nor seized spoils: Would you take your mother Aisha captive, making lawful with her what you make lawful with other women, when she is your mother?! If you say, 'We make lawful with her what we make lawful with other women,' then you have disbelieved. And if you say, 'She is not our mother,' then you have disbelieved, for Allah, Exalted is He, says: {The Prophet has a greater claim on the believers than they have on themselves, and his wives are their mothers} (3). So you are caught between two errors; find a way out of this. Have you withdrawn from this?"
They said, "Yes."
"As for his erasing the title 'Commander of the Faithful,' I shall give you something you will accept. On the day of al-Hudaybiyya, the Prophet of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) made a truce with the polytheists and said to Ali:
'Write, Ali: This is what Muhammad, the Messenger of Allah, has made a truce upon.' They said, 'If we knew you were the Messenger of Allah, we would not have fought you!'


[Page 75]

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, "Erase it, Ali. O Allah, You know that I am the Messenger of Allah. Erase it, Ali, and write: This is what Muhammad ibn Abd Allah agreed to."
By Allah, the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) is better than Ali. He erased his own name, but erasing his name did not erase him from prophethood. Have you drawn this conclusion from that?
They said, "Yes."
Two thousand of them then turned back, while the rest went out and were killed in their misguidance; the Emigrants and the Helpers killed them."
Good. It was transmitted by al-Nasa'i in al-Sunan al-Kubra (5/165–167/8575), or no. 190 in al-Khasa'is; Ahmad in al-Musnad (1/342), or no. 3187 (Shakir)—in abridged form; Abu Dawud (4037)—in abridged form, and this wording is not found in his version; Abd al-Razzaq in al-Musannaf (10/157/18678); al-Hakim (2/150) and (4/182); al-Bayhaqi in al-Sunan al-Kubra (8/179); al-Tabarani in al-Mu'jam al-Kabir (10, no. 15098); Ibn Abd al-Barr in Jami Bayan al-Ilm (2/962/1834); Abu Nuaym in Hilyat al-Awliya (1/318–320); al-Fasawi in al-Marifa wa al-Tarikh (1/522–524); al-Khwarizmi in al-Manaqib (244); Abu Ubayd al-Qasim ibn Sallam in al-Amwal (no. 444); Ibn al-Jawzi in Talbis Iblis (pp. 91–93); and al-Muafa al-Nahrawani in al-Jalis al-Salih (1/558–560, Alam al-Kutub).


[Page 76]

Through two chains of transmission from Ikrima ibn Ammar:

1 - Abd al-Rahman ibn Mahdi, from him.

2 - Umar ibn Yunus al-Yamami, from him.
The hadith scholar Ahmad Shakir graded its chain of transmission authentic in his edition of "al-Musnad" (5/67/3187), and the hadith scholar al-Albani graded its chain good in "Sahih Sunan Abi Dawud," no. (3406).

There are several points in this hadith.
First: What is apparent from it is that Ali (may Allah be pleased with him) did not hold the Kharijites to be unbelievers. Otherwise, if he had believed them to be unbelievers, how could he have refrained from carrying out the prescribed punishment for apostasy against them? This is not something one would think of him.
Second: Ibn Abbas did not hold the Kharijites to be unbelievers. This report states that clearly, where he said: "I said: 'As for your claim, "He fought but took no captives and seized no spoils"—would you then take your mother Aisha captive, deeming lawful with her what you deem lawful with other women, when she is your mother?!'" This clearly shows that he regarded them as believers, since Aisha is only the Mother of the Believers.


[Page 77]

Third: It has not been transmitted from any of the early generations or the transmitters of hadith who reported this hadith that they declared Ibn Abbas, who excused them, to be an unbeliever. This clearly indicates that, in the view of the majority of them, one who excuses someone acting on a mistaken interpretation is not an unbeliever.

Fourth: This shows that Ibn Abbas (may Allah be pleased with him) did not consider a statement's necessary implication to entail unbelief unless the speaker affirmed that implication. He said to them: "As for your claim that he fought but neither took captives nor seized spoils: would you take your mother Aisha captive, deeming lawful with her what you deem lawful with other women, when she is your mother? If you say, 'We deem lawful with her what we deem lawful with other women,' then you have disbelieved. And if you say, 'She is not our mother,' then you have disbelieved, for Allah, Exalted is He, says: {The Prophet has a greater claim on the believers than they have on themselves, and his wives are their mothers.} (3) So you are caught between two errors; find a way out of them."

Qadi Iyad said:

Section: Establishing the correct position on declaring those who act on mistaken interpretations to be unbelievers. We have already mentioned the positions of the early generations on declaring innovators and followers of whims to be unbelievers when they act on mistaken interpretations and make a statement whose implications lead to unbelief, even though, when confronted with those implications, they do not affirm what their statement entails. The jurists and theologians differed on this matter just as they differed on the question itself. Some endorsed the declaration of unbelief made by the majority of the early generations, while others rejected it and did not consider them outside the community of believers. This was the position of most jurists and theologians. They said that such people are disobedient, sinful, and astray, but that we inherit from them as Muslims and apply to them the rulings applicable to Muslims. For this reason, Sahnun said that one who prays behind them need not repeat the prayer. He said this was the position of all of Malik's companions: al-Mughira.


[Page 78]

Ibn Kinana and Ashhab said: because he is a Muslim, and his sin has not taken him out of Islam. Others were uncertain about this, refraining from declaring him an unbeliever or from denying that he was one. Malik's two differing opinions on the matter, and his hesitation about repeating the prayer behind them, stem from this. The judge Abu Bakr, the leading authority among the scholars of verification and truth, adopted a similar view, saying that this is one of the difficult questions, since the people did not explicitly name the...
Disbelief; rather, they uttered a statement that leads to it. His position on the issue was as inconsistent as that of his imam, Malik ibn Anas. In some of his statements, he said that those who held the view that they were unbelievers on account of their interpretation could not be married, their slaughtered animals could not be eaten, and prayers could not be offered over their dead; whether they could inherit from them was disputed, in accordance with the disagreement over the inheritance of an apostate. He also said: "We allow their Muslim heirs to inherit from their deceased, but we do not allow them to inherit from Muslims." For the most part, he leaned toward refraining from declaring them unbelievers on account of the eventual implications of their position. The position of his teacher, Abu al-Hasan al-Ashari, was likewise inconsistent on this matter, though his predominant view was to refrain from declaring them unbelievers.
Ibn al-Qayyim, may Allah have mercy on him, said in Zad al-Ma'ad, in the chapter "An indication of the legal lessons to be drawn from the Conquest of Mecca": "This shows that if a man, acting on his interpretation and out of anger for Allah, His Messenger, and His religion—not for his own desires or personal interests—accuses a Muslim of hypocrisy or disbelief (as Umar accused Hatib of disbelief), he does not thereby become an unbeliever; indeed, he does not incur sin for it. Rather, he is rewarded for his intention and purpose. This is unlike the people of whims and innovations, who declare others unbelievers and innovators because they oppose their whims, innovations, and sects. They are more deserving of those judgments than the people whom they declare unbelievers and innovators." End quote (3/423).


[Page 79]

- Al-Hafiz said in Fath al-Bari, in the Book of Prayer (1/523), among the lessons of the hadith in which someone said of Malik ibn al-Dukhshun that he was a hypocrite who defended the hypocrites: "Whoever accuses someone who outwardly professes Islam of hypocrisy or the like, on the basis of an indication that he perceives, does not thereby become an unbeliever or a sinner; rather, he is excused on the basis of interpretation." End quote.
Ibn al-Qayyim said, and he also said there (3/117): "The Lawgiver did not make those rulings depend on what is merely in people's hearts, without an indication in deed or speech; nor did He make them depend on mere words, knowing that the speaker did not intend their meanings and had no full knowledge of them. Rather, He has excused the community for what their hearts tell them, so long as they do not act upon it or speak of it; and He has excused them for what they say in error, forgetfulness, under compulsion, or without knowledge, when they do not intend or mean the sense of what they have said. When intention is joined with an indication in speech or deed, the ruling follows. This is a principle of the Sharia, and it is among the implications of Allah's justice, wisdom, and mercy." End quote.
- 610)

Ibn al-Wazir says: "His saying, Exalted is He, in this noble verse, 'But whoever opens his heart to disbelief,' supports the view that those who interpret [the texts] are not unbelievers, because their hearts have certainly not opened to disbelief, whether with certainty, conjecture, allowance, or possibility." End quote. Ithar al-Haqq ala al-Khalq, p. 437.


[Page 80]

(Faulty interpretation is not accepted.) This must be emphasized once again: the interpretation for which its author is excused is one that has some basis in linguistic or religious evidence, which led the interpreter to err in his interpretation. As for an interpretation that has no basis in either religion or language, such as the Shiites' interpretations of Allah's saying, "Indeed, Allah commands you to slaughter a cow," when they claimed that what was meant was Aisha, there is no excuse for this.

Al-Qadi Iyad quoted the scholars in al-Shifa as saying: "Claiming an interpretation of an explicit statement is not accepted." End quote. (2/217)
Shaykh al-Islam stated this explicitly in al-Sarim al-Maslul, p. 527.
If someone is known and notorious for heresy and for manipulating the proofs of religion, or engages in acts of disbelief that are explicit and clear and admit of no interpretation, his claim of interpretation is not accepted. There is no independent reasoning or interpretation that makes engaging in explicit disbelief permissible. For every disbeliever has faulty interpretations with which he tries to patch up his disbelief.
For this reason, Ibn Hazm said: "If a report from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) reaches someone through a reliable chain, and he is a Muslim, but interprets it contrary to its meaning—or perhaps another text reaches him, so that the proof of his error in abandoning what he abandoned and adopting what he adopted has not been established against him—then he is rewarded and excused, because he intended the truth but was ignorant of it. But if the proof has been established against him and he persists defiantly, then there is no interpretation after the proof has been established." End quote. Al-Durra (414)


[Page 81]

He says: "As for those who are not of the people of Islam, whether Christian, Jew, Magian, or adherent of any other religion, or the esotericists who claim that a human being is divine, or that someone was a prophet after the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), they are not excused on the basis of interpretation at all. Rather, they are unbelievers and polytheists in every case." End quote from Al-Durra fi ma yajibu i'tiqaduhu, p. 441.
"Al-Fasl fi al-milal wa al-ahwa wa al-nihal" (3/141):
"Some of them argued (that is, against excusing people on the basis of interpretation in any case), saying: Allah, Exalted is He, says: {Say, 'Shall We inform you of those who are greatest losers in their deeds? Those whose striving in the life of this world has gone astray, while they think that they are doing good.'}
Abu Muhammad said: The end of this verse refutes their interpretation, for Allah, Mighty and Majestic, connected His words 'they are doing good' to His words: {Those are the ones who disbelieved in the signs of their Lord and in meeting Him, so their deeds came to nothing, and on the Day of Resurrection We will assign them no weight. That is their recompense: Hell, because they disbelieved and took My signs and My messengers in mockery.} This makes clear that the beginning of the verse concerns the unbelievers who oppose the religion of Islam as a whole. Then we say to them: If this verse had been revealed concerning those who interpret matters among the people of Islam, as you claim, then every person who errs in an interpretation in a legal opinion would be included in its scope. This would entail declaring all the Companions unbelievers, may Allah be pleased with them, because they differed, and we know with certainty that each of them may be right or wrong. Indeed, it would entail declaring the entire community unbelievers, because every one of them must be right in some matters and wrong in others. It would even entail declaring oneself an unbeliever, because anyone who speaks about any matter of religion must at some point retract a statement he has made in favor of another statement that becomes clear to him to be more correct, except that..."


[Page 82]

If he is an imitator, that is worse, because all imitation is wrong and invalid. Whoever has reached this point has glimpsed the depths of his argument. And Allah Most High is the source of success. Umar ibn al-Khattab (may Allah be pleased with him) acknowledged to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) that he had not understood the verse concerning kalala, yet he did not declare him an unbeliever on that account, nor deem him sinful, nor tell him that he had thereby incurred sin. He only rebuked him sternly for repeatedly asking about it. Likewise, a number of the Companions (may Allah be pleased with them) gave incorrect legal opinions during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). When that reached him, he did not declare any of them an unbeliever on that account, nor deem them sinful, nor say that they had thereby incurred sin, for none of them opposed him deliberately. An example is the legal opinion of Abu al-Sanabil ibn Ba'kak concerning the two waiting periods, and that of those who ruled that an unmarried adulterer was to be stoned. We have examined this at length in our book entitled The Book of al-Ihkam on the Principles of Legal Rulings. Furthermore, the aforementioned verse cannot be reconciled with the position of any of those who disagree with us except by means of an omission. They say that the words in His saying, Exalted is He, {Those whose striving is lost in the life of this world} are the predicate of an omitted subject, and this can only be so by omitting the subject, as though He had said, "They are those ..." No one may claim that anything has been omitted from the Quran unless another clear text necessitates it, or there is consensus on it, or it is required by sensory necessity. Thus their position is invalid and becomes a claim without evidence. As for us, the word "those" retains its ordinary meaning, without any omission: it is an adjective describing "the greatest losers," and it is the predicate of the subject in His saying, Exalted is He, {Those are the ones who disbelieved}. The same applies to His saying, Exalted is He, {And they think that they are upon something, but they are indeed the liars}. Yes, this describes the people whom Allah Most High characterized in the beginning of the verse, and the pronoun refers back to them; they are the disbelievers, as stated explicitly at the beginning.


[Page 83]

The verse. Their spokesman said: "If you excuse those who exercise independent reasoning when they err, then excuse the Jews, Christians, Magians, and all other religious communities, for they too exercise independent reasoning, seeking what is good." Our answer—and Allah Most High is the source of success—is that we did not excuse those whom we excused on the basis of our own opinions, nor declare those whom we declared unbelievers to be so on the basis of our conjecture. This is a prerogative that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has granted to no one besides Himself. No one enters Paradise or Hell; rather, Allah Most High admits to them whomever He wills. We therefore call no one a believer except one whom Allah Most High has so called—all of this being conveyed through the words of His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him). No two people on earth disagree—not merely among the Muslims, but among people of every religious community—that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) unequivocally declared unbelievers the adherents of every religion other than Islam, whose followers disavowed every religious community except the one he (peace be upon him) brought. So we stopped at that, and no two people disagree either that he (peace be upon him) unequivocally called everyone who followed him, believed everything he brought, and disavowed every religion besides it, a believer. So we stopped at that as well, and there is nothing more to add. Thus, if there is an explicit text declaring someone outside Islam after the designation of Islam has applied to him, we declare him outside it, whether or not there is consensus on his exclusion. Likewise, if the Muslims unanimously agree that someone is outside Islam, it is obligatory to follow that consensus. As for someone whose exclusion from Islam after Islam has applied to him is established neither by an explicit text nor by consensus, it is not permissible to remove him from what has been established with certainty as applying to him. Allah Most High has explicitly stated what we have said, saying: {Whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will never be accepted from him, and in the Hereafter he will be among the losers.} And He Most High said: {They wish to make a distinction between Allah and His messengers, and say, "We believe in some and disbelieve in others," and wish to adopt a way in between.}


[Page 84]

{Those are truly the disbelievers.} And the Exalted said: {Say, "Was it Allah, His signs, and His Messenger that you were mocking?" Make no excuses; you have disbelieved after your faith.} All of these are disbelievers, according to the text. Consensus has been established that anyone who denies something which, by consensus, is known to us to have been brought by the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) has disbelieved. The text also establishes that anyone who mocks Allah, the Exalted, one of the angels, one of the prophets (peace be upon them), a verse of the Quran, or one of the religious obligations—all of which are signs of Allah, the Exalted—after proof has been conveyed to him, is a disbeliever. Whoever claims there was a prophet after the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), or denies something that he knows the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said, is a disbeliever, because he has not accepted the Prophet's judgment concerning the dispute between him and his opponent.

Ibn Hajar said in his commentary on the hadith: "Aisha said: 'We administered medicine to him through the side of his mouth during his illness, and he began gesturing to us, "Do not administer medicine to me through the side of my mouth." We said, "It is the sick person's aversion to medicine." When he recovered, he said, "Did I not forbid you to administer medicine to me through the side of my mouth?" We said, "It is the sick person's aversion to medicine." He said, "Let no one in the house remain without having medicine administered to him through the side of his mouth while I watch, except al-Abbas, for he was not present with you."'" Administering medicine through the side of the mouth means placing medicine at the side of the mouth, or putting it into the mouth with a finger and rubbing it around the mouth. (Fath al-Bari, Ibn Hajar, 8/147)

His words, "Let no one in the house remain without having medicine administered to him through the side of his mouth while I watch, except al-Abbas, for he was not present with you": It has been said that this establishes the legitimacy of retaliation for anything a person is deliberately subjected to. This is open to question, because they did not all undertake this; rather, he did this to them as a punishment for their having left him


[Page 85]

Complying with his prohibition of that: as for the one who did it himself, this is clear; as for the one who did not, it is because they failed to forbid what he had forbidden them. It may be inferred from this that an implausible interpretation does not excuse the person who adopts it. There is also a question about this, since the one who acted in opposition to the prohibition— Ibn al-Arabi said: "He intended that they should not come on the Day of Resurrection owing him a right, and thereby fall into a grave predicament." It was objected that he could have pardoned them, since he did not take revenge for himself. What seems apparent is that he intended thereby to discipline them so that they would not do it again; thus it was discipline, not retaliation or revenge.)) Ibn Hajar here transmitted the view of those who said that an implausible interpretation is no excuse: they opposed his explicit prohibition against pouring the medicine into the side of his mouth, on the basis of a far-fetched inference, namely, their assumption that he forbade them only because he disliked the medicine. This was an implausible interpretation, so he punished them all. And Allah knows best.
Shaykh al-Islam said, after mentioning the hadith about the man who instructed his sons, at the time of his death, to burn his body: "One who interprets matters on the basis of ijtihad, while earnestly striving to follow the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), is more deserving of forgiveness than someone like this." End quote (3/148).
Ibn al-Wazir said: "There is no disagreement that whoever denies something known by necessity to everyone, and conceals himself behind the name of interpretation in a matter that cannot be interpreted—like the heretics in their interpretation of all the Most Beautiful Names, indeed of the entire Quran, the religious laws, and the afterlife, including resurrection, the Day of Judgment, Paradise, and Hell—is a disbeliever." End quote.
Ithar al-Haqq ala al-Khalq, p. 415.


[Page 86]

Among these, certainly, is the foundation of monotheism, which entails devoting worship exclusively to Allah in all its forms. To undermine this foundation under the pretext of an interpretation that permits associating partners with Allah, Exalted is He, and taking rivals alongside Him is among the clearest forms of falsehood—the very falsehood that all the messengers were sent to abolish and denounce.

It is stated in al-Muhalla (al-Muhalla bi al-Athar, 11/334):
"It is thus established that interpretations differ. Any group that, in pursuing its aims, interprets matters in a way that erases some part of the Sunnah—for example, one who adopts the view of the Kharijites in order to remove authority from Quraysh, or to lead people back to the view that stoning is invalid, or that sinners are unbelievers, or to the expropriation of Muslims' property, or to the killing of children and women, or to proclaiming that divine decree is invalid, or that the vision of Allah is impossible, or that Allah, Exalted is He, knows nothing until it comes to be, or to disavowing some of the Companions, or to denying intercession, or to abandoning the practice of established Sunnahs from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), and calling people to refer instead to someone below the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), or to withholding zakat, or refusing to render a right owed to a Muslim or a right owed to Allah, Exalted is He—such people are not excused on the basis of a corrupt interpretation, for this is utter ignorance. As for one who advocates an interpretation that does not invalidate any Sunnah, but is like Muawiya's interpretation that he should exact retribution from Uthman's killers before pledging allegiance to Ali, he is excused, because this does not entail rejecting any part of the religion; rather, it is a particular error concerning a specific incident that does not extend beyond it.
"As for one who rose up solely for worldly gain, as Yazid ibn Muawiya, Marwan ibn al-Hakam, and Abd al-Malik ibn Marwan did when they rose against Ibn al-Zubayr; and as Marwan ibn Muhammad did when he rose against Yazid ibn al-Walid; and those who also rose on Marwan's behalf, they are not excused, because they had no interpretation at all: it was sheer aggression."


[Page 87]

As for one who calls people to enjoining what is right, forbidding what is wrong, making the Quran and the Sunnah manifest, and ruling with justice, he is not a rebel; rather, the rebel is the one who opposes him. And with Allah, Exalted is He, lies success." Here Ibn Hazm mentioned the matters for which the Kharijites declared others unbelievers and by which they opposed the definitive consensus. Among them he mentioned the invalidation of stoning, and he did not excuse those who held this view on the basis of an interpretation.
It says in al-Tanbih wa al-Radd ala Ahl al-Ahwa wa al-Bida (p. 185):
Hassan ibn Farukh said: "Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz (may Allah be pleased with him) asked me what the Azariqa say, so I told him. He said, 'What do they say about stoning?' I replied, 'They declare it unbelief.' He said, 'Allah is greatest! They have disbelieved in Allah and His Messenger.'" End quote.
It says in al-Fasl fi al-Milal wa al-Ahwa wa al-Nihal (4/144):
The rest of the Azariqa, who are the followers of Nafi ibn al-Azraq, held that stoning a married adulterer should be abolished. They cut off a thief's hand at the shoulder, and required menstruating women to pray and fast during their menstruation. Some of them said, "No; rather, she makes up the prayers when she becomes pure, just as she makes up the fasts." They deemed the blood of children who were not in their army lawful, and also killed women who were not in their army. The Azariqa disavowed anyone who stayed behind from going out to fight, whether due to weakness or for any other reason. They declared anyone who opposed this view an unbeliever after the death of the first among them to hold it, but did not declare him an unbeliever for opposing it during his lifetime. They said that they should test everyone they encountered who was not from their army, and kill him if he said, "I am a Muslim"; but they forbade killing anyone affiliated with the Jews, Christians, or Magians. It was concerning this that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) testified that they would depart from the religion as an arrow passes through its prey, when he said that they would kill the people of Islam and leave the idolaters alone. This is among the signs of his prophethood (peace and blessings be upon him), for he foretold this, and it is one of the particulars of the unseen; thus it came to pass exactly as he said." End quote.
(Reports from the early generations and those after them concerning excusing those who interpret texts)


[Page 88]

Ahmad ibn Mani (16) (may Allah have mercy on him) said: Whoever claims that the Quran is created is a Jahmi. Whoever suspends judgment concerning it: if he is someone who does not understand, such as grocers, women, and children, leave him alone and teach him; but if he is someone who understands, then his reward is in the valley of the Jahmiyya. Abu Hatim al-Razi (may Allah have mercy on him) said: Whoever claims that it is created and made is an unbeliever with an unbelief that takes him out of the religion. Whoever doubts the unbelief of such a person, while understanding and not being ignorant, is an unbeliever. Whoever is ignorant should be taught; if he accepts the truth regarding declaring him an unbeliever, then well and good; otherwise, he is deemed an unbeliever.
/ He said in the narration of Abu Talib(17): Whoever says, "The Quran is created," is an unbeliever. As for whoever does not declare an unbeliever the one who says, "The Quran is created," we do not declare him an unbeliever. [The Two Narrations and Two Positions, Issues of Creed (p. 111), and al-Furu (6/568)]
The author of al-Furu said: The author of al-Muharrar said: "The correct view is that a follower is not deemed sinful for any innovation that does not entail unbelief, because of its slightness—for example, someone who gives Ali precedence over all the other Companions, or refrains from declaring unbelievers those innovators whom we have declared unbelievers."
Then he mentioned the report of Ibn Hani' (18) cited above, and al-Marwadhi's (19) statement to Abu Abd Allah: "There are people who declare to be unbelievers those who do not declare others to be unbelievers." He rejected this, as well as his statement in the report of Abu Talib: "Who would dare say that he is"
