[Page 91]

Ibn Taymiyya said: "An apostate is one who associates partners with Allah, Exalted is He; or hates the Messenger and what he brought; or ceases to reject an evil in his heart; or supposes that any of the Companions, Successors, or their successors fought alongside the unbelievers or permitted that; or denies something established by definitive consensus; or places intermediaries between himself and Allah, relying on them, calling upon them, and asking them. Whoever doubts one of Allah's attributes, when someone like him would not be ignorant of it, is an apostate; but if someone like him could be ignorant of it, he is not an apostate. For this reason, the Prophet did not declare the man who doubted Allah's power and His restoring him to life an unbeliever, since this can only occur after the message has been conveyed. This includes the statement of Aisha (may Allah be pleased with her): 'Whatever people conceal, Allah knows it.' He said: 'Yes.'"
He said:
"Whoever doubts one of the attributes of Allah, Exalted is He, when someone like him would not be ignorant of it, is an apostate. But if someone like him could be ignorant of it, he is not an apostate. For this reason, the Prophet did not declare the man who doubted Allah's power and His ability to resurrect him an unbeliever, since this can only be known after the message has been conveyed. This is also illustrated by Aisha's statement (may Allah be pleased with her): 'Whatever people conceal, Allah knows.'"
And it is stated in The Creed of the People of the Sunnah by al-Lalaka'i (2/193):
Chapter on the comprehensive doctrine of the oneness of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and His attributes and names,
and that He is Living, Powerful, Knowing, Hearing, Seeing, Speaking, Willing, and Everlasting.
An account of what is indicated by the Book of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and what has been narrated from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), concerning that...
The obligation to know Allah, Exalted is He, and His attributes through revelation, not reason ... This indicates that knowledge of Allah and the messengers is through revelation, as Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has informed us.
And this is the doctrine of Ahl al-Sunna wa al-Jama'a." This is a clear statement from him that knowledge of Allah's attributes is attained only through transmitted evidence, not reason, and he attributes this view to Ahl al-Sunna.


[Page 92]

It is stated in I'tiqad Ahl al-Sunna, by al-Lalaka'i (1/176):
Muhammad ibn al-Muzaffar al-Muqri(28) informed us; he said: al-Husayn ibn Muhammad ibn Habash al-Muqri(29) narrated to us; he said: Abu Muhammad Abd al-Rahman ibn Abi Hatim(30) narrated to us; he said:
I asked my father(31) and Abu Zur'a(32) about the doctrines of Ahl al-Sunna concerning the fundamentals of religion, what they had found the scholars in all regions adhering to, and what they themselves believed regarding these matters. They said: We found the scholars in all regions—Hijaz, Iraq, Syria, and Yemen—adhering to these doctrines: ... ... Whoever claims that the Quran is created is an unbeliever in Allah the Almighty, an unbelief that takes him out of the religion; and whoever doubts the unbelief of such a person, if he understands, is an unbeliever.(33) (There are two truthful narrators in its chain, and the rest of its chain consists of trustworthy narrators. Note here that they qualified this by saying, "if he understands.")

(33) Its chain of transmission is sound back to the two of them, and this creed is established as that of Ibn Abi Hatim.


[Page 93]

And Ibn al-Qayyim said: "There can be no establishment of religion without the messengers. If reason has discerned the goodness of some actions and the badness of others, how can it know Allah, Exalted is He, through His names and attributes, or the signs by which He made Himself known to His servants through the tongues of His messengers? How can it know the details of His law and religion, which He prescribed for His servants? How can it know the particular things that He loves, is pleased with, is angry with, and dislikes? How can it know the details of His reward and punishment, what He has prepared for His allies and what He has prepared for His enemies, or the measures, nature, and degrees of reward and punishment? How can it know the unseen, which Allah has not disclosed to any of His creation except those of His messengers whom He has chosen? And how can it know all the other matters brought by the messengers and conveyed from Allah, which reason has no means of knowing? How, then, can reason's knowledge of the goodness and badness of some actions suffice in place of what the messengers brought? It is thus clear that what you have said is mere intimidation, filled with falsehoods. Praise be to Allah." ( )
(According to the creed of Ahl al-Sunna, al-Lalaka'i, 2/193) The chapter on the oneness of Allah, Almighty, and His attributes and names, and that He is powerful, hearing, all-knowing, seeing, wise, everlasting, and speaking. The context of what is indicated by the Book of Allah, Almighty, and what has been narrated from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), namely, that knowledge of Allah, Almighty, and His attributes is obligatory, and that this knowledge is acquired through transmitted evidence (the proofs from the Quran and Sunna), not through reason ... indicating that knowledge of Allah and the messengers is acquired through transmitted evidence (the proofs from the Quran and Sunna), as Allah, Almighty, has stated. This is the creed of Ahl al-Sunna wa al-Jama'a.


[Page 94]

Ibn Taymiyyah said: ((Declaring unbelief in general terms, like a threat of punishment in general terms, does not entail declaring a particular person an unbeliever until the proof is established against him, the proof whose rejection makes a person an unbeliever.
As is established in the Sahih collections from the Prophet (peace be upon him) about the man who said: "When I die, burn me, then grind me, then scatter me in the sea, for by Allah, if Allah has power over me, He will punish me with a punishment He has not given anyone in all the worlds." Allah said to him: "What made you do what you did?" He said: "Fear of You." So He forgave him.
This man believed that Allah would not be able to gather him if he did that, or he doubted it, and that He would not resurrect him. Each of these two beliefs is unbelief that makes a person an unbeliever once the proof has been established against him. But he was ignorant of that, and the knowledge that would have lifted his ignorance had not reached him, and he had faith in Allah, in His command and prohibition, and in His promise and threat, so he feared His punishment, and Allah forgave him for his fear.
So whoever among the people of faith in Allah, His Messenger and the Last Day, and of righteous deeds, errs in some matters of belief is in no worse a state than this man: Allah forgives his error, or punishes him if he fell short in following the truth, according to his religion. As for declaring a person whose faith is known an unbeliever merely for a mistake in this, it is a grave matter.
It is established in the Sahih from Thabit ibn al-Dahhak, from the Prophet (peace be upon him), that he said: "Cursing a believer is like killing him, and whoever accuses a believer of unbelief, it is like killing him."
For absolute takfeer ( saing that whoever does this thing is disbeliver )  , like an absolute threatening, does not require the  accusing of the appointed person of being kafer (disbeliver) until the proof is based on the one who denies it.
As it was proven in the Sahih on the authority of the Prophet, peace be upon him, who said ((one man said   that if I died, then   burn me and then crush me, then shed me in the sea, if God is able to torture me,  he will torture me with a torture he does not torture anyone from the worlds. Then God said to him: What makes you did what u did?  he said Your fear, so  he forgave him))
This man believed that God could not collect him if he did that. or doubted that, and that he did not resurect him and each of these two beliefs is a disbelief (kofr), a one will be disbeliever  if  the argument is based on him, but because  he was ignorant of that and had no knowledge about that which can  makes him away of his ignorance  .and had faith in God and his command and forbidding and his promise and his threatening  and feared his punishment so God forgave him because of  His fear
He whoever  has erred in some matters of belief from the people of faith in God and His Messenger and on the other day and have  righteous deed is not worse than this  man then God will forgive his mistake. or torment him if he neglects to follow the truth according to the extent of his religion.  As for saying  that  an appointed  person who known  that he is a believer is disbeliever  simply because of bmaking a mistake , it is a  great(sin).
It was proven in Al-Sahih on the authority of Thabet Bin Al-Dahhak on the authority of the Prophet, PBUH. He said: The curse of the believer is like his murder, and whoever throws a believer in disbelief(saying that he is kafer) is like killing him((


[Page 95]

Some have rejected the use of the hadith about the man who had himself burned, saying that he repented of this. They cited a weak narration and said: Ibn Abi Hatim said, "My father narrated to us; Muhammad ibn Musaffa narrated to us; Baqiyya narrated to us, from Abu Bakr ibn Abi Maryam, from Atiyya ibn Qays, concerning His words, {But for whoever fears standing before his Lord there will be two gardens}: 'It was revealed concerning the man who said, "Burn me in the fire, so that perhaps I may escape Allah." He repented for a day and a night after saying this, so Allah accepted his repentance and admitted him to Paradise.'" Ibn Kathir, Tafsir.
In this chain is "Abu Bakr ibn Abd Allah ibn Abi Maryam al-Ghassani al-Shami." It is said that his name was Bukayr, and it is also said that it was Abd al-Salam. (He may be attributed to his grandfather.)
Generation: 7; among the senior Successors of the Successors. His rank according to Ibn Hajar: weak; his house was burgled, and he became confused.
His rank according to al-Dhahabi: "They considered him weak; he had knowledge and religious devotion."
Also in the chain is "Baqiyya ibn al-Walid ibn Sa'id ibn Ka'b ibn Hurayz al-Kila'i al-Himyari al-Maytami," Abu Yahmad al-Himsi.
Birth: 110 AH

His rank according to Ibn Hajar: Truthful, but frequently practices tadlis from weak narrators.

(The doctrines of the Companions and the evidence against declaring one who interprets texts figuratively to be an unbeliever)

(Abu Hanifa and the view that the Quran was created)
Shaykh Amr ibn Abd al-Mun'im al-Misri said in his book
:

*Abu Hanifa's attribution to the view that the Quran was created, and the Book of Stratagems attributed to him*

It is reported from Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal, may Allah have mercy on him, that he said:


[Page 97]

It has not been established to our satisfaction that Abu Hanifa held that the Quran was created.

Its chain of transmission is sound—in fact, exceptionally sound, as Shaykh Amr al-Misri said.

Furthermore, for the sake of argument, let us concede to these Jahmi innovators that Abu Hanifa held that the Quran was created.

Ibn Abi Hatim narrated it in his book al-Sunna—as Imam al-Dhahabi mentioned in al-Uluw:
He said:
Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Muslim narrated to us; Ali ibn al-Hasan al-Kara'i narrated to us, saying: Abu Yusuf said:

"I debated Abu Hanifa for six months, and we came to agree that whoever says the Quran is created is an unbeliever."

Its chain of transmission is fair, as Shaykh Amr al-Misri said in his book Abu Hanifa and the Attribution to Him of the View That the Quran Is Created.

Imam al-Bayhaqi, may Allah have mercy on him, also recorded in al-Asma wa al-Sifat, and said: "[Its narrators are trustworthy],"
that Abu Yusuf, the companion of Imam Abu Hanifa, may Allah have mercy on them both, was asked whether Abu Hanifa held Jahm's view—that the Quran was created. He replied: "Allah forbid! I do not say that either."

(Ahmad's position on declaring someone who interprets the texts figuratively an unbeliever) Ibn Taymiyya said in al-Iman al-Awsat (1/56):
Ahmad and other imams explicitly stated that these Murji'ites are not to be declared unbelievers. Whoever reports from Ahmad or another of the imams that they declared these people unbelievers, or who makes...


[Page 98]

These people are among the innovators whose excommunication is disputed; whoever claims otherwise has made a grave error. What is reliably reported from Ahmad and other imams is only the excommunication of the Jahmiyya, the anthropomorphists, and the like. Ahmad did not excommunicate the Kharijites or the Qadariyya when they affirmed divine knowledge but denied that actions are created and that the divine will is all-encompassing. However, two reports are related from him concerning their excommunication. As for the Murji'a, his position that they are not to be excommunicated is undisputed. Yet Ahmad did not excommunicate individual Jahmis, nor did he excommunicate everyone who was called a Jahmi, nor everyone who agreed with the Jahmiyya in some of their innovations. Rather, Ahmad and those like him prayed behind Jahmis who called people to their doctrine, subjected them to an inquisition, and inflicted severe punishments on those who did not agree with them. He did not excommunicate them; rather, he considered them believers and legitimate leaders, prayed for them, and held that one should pray behind them, perform the pilgrimage and fight alongside them, and refrain from rebelling against them, just as he held regarding other leaders like them. He condemned the false doctrine they had introduced, which was grave unbelief, even though they themselves did not know that it was unbelief. He would denounce it and strive, as far as he could, to make them abandon it. Thus he combined obedience to Allah and His Messenger in upholding the Sunnah and religion and condemning the innovations of the heretical Jahmiyya, with upholding the rights of the believers among the leaders and the community, even when they were ignorant innovators and unjust sinners. Those well-known figures, such as Hammad ibn Abi Sulayman, Abu Hanifa, and other jurists of Kufa, counted verbal profession and belief in the heart as part of faith. This was also the position of Abu Muhammad ibn Kullab and those like him. Their position on this did not differ, nor is it reported that they said faith is merely affirmation in the heart. Rather, they attributed this view to "Jahm ibn Safwan," saying that he held that faith is merely knowledge in the heart, even without professing it verbally. They strongly condemned this view.))


[Page 99]

Al-Hafiz Ibn Abd al-Barr said in al-Tamhid (18/46): In the story of one who poisoned himself
This man's ignorance of one of Allah's attributes concerning His knowledge and decree does not take him out of faith. Do you not see that Umar ibn al-Khattab, Imran ibn Husayn, and a group of the Companions asked the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) about divine decree? It is well known that they asked him about it while ignorant of it, and no Muslim could hold that by asking about it they became unbelievers, or that at the time they asked they were not believers.... He went on to say: These Companions of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him)—the learned and virtuous among them—asked about divine decree as learners who were ignorant of it, not as obstinate opponents. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) taught them what they did not know about it, and their ignorance of it before they learned did them no harm. Had it been impermissible for them to be ignorant of it at any time, he would have taught it to them along with their profession of faith, required it of them when they entered Islam, and made it a sixth pillar of Islam. So reflect, and seek Allah's help.
What is apparent from Ibn Abd al-Barr's words here is what we established earlier: people are only required to have general faith, without affirming or denying anything in religion except on the basis of proof. The example Ibn Abd al-Barr mentioned of some of the Companions being unaware of the subject of predestination is like it.
A hadith in Sahih al-Bukhari (4/105):
Then some people from Yemen came in to see him, and he said, "Accept the good news, people of Yemen, since Banu Tamim did not accept it." They said, "We have accepted it, Messenger of Allah." They said, "We have come to ask you about this matter." He said, "Allah was, and there was nothing besides Him. His Throne was upon the water, and He recorded everything in the Reminder, and He created the heavens and the earth."
He did not declare them unbelievers for their ignorance of what was mentioned.

Likewise, Aisha's belief that Allah will call the righteous believers to account on the Day of Resurrection is an affirmation of an act of Allah based on ignorance and interpretation, and it is a matter of belief.
It is related in Sahih al-Bukhari (1/32):
Sa'id ibn Abi Maryam narrated to us, saying: Nafi' ibn Umar informed us, saying: Ibn Abi Mulaykah told me that A'ishah, the wife of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), would never hear something she did not understand without asking about it until she understood it. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said, "Whoever is called to account will be punished." A'ishah said: I asked, "Does Allah, Exalted is He, not say: {Then he will be called to account...


[Page 100]

"Then he will be given an easy reckoning" [al-Inshiqaq: 8]. She said: He replied, "That is only the presentation of the deeds. But whoever is examined closely in the reckoning will perish."

Likewise, Muadh was unaware of one of Allah's acts: that Allah holds us accountable for what we say. This is a matter of belief.

I consider this hadith one of the strongest proofs that a person who suspends judgment on whether someone who interprets matters incorrectly is to be declared an unbeliever should not himself be declared an unbeliever. Muadh (may Allah be pleased with him) did not know whether Allah would hold people accountable for what they said or not. There is no doubt that what they say includes words of unbelief, which are spoken by the tongue. Yet, despite Muadh's ignorance of this matter, the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) excused him and did not declare him an unbeliever. He did no more than explain it to him. The hadith relates Muadh's words:

"He said, 'Are we held accountable for what our tongues say?' The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) struck Muadh on the thigh and then said, 'O Muadh, may your mother be bereaved of you'—or whatever else Allah willed him to say to him—'and what is it that casts people on their faces into Hell except what their tongues have uttered?'" I do not know what difference there is between being ignorant of one of Allah's attributes and being ignorant of one of Allah's acts. Whoever knows the difference, let him assist us by providing clear evidence from the Book and the Sunnah; otherwise, there is no difference between one who is ignorant of this and one who is ignorant of that.

Also, the following hadith, although it is narrated by Abu al-Zubayr (Muslim ibn Tadrus), whom I consider weak in hadith, is in Sahih Muslim. So they are obliged to act upon it if they consider his hadith sound. It is also found in Ahmad's Musnad, but with different wording and through narrators other than Abu al-Zubayr; its editor, Shuayb, declared it authentic. I present here the two narrations.

First: the narration in Muslim: 2496) Harun ibn Abdullah narrated to me; Hajjaj ibn Muhammad narrated to us; he said: Ibn Jurayj said: Abu al-Zubayr informed me that he heard Jabir ibn Abdullah,


[Page 101]

He says: Umm Mubashshir told me that she heard the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) say in Hafsa's presence, "None of the people of the Tree who pledged allegiance beneath it will enter the Fire, if Allah wills." She said, "Indeed, O Messenger of Allah!" So he rebuked her. Hafsa then recited: {There is none of you who will not come to it} [Maryam: 71]. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said, "Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has said: {Then We will save those who were mindful of Allah, and leave the wrongdoers there, fallen on their knees}" [Maryam: 72].

Here, Hafsa denied one of Allah's attributes of action, namely, His saving the people of Badr and al-Hudaybiyya after their coming to the Fire, and affirmed for Him an attribute of action, namely, His punishing them first. Yet he (peace and blessings be upon him) did not declare her an unbeliever, if the hadith is authentically established from him. Both matters belong to the tenets of belief.

It is related in Musnad Ahmad (44/590, al-Risala edition):
" Ibn Idris narrated to us, saying: al-A'mash narrated to us, from Abu Sufyan, from Jabir, from Umm Mubashshir, the wife of Zayd ibn Haritha, who said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) was in Hafsa's house and said, 'No one who witnessed Badr and al-Hudaybiyya will enter the Fire.' Hafsa said, 'Does Allah, Mighty and Majestic, not say: {There is none of you who will not come to it} [Maryam: 71]?' She said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, 'Enough! {Then We will save those who were mindful of Allah} (2) [Maryam: 72].'"
Shu'ayb said: "Authentic."

This narration, too, explicitly shows that Hafsa was unaware of these attributes of action belonging to Allah, yet he (peace and blessings be upon him) did not declare her an unbeliever on that account. This matter, too, is one of the matters of belief.


[Page 102]

Al-Nawawi said, concerning the man who killed himself: "A group said: This man was ignorant of one of the attributes of Allah, Exalted is He. The scholars differed over whether someone ignorant of an attribute is to be declared an unbeliever."
The judge said: Among those who declared him an unbeliever on that account was Ibn Jarir al-Tabari; Abu al-Hasan al-Ashari also said this initially.
Others said: One does not become an unbeliever through ignorance of the attribute, nor does one thereby cease to be called a believer, unlike one who denies it. This was the position to which Abu al-Hasan al-Ashari returned, and upon which his view settled, because he did not hold that belief with certainty, deeming it correct and regarding it as religion and law. Rather, only one who believes his own doctrine to be true becomes an unbeliever. They said: If people were asked about the attributes, those who knew about them would be found to be few.
A group said: This man lived during an interval when mere affirmation of divine oneness was sufficient, and there was no obligation before the revealed law had been conveyed, according to the soundest position, because of the words of the Exalted: {We would never punish until We had sent a messenger.}
A group said: It is possible that, in the time of their law, pardoning an unbeliever was permissible, unlike in our law. This is among the things deemed possible by reason according to the Sunnis; we have only prohibited it in our law on the basis of revelation, namely His saying, Exalted is He: (Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating anything with Him). And other proofs. Allah knows best (1). End of quotation.
Sahih Muslim with al-Nawawi's commentary, vol. 7, p. 70:


[Page 103]

Ibn Hajar said regarding "one ignorant of the attribute": "Al-Hafiz said: Al-Khattabi said: This may seem problematic, and one might ask: How can he be forgiven when he denies the resurrection and the power to bring the dead back to life? The answer is:
He did not deny the resurrection; rather, he was ignorant and thought that if this were done to him, he would not be brought back and so would not be punished. His faith was evident in his admission that he had done this only out of fear of Allah.
Ibn Qutayba said: "Some Muslims may err regarding certain attributes, but they are not thereby declared unbelievers."
Al-Tabari said in his book Al-Tabṣir fi Ma'alim al-Din, after mentioning some of the texts concerning the divine attributes: "These meanings that have been described, and their like among those with which Allah has described Himself and His Messenger, are such that their true nature cannot be established through thought and reflection; nor is anyone deemed an unbeliever for being ignorant of them until they have reached him." (1)
It is stated in Tabaqat al-Hanabila (1/283):
Al-Maimuni said: I heard Ahmad ibn Hanbal say, "There are six for whom I pray at dawn; one of them is al-Shafi'i." Let us now mention his creed.
I read to al-Mubarak and said: "Did he tell you?" Muhammad ibn Ali ibn al-Fath al-Ashari said: "Ali ibn Mardak informed us." He said: "Abd al-Rahman ibn Abi Hatim informed us." He said: "Yunus ibn Abd al-Ala al-Misri narrated to us: I heard Abu Abd Allah Muhammad ibn Idris al-Shafi'i say, when he was asked about the attributes of Allah and what one ought to believe: 'Allah, Blessed and Exalted is He, has names and attributes that His Book has brought and His Prophet has reported—'"


[Page 104]

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him)—his community: no one among Allah's creation against whom proof has been established may deny that the Quran was revealed with this, and that it is authentically reported from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) through a reliable narrator. If someone contradicts this after proof has been established against him, he is an unbeliever in Allah. But before proof has been established against him through a report, he is excused on account of ignorance, because this knowledge cannot be attained through reason, reflection, thought, or the like. Allah, Glorified is He, has informed us that He is All-Hearing, and that He has two hands, in His words: "Rather, both His hands are outstretched"; that He has a right hand, in His words: "And the heavens will be folded in His right hand"; that He has a face, in His words: "Everything will perish except His face," and His words: "And the face of your Lord, full of majesty and honor, will remain"; and that He has a foot, in the Prophet's words (peace and blessings be upon him): "Until the Lord places His foot in it"—meaning Hell. He laughs at His believing servant, as the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said of the man who was killed in the path of Allah: "He met Allah while He was laughing at him." He descends every night to the lowest heaven, according to the report of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). He is not one-eyed, as the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said when mentioning the Dajjal: "He is one-eyed, but your Lord is not one-eyed." The believers will see their Lord on the Day of Resurrection with their eyes, just as they see the moon on the night of the full moon. And He has a finger, as the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said: "There is no heart that is not between two fingers of the fingers of the Most Merciful, Mighty and Majestic." These meanings by which Allah has described Himself, and by which His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) has described Him, are such that their true nature cannot be grasped through reflection or thought. No one is deemed an unbeliever for being ignorant of them unless the report concerning them has reached him. If what is transmitted in this regard is a report that, in understanding, stands in the place of direct observation, then …


[Page 105]

Hearing it entails that the listener affirm it as true and testify to it, as though he had seen and heard it from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). Yet he affirms these attributes and denies any likeness, just as He, exalted is He, denied this of Himself, saying: "There is nothing like unto Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing."

He said: "(Al-Jaza'iri) is the shaykh of this group." ((Al-Alusi, Ibn Hajar, and Ibn Jama'a attributed it to Ibn Abi Hatim in his book al-Manaqib, but they were mistaken; it is not found in the printed edition of that book.))

I said (M): That is so, but it is mentioned in Tabaqat al-Hanabila with the chain of transmission cited.

Al-Tabari said in his book al-Tabṣir:

((Abu Ja'far said:
As for that without knowledge of which a person's profession of faith is not valid for us, and without which the ruling of unbelief is not lifted from him, it is what we mentioned earlier. (He had mentioned that He is the Creator and Disposer, unlike anything else, powerful, speaking, and knowing.)
This is because no one who has reached the age of religious responsibility is excused for being ignorant of the attributes we mentioned earlier, whether a messenger from Allah, exalted is He, came to him or not, and whether he has seen any creation besides himself or has seen no one but himself.
Allah, exalted is He, has names and attributes that His Book has brought and that His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) has conveyed to his community. No one among Allah's creation, once the proof has been established against him that the Quran was revealed with them and the statement of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) has been confirmed to him, may ...))


[Page 106]

A report was narrated from him that contradicts it. If he contradicts it after the proof has been established against him through the report, as I have explained, in a matter whose true nature cannot be known, then the one ignorant of it is excused, for such knowledge cannot be attained through reason, reflection, or thought.
This includes God, Exalted is He, informing us that He is All-Hearing and All-Seeing, and that He has two hands, as He says: {Rather, His two hands are outstretched}. And that He has a right hand, as He says: {And the heavens will be folded in His right hand}. And that He has a face, as He says: {Everything will perish except His face}, and: {And the face of your Lord, full of majesty and honor, will remain}. And that He has a foot, according to the Messenger of God (peace and blessings be upon him), who said: "Until the Lord places His foot in it"—meaning Hell.
And that He laughs at His believing servant, according to the Prophet's words about the man who was killed in the path of God: "He met God, Mighty and Majestic, while He was laughing at him."
And that He descends every night to the lowest heaven, according to a report from the Messenger of God (peace and blessings be upon him).
And that He is not one-eyed, according to the Prophet's words when he mentioned the Antichrist: "He is one-eyed, but your Lord is not one-eyed." And that the believers will see their Lord on the Day of Resurrection with their eyes, just as they see the sun when no cloud veils it, and just as they see the full moon, according to the Prophet's words. And that He has fingers, according to the Prophet's words: "There is no heart except that it is between two fingers of the fingers of the Most Merciful."


[Page 107]

These meanings that have been described, and their like, are among those by which Allah, Mighty and Majestic is He, has described Himself, or by which His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) has described Him. Their true nature cannot be grasped through thought and reflection. We do not declare anyone an unbeliever for being ignorant of them unless the matter has been conveyed to him.
If the report transmitted concerning this is one that establishes proof in the same way as direct observation and hearing, then the one who hears it is obliged to affirm its truth as testimony concerning it." Consider how al-Tabari transmits al-Shafi'i's creed in full and attributes it to himself. However, may Allah have mercy on him, he does not excuse ignorance regarding the attributes that, in his view, are known through reason or innate disposition; these are the ones he mentioned at the beginning of his discussion. One observation about this group, namely those who declare the one who excuses a person acting on an erroneous interpretation to be an unbeliever, is that they often cite the words of al-Tabari, one of their leading authorities on this subject, because he does not excuse ignorance of certain attributes which, in his view, are established by innate disposition. Yet here, when al-Tabari disagrees with them by not declaring one who denies the attributes of hearing and sight an unbeliever—since, in his view, these are not established by reason or innate disposition, but by transmitted revelation—they do not declare him an unbeliever. Here, he excuses the person acting on an erroneous interpretation and the one ignorant of these attributes, even though, according to them, ignorance of them is unbelief, and one who excuses the ignorant person is an unbeliever. Yet they do not declare al-Tabari an unbeliever in this instance. This is a clear contradiction."
"Al-Lalaka'i said in I'tiqad Ahl al-Sunna, by al-Lalaka'i (1/180):
The Quran—the speech of Allah—His knowledge, names, attributes, commands, and prohibitions are not created in any respect. Whoever claims that they are created and made is an unbeliever in Allah, guilty of unbelief that takes him out of the religion. Whoever doubts the unbelief of such a person, if he understands and is not ignorant, is ...


[Page 108]

...a disbeliever." This is al-Lalaka'i's clear transmission regarding excusing the one who excuses another, and the requirement that knowledge and understanding of the proof be established before declaring him a disbeliever. May Allah have mercy on him.
(Ali, may Allah be pleased with him, dealing with the Kharijites and excusing them on account of their interpretation.) This is alongside al-Ash'ari's explicit statement: "All the Kharijites say that the Quran was created" (Maqalat al-Islamiyyin, p. 108).
I cite this statement here even though I consider the Kharijites disbelievers, because of the Prophet's statement about them: "They are the worst of creation; they leave the religion and then do not return to it; they are the dogs of Hell." But I present what is reported from Ali here to show that he excused these people on account of their interpretation. This is evidence that his position was to excuse on the basis of interpretation, and he was a rightly guided caliph and one of the leading figures among the early generations. We also mentioned Ibn Abbas excusing them when he said to them, "Isn't your mother Aisha?" This indicates that he regarded them as believers, since Aisha is the Mother of the Believers, so there is no need to repeat it here. Likewise, it is not reported that any of the Companions declared Ali a disbeliever over this matter. This shows that the position of everyone who learned of Ali's action, whether through a report or directly, was not to declare the one who excuses someone acting on an interpretation a disbeliever. Here are the texts:
Ibn Abi Shayba recorded in his Musannaf (5/332), from Tariq ibn Shihab, who said: I was with Ali when he was asked about the people of al-Nahr: "Are they polytheists?" He replied, "They fled from polytheism." He was asked, "Are they hypocrites, then?" He replied, "The hypocrites remember Allah only a little." He was asked, "What are they, then?" He replied, "People who have rebelled against us." Its editor said: "Its chain of transmission is hasan." And Allah, Exalted is He, knows best.


[Page 109]

Ibn Abi Shayba related in his Musannaf (15/327), from Kathir ibn Numayr, who said: "While I was at Friday prayer and Ali ibn Abi Talib was on the pulpit, a man came and said, 'There is no judgment but Allah's.' Then another stood up and said, 'There is no judgment but Allah's.' Then they rose from different parts of the mosque, declaring Allah's judgment. He gestured to them with his hand: 'Sit down. Yes, there is no judgment but Allah's—a word of truth by which falsehood is sought. Allah's judgment awaits you. For now, you have three guarantees from me, so long as you remain with us: we will not prevent you from using Allah's mosques to remember His name; we will not withhold from you any share of the spoils so long as your hands are with ours; and we will not fight you unless you fight us.' Then he resumed his sermon." Its editor said: "Its chain of transmission is sound." Al-Tabari also related the hadith (5/73) through the route of Abu Mikhnaf.

Al-Tabari (5/73) and Ibn Abi Shayba (15/312) related from Abu Razin, who said: "When arbitration took place at Siffin and the Kharijites separated from Ali, they returned in opposition to him, while Ali remained with his army until he entered Kufa with the people and his army. They went on to Harura with their army. Ali sent Ibn Abbas to them, and he spoke with them, but his words did not persuade them. Then Ali went out to them and spoke with them until they and Ali agreed to accept a settlement. They returned and entered Kufa, both he and they having agreed to it. They stayed for two days or thereabouts. He said: Al-Ash'ath ibn Qays came in—he used to visit Ali—and said, 'People are saying that you have recanted your disbelief for their sake.' The next day, Friday, Ali ascended the pulpit, praised and extolled Allah, and delivered a sermon in which he spoke of them and their separation.


[Page 110]

the people and the matter over which they had parted ways. He criticized them and their position. He said: When Ali came down from the pulpit, they called out from different parts of the mosque, "There is no judgment except Allah's." Ali said, "Allah's judgment is what I await from you." Then he gestured with his hand like this, signaling them to be quiet, while he was still on the pulpit, until one of them came, placing his two fingers in his ears and saying: {If you associate others with Allah, your deeds will surely come to nothing, and you will surely be among the losers.} In al-Tabari's account there is an additional passage: [Ali said: {So be patient. Surely Allah's promise is true, and do not let those who have no certainty unsettle you.}]. Its chain of transmission is sound.

(Abu Bakr's position toward Umar in the incident of those who withheld zakat)

It has been reported that those who declare the person who excuses someone acting on an interpretation to be an unbeliever say that those who withheld zakat were unbelievers, and that Abu Bakr did not excuse them on the basis of their interpretation. They said: "The Companions unanimously agreed that those who withheld zakat were unbelievers, as related by Imam Abu Ubayd, Abu Bakr al-Jassas, Qadi Abu Ya'la, Hafiz Ibn Abd al-Barr, Abu al-Faraj al-Maqdisi, and Shaykh al-Islam Ibn Taymiyya; they did not excuse them on the basis of interpretation or ignorance. No informed person can doubt that they were ignorant and acting on an interpretation, given how recently they had entered Islam and the religion." End quote.

But if their claim is correct—that those who withheld zakat were unbelievers and were acting on an interpretation, yet Abu Bakr did not excuse them on that basis—then what is your position on Umar, who excuses those acting on interpretations, in this case? Umar excused those who withheld zakat and initially objected to fighting them. His objection is recorded in Sahih al-Bukhari: "Abu Hurayra (may Allah be pleased with him) said: When the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) died, and Abu Bakr (may Allah be pleased with him) ...


[Page 111]

Some of the Arabs apostatized, so Umar (may Allah be pleased with him) said: "How can you fight the people, when the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, 'I have been commanded to fight the people until they say, "There is no god but Allah." Whoever says it has thereby protected his property and his life from me, except by its due right, and his reckoning is with Allah.'" The question here is this: if Abu Bakr's position was that those who withheld zakat were unbelievers and were not excused on account of their interpretation, then his position also entails excusing the one who excuses those who interpret matters in this way—Umar here—on the basis of the hadith's wording at the outset, since Umar did not immediately declare them unbelievers. And according to your claim that the statements of the early generations are authoritative, Abu Bakr is the foremost of the early generations, a follower of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), and one of the Rightly Guided Caliphs, concerning whom it was said, "You must adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the Rightly Guided Caliphs." So you are obliged to follow him here and excuse the one who excuses the person who interprets matters in this way. Otherwise, you are obliged to declare Umar an unbeliever, and Abu Bakr too, since Umar did not regard withholding zakat—which, according to them, is an act that nullifies faith—as unbelief, and he objected to fighting those involved in it—merely fighting them, as you claim—but even regarded them as Muslims who affirm the oneness of God.
We ask them: did Umar remain an unbeliever throughout this period? If they say yes, they have made a grave false accusation and have ended up declaring the most noble of the early generations after al-Siddiq an unbeliever. If they say, "No, he did not become an unbeliever," then we say to them: excuse the one who excuses those whom you consider polytheists, but whom he called Muslims because of an interpretation he held. This person who excuses them did nothing beyond what Umar did. All of this is granting them, for the sake of argument, that the Companions unanimously agreed that those who withheld zakat were unbelievers.


[Page 112]

(Hazimi's own words on the one who interprets and the one who excuses) Hazimi said:
"What is meant is that he makes an outrageous statement, as some have put it, one that is far removed from the truth. At that point we say: What is this considered? It is considered a slip. An example of this in our present age is the one who says that ruling by other than the law of Allah, Exalted is He, is lesser disbelief. This is a grave slip, and some of the great scholars have said it. But they are not to be followed in that; their standing is to be respected, but they are not to be followed. It should be made clear that this is a slip, and that the statement is, in principle, disbelief. But we do not declare everyone who says something that constitutes disbelief in matters such as these to be a disbeliever. Rather, we say: This is a slip, and this is an ugly, outrageous statement. But if it becomes clear that he is a scholar and that his intention is good, he is not declared a disbeliever. If we know that he is a person of caprice—and this is the more common case—then they are disbelievers, and are included among those who rule by false deities and the like, because this is considered something necessarily known to be part of the religion.
Likewise, regarding the issue of excusing ignorance: if these polytheists are excused, then if he is a scholar, the case is like the one just mentioned. Otherwise, the basic ruling is that he is a disbeliever, for he has not declared disbelievers those whom Allah, Exalted is He, and His Messenger declared disbelievers.
Second: You must not follow this scholar in his slip. If you follow him out of caprice, then he may not be a disbeliever, but you are. Is that correct? It does not necessarily follow. Some people, when they want to corner you, say: 'Then, if you say that ruling by other than the Sharia is disbelief, you must declare so-and-so and so-and-so disbelievers.' We say: No, that does not follow. I declare you a disbeliever, but I do not declare him one. It does not necessarily follow. Why? Because we know that the scholar may have some doubt, and his intention may be good; in that case, this is an excuse. The scholars have proceeded on this basis. But that has not become apparent in your case, and you are not even one of the scholars. Who has permitted you to follow him? One must return to the Book and the Sunnah. So, if that is the case, it does not necessarily follow that if you declare someone a disbeliever, you must declare the person who followed him a disbeliever. Say: It absolutely does not follow, for this is knowingly following an error. Whoever does not know that it is a slip is more excusable than him, though both are negligent. That is, even if he does not know it is a slip and follows him, he is considered negligent, because he has not returned to the source. And what is the source? The Book and the Sunnah. He must investigate the Book and the Sunnah, not the statements of so-and-so and so-and-so among men."


[Page 113]

(Statements of the scholars and reports from the early generations that they transmitted concerning the absence of an excuse for those who interpret the texts)
The statement of Harb al-Kirmani (34): He said, may Allah have mercy on him: "Whoever claims that our utterances of the Quran and our recitation of it are created, while the Quran is the speech of Allah, is a wicked Jahmi innovator. Whoever does not declare these people and all the Jahmiyyah to be unbelievers is like them." [Questions of Imam Ahmad, as narrated by Harb al-Kirmani, p. 360]
The statement of Ziyad ibn Ayyub al-Tusi, known as Dalawayh (35): He said, may Allah have mercy on him: "Whoever says that the Quran is created is an unbeliever; there is no doubt about it." He was asked, "Should one listen to someone who does not declare them unbelievers?" He replied, "No, absolutely not." He was asked, "I have relatives among them whom I treat kindly and greet. What should I do?" He replied, "No; do not attend their funerals or visit them." (Weak) [Reported by al-Qadi Abu Ya'la in Tabaqat al-Hanabila (1/422).] He said: Khal informed us

[Page 122]

I asked my father (84) and Abu Zur'a (85) about the doctrines of the People of the Sunnah concerning the fundamentals of religion, what they had found the scholars in all the regions adhering to, and what they themselves believed regarding these matters. They said: "We found the scholars in all the regions—Hijaz, Iraq, Syria, and Yemen—adhering to the following doctrine: … Whoever claims that the Quran is created is an unbeliever in Allah the Almighty, an unbelief that takes him out of the religion. Whoever doubts the unbelief of such a person, if he understands, is an unbeliever."(86) (Among its narrators are two truthful narrators, and the rest of its chain consists of trustworthy narrators.) This does not say that a particular individual is always an unbeliever. Likewise, note that it restricts declaring the one who doubts his unbelief an unbeliever to someone who understands. This indicates that an excuser is excused until the proof is established against him and he understands it.

It is related in al-Sunnah by Abd Allah ibn Ahmad (1/112):
Ghayath ibn Ja'far (87) told me: I heard Sufyan ibn Uyaynah (88) say, "The Quran is the speech of Allah, Mighty and Majestic. Whoever says it is created is an unbeliever, and whoever doubts his unbelief is an unbeliever." // Its chain of transmission is good.
__________

[Page 123]

So consider, may Allah have mercy on you, how no statement resembling theirs has been established from anyone among the early generations except the statement of Sufyan ibn Uyaynah. As for what is transmitted from Ahmad ibn Hanbal, if it is authentic, it has already been noted that Ahmad is not counted among the early generations, since he did not belong to the generation of the Successors of the Successors. It is also possible that these words are a general statement about the category, not about particular individuals, since it is well known of him, may Allah have mercy on him, that he did not declare the rulers of his time who held that the Quran was created to be unbelievers as individuals, as has already been transmitted from him. His words should therefore be understood in the general sense.

(Ahmad's position on excusing people on the basis of interpretation) Ibn Taymiyyah said in al-Iman al-Awsat (1/56):

Ahmad and other imams explicitly stated that these Murji'ah are not to be declared unbelievers. Whoever transmits from Ahmad or another of the imams that they declared these people unbelievers, or classifies them among the innovators whose status as unbelievers is disputed, has made a grave error. What is reliably transmitted from Ahmad and imams like him is only the declaration that the Jahmiyyah, the anthropomorphists, and people like them are unbelievers. Ahmad did not declare the Kharijites or the Qadariyyah unbelievers when they affirmed divine knowledge but denied that actions are created and that the divine will is all-encompassing, though two reports are related from him concerning declaring them unbelievers. As for the Murji'ah, his position that they are not to be declared unbelievers is undisputed. Moreover, Ahmad did not declare particular Jahmiyyah to be unbelievers, nor did he declare everyone who was called a Jahmi to be an unbeliever, nor everyone who agreed with the Jahmiyyah in some of their innovations. Rather, he prayed behind Jahmiyyah who called people to their doctrine, subjected them to an inquisition, and inflicted severe punishments on those who did not agree with them. Ahmad and imams like him did not declare them unbelievers. Rather, he believed them to be believers and imams, prayed for them, and considered it permissible to follow them in prayer, to perform the pilgrimage and fight alongside them, and to refrain from rebelling against them, just as he held regarding rulers like them. He denounced what they introduced of the…


[Page 124]

of false speech that is grave disbelief, even if they themselves did not know that it was disbelief. He would denounce it and strive to make them reject it to the extent possible. Thus, he combined obedience to Allah and His Messenger in upholding the Sunnah and religion and denouncing the innovations of the heretical Jahmiyya, with safeguarding the rights of the believers among the rulers and the community, even when they were ignorant innovators and unjust sinners. Those well-known figures, such as Hammad ibn Abi Sulayman, Abu Hanifa, and other jurists of Kufa, regarded the utterance of the tongue and the belief of the heart as part of faith. This was also the view of Abu Muhammad ibn Kullab and those like him. Their position on this did not differ, nor is it reported that they said faith is merely affirmation of the heart. Rather, they attributed this view to "Jahm ibn Safwan," saying that he held that faith is merely knowledge in the heart, even if one does not affirm it with the tongue. They denounced this so vehemently that Waki ibn al-Jarrah went so far as to declare ...
After mentioning what has preceded, the following becomes clear.
First: Only a very small number of such cases have been established as coming from the early generations, and even these may concern the general category rather than particular individuals. Here, a distinction must be made between two issues. The first is the ruling on someone who turns away from learning and falsely attributes to Allah a lie involving polytheism or something else; this is the person who adopts a corrupt interpretation of the kind explained above. The same applies to anyone who has not entered Islam. My view is that such a person is not excused. The second is a person who entered Islam and embraced the general faith, then exerted his utmost effort regarding a matter of creed but erred in it through interpretation. In this case, the stronger view is that he is excused. This applies, for example, to questions concerning the divine attributes and other matters. This is a response to those who declare Asharis and Maturidis to be unbelievers as individuals, and who generalize the ruling of disbelief to anyone ignorant of an attribute or interpreting it incorrectly. It is said to them: There is a difference between two issues. The first is a person who is ignorant of an attribute, remains silent, and says, "Allah knows best; I believe in the general faith." There is no doubt that such a person is not an unbeliever, since it is not obligatory for everyone to ...


[Page 125]

One is knowledge of the names of Allah and of what He has told us about Himself in detail. I will mention the evidence for this in detail later. Some of the Companions were unaware that Allah would be seen, that He knows what we conceal, that He holds us accountable for what our tongues have uttered, and that He had decreed our deeds for us in advance, among other things. The same applies to the question posed by the disciples and to the man who scattered his own ashes. Thus, detailed belief in knowledge of every particular is not a condition for someone to be a believer; the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) had already accepted the faith of his people without that. This also includes someone who knows one of Allah's attributes but interprets it as something linguistically sound and plausible, citing the words of the Exalted: {There is nothing like unto Him}. Likewise, someone may believe that judgment belongs to none but Allah, yet support one of the candidates—not so that he may rule by other than what Allah has revealed, but intending to lessen harm to the Muslims—citing his words (peace and blessings be upon him): "Actions are only by intentions." This is while remaining free of approving the wrong of ruling by other than what Allah has revealed, whether in word or deed, and while disapproving of it; and while not thereby assisting in violating the sanctity of a Muslim through killing, fornication, sodomy, or anything else. For these things—that is, approving the wrong, fabricating lies about Allah, changing the religion, and violating the sanctity of a Muslim—are not made permissible by coercion, as we have explained previously elsewhere. This, then, is an interpretation that permits committing an act of disbelief in order to avert harm from the Muslims, citing the concession granted to Muhammad ibn Maslama, who sought the Prophet's permission to speak ill of Ka'b ibn al-Ashraf in order to save the Muslims from his evil. The story of al-Hajjaj ibn Ilat, when he sought to recover his property from the polytheists, is exactly the same. This is a kind of interpretation subject to the conditions stated above. And Allah knows best.


[Page 126]

(Why is an excuse granted on the basis of interpretation?) An excuse is granted on the basis of interpretation because the person interpreting has exerted his utmost effort to learn Allah's ruling. He is not like one who turns away. Thus, it is valid to say that the person interpreting is mistaken, and the mistaken person is excused. He may not, however, be excused from punishment; rather, he may be excused from being declared an unbeliever alone.
Ibn Taymiyya said: "What I have mentioned concerning a Muslim's neglecting an obligation or committing a prohibition on the basis of an interpretive judgment or imitation is clear to me, and his situation in this regard is better than that of an unbeliever acting on an interpretation. This does not prevent me from fighting a rebellious interpreter, flogging an interpreter who drinks wine, and the like. For interpretation does not remove worldly punishment absolutely, since the purpose of punishment is to prevent the harm caused by transgression." (Majmu al-Fatawa, 22/14)
He also said: "Among these people there is no one who is from the early generations of the community or its leading scholars; rather, they are Muslims who have been criticized for innovation, even if they hold it on the basis of interpretation, and whose error is forgiven." (Bayan Talbis al-Jahmiyya, 2/358) He also said: "It may be that both parties to a dispute are innovators, and that both are ignorant and acting on an interpretation." (Majmu al-Fatawa, 23/356)
Error in interpretation: Interpretation is placing a legal proof in a context other than its proper one, on the basis of independent reasoning or a doubt arising from failure to understand the implication of the text. Thus, a legally responsible person proceeds to commit an act of unbelief while not considering it unbelief, citing as proof a text whose meaning he has misunderstood. This error means that the condition of deliberate intent is absent, and error in interpretation prevents him from being declared an unbeliever. But if the proof is established against him and his error is made clear to him, and he persists in his act, he then becomes an unbeliever. The evidence for this is the incident of Qudama ibn Maz'un—which I mentioned in the important note in my commentary on al-Aqida al-Tahawiyya—in which Qudama deemed drinking wine permissible, and deeming it permissible is unbelief, citing the words of the Exalted: (There is no blame upon those who believe ...


[Page 127]

"And those who believe and do righteous deeds incur no blame for what they have consumed" (al-Ma'idah: 93). He cited this verse as evidence to Umar when Umar intended to carry out the prescribed punishment on him. Umar explained his error to him and carried out the punishment. Concerning this, Ibn Taymiyyah said: "Or he was mistaken and thought that those who believe and do righteous deeds were exempted from the prohibition of wine, just as those whom Umar required to repent were mistaken in this regard, and the like. They are to be asked to repent and the proof is to be established against them; if they persist, they then become unbelievers. They are not judged to be unbelievers before that, just as the Companions did not judge Qudamah ibn Maz'un and his companions to be unbelievers when they were mistaken in their interpretation."(1)
(The one who interprets) This incident indicates that error in interpretation prevents a judgment of unbelief, by the consensus of the Companions. It also falls under the general meaning of the words of Allah, Exalted is He: "There is no blame upon you for what you do by mistake" (al-Ahzab: 5).
Nevertheless, not every error in interpretation is considered an acceptable excuse and a bar to declaring someone an unbeliever:
An error in interpretation that is excusable is one that arises from examining a legal proof, but misunderstanding it.
An error in interpretation that is not excusable is one that arises from mere opinion and caprice, without relying on a legal proof. An example is Iblis's refusal to prostrate to Adam, on the grounds that "I am better than him: You created me from fire and created him from clay" (al-A'raf: 12). This is mere opinion. The esoteric interpretations by which the Batinis set aside religious obligations are likewise mere caprice.


[Page 128]

(The one who interprets) Qadi Iyad said: "As for one who denies an essential attribute of Allah, Exalted is He,
Or denies them knowingly, saying, for example, "He is neither knowing, powerful, willing, nor speaking," or the like, concerning attributes of perfection that are necessarily His, Exalted is He. Our imams have explicitly stated that there is consensus that whoever denies these attributes of Him, Exalted is He, and strips Him of them is an unbeliever. It is in this sense that the statement of Sahnun is understood: "Whoever says that Allah has no speech is an unbeliever." He does not declare those who interpret the texts to be unbelievers, as we have explained above. As for someone who is ignorant of one of these attributes, the scholars differed over this: some declared him an unbeliever, and this was reported from Abu Ja'far al-Tabari and others. Abu al-Hasan al-Ash'ari held this view at one point. Another group held that this does not remove him from the designation of faith, and al-Ash'ari later returned to this position. He said: "That is because he did not hold this belief with the conviction that it was correct, nor regard it as a religion and a law. Rather, only someone who believes his statement to be true is an unbeliever." They cited as evidence the hadith of the black woman, in which the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) only…
He asked them for nothing but affirmation of the oneness of God, and by the hadith about the man who said, "If Allah has power over me"—and in one narration, "If Allah should cause me to go astray"—then he said, "So Allah forgave him." They said: If most people were examined about the divine attributes and these were disclosed to them, only a few would be found who knew them. The other scholar answered this hadith in several ways, including that "has power" means "decrees," and that his doubt was not about Allah's power to bring him back to life, but about the resurrection itself, which can only be known through revelation; perhaps he...
There had been no revealed ruling concerning it that would decisively settle the matter, such that doubt about it would then constitute disbelief. As for what no revealed ruling addresses, it is among the things deemed possible by reason. Or "decreed" may mean "constrained," and what he did to himself was an act of self-reproach and anger at his own disobedience. It was also said: "He said only what"


[Page 129]

He said this while neither understanding his own words nor controlling his speech, overcome by such anguish and fear that they had taken away his reason; therefore, he was not held accountable for it. It was also said that this occurred during the interval between prophets, when mere affirmation of divine oneness was sufficient. And it was said, rather, that this is a figure of speech in Arabic whose outward form suggests doubt, while its meaning is certainty; this is called feigning ignorance, and examples of it occur in their speech, such as the words of the Exalted: {Perhaps he will take heed or fear}, and His words: {And indeed, either we or you are surely upon guidance or in clear error}. As for those who affirm the description but deny the attribute, saying, "I say that He is Knowing, but He has no knowledge; and Speaking, but He has no speech," and so on with the other attributes, following the doctrine of the Mu'tazilites, whoever judges them by the implication of what their statement leads to, and where their doctrine takes them, declares them unbelievers. For if they deny knowledge, the description "Knowing" is negated, since only one who possesses knowledge can be described as knowing. It is as though, in his view, they had explicitly stated what their words entail. The same applies, in his view, to the other groups among the proponents of figurative interpretation, such as the anthropomorphists, the Qadariyya, and others. But whoever does not consider them bound by the implications of their words, or does not hold them to what their doctrine entails, does not consider them unbelievers. He says: "For when they are confronted with this, they say, 'We do not say that He is not Knowing. We disavow the implication you have attributed to us, which we and you believe to be unbelief. Rather, we say that our statement does not lead to that, according to the principles we have laid down.'" It is on the basis of these two approaches that people have differed over declaring the proponents of figurative interpretation to be unbelievers. Once you understand this, the reason for people's disagreement on this matter will become clear to you, and the correct position is to refrain from declaring them unbelievers.
And refraining from definitively declaring them doomed to perdition, while applying the rulings of Islam to them in matters of retaliation, inheritance, marriage, blood money, funeral prayers, burial in Muslim cemeteries, and all their other dealings; but they are subjected to severe disciplinary punishment.


[Page 130]

And severe rebuke and ostracism, until they turn back from their innovation. This was the practice of the earliest generation toward them. The Companions' position on declaring those who interpret texts erroneously to be unbelievers was that, during the time of the Companions and afterward among the Successors, people arose who held these views of the Qadariyya, and the Kharijites and Mu'tazilites appeared. Yet they neither exhumed their graves nor deprived any of them of inheritance. Rather, they ostracized them and disciplined them with beating, exile, and execution, according to their circumstances, because they were dissolute, misguided, disobedient sinners who committed major sins. (I say: See here the hadith about Ali and the Kharijites.)
(Faulty interpretation is not accepted)
Al-Qadi Iyad quoted in al-Shifa the scholars' statement: "Claiming an interpretation of an explicit expression is not accepted." End quote (2/217).
Shaykh al-Islam stated this explicitly in al-Sarim al-Maslul, p. 527.
Thus, if someone is known and notorious for his heresy and manipulation of the proofs of the Sharia, or engages in acts of unbelief that are explicit and clear and admit of no interpretation, his claim of interpretation is not accepted. There is no ijtihad or interpretation that makes engaging in explicit unbelief permissible, for no unbeliever is without faulty interpretations with which he tries to patch up his unbelief.
For this reason, Ibn Hazm said: "If a person who is Muslim receives a report from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) through a reliable chain, then interprets it contrary to its meaning—or perhaps another text has reached him, so that the proof of his error in abandoning what he abandoned and adopting what


[Page 131]

He has erred, yet is rewarded and excused, because he sought the truth but was unaware of it. But if proof has been established against him in that matter and he persists defiantly, there is no room for interpretation after proof has been established." End quote from al-Durra (414).
He says: "As for those who are not of the people of Islam, whether Christians, Jews, Magians, or adherents of other religions, or the esotericists who claim that a human being is divine, or that someone became a prophet after the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), they are not excused on the basis of interpretation at all. Rather, they are unbelievers and polytheists in every case." End quote from al-Durra fi ma yajibu i'tiqaduhu, p. 441.
It says in al-Fasl fi al-milal wa al-ahwa wa al-nihal (3/139):
"Abu Muhammad said: As for one against whom proof of the truth has not been established, whatever the matter may be, he is not an unbeliever unless there is a text declaring him an unbeliever, in which case one must stop at that—such as a person in the farthest reaches of the Zanj to whom only mention of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) has reached, and who refrains from seeking further information about him: he is an unbeliever."
16
And in al-Fasl fi al-milal wa al-ahwa wa al-nihal (3/142):
"One of their spokesmen said: 'If you excuse those who exercise independent reasoning when they err, then excuse the Jews, Christians, Magians, and adherents of all other religions, for they too exercise independent reasoning and seek what is good.' Our answer—and success is from Allah, Exalted is He—is that we did not excuse those whom we excused on the basis of our own opinions, nor declare those whom we declared unbelievers on the basis of our own conjecture. This is a prerogative that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has granted to no one besides Himself. No one admits anyone to Paradise or Hell; rather, Allah, Exalted is He, admits to them whomever He wills. Thus, we call no one a believer except one whom Allah, Exalted is He, has so called, all of this being conveyed through the words of His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him)..."


[Page 132]

Upon him, and no two people on earth disagree—not merely among the Muslims, but among people of every religion—that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) declared as unbelievers the adherents of every religion other than Islam, whose people disavowed every religion except the one he brought, peace be upon him. So we stopped at that. Nor do two people disagree that he, peace be upon him, designated as believers all who followed him, affirmed everything he brought, and disavowed every religion besides that. So we likewise stopped at that, and there is nothing more. Thus, anyone for whom there is an explicit text establishing that he has left Islam, after the designation of Islam had applied to him, we declare outside it, whether or not there is consensus that he has left it. Likewise, if the Muslims unanimously agree that someone has left Islam, it is obligatory to follow the consensus in that matter. As for someone whose departure from Islam, after Islam had applied to him, is established neither by an explicit text nor by consensus, it is not permissible to remove him from what has been established with certainty as applying to him. Allah Most High has explicitly stated what we have said, saying: {Whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will never be accepted from him, and in the Hereafter he will be among the losers.} And He Most High says: {They wish to make a distinction between Allah and His messengers, and say, "We believe in some and disbelieve in others," and wish to adopt a way in between. Those are truly the unbelievers.} And He Most High says: {Say, "Was it Allah, His signs, and His Messenger that you were mocking? Make no excuses; you have disbelieved after your faith."} All of these people are unbelievers by explicit text. It is established by consensus that anyone who denies something which, as we know by consensus, the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) brought has disbelieved. And it is established by explicit text that anyone who mocks Allah Most High, or one of the angels, or one of the prophets (peace be upon them), or a verse of the Quran, or one of the religious obligations,


[Page 133]

For all of them are the signs of Allah, Exalted is He. Once the proof has reached him, he is an unbeliever. Whoever claims that there is a prophet after the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), or denies something that he knows the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said, is an unbeliever, because he has not accepted the Prophet's judgment in the matter disputed between him and his opponent."

Likewise, Ibn Hajar says (Fath al-Bari, Ibn Hajar, 8/147):
"His words, 'No one will remain in the house except that I will have him flogged, while I look on—except al-Abbas, for he was not present with you': It has been said that this establishes the legitimacy of retaliation for everything a person is deliberately subjected to. This is open to question, because not all of them had done that; rather, this was done to them as a punishment for failing to comply with his prohibition of it. As for the one who did it, the matter is clear. As for the one who did not do it, it was because they failed to forbid what he had forbidden them. It may be inferred from this that a far-fetched interpretation does not excuse its proponent. This too is open to question, because the one who acted contrary to the prohibition—" Ibn al-Arabi said: "He intended that they should not come on the Day of Resurrection bearing a claim against him, and so fall into a grave predicament." It was objected that he could have pardoned them, since he did not take revenge for himself. What seems apparent is that he intended thereby to discipline them so that they would not do it again; thus it was discipline, not retaliation or revenge."
Shaykh al-Islam said, after mentioning the hadith about the man who instructed his sons, at his death, to burn his body: "The person who interprets the matter, being among the people of independent reasoning and eager to follow the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), is more deserving of forgiveness than someone like this." End quote. (3/148)


[Page 134]

Ibn al-Wazir said: "There is no disagreement that whoever denies what is necessarily known to everyone is an unbeliever, and cloaks himself in the name of interpretation where interpretation is impossible, as do the heretics in interpreting all the Most Beautiful Names, indeed the entire Quran, the revealed laws, and the Hereafter, including resurrection, the Day of Judgment, Paradise, and Hell." End quote from Ithar al-Haqq ala al-Khalq, p. 415.

Among such matters, certainly, is the foundation of monotheism (tawhid), which entails devoting every kind of worship exclusively to Allah. To undermine this foundation by claiming an interpretation that permits associating partners with Allah, Exalted is He, and taking rivals alongside Him is among the clearest forms of falsehood—the very falsehood that all the messengers were sent to refute and condemn.

(Disagreement over declaring the interpreter an unbeliever, and scholars' views on declaring the interpreter an unbeliever)

Ibn Taymiyya said: "The scholars have disagreed over declaring the people of innovation and caprice unbelievers and consigning them to remain eternally in the Fire. There is no imam from whom only one view has been reported on this matter; rather, two views have been reported from Malik, al-Shafi'i, Ahmad, and others. Some of their followers came to report this disagreement as applying to all people of innovation and to their remaining eternally in the Fire, to the point that they committed themselves to consigning everyone whom they personally considered an innovator to eternal punishment. This involves an incalculable degree of error. Others reacted in the opposite way, coming to think that no one among the people of caprice should be categorically declared an unbeliever, even if they have adopted heretical views and the doctrines of those who deny Allah's attributes and those who espouse union with Him.

"The correct view is that a statement may constitute unbelief, as with the doctrines of the Jahmiyya, who said that Allah does not speak and will not be seen in the Hereafter. Yet this may be unclear to some


[Page 135]

that it is disbelief, and so the ruling that the one who says it is a disbeliever is stated in general terms, as the early scholars said: "Whoever says that the Quran is created is a disbeliever," and, "Whoever says that Allah will not be seen in the Hereafter is a disbeliever." But a particular person is not declared a disbeliever until proof has been established against him ... End quote from Majmu al-Fatawa (Dar Ibn Hazm edition), 7/375–377.
Al-Shawkani's statement in al-Sayl al-Jarrar (4/373), commenting on the author of Hada'iq al-Azhar's statement, "Execution is the prescribed punishment for a belligerent disbeliever and an apostate, whatever the form of disbelief": He, may Allah have mercy on him, said: "As for his words, 'whatever the form of disbelief,' the author intended to include, by convention, those who disbelieve on the basis of interpretation under the category of apostasy. This is a grievous misstep, one that makes the hands and mouth cry out; a stumble that cannot be excused, and a slip that cannot be forgiven. If this were sound, most of the Muslims on the face of the earth would be apostates, because the followers of the four schools are Asharis and Maturidis, and they declare the Mu'tazilites and their followers to be disbelievers, while the Mu'tazilites declare them disbelievers. All of this is one of the promptings of Satan the accursed, and a pulse of extreme partisanship and great injustice." End quote.

Al-Shawkani also said, commenting on the statement, "One who interprets [the texts] is like an apostate": "I say: Here tears are shed, and Islam and its people are mourned for what religious partisanship has brought upon most Muslims: accusations of disbelief hurled at one another, not on the basis of the Sunnah, nor the Quran, nor a clarification from Allah, nor proof, but because the fires of religious partisanship burned fiercely, and Satan the accursed gained the power to divide the Muslims. He prompted them to make accusations against one another based on things as insubstantial as dust in the air and a mirage in a barren plain. Alas for the Muslims, in the face of this ..."


[Page 136]

The calamity that is among the greatest calamities to befall religion, and the disaster unlike any that has struck the path of the believers. If you still possess some reason, some awareness of Allah, Exalted is He, and a measure of Islamic zeal, you know—and everyone who knows this religion knows—that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) was asked about Islam and, explaining its reality and clarifying its meaning, said: "It is establishing prayer, paying zakat, performing the pilgrimage to the House, fasting Ramadan, and bearing witness that there is no god but Allah." Hadiths to this effect are mass-transmitted. Whoever fulfills these five pillars and observes them as they should be observed is a Muslim, whether anyone likes it or not, whoever that may be. So if someone comes to you with something contrary to this, whether worthless talk, false knowledge, or indeed ignorance, strike it in his face and say to him: "Your ravings have been preceded by the proof of Muhammad ibn Abd Allah, may Allah's blessings and peace be upon him."
Is it not incumbent upon us to recognize that the circumstances and situation of the three whom the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) shunned in the abode of Islam are not like those of Muslim youth today in the abode of disbelief and under conditions of weakness, and that the faith of those Companions is not like that of other people? That is why he did not shun the other people of weak faith who would offer him excuses for staying behind, and he would excuse them. (2)

(Interpretation, Ahmad's doctrine, and the sects) Ibn Taymiyya, al-Iman al-Awsat, 1/56
Ahmad and other leading scholars explicitly stated that these Murji'a are not to be declared unbelievers. Whoever attributes to Ahmad or any other leading scholar the declaration that these people are unbelievers, or claims that


[Page 137]

If someone says that these people are among the innovators whose excommunication is disputed, he has made a grave error. What is reliably reported from Ahmad and other imams is only that they declared the Jahmiyya, the anthropomorphists, and the like to be unbelievers. Ahmad did not declare the Kharijites or the Qadariyya to be unbelievers when they affirmed divine knowledge but denied that human acts are created and that the divine will is all-encompassing. However, two reports are related from him concerning whether they are unbelievers. As for the Murji'a, there is no disagreement in his position that they are not to be declared unbelievers. Yet Ahmad did not declare specific individuals among the Jahmiyya to be unbelievers, nor did he declare everyone who was called a Jahmi to be an unbeliever, nor everyone who agreed with the Jahmiyya in some of their innovations. Rather, Ahmad and those like him prayed behind Jahmiyya who promoted their doctrine, put people to the test, and severely punished those who did not agree with them. Ahmad did not declare them unbelievers. Instead, he considered them believers and imams, prayed for them, and held that one could follow them in prayer, perform the pilgrimage and fight alongside them, and refrain from rebelling against them, just as he held regarding other imams like them. He condemned the false doctrine they had introduced, which was grave unbelief, even though they themselves did not know that it was unbelief. He would denounce it and strive, as far as possible, to make them abandon it.
