Thus he combined obedience to Allah and His Messenger in upholding the Sunnah and religion and condemning the innovations of the heretical Jahmiyya, with observing the rights of the believers among the rulers and the community, even when they were ignorant innovators and unjust sinners. Those well-known figures, such as Hammad ibn Abi Sulayman, Abu Hanifa, and other jurists of Kufa, held that verbal profession and belief in the heart were part of faith. This was also the position of Abu Muhammad ibn Kullab and those like him. Their position on this did not differ, nor is it reported that they said faith is merely affirmation in the heart. Rather, they attributed this position to "Jahm ibn Safwan," saying that he held faith to be merely knowledge in the heart, even without professing it verbally. They denounced this so vehemently that Waki ibn al-Jarrah went so far as to declare...
And he said: Majmu al-Fatawa, 15/29.


[Page 138]

Ibn Taymiyya, may Allah have mercy on him, said (12/189): "As for declaring someone an unbeliever, the correct view is that whoever among the community of Muhammad (peace and blessings be upon him) exercises independent judgment, seeks the truth, but errs, is not an unbeliever; rather, his error is forgiven. But whoever learns what the Messenger brought (peace and blessings be upon him), then opposes the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) after guidance has become clear to him, and follows a path other than that of the believers, is an unbeliever."
It says in Questions of Abu Ubayd al-Ajuri to Abu Dawud al-Sijistani on Discrediting and Accrediting Narrators (p. 35):
"Those who categorically barred transmission from them (that is, from innovators and followers of sectarian whims) held that innovators were unbelievers, according to the view of those who declared those who interpret texts figuratively to be unbelievers, or sinners, according to the view of those who did not rule that they were unbelievers. Some of them, however, deliberately refrained from transmitting from them as a form of punishment and humiliation, or because they did not consider these people trustworthy against lying. According to this latter view, the following statement by Abu Dawud, may Allah have mercy on him, is an exception: 'Among the followers of sectarian whims, none has more authentic hadiths than the Kharijites.' He then mentioned Imran ibn Hittan and Abu Hassan al-A'raj."5 (Abu Dawud did not declare the Kharijites unbelievers.)
Ibn al-Arabi said in al-Qabas fi Sharh Muwatta Malik ibn Anas (p. 404):
"People have differed over declaring those who interpret texts figuratively to be unbelievers. These are people who do not intend unbelief, but seek faith and end up in unbelief, and seek knowledge, but are led by their interpretation into ignorance. This is a grave question in which the proofs conflict. I have considered it at times: sometimes I declare them unbelievers, and sometimes I withhold judgment, except in the case of one who says that the Quran is created, or that there is another creator besides Allah. In such a case, I have no doubt, and I leave him no share of faith." (6)
(A very important principle concerning figurative interpretation)
There is no difference in figurative interpretation between what they call matters of belief and practical matters. This is because they fell into a contradiction: they said that if figurative interpretation concerns practical matters, they mean matters of


[Page 139]

Jurisprudence, such as the jurisprudence of ablution, prayer, and sales: this interpretation is excusable, and its proponent is not declared an unbeliever. But if this interpretation concerns a matter of belief, such as the attributes of Allah, then he is declared an unbeliever. Their distinction is supported by no evidence from the Book, the Sunnah, or the words of a Companion. If they are asked, "Why did you excuse the one who interprets matters of practice?" they begin citing the verse, "There is no blame upon you for what you do by mistake," and other verses and hadiths that excuse one who errs in interpretation. It is then said to them: What is the difference between matters of knowledge and matters of practice in their being covered by these general texts? And on what basis have you restricted this general wording to matters of belief alone? Second, every matter of practice entails a matter of belief. For example, a man holds that the witr prayer is obligatory. This act entails several matters of belief, including the belief that Allah recorded it in the Preserved Tablet—and this is a matter of belief—and the belief that Allah said to this matter, "Be," and it came to be; this is an attribute of action belonging to Allah, Glorified and Exalted is He. Likewise, he believes that He, Exalted is He, commanded Gabriel to convey this to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him); this too is an attribute of action belonging to Allah, Exalted is He. By implication, he also denies the attribute that Allah, Exalted is He, permitted its omission; likewise, he denies that He recorded permission to omit it in the Preserved Tablet, and denies that Allah commanded Gabriel to convey permission to omit it. All of these, as you can see, are matters of belief, inseparable from any of the matters they have called practical. And I wonder: what is the difference between someone who denies an attribute of action belonging to Allah, Glorified and Exalted is He, that is connected to a practice—such as His making something obligatory—while interpreting the matter mistakenly, like someone who denies that Allah made rinsing the mouth and sniffing water into the nose obligatory in ablution, for example, and someone who denies an attribute of action belonging to Allah that is not connected to it…


[Page 140]

In this regard, the case is like that of someone who denied the actual descent to the lowest heaven, interpreting the texts and being mistaken. Does merely having an act accompany a belief make him undeserving of excuse? And who is more deserving of excuse: a man who held a mistaken belief based only on interpretation, or a man who held a mistaken belief based on interpretation and, as a result, performed an incorrect act by which he sought nearness to Allah, mistakenly adding to Allah's religion something that is not part of it as an innovation, if he did so deliberately? So they excused someone who committed two innovative acts, in belief and in practice, but did not excuse someone who committed only one act, namely belief alone.
Ibn Taymiyya said: "He said in Mukhtasar al-Fatawa al-Misriyya: 'Dividing issues into foundational issues, denial of which constitutes disbelief, and subsidiary issues, denial of which does not constitute disbelief, has no basis among the Companions, the Successors, or the imams of Islam. Rather, it was adopted from the Mu'tazila and similar imams of innovation, and they are inconsistent. For when they are asked, "What defines the foundations of religion?" and they answer, "Issues of belief," they may be told: People have disagreed over whether Muhammad saw his Lord, whether Uthman or Ali was superior, many meanings of the Quran, and the authentication of some hadiths. These are matters of belief, yet there is no disbelief in them, by agreement of the Muslims.
"The obligation of prayer, zakat, and pilgrimage, and the prohibition of indecencies and wine, are practical matters, yet one who denies them is an unbeliever, by agreement...
"If they say that the foundations are matters that are definitive, they may be told: Many matters of reasoning are not definitive. Whether an issue is definitive or speculative varies from person to person. What is conclusive for one person may not be conclusive for another, as in the case of someone who hears the wording of the text"


[Page 141]

And he was certain of what it meant, while it did not attain, in his view, the strength of the other text; thus, it was not even speculative in his view, let alone definitive...

The point is that the imams' positions distinguish between a category and an individual. Whoever reports a disagreement has not grasped the full import of their words...

One group reports two narrations from Ahmad concerning the unrestricted excommunication of the people of innovation. But this was not the position of Ahmad or any of the other imams. Likewise, al-Shafi'i's excommunication of Hafs al-Fard when he said, "The Quran is created." Al-Shafi'i said to him, "You have disbelieved"—that is, your statement is disbelief. For this reason, he did not seek to have him killed; had he considered him a disbeliever, he would have sought to have him killed."

It is stated in Mukhtasar al-Sawa'iq al-Mursala ala al-Jahmiyya wa al-Mu'attila by Ibn al-Qayyim (p. 594):
"The aim is not to examine this issue exhaustively. Rather, the point is that error occurs in what they have called subsidiary matters, just as it occurs in what they have made foundational matters. So we ask them to provide a sound distinction between what may be established in religion by a solitary report and what may not. They will find no way to make such a distinction except through false claims. Then we ask them to distinguish between matters of fundamentals and matters of branches, and to state the criterion for doing so. Then we ask them to distinguish between what its denier incurs sin for—whether the sin of disbelief or of disobedience—and what its denier does not incur sin for. And we ask them to distinguish between what requires definitive certainty and what may be based on probability. They have absolutely no way to establish any of this.


[Page 142]

Al-Juwayni said: They have discussed the distinction between fundamentals and subsidiary matters, and said: "A fundamental is that for which there is definitive evidence, while a subsidiary matter is the opposite." I say: This entails circular reasoning. For if it is said, "Fundamentals are established only by definitive evidence," and then it is said, "A fundamental is that for which there is definitive evidence," this is manifestly circular.
Moreover, many practical rulings—in fact, most of them—are supported by definitive evidence, such as the obligation of purification, prayer, fasting, pilgrimage, and almsgiving; the invalidation of ablution by urination and defecation; and the obligation of ritual bathing after a wet dream. Indeed, most of the Sharia's rulings are supported by definitive evidence, while many issues that they classify as fundamentals are supported by probabilistic evidence.
The same is true of the fundamentals of religion and the fundamentals of jurisprudence: there are more examples than can be listed, such as accepting implications, analogical reasoning, their precedence over general texts, the status of a command after a prohibition, the question of whether an era must pass without dissent, the opinion of a Companion, using mursal reports as evidence, the law of those who came before us, and many times as much besides.
Likewise, among the fundamentals of religion are the question of the state, the Lord's subsistence and pre-eternity—whether these are subsistence and pre-eternity additional to His essence—the question of whether necessary existence is intrinsic to the quiddity or additional to it, affirming an attribute subsisting in the self, and other matters. According to this distinction, these and similar issues would be subsidiary, while those practical rulings would be fundamental.
He said: And it has been said: "A fundamental is that in which worship may be performed only in accordance with one specific command, while a subsidiary matter is the opposite."


[Page 143]

I say: This distinction is more corrupt than the first, for most subsidiary rulings may be observed as acts of worship only in accordance with what was prescribed through the tongue of each prophet. It is not permissible to worship by prostrating to idols, permitting indecencies, killing people, wrongfully taking property, violating people's honor, bearing false witness, and the like. Although those who deny that things are intrinsically good or bad deem it permissible to worship in these ways and say that it is possible for revealed laws from Allah, Exalted is He, to prescribe them, their claim is among the most false of falsehoods. We have explained its corruption in more than sixty ways elsewhere in this book, and it is something whose invalidity is known necessarily.
He said: It has also been said that the foundational matter is one that may be known without the prior advent of revealed law, while the subsidiary matter is otherwise. This distinction, too, is utterly corrupt, for most of the issues they call foundational were not known until after the advent of revealed law, such as that a command entails obligation and a prohibition entails forbiddance, that analogy is authoritative, and that consensus is authoritative. Indeed, most issues of the foundations of religion were known only through transmitted revelation. The permissibility of seeing the Lord, Blessed and Exalted is He, on the Day of Resurrection, and His establishing Himself over His Throne, are unlike the question of His being, in His essence, above created things, for that is innate and self-evident. Most issues concerning the Resurrection and its details are not known before the advent of revealed law, including the issues of the punishment and bliss of the grave, the questioning by the two angels, and other foundational issues that are not known before the advent of revealed law.
Qadi Abu Bakr ibn al-Baqillani said: Any issue concerning which disagreement is forbidden once revealed law has been established, and whose contrary belief makes a person ignorant, is a foundational issue, whether rational or revealed. A subsidiary issue is one concerning which disagreement is not forbidden, or in which the one who errs does not incur sin. Although this is closer than what came before it, it too is false, for if many of the


[Page 144]

The issues of the branches are definitively established, even if there is disagreement about them, and even if one who errs in them is not sinful because the evidence is obscure to him. Even if they are definitive, it does not follow that there must be agreement on definitive matters. The judge conceded this in cases where the text was obscure to someone.
Some have mentioned another distinction, saying: "Issues of the foundations are matters of knowledge, while issues of the branches are matters of knowledge for which two things are required: knowledge and action." What is required in practical matters is also knowledge and action: the heart's love and aversion—its love for the truth indicated and contained in them, and its aversion to the falsehood that contradicts them. Thus, action is not confined to the deeds of the limbs. Rather, the deeds of the heart are the basis of the deeds of the limbs, and the deeds of the limbs follow from them. Every matter of knowledge is therefore accompanied by the heart's faith, affirmation, and love; that too is action—in fact, it is the basis of action. This is something many theologians discussing matters of faith have overlooked, supposing that faith is merely affirmation, without deeds. This is among the ugliest and gravest errors, for many unbelievers were certain of the truthfulness of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), with no doubt about it; yet that affirmation was not accompanied by the heart's love for what he brought, acceptance of it, desire for it, or allegiance and enmity on its account.
Do not neglect this point, for it is extremely important; through it, the true nature of faith is known.
Matters of knowledge are matters of action, and practical matters are matters of knowledge. For the Lawgiver did not deem mere action, without knowledge, sufficient from those legally responsible in practical matters, nor mere knowledge, without action, in matters of knowledge.


[Page 145]

Others distinguished between the fundamentals and the subsidiary matters by saying that the fundamentals are matters whose denial constitutes disbelief, such as divine oneness, prophethood, the Resurrection, and affirming the divine attributes; while subsidiary matters are those whose denial does not constitute disbelief, such as the obligation to recite al-Fatiha in prayer, the requirement of composure, the obligation to wipe the entire head in ablution, and the like. This distinction is neither universally applicable nor reversible, for many subsidiary matters are such that denying them constitutes disbelief, while many fundamental matters are such that denying them does not, as has been explained above.
Moreover, declaring someone a disbeliever is a ruling of the Sharia: a disbeliever is one whom Allah and His Messenger have declared a disbeliever. Disbelief is the denial of something known to have been brought by the Messenger, whether it belongs to the matters you call doctrinal or practical. Whoever denies something brought by the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), after knowing that he brought it, is a disbeliever, whether the matter is subtle or weighty in religion.
Others distinguished between the fundamentals and subsidiary matters by saying that the fundamentals concern reports, while subsidiary matters concern commands. This distinction is no different from the distinctions mentioned above, and it too is unsound, for the servant is charged with affirming both, through knowledge, faith, action, love, acceptance, allegiance, and enmity, as stated above.
Others distinguished between them by saying that the fundamentals are matters in which following another's opinion is impermissible, while it is permissible in subsidiary matters. Besides being an impermissible circular argument, they may be asked: "In what matters is following another's opinion permissible?" They would answer: "In subsidiary matters." And in what matters is it impermissible? "In the fundamentals." This distinction is also unsound both in its application and its converse, for following another's opinion is impermissible in many subsidiary matters, such as the obligation of purification, prayer, and zakat, and the prohibition of wine, usury, indecencies, and injustice. For whoever does not know that the final word is cut off here.


[Page 146]

The Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) brought that, yet someone who doubted it did not know that he was a Messenger. Likewise, whoever did not know that he brought monotheism, affirmation of the Messengers, affirmation of the bodily resurrection, and affirmation of the divine attributes, exaltedness, and speech, did not know that he was a Messenger. Thus, many matters of transmitted reports and religious obligations may be followed on authority by one unable to reason from evidence, just as many practical matters may not be followed on authority.
Dividing religion into what is established by a solitary report and what is not established by it is a division that is neither consistently applicable nor comprehensive, and has no sound proof to support it.
Moreover, following on authority is accepting another person's statement without proof. Whoever accepts another person's statement about what he relates from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) that he brought, whether as a report or an obligation, accepts it only because he attributes it to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). This is proof; however, not everyone may be able to formulate its premises and rebut the doubts that oppose it. Not everyone who knows something through its proof can set it out with all its premises, express it, or rebut what opposes it. If inability to do so counts as following on authority, then the majority of the community would be following on authority in monotheism, affirmation of the Messenger's mission, and the resurrection. If inability to do so does not count as following on authority, then they would not be following on authority in monotheism, affirmation of the Messenger's mission, and the resurrection; nor would they be following on authority in most of the practical rulings they need. This is the truth: the majority of the community base their acts of worship, prohibitions, and permissions on what they have necessarily learned from their Prophet, namely, that he brought it. If they were asked to explain its proof, most of them would be unable to do so, just as they are certain of monotheism, and that ...


[Page 147]

That Allah is above His creation, that the Quran is His speech, and that He will resurrect those in the graves. If most of them were asked about this, they would be unable to answer.
As for the sixth point: namely, that the conjecture derived from the reports of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) is, in their view, stronger than the certainty based on those illusory propositions—this is known to anyone familiar with both. Someone with no experience of either hears them call their false propositions "conclusive rational proofs" and "demonstrations that yield certainty," while calling the explicit texts of the Quran and Sunnah "apparent transmitted reports that do not yield certainty." He may then come to believe they are right, out of deference to them, thinking well of them, and relying on some of the doubts they raise. But one who has insight into what the Messenger brought (peace and blessings be upon him) and into what these people hold knows with certainty that the matter is otherwise, and that the propositions by which they oppose the texts yield neither knowledge nor even conjecture. Indeed, they say: "Sound reason and innate human nature testify that these propositions are false and invalid." Even if a large group agrees on them, among all the groups of people of falsehood you will find each group agreeing on something whose corruption is necessarily evident to reason and to the innate disposition with which Allah created people. Each group of theologians bears witness against its opponents that they have contradicted sound reason and innate human nature. We have already mentioned some examples of this earlier in this book, concerning matters in which the theologians and philosophers contradicted sound reason. What is astonishing is that you see many of them assert their position categorically and declare anyone who disagrees with them an unbeliever, then themselves assert the opposite or suspend judgment on the matter. This is extremely common among them.


[Page 148]

Abu al-Muzaffar al-Sam'ani said: Every group among the innovators believes that what it says is the truth upheld by the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) and his Companions. For they all claim the law of Islam, outwardly adhere to its symbols, and believe that what Muhammad brought is the truth. But afterward, the paths diverged for them, and they introduced into the religion what Allah and His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) had not authorized. So each group claimed that it was the one adhering to the law of Islam, and that the truth upheld by the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) was what it believed and professed.
However, Allah, Exalted is He, decreed that the truth and sound creed should be found only with the people of hadith and transmitted reports, because they received their religion and beliefs from one generation to the next, and from one era to the next, until they reached the Successors. The Successors received it from the Companions of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), and the Companions received it from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). There is no way to know the upright religion and straight path to which the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) called people except by this path followed by the people of hadith.
As for the other sects, they sought religion by a path other than its own, because they turned to their own reasoning, thoughts, and opinions. When they heard something from the Book and the Sunnah, they measured it against the standard of their intellects: if it seemed sound to them, they accepted it, but if it did not weigh up according to their intellects, they rejected it; and if they were compelled to accept it, they distorted it.
Through far-fetched interpretations and repugnant meanings, they turned away from the truth and strayed from it, cast religion behind their backs, and trampled the Sunnah underfoot.


[Page 149]

As for the People of the Sunnah, they placed the Book and the Sunnah before them and sought the religion through them. Whatever occurred to them by way of reason, thoughts, or opinions, they presented to the Book and the Sunnah. If they found it in agreement with them, they accepted it and thanked Allah for showing it to them and granting them success in it. If they found it contrary to them, they abandoned what had occurred to them and turned to the Book and the Sunnah, directing suspicion toward themselves. For the Book and the Sunnah guide only to the truth, whereas a person's opinion may be true or false.
This is the view of Abu Sulayman al-Darani, who was the most singular of the people of his time. He said, "Whenever my soul suggested something to me, I sought two witnesses for it from the Book and the Sunnah. If they were produced, I accepted it; otherwise, I rejected it."
(He said): Among the things that show that the people of hadith are upon the truth is that if you were to examine all their authored books, from beginning to end, old and new, you would find that despite the differences in their lands and times, the great distances between their homes, and each one of them residing in a different region, they follow a single course and a single pattern in matters of belief. They adhere to a way from which they neither deviate nor turn aside; their hearts in this regard are as one heart. Their transmitted reports show no disagreement or division in anything, however slight. Indeed, if you gathered everything they have said and transmitted from their predecessors, you would find it as though it had come from one heart and been spoken by one tongue. Is there any clearer proof of the truth than this? Allah, Exalted is He, says: {Do they not reflect upon the Quran? Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have found much inconsistency in it} [al-Nisa: 82]. And He, Exalted is He, says: {And hold fast, all together, to the rope of Allah, and do not be divided. And remember Allah's favor upon you when you were}


[Page 150]

"...enemies, then He brought your hearts together, and by His blessing you became brothers" [Al Imran: 103].
But when you look at the people of innovation, you see them divided and at odds, factions and parties. You can hardly find two of them who share the same creed. They declare one another innovators; indeed, they go so far as to declare one another unbelievers: a son declares his father an unbeliever, a brother his brother, and a neighbor his neighbor. You see them perpetually in dispute, mutual hatred, and disagreement; their lives come to an end without their views ever being united. "You think them united, but their hearts are divided. That is because they are a people who do not reason" [Al-Hashr: 14]. Have you not heard that, despite the Mu'tazilites' agreement in this regard, the Baghdadis among them declare the Basrans unbelievers, and the Basrans declare the Baghdadis unbelievers? The followers of Abu Ali al-Jubba'i and his son declare Abu Hashim and his followers unbelievers, while the followers of Abu Hashim declare Abu Ali and his followers unbelievers. The same is true of the rest of their leading figures and the authors of their doctrinal treatises: when you consider their views, you see them divided, each declaring the others unbelievers. The same is true of the Kharijites and the Rafidites among themselves, and likewise all the other innovators. Is anything a clearer sign of falsehood than this? Allah, Exalted is He, said: "Those who divided their religion and became factions—you have nothing to do with them. Their affair rests with Allah" [Al-An'am: 159]. Thus Allah absolved His Messenger of those who are characterized by such division and disagreement.
(He said): The reason the people of hadith were united was that they took their religion from the Book and the Sunnah, and through transmission; this brought them unity and harmony. The people of innovation took their religion from their own reason, and this brought them division and disagreement. For reports and narrations transmitted by reliable and precise authorities rarely differ; and even if they differ in wording or...


[Page 151]

of His words, then that disagreement does not harm the religion or undermine it. As for rational judgments, thoughts, and opinions, they rarely agree; rather, each person's intellect, opinion, and thought show him something different from what they show another.

He said: In this way, the difference between disagreement over subsidiary legal questions and disagreement over the creeds that form the foundations becomes clear. We found that the Companions of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), may Allah be pleased with them, disagreed after him over religious rulings, yet they did not split apart or become factions, because they did not depart from the religion. They considered matters in which they had been given latitude, and their statements and opinions differed on many questions, such as the questions of the grandfather, the polytheistic inheritance case, distant kindred, mothers of children, and other matters. Thus, their disagreement over these matters was praiseworthy, and this kind of disagreement was a mercy for this community, as Allah granted them success and certainty. He then gave scholars latitude to exercise judgment concerning matters whose rulings they did not find in the Revelation and the Sunnah. Despite this disagreement, they were people of affection and sincere counsel; the brotherhood of Islam remained between them, and the bond of harmony among them was not severed. But when these ruinous whims arose, calling their adherents to the Fire, and they became factions, brotherhood in religion was severed and harmony fell away. This shows that estrangement and division arose only over newly introduced matters, which Satan invented and put on the tongues of his allies so that they would disagree and denounce one another as unbelievers. Thus, whenever a matter arose in Islam over which people debated and disagreed, if that disagreement did not give rise among them to enmity, rancor, or division, but harmony, sincere counsel, affection, mercy, and compassion remained between them, then


[Page 152]

We know that this is one of the matters of Islam in which it is permissible to investigate. The other person holds that some of those opinions do not entail declaring anyone an innovator or an unbeliever. Differences of this kind appeared among the Companions and the Successors, while affection and love remained between them. Whenever a new matter arose over which they differed, and their disagreement led to allegiance and aversion, turning away from one another and severing ties—and perhaps even reached the point of declaring one another unbelievers—you know that this has nothing to do with religion. Rather, everyone of sound reason must avoid such matters and refrain from delving into them.
Allah Most High has made it a condition of our adherence to Islam that we become brothers in this regard. He, Exalted is He, says: {And remember Allah's blessing upon you: when you were enemies, He brought your hearts together, and by His blessing you became brothers} [Al Imran: 103].
He said: "If someone says: Delving into matters of divine decree, the divine attributes, and faith leads to severing ties and turning away from one another, so they must be set aside and avoided, as you have established."
"The answer is: We said this only about newly introduced matters. As for these matters, they must be accepted according to what has been established through transmission from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) and his Companions. It is not permissible for us to turn away from transmitting, narrating, and explaining them, just as with the foundations of Islam, calling people to affirm the oneness of Allah, and proclaiming the two testimonies of faith. We have made clear that the straight path is with the people of hadith, and that the truth lies in what they narrated and transmitted."))


[Page 153]

(Note) It is stated in Mukhtasar al-Sawa'iq al-Mursala ala al-Jahmiyya wa al-Mu'attala (p. 590):
"What, then, is the difference between the domain of commands and the domain of reports, such that these reports may be used as proof in one but not the other? This distinction is invalid by the consensus of the Muslim community, for it has always used these hadiths as proof in matters of transmitted knowledge, just as it uses them as proof in practical matters involving commands. This is especially so since practical rulings entail reporting about Allah that He prescribed such-and-such, made it obligatory, and approved it as religion; His legislation and religion relate back to His names and attributes. The Companions, the Successors, those who followed them, and the people of hadith and Sunnah have always used these reports as proof in questions of the attributes, divine decree, names, and rulings. It has never been transmitted from any of them that he permitted using them as proof in questions of rulings but not in reports about Allah, His names, and His attributes.
Where, then, are the early authorities who distinguished between the two domains? Indeed, their predecessors were some of the later theologians, who had no concern for what came from Allah, His Messenger, and his Companions. Rather, they turn hearts away from seeking guidance in this domain through the Book, the Sunnah, and the statements of the Companions, and refer them instead to the opinions of the theologians and the contrived principles of those who strain after argument. They are the ones known to distinguish between the two matters. They divided religion into matters of knowledge and matters of practice, calling them fundamentals and branches, and said: 'The truth in matters of fundamentals is one, and whoever opposes it is either an unbeliever or a sinner. As for matters of the branches, Allah Most High has no specific ruling concerning them, error in them is inconceivable, and every person exercising independent reasoning arrives at Allah Most High's ruling, which is His ruling.' If this division were merely a matter of terminology, it would not distinguish by it …"


[Page 154]

What they called fundamentals and what they called subsidiary matters—how could this distinction be valid when they have based rulings upon it that they devised with their own intellects and opinions? Among these is declaring someone an unbeliever for erring in matters of fundamentals, but not in matters of subsidiary rulings. This is among the most false of falsehoods, as we shall explain. Another is affirming subsidiary rulings on the basis of solitary reports, but not fundamentals, and so on. Any division that is not supported by the Book, the Sunnah, and the principles of the Sharia is a false division that must be discarded.
This division is one of the foundations of the people's misguidance. They distinguished between what they called fundamentals and what they called subsidiary matters, and stripped the subsidiary matters of any specific ruling of Allah concerning them. Rather, they held that Allah's ruling in them varies according to the differing opinions of the jurists. As for what they called fundamentals, they held that whoever errs in them is an unbeliever or a sinner. They claimed consensus on this distinction, yet what they presented as consensus is not recorded from any of the imams of the Muslims, nor from any of the Companions or Successors. This is the habit of the people of speculative theology: they report consensus on something that none of the imams of the Muslims said; indeed, the imams of Islam held the opposite view. Imam Ahmad said, "Whoever claims consensus has lied." This is the claim of al-Asamm, Ibn Ulayya, and those like them: they seek to invalidate the Sunnahs of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) by means of the consensus they claim.
It is known with certainty from the textual proofs and the consensus of the Companions and Successors, and is explicitly stated by the four imams, that jurists who disagree over legal rulings are not all alike; rather, among them are those who are correct and those who are mistaken. Thus, discourse on what they called fundamentals and what they called subsidiary matters divides into that which accords with the truth as it actually is and that which does not. The division of belief concerning a ruling into that which accords with the truth and that which does not is like the division of


[Page 155]

Belief concerning wine is divided into that which corresponds to reality and that which does not. The one who says of something, "It is lawful," and the one who says, "It is forbidden," with one of them being right and the other wrong, are like the one who says that He, Glorified is He, will be seen and the one who says that He will not be seen, with one of them being right and the other wrong. Lying about Allah, Exalted is He, whether by mistake or deliberately in this case, is like lying about Him deliberately or by mistake in the other case. For one person reports of Allah that He commanded such-and-such and permitted it, while the other reports that He forbade it and declared it unlawful. One of them is certainly mistaken.
If it is said: "The difference between the two is that it is possible that, in reality, it is neither lawful nor forbidden; rather, it is lawful for the one who believes it to be lawful and forbidden for the one who believes it to be forbidden."
It is said: "This is false for many reasons. We have mentioned in the book al-Miftah and elsewhere (among them) that it contradicts the explicit text of the Quran and the Sunnah, and the consensus of the Companions."
This preceding discussion is directed at those who declare others unbelievers on the basis of interpretation, because they say that creed is established by solitary reports; thus, the argument applies to them.
As for those who say that creed is not established by a solitary report, the following is the response to them:
First: His saying, Exalted is He, "When Allah and His Messenger have decreed a matter": "matter" is indefinite in the context of a conditional clause, so it encompasses knowledge and practice.
Second: "Whatever the Messenger gives you, take it": the word "whatever" is general.
Third: "So that they may gain understanding of the religion": religion encompasses knowledge and practice.


[Page 156]

Fourth: The claim that a solitary report does not establish a creed is itself a creed. So did you believe this creed with certainty or conjecture? According to your own classification, it is conjectural, because the interpretation of the verse, "They follow nothing but conjecture," is conjectural, as you believe.
Fifth: He said in al-Nihaya: "Conjecture is doubt that occurs to you, then you ascertain it."
Sixth: "Al-Ihkam fi Usul al-Ahkam — Ibn Hazm" (1/120):
"If we find proof that a solitary report, with an unbroken chain of transmission to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), concerning the rulings of the Sharia, cannot be subject to falsehood or error, then our position and theirs—that a report from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) concerning the Sharia cannot be subject to falsehood or error—is established. If we find no proof of this, then that is their position. Proof of it has indeed been established, and praise be to Allah, as we shall mention, if Allah Most High wills.
We know that the Quran contains verses that have undoubtedly been abrogated, because Allah Most High says: {Whenever We abrogate a verse or cause it to be forgotten, We bring one better than it or the like of it. Do you not know that Allah has power over all things?} Scholars have differed concerning them: one group has said that a particular verse is abrogated, while another has said that it is not abrogated but remains in force. Yet no Muslim has ever said—not Ibn Kaysan nor anyone else—that one must refrain from acting upon any part of the Quran for this reason, out of fear of acting upon something abrogated, which it is impermissible to act upon. Rather, one must act upon every verse in it until its abrogation is established, at which point one ceases to act upon it.
{That a solitary report from a trustworthy narrator, with an unbroken chain of transmission to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), concerning the rulings of the Sharia, establishes knowledge and can never be subject to falsehood or error. We say, and Allah Most High is the source of success: Allah, Mighty and Exalted, said of His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him): {And what…


[Page 157]

He does not speak from his own desire; it is nothing but revelation revealed." And the Exalted said, commanding His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) to say: "Say: I am no unprecedented messenger, and I do not know what will be done with me or with you. I follow only what is revealed to me, and I am only a clear warner." And the Exalted said: "Indeed, We have sent down the Reminder, and indeed, We will surely preserve it." And the Exalted said: "With clear proofs and scriptures. And We sent down to you the Reminder so that you may make clear to people what was sent down to them, and so that they may reflect." Thus it is established that all the words of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) concerning religion are revelation from Allah, Mighty and Majestic, without doubt; and there is no disagreement among any of the people of language and the Sharia that
every revelation sent down from Allah, Exalted is He, is a Reminder sent down. Therefore, all revelation is certainly preserved by Allah, Exalted is He, through His preservation of it. Everything whose preservation Allah has undertaken is guaranteed not to be lost, nor will anything in it ever be distorted in a way that clarification cannot expose as false. For if anything else were possible, the words of Allah, Exalted is He, would be a lie, and His guarantee would be untrustworthy. This would not occur to anyone possessing even a measure of reason. It is therefore necessary that what Muhammad (peace and blessings be upon him) brought to us is preserved, with Allah, Exalted is He, undertaking its preservation, and conveyed exactly as it is to everyone who seeks it, continuously until the end of the world. The Exalted said: "Say: What is the greatest testimony? Say: Allah is witness between me and you. And this Quran has been revealed to me so that I may warn you by it and whomever it reaches. Do you truly testify that there are other gods alongside Allah? Say: I do not testify. Say: He is only One God, and I am free of what you associate with Him."
Since this is so, we know necessarily that there is absolutely no way for anything the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said concerning religion to be lost, nor is there any way for it to be


[Page 158]

Falsehood may be mixed into it so thoroughly that no one can distinguish it with certainty. For if that were possible, the Reminder would not be preserved, and the words of Allah, Exalted is He, "Indeed, it is We who sent down the Reminder, and indeed, We will preserve it," would be a lie and a broken promise. No Muslim would say this. If someone says that Allah meant by this the Quran alone, and that it is the Quran whose preservation He guaranteed, but not the rest of the revelation that is not Quran, we say to him—and Allah is the source of success—this is a baseless claim, unsupported by proof, and an unjustified restriction of the meaning of the Reminder. Anything of this kind is false, in light of Allah's words: "And they say, 'None will enter Paradise unless he is a Jew or a Christian.' Those are their wishes. Say, 'Produce your proof, if you are truthful.'" It is thus established that he has no proof for his claim, and so he is not truthful in it. The word "Reminder" applies to everything Allah sent down to His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), whether Quran or Sunnah—revelation by which the Quran is explained. Moreover, Allah, Exalted is He, says: "With clear proofs and scriptures. And We sent down to you the Reminder so that you may make clear to people what was sent down to them, and so that they may reflect." It is thus established that he (peace and blessings be upon him) was commanded to explain the Quran to people. The Quran contains many matters stated in general terms, such as prayer, zakat, pilgrimage, and other things whose details of what Allah, Exalted is He, has made obligatory upon us are not known from its wording alone; rather, they are known through the explanation of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). If his explanation of those general matters were not preserved and guaranteed to be free of what does not belong to it, then the benefit of the Quran's text would be lost, and most of the religious duties it makes obligatory upon us would be invalidated. If we did not know what Allah, Exalted is He, truly intended by them, then what would remain would be the error of the one who erred or the deliberate lie of the liar—and Allah forbid this. We also say to the one who claims that the report of a single trustworthy narrator from another like him, tracing back to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), does not yield knowledge, and that it may contain a lie:


[Page 159]

And conjecture, and that it is not guaranteed to be preserved. Tell us: Is it possible, in your view, for a law of obligation or prohibition brought by the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) to remain in force for the Muslims after his death, without being abrogated, yet to be unknown, so that no one knows it with certainty?
among the people of Islam anywhere in the world? And is it possible, in your view, for a ruling founded on a lie or an erroneous supposition to have been accepted and passed into effect, and become so thoroughly mixed with the rulings of the Sharia that no one among the people of Islam anywhere in the world could ever distinguish it, or is neither of these two possibilities possible in your view?
If they say, "They can never occur; rather, we have secured against that," then they have adopted our position and affirmed that every report transmitted by a trustworthy narrator from another trustworthy narrator, with an unbroken chain back to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), concerning religion, is true and was indeed said by him, and that it yields certain knowledge. We affirm its soundness and hold that a fabricated report, or one mistakenly attributed to him, which the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) never said, can never become mixed with it in such a way that falsehood can never be distinguished from truth. But if they say, "Rather, all of that is possible," then they have judged that the religion of Islam has been corrupted and most of it invalidated, and that what Allah, Exalted is He, commanded has become mixed with what He did not command in a way that no one can ever distinguish; and that they can never know what Allah, Exalted is He, commanded them to do, as distinct from what He did not command them to do, nor what liars and frivolous people have fabricated, as distinct from what the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) brought, except through conjecture, which is the most false of speech and avails nothing against the truth. This is a casting off of Islam, a demolition of the religion, and a sowing of doubt in


[Page 160]

the revealed laws. Then we say to them: Tell us, if all of that is possible in your view, did Allah, Exalted is He, command you to act upon what reliable transmitters have narrated with a chain of transmission going back to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), or did He not command you to act upon it? One of the two must be the case. If they say, "Allah, Exalted is He, did not command us to do that," they have joined the Mu'tazilites, and their answer to this claim will come, if Allah, Exalted is He, wills. But if they say, "Rather, Allah, Exalted is He, commanded us to act upon that," we say to them: You have thereby claimed that Allah, Exalted is He, commanded you, in your religion, to act upon what He did not command you to act upon: things fabricated by liars and mistaken by those prone to error; and that He commanded you to attribute to Him, Exalted is He, and to His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), what never came to you from them and what Allah, Exalted is He, and His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) never said. This is a definitive proof that He, Mighty and Majestic is He, commanded lying about Him, made it obligatory to act upon falsehood, upon what is not part of the religion, and upon what liars prescribed without His permission.
Allah, Exalted is He—and this is an exceedingly grave matter, which no Muslim would deem permissible to say. Then we ask them about what they claim is possible: that some of the rulings concerning religion which the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) stated, whether imposing an obligation or a prohibition, might have been lost, so that no one knows whether we are still bound to act upon them or whether that obligation has lapsed. One of these two must be the case. If they say, "Rather, we remain bound by them," we say to them: How can we be bound to act upon what we do not know, what has not reached us, and what will never reach us? This is precisely the burden, hardship, and difficulty from which Allah, Exalted is He, has granted us security. But if they say, "Rather, the obligation to act upon them has lapsed," we say to them: You have thus permitted the abrogation of some of the laws of Islam, laws that were established, confirmed, and binding when the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) died. So tell us: who abrogated them?


[Page 161]

and abrogated it, even though the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) had died while it remained binding upon us and had not been abrogated. This is contrary to Islam and constitutes a complete departure from it. If they say, "It is not permissible for the ruling of a revealed law to lapse when the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) died while it remained binding upon us and had not been abrogated," we say to them: "Then on what basis did you permit this kind of preservation of the revealed law, but not the complete preservation of the revealed law, such that no falsehood ever commanded by Allah Most High could become mixed with it in a way that makes it impossible to distinguish the truth commanded by Allah Most High from the falsehood He never commanded?" They have no way out of this, and there is no difference between someone who forbids the lapse of a true revealed law but permits its being mixed with falsehood, and someone who forbids truth in the revealed law from being mixed with falsehood but permits the lapse of a true revealed law. All of this is false and utterly impermissible, and we are assured that it has not occurred, praise be to Allah. Once this is established, it is certain that we are assured, praise be to Allah, that the report of a trustworthy narrator from another like him, traced back to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), is true beyond doubt and entails both action and knowledge. Allah Most High also said: {With clear proofs and scriptures. And We sent down to you the Reminder so that you may make clear to people what was sent down to them, and so that they may reflect.} And He Most High said: {O Messenger, convey what has been sent down to you from your Lord. If you do not, then you have not conveyed His message. And Allah will protect you from the people. Indeed, Allah does not guide the disbelieving people.} We ask them: Did the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) make clear what Allah had sent down to him, or did he not make it clear? And did he convey what Allah had sent down to him, or did he not convey it? One of these must be the case. They say that he (peace and blessings be upon him) conveyed what Allah had sent down to him, made it clear to the people, and established proof against those to whom it was conveyed. We then ask them: Does that conveyance and clarification remain?


[Page 162]

For us, and until the Day of Resurrection—or are they not enduring? If they say, "Rather, they are enduring until the Day of Resurrection," they have returned to our position and acknowledged that the truth in all that Allah, Exalted is He, revealed concerning religion is clear: what He did not reveal has not been conveyed to us, and this remains so until the Day of Resurrection. This is precisely our position: that a report transmitted by a trustworthy individual from another like him, with an unbroken chain back to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), is certainly true in what it makes clear, and entails both knowledge and action.
But if they say, "Rather, they are not enduring," they have fallen into a grave matter and declared that much of the religion has been lost, that the conveyance of many of the laws has ceased, and that the Messenger of Allah's (peace and blessings be upon him) clarification of much of the religion has vanished so completely that it can never be recovered. This is the position of the Rafidites—or worse than theirs, for the Rafidites claimed that the true religion exists with a person whose presence in the world is assured, whereas these people have abolished it from the entire world. We seek refuge in Allah from both positions.
Furthermore, Allah, Exalted is He, says: {Say, "My Lord has forbidden only indecencies, both open and hidden, sin, wrongful aggression, associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and saying about Allah what you do not know."} And He, Exalted is He, says: {They are nothing but names that you and your fathers have given them; Allah has sent down no authority for them. They follow nothing but conjecture and what their souls desire, though guidance has come to them from their Lord.} And He, Exalted is He, says: {They have no knowledge of it; they follow nothing but conjecture, and conjecture can avail nothing against the truth.} And Allah, Exalted is He, censures a people who said: {When it was said, "Allah's promise is true, and there is no doubt about the Hour," you said, "We do not know what the Hour is. We only suppose; we are not certain."} And He, Exalted is He, says: {Those who associate others with Allah will say, "Had Allah willed, we would not have associated anything with Him, nor would our fathers, nor would we have forbidden ..."}


[Page 163]

Something similar was denied by those before them, until they tasted Our might. Say, "Do you have any knowledge that you can produce for us? You follow nothing but conjecture, and you do nothing but guess."} It has been established that Allah, Exalted is He, has made it obligatory for us to act upon the report of a trustworthy individual, transmitted from another like him, all the way back to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him); and to say, "The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) commanded such-and-such," "He (peace and blessings be upon him) said such-and-such," and "He (peace and blessings be upon him) did such-and-such." He has forbidden speaking about His religion on the basis of conjecture, and Allah, Exalted is He, has forbidden us to say anything about Him without knowledge. If the report in question could contain a lie or an error, Allah, Exalted is He, would have commanded us to say about Him what we do not know, and would have made it obligatory for us to rule in religion on the basis of conjecture, which we cannot be certain of, which is falsehood that avails nothing against the truth, and which is not the guidance that has come to us from Allah, Exalted is He. This is the lie, the slander, and the falsehood that it is unlawful to utter, and that Allah, Exalted is He, has forbidden us to utter, as well as the forbidden fabrication. It is therefore certain that the report in question is true, its unseen content being established beyond doubt, and that it entails both knowledge and action. And Allah, Exalted is He, is the source of success. Thus, anyone who says that acting upon a solitary report is obligatory, yet that it is still conjecture, that its unseen content is not established beyond doubt, and that it does not entail knowledge, is saying that Allah, Exalted is He, has charged us to say about Him what we have no knowledge of, and to rule in our religion on the basis of conjecture, which He, Exalted is He, has forbidden us to use as a basis for judgment.
in religion, and this is very grave. Also, Allah Most High says:

{Forbidden to you are carrion, blood, the flesh of swine, and that over which a name other than Allah's has been invoked; and the strangled animal, the one beaten to death, the one killed by a fall, the one gored to death, and that which a wild beast has eaten—except what you slaughter; and that which is sacrificed upon


[Page 164]

"...and that you seek to determine your fate by means of divining arrows. That is wickedness. Today those who disbelieve have despaired of your religion, so do not fear them; fear Me. Today I have perfected your religion for you, completed My favor upon you, and chosen Islam as your religion. But whoever is compelled by severe hunger, without inclining toward sin, then Allah is Forgiving, Merciful." And the Exalted says: "Whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will never be accepted from him, and in the Hereafter he will be among the losers." And the Exalted says: "Indeed, the religion with Allah is Islam. Those who were given the Scripture differed only after knowledge had come to them, out of mutual envy. And whoever disbelieves in the signs of Allah, then Allah is swift in reckoning." And the Exalted says: "Humanity was one community, then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of good news and warners, and sent down with them the Book in truth to judge between people concerning that over which they differed. None differed over it except those who had been given it, after clear proofs had come to them, out of mutual envy. Then Allah guided those who believed to the truth concerning that over which they differed, by His permission. And Allah guides whomever He wills to a straight path." Abu Muhammad said: We say to those who hold it possible that what Allah, Exalted is He, commanded His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) to explain to us of the law of Islam has not been preserved, and that it may have been altered and mixed with fabricated lies in such a way that they can never be distinguished: Tell us, does Allah's perfecting the religion, His choosing Islam as our religion, and His refusal to accept any religion other than Islam all remain in force for us until the Day of Resurrection, or were they only for the Companions (may Allah be pleased with them), or for neither the Companions nor us? One of these positions must be taken. If they say, "For neither the Companions nor us," then whoever says this is an unbeliever, for he openly denies Allah, Exalted is He. No Muslim would say this. But if they say, "Rather, all of this remains in force for us until the Day of Resurrection," then they are necessarily committed to our position, and it is established that the laws...


[Page 165]

The entirety of Islam, complete, has reached us and will continue to reach us until the Day of Resurrection. If they say, "Rather, it was for him, peace be upon him, only with those who witnessed him, not in the transmission of the religion to those who came after them," we say to them: If you allow the protection from error in transmitting the religion to have lapsed after his death, peace be upon him, and allow corruption, decay, invalidity, addition, omission, and distortion to enter the religion, then on what basis do you distinguish between what you allow to happen after him, peace be upon him, and what you forbid from happening during his lifetime? If they say, "Because then he, peace be upon him, would not have conveyed what he was commanded to convey, nor would he have been protected from error, and Allah, Exalted is He, says: {O Messenger, convey what has been sent down to you from your Lord. If you do not, then you have not conveyed His message. And Allah will protect you from the people. Indeed, Allah does not guide the disbelieving people}," it is said to them: Yes, and this very conveyance that you dispute, in which, by your unanimous agreement with us, he, peace be upon him, was protected from lying and error, has reached us just as it reached the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them. There is no difference. The religion is binding upon us just as it was binding upon them, equally in every respect.
Thus, protection from error in conveying the religion is necessary, remains assured, and must continue until the Day of Resurrection, just as it was in force for the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, equally in every respect. Whoever denies this has concluded that the proof in matters of religion is not established against us; yet proof cannot be established by something whose truth or falsehood is unknown. Then we say to them: Allah, Exalted is He, likewise says: {Indeed, it is We who sent down the Reminder, and indeed, We will preserve it} … If they claim consensus, we say to them: Among the Karramiyya are those who say that he, peace be upon him, was not protected from error in conveying the Sharia. If they say, "These are not counted among those whose agreement constitutes consensus," we say: You are right; those who say that the religion is not preserved, and that many of the laws Allah, Exalted is He, sent down have indeed…


[Page 166]

It became invalid and was mixed with fabricated falsehood and imagined notions so thoroughly that right guidance could no longer be distinguished from error, truth from falsehood, or the religion of Allah, Exalted is He, from the religion of Satan—ever. If they say, "Rather, the virtue of what the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) brought as religion remains protected from error until the Day of Resurrection," then they have arrived at the truth, which is our position, and praise belongs to Allah, Exalted is He. If they say, "The attribute of every reporter and his nature are such that his report may be true or false, or mistaken; and your claim that the report of a single upright narrator in the Sharia necessitates knowledge is contrary to the nature of reports and reporters, and violates the attributes and customary patterns of all of them," we say to them: It is not to be denied that Allah, Exalted is He, may alter whatever natural properties He wills, once proof establishes that He has done so. What is astonishing is that you deny this while holding precisely the same position when you affirm that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) was protected from lying and error in conveying the Sharia. This is exactly what you have denied. Indeed, you were not content with contradicting yourselves by affirming it where you were right and denying it in the case of the report of a single upright narrator; you went further and asserted sheer falsehood when you allowed all communities to agree upon error in their consensus of opinion. That is the nature and attribute of all of them. Yet you denied the possibility of error and delusion in what you claim to be the consensus of the Muslim community alone in its reasoning by analogy. Far be it from Allah that the community should agree upon falsehood, while analogy is itself falsehood. Thus you violated customary patterns and declared natural properties impossible without proof—especially if the one opposing us is among the Murji'a, who categorically assert that it is impossible for a Jew or Christian to know in his heart that Allah, Exalted is He, is true. Such people have declared natural properties impossible without proof, yet denied that they could be altered when …


[Page 167]

The proof has established it. If they say, "Then you must say that those who transmitted the religious reports uttered by the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) are infallible in transmitting them, and that each one of them is protected in his transmission from deliberately lying and from falling into error," we say to them: Yes, this is what we say, and on this we are certain and firm. Every upright person who transmitted a report from
The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), in matters of religion, or his action, peace be upon him: that narrator is protected from deliberately lying, and this is certain in the sight of Allah, Exalted is He. He is also protected from the possibility of erring in it, unless a clarification is conveyed; and Allah, Exalted is He, must clarify what he has erred in, just as He did with His Prophet, peace be upon him, when he mistakenly performed two rak'ahs instead of three, on account of the proofs we have already presented concerning the preservation of the entire Sharia and the clarification of what is not part of it. We know necessarily that anyone who speaks truthfully in any report is, without doubt, protected from lying and error in that report. So what is objectionable about this? If they say, "Allah, Exalted is He, has charged us to think well of Him, and the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, 'Allah, Exalted is He, says: I am as My servant thinks of Me,'" we say: This has nothing to do with making religious rulings on the basis of conjecture. Rather, it is all one matter, because He, Exalted is He, has forbidden us to say of Him what we do not know. We do not know whether He will forgive us or punish us, so we are obliged to refrain from asserting anything about that, while maintaining hope and fear. He has forbidden us to make claims about religion, prohibition, permissibility, and obligation that we do not know, and He has clarified everything of this that He has imposed upon us. Thus, we must be certain of all of it, just as we must be certain that the unbelievers will remain forever in the Fire and the believers forever in Paradise; there is no difference. It is not permissible to make conjectural claims about any of this. If they say, "You yourselves say that Allah


[Page 168]

The Exalted has commanded us to judge according to the testimony of one upright witness together with the claimant's oath, according to the testimony of two or more upright witnesses, and according to the oath of the defendant when the claimant has no evidence, in cases involving the unlawful taking of lives, unlawful sexual relations, unlawful bodily harm, and unlawful property. By your own admission, all of this could, in reality, be contrary to what the witness testified to and what the person swearing an oath swore to. This is judgment based on conjecture, which you condemned us for in our position on the report of a single narrator. There is no difference. We said to them:
By Allah's enabling grace, there are clear distinctions between the two matters, as clear as the sun. The first is that Allah, Exalted is He, has undertaken to preserve the religion, complete it, and make clear what belongs to it and what does not. But He has never undertaken to preserve our lives, our sexual integrity, our bodies, or our property in this world. Rather, the Exalted has decreed that much of all this will be taken unjustly in this world.
The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) explicitly stated this when he said, "You bring your disputes to me, and I am only a human being. Perhaps one of you is more persuasive in his argument than the other, so I may rule in his favor according to what I hear. If I rule in someone's favor concerning any part of his brother's right, let him not take it, for I am only allotting him a portion of the Fire." And when he said to the two spouses who had invoked mutual imprecation, "Allah knows that one of you is lying. Will either of you repent?" Or words to that effect, in all these cases. The second distinction is that our ruling on the basis of a witness's testimony and the oath of the person swearing is not judgment based on conjecture, as they claim. Rather, we are certain and affirm that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has made it obligatory upon us to judge on the basis of the claimant's oath together with the testimony of an upright witness, and on the basis of the defendant's oath when the claimant has no


[Page 169]

A ruling is made on the basis of evidence and the testimony of one upright witness, two upright witnesses, or upright witnesses, according to us, even if in reality they are liars or mistaken. A judgment based on any of this is true in the sight of Allah, Exalted is He, and, in our view, its unseen reality is beyond our knowledge. The proof of this is that if two people bring a dispute before a judge, and the claimant has no evidence, but the judge does not rule in favor of the defendant on the basis of an oath; or if two upright witnesses testify before him, but he does not rule on the basis of their testimony, then that judge is a sinner, disobedient to Allah, Exalted is He, whose testimony is discredited, and an oppressor—whether the defendant is wrong in his denial or in the right, or the witnesses are liars, mistaken, or truthful, so long as he does not know the reality of their case. We are certainly commanded by Allah, Exalted is He, to kill this innocent person against whom false testimony has been given, and to declare lawful this inviolable person and this unlawful property concerning which false testimony has been given. It is forbidden for the wrongdoer to take any of that. Our Lord has decreed that if we do not rule accordingly, we are sinners and disobedient to Him in religion, oppressors, threatened with the Fire for doing so. Allah, Exalted is He, has never commanded us to rule in religion on the basis of a report fabricated by a sinner or misunderstood by someone who is mistaken. Allah, Exalted is He, said: {O you who believe, do not follow the footsteps of Satan, and whoever}
"...follows the footsteps of Satan, for he commands indecency and wrongdoing. Were it not for Allah's grace and His mercy upon you, none of you would ever have been purified; but Allah purifies whom He wills, and Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing."} This is a distinction of the utmost clarity. A third distinction is that we say: Allah, Exalted is He, has made it obligatory upon us to say, throughout the entire Sharia, "The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said," and "Allah, Exalted is He, has commanded such-and-such," because He, Exalted is He, says: {O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it back...


[Page 170]

to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and fairer in outcome." … So it is incumbent upon us to say: "Allah, Exalted is He, and His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) have forbidden us to do such-and-such."
And He commanded us to do such-and-such, but the Exalted never commanded us to say, "This witness has spoken truthfully," or, "This party has sworn truthfully," or, "What we have ruled in favor of this person is certainly his right." Nor did the Exalted say, "This witness has spoken the truth." Rather, the Exalted said to us: "Judge according to the testimony of upright witnesses and the oath of the defendant when no proof has been brought against him." This is a distinction that is unmistakable. Thus, in none of this have we judged on the basis of conjecture at all, praise be to Allah. Rather, we have judged with decisive knowledge and firm certainty that everything we have ruled upon on the basis of a report transmitted by an upright narrator from another upright narrator, back to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), is truth from the Exalted Allah, revealed by our Lord, the Exalted, and attributed to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), as something he is reported to have said. And everything we have ruled upon on the basis of testimony from witnesses we deem upright is a truth established with certainty from the Exalted Allah, because He commanded us to judge by it. He did not command us to say, regarding what they testified to or what the person taking an oath swore, that it is from the Exalted Allah or that it is a truth established with certainty. If they say, "The Exalted said only, {O you who believe, avoid much suspicion; indeed, some suspicion is sin}, and He did not say that all suspicion is sin," we reply: Allah, the Exalted, has made clear the distinction between sin and righteousness: to speak about Him without knowledge is forbidden. This is undoubtedly a kind of suspicion that is sin. Ali said: The Mu'tazilites resorted to refusing to rule on the basis of a solitary report, because of the arguments we have mentioned. They thought that by doing so they had escaped the problem, but they have not escaped it. Rather, everything we have shown to be incumbent upon others is incumbent upon them as well. We therefore say to them—and Allah, the Exalted, is the source of success—tell us about the reports narrated by ...


[Page 171]

Are solitary reports all true if they are transmitted specifically by reliable narrators, or are they all false, or do they contain both truth and falsehood? If they say that they contain both truth and falsehood—and this is their position—we say to them: Is it permissible for a law that Allah, Exalted is He, revealed to His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) so that he might explain it to His servants to be corrupted, until it becomes mixed with a lie fabricated by a wicked person and attributed to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), or with an error made by someone who errs, so that the truth enjoined upon us becomes mixed with fabricated falsehood in such a way that the truth can never be distinguished from falsehood by anyone? And is it permissible that
Are all the Islamic laws preserved and binding upon us, or are they not preserved, and are not all of them binding upon us, but rather many of them ceased to apply after the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him)? Has Allah, Exalted is He, established proof against us regarding the laws He prescribed, in that they are clear to us and distinguishable from what He did not command us to do? Or has Allah, Exalted is He, not established proof against us in religion, because much of it is mixed with lies and can never be distinguished from them? If they allow the laws of religion that Allah, Exalted is He, revealed to His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) to be mixed with what is not in
the religion, and they said, "No proof from Allah, Exalted is He, has been established against us concerning what He commanded us to do," they would fall into the same argument for the corruption of the Sharia, the disappearance of Islam, and the invalidation of Allah's guarantee to preserve the Reminder as others have fallen into, letter for letter, exactly alike. They would be bound to have abandoned much of the sound religion, just as others are bound to be acting on what is not part of the religion, and that the Prophet's (peace and blessings be upon him) clarification has been nullified, and that Allah's proof through him has not been established against us, exactly alike. This is self-evident. If they resort to restricting themselves to reports transmitted by mass transmission (tawatur), they will still be unable to escape the fact that much of the religion has been nullified through its being mixed with fabricated lies and what is merely conjectured, and that it is possible that there be a ...


[Page 172]

Many of the laws of Islam have not been transmitted to us, since Allah Most High's guarantee of their preservation no longer applies to them. Moreover, no one is incapable of claiming that any report he wishes was transmitted by mass transmission (tawatur). Indeed, the proponents of chains of transmission have the stronger claim in this regard, given the large number of transmitters and the variation in their chains, which attest to the soundness of their claim that they were transmitted by mass transmission. And Allah Most High is the source of success. If someone resorts to saying that every report transmitted through the route of reliable individuals is a fabricated lie, none of which the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) ever said, we say—and Allah Most High is the source of success—that this is open defiance and a rejection of what we know with certainty to be otherwise. It is a denial of all the Companions, from the first to the last, of all the eminent Successors, and of every generation of scholars, one after another. For all those we have mentioned undoubtedly transmitted reports from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him); some cited them as proof against others, acted upon them, and issued religious rulings based on them. This is a rejection of a consensus known with certainty, and it is plainly false, beyond any possibility of doubt. For we know necessarily that it is utterly impossible that every one of those we have mentioned never spoke truthfully in a single report he transmitted, and that they all fabricated everything they transmitted. Moreover, this would invalidate the laws that no Muslim or non-Muslim doubts are not set out in detail in the Quran, such as prayer, zakat, pilgrimage, and other matters, whose details were taken only from the words of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). This entails the certainty that every Companion who transmitted from the Messenger of Allah...
Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for he is the one who fabricated and invented the lie about the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and no one on earth doubts that every


[Page 173]

A Companion who had heard from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) would relate it to his family and neighbors. This establishes that the laws were laid down for all of them, from the first to the last. The Rafidites and Kharijites have never gone so far as this, even though it is a claim without proof. Whatever is like that is certainly false, according to three positions, as you can see; there is no fourth.
