Either every report transmitted by a trustworthy narrator from another trustworthy narrator, reaching back to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), is entirely false, fabricated from beginning to end—and this is certainly false, as we have shown—or else it would mean that every Companion, Successor, and scholar, without exception, agreed to invent the laws and lie about them in the name of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). This is a renunciation of Islam. Or else some of these reports are true and some false, but no one can ever distinguish the true from the false. This is to deny Allah, Exalted is He, in His declaration that He would preserve the revealed Reminder, that He had perfected the religion for us, and that He accepts from us only the religion of Islam and nothing else. It also entails the corruption of the religion and its mingling with things Allah, Exalted is He, never commanded, and that no one in the world can know what Allah has commanded him in his religion and what He has never commanded him. It means that the reality of Islam and its laws have certainly been nullified. This is a renunciation of
Islam, or that they are all definitively true in their unseen reality with Allah, Exalted is He, and all necessarily yield certain knowledge, because Allah, Exalted is He, has stated that He preserves the Reminder He sent down; because He has forbidden judging in religion on the basis of conjecture and speaking about Him without knowledge; and because He has stated that He has made clear the right guidance as distinct from error. Right guidance is nothing other than what Allah, Exalted is He, sent down through the words of His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and in his actions, while error is nothing other than what Allah, Exalted is He, did not send down.


[Page 174]

on the tongue of His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him). This is our position, and praise be to Allah, Lord of the Worlds. Ali said: Since this position has now been established with certainty, and everything else has been invalidated, let us, with the help of Allah Almighty, discuss its classification. We say, and through Allah Almighty we seek support: We are certain, praise be to Allah, that no law which the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) commanded, recommended, or practiced could be lost without reaching someone among his community, either through mass transmission or through the transmission of a trustworthy narrator from another trustworthy narrator, until it reached him (peace and blessings be upon him). We are also absolutely certain that Allah Almighty would not single out for transmitting it someone whose transmission, even if he were upright, could not establish proof. We are also absolutely certain that there could not be a law in which its trustworthy narrator errs without a clear and evident explanation establishing that he has erred in it. And we are also absolutely certain, or that He would declare…
Allah, Mighty and Majestic, from one whose transmission has established proof for us, concerning the wording of a hadith containing a ruling that he attributes to someone whose transmission establishes proof, until he traces it back to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). Likewise, we state categorically and affirm that every report that has only ever been transmitted as a mursal report, or has only ever been narrated by an unknown narrator or one whose discrediting is established, is undoubtedly a false report, fabricated and not uttered by the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). For if it were possible that it was true, it would be a valid ruling of the Sharia, but one not binding upon us, since proof has not been established against us concerning it.
Ali said: This ruling that we have set out applies only to what has been transmitted by those whose uprightness is agreed upon, such as the Companions and the trustworthy Successors, then Shu'ba, Sufyan, Sufyan, Malik, and the other imams of their time and those who came after them, down to us and until the Day of Resurrection; and to everyone whose criticism is established, such as al-Hasan ibn Umara, Jabir al-Ju'fi, and all the other narrators whose criticism is established. As for those concerning whom there is disagreement, with some declaring them upright and others criticizing them, if it is established for us that


[Page 175]

If we are certain of his uprightness, we are certain that his report is sound; and if his discredit is established for us, we are certain that his report is invalid. If neither of these is established for us, we suspend judgment on the matter, while being absolutely certain that someone else must have established one of the two concerning him. Our error, if we err, and our ignorance, if we are ignorant, cannot be proof that Allah's religion must be lost. Rather, the truth is established and known to one group, even if another is ignorant of it; and likewise with falsehood, just as some people are ignorant of what we know. Grace is in Allah's hand; He grants it to whomever He wills. An error in a trustworthy narrator's report can be established only in one of three ways: either the narrator verifies it and admits that he made an error in it; or a reliable person testifies that he heard the report alongside its narrator and that so-and-so made a mistake in it; or direct observation makes it certain that he made an error. Ali said: Likewise, we are certain and decisive regarding any two sound reports that conflict, any two verses that conflict, any verse and sound report that conflict, and any two conflicting texts for which no explicit text has established that one abrogates the other: the ruling that adds to the ruling established by the original precedent is the abrogating one, while the ruling that accords with the original precedent is certainly and unquestionably the abrogated one. This follows from the proofs we have already presented: that the religion is preserved. For if it were possible for an abrogating text to be concealed among abrogated ones, or for there to be a general text that no sound text specifies, while its intended meaning is particular, then the religion would not be preserved, the proof in the Sharia would not be binding on anyone, and we would be charged with acting on false conjecture, which is forbidden, indeed with acting on something Allah, Exalted is He, never commanded. This is certainly false. Ali said: If one day it is found that we have said otherwise, then we repent to Allah, Exalted is He, for it. It is a slip for which we ask Allah, Mighty and Majestic, to forgive us, and we hope that no such thing will be found in our words.


[Page 176]

Ibn Hazm said: "Another proof is that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) sent a messenger to every king among the kings of the earth who were neighbors to the lands of the Arabs. Some of those who disagree with us have objected to this, saying that delegations and merchants came at the Prophet's command, so he did not rely on the messenger alone. Abu Muhammad said: This is sophistry and deception, which can only persuade the weak. We have no doubt that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did not limit the aforementioned messengers to informing people of his appearance and miracles, which were reported by delegations and travelers. Rather, he instructed them to teach those who embraced Islam the laws of Islam and the rulings on worship and other matters, none of which had been conveyed by delegations and travelers. The sending of these messengers is well known and reliably transmitted by numerous narrators. Thus, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) required every king and his subjects to accept what the messenger sent to them informed them of regarding the laws of their religion. Ali said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) likewise sent Muadh to al-Jund and parts of Yemen, and Abu Musa to another region, namely Zabid and elsewhere;
and Abu Bakr to lead the people in the pilgrimage, and Abu Ubayda to Najran, and Ali to Yemen as a judge. Each of these went to a region to teach its people the laws of Islam. He likewise sent a commander to every region that embraced Islam, whether far from him or near, such as the farthest parts of Yemen, Bahrain, and all the other regions, districts, and tribes that embraced Islam. To each group he sent a man to teach them their religion and the Quran, give them legal rulings on matters of their religion, judge disputes among them, and convey to them what Allah required of them.


[Page 177]

Exalted is He, and His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), and they are commanded to accept what they report to them from their Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him).
The sending of these people mentioned above is well known through mass transmission, from both disbelievers and believers. None of the scholars or Muslims doubts it, nor that they were sent only for the reason we have stated. It is impossible and false to suppose that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) sent them to people who would not be bound by the proof established through their conveying the message, who would not be obliged to accept what they taught them of the Quran and the rulings of religion, what they gave them as legal opinions, or to submit to what they reported to them in all these matters from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). For if that were so, his sending them would have been pointless, and he would have been saying to the Muslims: "I have sent you people whom you are not obliged to accept when they convey to you what I have said, and whom you are required to disregard when they transmit something from me or tell you what I have said." Whoever holds this view has departed from Islam. The same applies to someone who grows up in a village or city where there is only one Quran reciter, one hadith scholar, or one mufti. We say to those who disagree with us: What do you say? Is someone obliged, when he studies the Quran with that reciter, to believe what he teaches him and affirm that it is the word of Allah, Exalted is He, and remain firm in that belief? Or must he doubt and not affirm that it is the word of Allah, Mighty and Majestic? If they say that he is obliged to affirm that it is the word of Allah, Exalted is He, we say: You are right. So what difference is there between their transmission of the Quran and their transmission of the other Sunnahs, when both are from Allah, Exalted is He, and acceptance of both is obligatory? But if they say that he must doubt it until he meets a large number of people, they have committed a grave offense against religion. We then ask them about someone who meets two, three, or four people in this way: they must set a limit.


[Page 178]

They stop at the number mentioned there, so their claim is absurd and false, and an assertion without proof; or they refer to something nonexistent in a matter without which, according to their position, accepting the Quran and religion would not be valid. This would invalidate the religion and the Quran altogether and prohibit relying on them. We seek refuge in Allah from that. The same applies to the obligation to obey anyone who received a Quran or Sunnah from those messengers and conveyed it to someone else. For those lands are vast, and it is impossible for each of those messengers to meet every man and woman among them. Rather, he conveys it, and the one to whom he conveys it conveys it in turn, and so on indefinitely, lest some ignorant person say, "This was specific to those messengers."

No two Muslims disagree that if a trustworthy Muslim entered the land of unbelief, called a people to Islam, recited the Quran to them, and taught them the religious laws, they would be obliged to accept it, and proof would thereby have been established against them. The same would apply if the caliph or ruler sent a messenger to one of the kings of unbelief or to one of the nations of unbelief,

calling them to Islam and teaching them the Quran and the laws of religion. There is no difference. No Muslim has ever said that the people of Yemen were entitled to say to Muadh, or to anyone whom the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) sent to any region as a teacher, jurist, and Quran reciter: "Yes, you are the Messenger of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), and the bond of faith is binding

upon us; but we will not accept from you or take from you what you have given us as legal rulings and taught us of the rules of prayer, the cases concerning zakat, and the rest of the religion, from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), nor what you have recited to us of the Quran on his authority, because lying is possible on your part."


[Page 179]

And it is inconceivable that you should suppose that all of this would reach us only through complete and mass-transmitted reports. Indeed, if they had said that, they would not have been Muslims. Likewise, no two people disagree that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) sent his messengers to distant lands only to convey to their people the Quran, the Sunnah, and the laws of religion, and that he did not send them to establish for them a religion that he himself had not brought from Allah, Exalted is He. All of this makes it clear that everything reliably transmitted from one trustworthy person to another, reaching back to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), whether Quran or Sunnah, must be accepted, acknowledged, affirmed, believed in, and followed as religion.
Second: Their claim that an error may occur on the part of someone transmitting the Sunnah. We say: By the same token, an error may occur on the part of someone transmitting the Quran. A reciting teacher may make a mistake, but that does not prevent the Quran from conveying knowledge.
It is likewise established that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) made certain judgments through personal reasoning, such as taking ransom from the captives of Badr, and that he was not left to that judgment; rather, the revelation came down: "Had it not been for a prior decree from Allah, you would have been afflicted by a great punishment for what you took ..." [end of verse]. Likewise, Allah's saying, "And you fear the people," and His saying, "He frowned and turned away." He (peace and blessings be upon him) was not left to such matters; rather, clarification came down to guide him. It is also established in the Sunnah that he (peace and blessings be upon him) forgot, but he was reminded, even though he was a model to follow. He said, "I am only a human being; I forget just as you forget. So when I forget, remind me."
None of this prevented his words (peace and blessings be upon him) from being authoritative in matters of belief and practice.


[Page 180]

Third: They say that solitary reports (akhbar al-ahad) establish only practice, but there are hadiths from which practice can be derived only by establishing knowledge. An example is: "Allah has cursed the woman who tattoos and the woman who has herself tattooed, the woman who joins hair extensions and the woman who has her teeth filed for beauty." The cursing of one who alters the landmarks of the land
"Whoever plays with dice has disobeyed Allah." "He cursed men who imitate women, and women who imitate men."
And many other hadiths containing the words "will not enter Paradise," such as "A talebearer will not enter Paradise." Likewise, some recommended acts, as in: "I guarantee a house in the middle of Paradise."
And likewise, "There is no marriage without a guardian,"
"There is no prayer without the Mother of the Quran,"
and many other examples. The obligation, prohibition, or recommendation is not derived from the words "Do" or "Do not," or "You should do," but rather from reports conveying knowledge that one will enter Paradise or Hell, or not enter them, that the person who does it is cursed, or that the act is invalid. All of these are matters of knowledge that must first be established; only then can a practical ruling be derived from them, namely, that the act is prohibited, obligatory, or recommended. This is so only after believing in the reward and punishment stated in the hadith. If it does not establish a belief, then consequently it does not establish a practical ruling.
Fourth: Those Who Held That a Solitary Report Accompanied by Corroborating Indicators Yields Knowledge
"Al-Sijzi's Epistle to the People of Zabid in Refutation of Those Who Denied the Letter and Sound" (p. 284):


[Page 181]

Ibn Taymiyya, Shaykh al-Islam, transmitted this from the followers of the four imams and others, saying:
"As for the second category of reports, these are reports narrated only by a single upright narrator or the like, whose wording and meaning have not been transmitted by mass transmission, but which the community has received with acceptance, either by acting upon them or affirming them … These yield certain knowledge according to the vast majority of the community of Muhammad (peace and blessings be upon him), among both the early and later generations. There was no disagreement about this among the early generations. As for the later generations, this was the position of the leading jurists among the followers of the four imams. The issue is recorded in the books of the Hanafis, Malikis, Shafi'is, and Hanbalis, such as al-Sarakhsi and Abu Bakr al-Razi among the Hanafis; Shaykh Abu Hamid, Abu al-Tayyib, and Shaykh Abu Ishaq among the Shafi'is; Ibn Khuwaz Mindad and others among the Malikis; and Abu Ya'la, Ibn Abi Musa, Abu al-Khattab, and others among the Hanbalis. It was also the position of Abu Ishaq al-Isfarayini, Ibn Furak, and Abu Ishaq al-Nazzam among the theologians. Only a group, such as Ibn al-Baqillani and those who followed him, including Abu al-Ma'ali, al-Ghazali, and Ibn Aqil, disputed this. See: Mukhtasar al-Sawa'iq al-Mursala, 2/372–373."
In Mukhtasar al-Sawa'iq al-Mursala ala al-Jahmiyya wa al-Mu'attila (p. 553), it says:
"This is what those who deny that reports from the Messenger of Allah yield knowledge have relied upon. By doing so, they violated the consensus of the Companions, which is known with certainty through the consensus of the Successors and the consensus of the imams of Islam. They thereby agreed with the Mu'tazila, Jahmiyya, Rafida, and Kharijites, who violated this sanctity. Some of the scholars of legal theory and jurists followed them; otherwise, no early imam is known to have held this position. Rather, the imams explicitly stated the opposite of what they say.


[Page 182]

Among those who explicitly stated that a solitary report (khabar al-wahid) yields knowledge are Malik, al-Shafi'i, the companions of Abu Hanifa, Dawud ibn Ali and his companions, such as Abu Muhammad ibn Hazm; this was also explicitly stated by al-Husayn ibn Ali al-Karabisi and al-Harith ibn Asad al-Muhasibi.
Ibn Khuwaz Mindad said in his book on the principles of jurisprudence, after mentioning the solitary report narrated by only one or two people: "This kind of report, too, can yield necessary knowledge." Malik explicitly stated this. Ahmad said regarding the hadith about the vision: "We know that it is true, and we are certain of our knowledge of it." Al-Marwadhi likewise narrated: "I said to Abu Abd Allah, 'There are two people here who say that a report entails action but does not entail knowledge.' He disapproved of this and said, 'I do not know what this is.'" The Qadi said: "The apparent meaning of this is that he equates knowledge with action." The Qadi also said at the beginning of the section on reports: "A solitary report entails knowledge if its chain of transmission is sound, there is no disagreement in its transmission, and the community has received it with acceptance. Our companions state this categorically, saying that it entails knowledge even if the community has not received it with acceptance." He said: "The school's position is as I have described, and no other."
He thus explicitly stated that this is the school's position. In the narration of Ahmad ibn al-Husayn al-Tirmidhi, he stated that one must affirm a report from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). Another narration from him indicates that a solitary report does not yield knowledge, for in the narration of al-Marwadhi he said: "When a hadith comes from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) with a sound chain of transmission and contains a ruling or an obligation, I act upon it and worship Allah in accordance with it, but I do not testify that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said it." The discussion of the meaning of this narration will come later, if Allah Most High wills.


[Page 183]

Ibn Abi Yunus said at the beginning of al-Irshad: "A solitary report (khabar al-wahid) entails both knowledge and action." Qadi Abu Ya'la stated that this position is held by the majority.
Shaykh Abu Ishaq al-Shirazi said in his works on legal theory, such as al-Tabsira, Sharh al-Luma, and others. These are his words in the commentary: "A solitary report, when the community receives it with acceptance, entails knowledge and action, whether everyone acts upon it or only some do." He did not report any disagreement on this among the companions of al-Shafi'i. Qadi Abd al-Wahhab, among the Malikis, attributed this position to a group of jurists. The Hanafis explicitly state in their books that a widely transmitted report (khabar al-mustafid) entails knowledge. They cite as an example the Prophet's statement (peace and blessings be upon him): "There is no bequest for an heir." They say that although it was transmitted only through solitary chains, it is accepted. They also cite the hadith of Ibn Mas'ud concerning two parties to a sale who disagree: the seller's word is accepted, or they rescind the sale. They further cite the hadith of Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf concerning the collection of jizya from the Magians, and likewise the hadith of al-Mughira ibn Shu'ba and Muhammad ibn Maslama concerning giving a grandmother one-sixth. The early and later generations agreed to apply the rulings of these reports when they heard them. This demonstrates that their transmission is sound and that they are free of defect. Even if some people opposed them, in our view these are anomalous opinions, and their proponents are not counted in determining consensus."
He said: "We have said that reports following this course entail knowledge of the truthfulness of the one who reported them because, when we find that the early generations agreed to accept a report of this description without verifying it or opposing it on the basis of the foundational principles, or when a similar report is related, while we know their methods of accepting reports, examining them, and comparing them with the foundational principles, this indicates to us"


[Page 184]

That is among their affairs, since they did not adopt his ruling except because its soundness and correctness had been established for them; this therefore made it necessary for us to know that it was sound. These are the words of Abu Bakr al-Razi in his book Principles of Jurisprudence.
It is known to everyone of sound senses and sound reason that the hadiths concerning the vision, the call, the descent, the speaking, and other attributes have been transmitted through numerous channels, and that the community received them with acceptance to a degree far greater than the widespread transmission of the hadith about the two parties to a sale having the option to rescind, the hadith "There is no bequest for an heir," and the hadith concerning the grandmother's prescribed share. Indeed, there is no comparison between the widespread transmission of the hadiths concerning the attributes and that of these hadiths. Can any rational person reasonably say that the latter establish knowledge while the former do not, unless he is being obstinate?
Al-Shafi'i explicitly stated in his books that a solitary report yields knowledge. He stated this plainly in his book The Disagreement of Malik, and in the Egyptian Epistle he argued that it does not establish the kind of knowledge established by a text of the Book or a mass-transmitted report. We shall cite his wording in both books.
In the Epistle he said: "As for a practice established by a report from a particular source, where reports may differ and the report may admit of interpretation, and the report has come by way of a single transmitter, the proof concerning it, in my view, is that it must bind the scholars, so that they may not reject what has been explicitly stated in it, just as they are bound to accept the testimony of upright witnesses. This is not, however, certainty of knowledge, as is the case with a text of the Book and a report transmitted by the public from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). If anyone were to doubt this, we would not tell him to repent; rather, we would say: If you are a scholar, you have no right to doubt, just as you have no choice but to rule on the basis of the testimony of upright witnesses, even if"


[Page 185]

It is possible that they made a mistake, but you judge by that according to the apparent truthfulness of their testimony; and Allah is the Guardian of what is hidden from you in this matter. End quote.
This is his explicit statement concerning a report open to interpretation, with nothing to support it other than its being a solitary report. There is no dispute about this, for it is open to error in both its chain and its wording. Our discussion concerns reports that were received with acceptance and became well known among the community, and that one person stated in the presence of a group, none of whom objected; rather, the hearer accepted it and affirmed by it an attribute of the Lord, Exalted is He, and denounced those who denied it, just as all the imams of Islam denounced those who denied the Lord's attributes reported in the texts and branded them innovators.
As for what he mentioned in his later book, he said: I said to him—meaning the person debating him: "What do you think if someone says to you, 'I suspect everything you have narrated from those from whom you narrated it, for I fear that every hadith transmitter among them may have erred in what he heard from the person who narrated to him, when something contrary to it is narrated from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him)'?" It is not permissible to suspect the reports of trustworthy people. I said: "Did any of them narrate it except as one person from another?" He said: "No." I said: "And was it narrated from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) as one person from another?" He said: "Yes." I said: "We know that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said it only through the truthfulness of the transmitter in our view, and we know that the person whose statement we have cited said it on the basis of a single report." He said: "Yes." I said: "And our knowledge that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said it is like our knowledge that the person we have named said it." He said: "Yes." I said: "So, when the two kinds of knowledge derived from the report of a truthful person are equal, which report should we give precedence to: the report from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), and act upon it, or the report from someone below him?" He said: "Rather, the report from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), if it is established." I said: "Their establishment..."


[Page 186]

One, he said: The report from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) has greater claim on us that we should turn to it. If you introduce the possibility of error among those who report from him, then that possibility will arise for you in every report transmitted that contradicts the report that came from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). (If you say: "We establish it on the basis of the report of truthful people,") then what is established from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) has greater claim, in our view, to be followed.
As you can see, he explicitly stated that if one person reports from another, from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), and it is known that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said it, then the narrator is to be believed in our view and not contradicted. This is his explicit statement in al-Risala. There, he only denied that the knowledge gained from it is equal to the knowledge gained from the text of the Book and from a mass-transmitted report. This is correct, for knowledge varies in strength and weakness. Al-Qadi said, in the narration of Hanbal from Ahmad concerning the hadiths about the vision: "We believe in them and know that they are true." He said: "Thus he affirmed certain knowledge of them." A group of our companions adopted the apparent meaning of these words and said: "A solitary report, if it concerns the Sharia, yields knowledge." He said: "In my view, this is to be understood in a sound sense of Ahmad's words: that it yields knowledge by way of inference, not by way of necessity."
Inference yields knowledge in four ways:
The first is that the community receives it with acceptance; this indicates that it is true, because the community does not agree upon error. The community's acceptance indicates that proof of its soundness has been established for them, since it is customary that the community does not collectively accept a solitary report for which proof has not been established; rather, some people accept it and others reject it. The second: the report of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) ...


[Page 187]

Allah bless him and grant him peace, and he is one person, so his truthfulness is established with certainty, because the proof has established his infallibility. Third: that one person reports something and claims to have heard it from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), and he does not deny it. What indicates that it is true is that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) does not tacitly approve of a lie.
Fourth: that one person reports something and claims that a large number of people heard it from him, and none of them denies it. This indicates that it is true, because if it were a lie, they would not all have agreed to remain silent rather than declare him a liar. The knowledge arising from all of this is acquired, because it results from reflection and inference.
Shaykh al-Islam Ibn Taymiyya said: I say, his restriction of the solitary reports that yield knowledge is not comprehensive, because among the things that yield knowledge are reports that the Messenger himself also received with acceptance, such as his reports concerning Tamim al-Dari and what he reported, affirming him in it. Another example is two people reporting an incident when one knows that they did not collude over it, and it is ordinarily unlikely that they would agree to lie about it or make a mistake, and so on.
I say: The solitary reports that yield knowledge are not confined to these cases. Rather, a person receiving a report may attain knowledge of which he has no doubt through many such reports. For example, if someone who has never been found to lie tells him that he witnessed it, he then becomes certain of it with certainty that is necessary or nearly so. Likewise, if reporting it would bring harm upon the person, yet he reports it out of religious devotion and fear of Allah, Exalted is He; for example, if he comes forward voluntarily and reports against himself a crime for which he has incurred a prescribed punishment, seeking to be purified of it through that punishment; or if he admits against himself a right claimed against him when there is no evidence and no oath being demanded of him, and he faces no fear of harm if he denies it; or if he informs the mufti of something he did so that he may obtain for himself


[Page 188]

The way out of it, or someone telling a doctor about a pain he is experiencing and seeking its relief, involves reports many times more numerous than this, whose truthfulness the hearer is certain of. How, then, can anyone feel at ease and speak freely, claiming that the report of al-Siddiq, Umar ibn al-Khattab, Uthman ibn Affan, Ali ibn Abi Talib, and Abd Allah ibn Masud (may Allah be pleased with them), when they said, "We heard the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) say such-and-such," does not yield knowledge at all? Glory be to You! This is a tremendous slander.
We testify by Allah that when these men reported something from the Messenger of Allah, their truthfulness was taken as certain. We testify by Allah that when they reported something from other Companions and Successors, their truthfulness was likewise taken as certain. Indeed, we testify by Allah that Salim, Nafi, Said ibn al-Musayyib, and those like them held this same standing; and Malik, al-Awzai, al-Layth, and those like them did too. Neither we nor anyone who knows these men would entertain any doubt about what Malik says: "I heard Nafi say, 'I heard Ibn Umar say, "I heard the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) say..."'" We are certain that our opponents are mistaken in this matter.
A group of scholars of legal theory held that when consensus is reached on acting upon a solitary report, it becomes like a mass-transmitted report. Ibn Burhan related this view, but preferred the opinion that it does not become like a mass-transmitted report. Another group held that if one person makes a claim before a group, asserting its truth in their presence, and they remain silent, his report becomes like a mass-transmitted report. A group of Ahmad's followers and others held that anyone who denies what is established by the report of a trustworthy, upright narrator is an unbeliever. This was the position of Ishaq ibn Rahawayh. The one who denies that a solitary report yields knowledge has fallen into error because of a false analogy: he likened the person reporting from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) to ...


[Page 189]

Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) with a law applying generally to the community, or with one of the attributes of the Lord, Exalted is He, to the testimony of a witness concerning a particular case. How great the difference between the two! For if one reporting from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) were assumed to have lied deliberately or by mistake, and nothing appeared to indicate his falsehood, that would necessarily entail leading people astray. The discussion concerns a report that the community received with acceptance and acted upon, and by which it affirmed the attributes and acts of the Lord. For reports that must be accepted according to the Sharia cannot be false in reality, especially when the entire community has accepted them. The same must be said of every proof that must be followed according to the Sharia: it can only be true, and what it indicates must therefore be established in reality.
This applies to what is reported concerning the law of the Lord, Exalted is He, and His names and attributes, unlike a particular testimony concerning a particular person against whom testimony is given; the implication of such testimony may not be established in reality. He distorted the issue by claiming that a report through which Allah has made the community accountable and made Himself known to them, on the tongue of His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), in affirming His names and attributes, could be false and untrue in reality. For it is one of Allah's proofs against His servants, and Allah's proofs cannot be false or untrue; rather, they can only be true in reality. It is not permissible for the proofs of truth and falsehood to be equal, nor is it permissible for a lie about Allah, His law, and His religion to be indistinguishable from the revelation He sent down to His Messenger and by which He made His creation accountable, such that one cannot be distinguished from the other. For the difference between truth and falsehood, truthfulness and lying, and the revelation of Satan and the revelation of the angel from Allah is too clear for either to be confused with the other. Indeed, Allah has placed a light upon the truth.


[Page 190]

Like the light of the sun, it appears to those whose insight is illuminated; and he clothed falsehood in the darkness of night. It is not surprising that falsehood should be confused with daylight by one blind in sight, just as truth and falsehood are confused by one blind in insight.
Muadh ibn Jabal said in his judgment: "Accept the truth from whoever says it, for the truth has a light." But when hearts grew dark and insights were blinded by turning away from what the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) brought, and the darkness increased as they contented themselves with the opinions of men, truth became confused with falsehood for them. They deemed it possible that his sound hadiths, transmitted by the most upright and truthful members of the community, could be lies, while they deemed it possible that the false, fabricated, and contradictory hadiths that accorded with their desires could be true, and so they took shelter behind them.
The crux of the matter is this: can the report of upright, trustworthy transmitters, which Allah Most High has made it obligatory for Muslims to act upon, be false or mistaken in reality, with Allah providing no indication of that? Whoever says that it yields certain knowledge says that this is not possible; rather, whenever the conditions that make acting upon it obligatory are met, what it reports must be established in reality. On this basis, they disputed whether one who rejects it is an unbeliever, since it is among the proofs that yield certain knowledge, just as they discussed whether one who denies consensus is an unbeliever. If someone rejects a sound report because he believes the transmitter erred or lied, or because he believes that the infallible would not say such a thing, or because he believes it was abrogated, or for a similar reason, and rejects it through independent reasoning and out of zeal to uphold the truth, he does not thereby become an unbeliever or a sinner. More than one of the Companions rejected some sound solitary reports, just as Umar rejected the hadith of Fatima bint Qays concerning the annulment of


[Page 191]

the maintenance of a woman divorced three times. Likewise, Aisha (may Allah be pleased with her) rejected Ibn Umar's hadith about the deceased being punished because of his family's weeping for him, and other such reports.

Section

As for al-Athram's report from Ahmad that he would not testify on the basis of a report from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), yet act upon it, this is a report transmitted by al-Athram alone. It is not found in Ahmad's Questions or in the Book of the Sunnah. Rather, al-Qadi related that he had found it in the Book of the Meanings of Hadith. Al-Athram did not say that he had heard this from Ahmad; perhaps it had reached him through someone who had mistakenly attributed those words to him. None of Ahmad's companions transmitted this from him. Rather, what is authentically transmitted from him is that he affirmed that the ten were in Paradise, although the report concerning this is a solitary report. Perhaps his hesitation to testify was out of scrupulousness. He would affirm that certain things were forbidden, yet refrain from applying the word "forbidden" to them; and he would affirm that certain things were forbidden, yet refrain, out of scrupulousness, from applying the word "obligatory" to them. Instead, he would say, "I disapprove of such-and-such," or, "I recommend such-and-such." This is common in his answers.

In one place he said: "We do not testify that any of the people of the qibla is in the Fire on account of a sin he committed or a major sin he perpetrated, unless there is a hadith about it; then we affirm it and know that it is as reported, but we do not state the testimony explicitly. Nor do we testify that anyone is in Paradise on account of a righteous deed he performed or a good deed he did, unless there is a hadith about it; then we accept it as reported, according to what has been transmitted, but we do not state it explicitly." Al-Qadi said: "By 'we do not state the testimony explicitly,' I understand him to mean that we do not make a definitive judgment about it."


[Page 192]

Shaykh al-Islam said: The wording "we affirm" means that we attest to its meaning, not that we testify about a specific individual. Otherwise, he said, "We know that it is as it has come down," and this entails that it conveys knowledge.
Moreover, it is part of his foundational position that he testifies that the ten are in Paradise, on the basis of the report that has been transmitted; and that report is a solitary report. He said, "I testify" and "I know" as one and the same thing. This is evidence that he testifies on the basis of what a solitary report entails, although Ibn al-Madini and others disagreed with him.
Ibn al-Madini said: They say, "The reports of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) convey knowledge." Allah, Exalted is He, says: {He does not speak from his own desire; it is only revelation sent down to him} [al-Najm: 3].
And He, Exalted is He, said, commanding His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) to say: {I follow only what is revealed to me} [al-An'am: 50]. And He, Exalted is He, said: {Indeed, We have sent down the Reminder, and indeed, We will preserve it} [al-Hijr: 9]. And He, Exalted is He, said: {And We sent down the Reminder to you so that you may make clear to people what was sent down to them} [al-Nahl: 44]. They said: It is thus known that all of the Messenger of Allah's (peace and blessings be upon him) speech concerning religion is revelation from Allah, and every revelation from Allah is a Reminder that Allah sent down. Allah, Exalted is He, said: {And Allah sent down to you the Book and the Wisdom} [al-Nisa: 113]. The Book is the Quran, and the Wisdom is the Sunnah. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said: "I have been given the Book and something like it along with it." He thus informed us that he was given the Sunnah just as he was given the Book. Allah, Exalted is He, has guaranteed the preservation of what He revealed to him and sent down upon him, so that through it He may establish His proof against His servants until the end of time. They said: If it were possible for these reports to be false, they would not be from Allah, nor would they be among what He sent down.


[Page 193]

Allah upon His Messenger and granted him its explanation and clarification of it. How, then, could His proof against His creation be established by something that might in reality be false? For the Sunnah serves as an explanation of the Book and a clarification of what is intended; it is what makes known to us what Allah intends by His Book. If it were possible for it to be false and mistaken, Allah's proof against His servants would be invalidated. Anyone confronted with a Sunnah that clarifies and explains the Quran could say, "This is a solitary report that does not yield knowledge, so no proof can be established against me by something that does not yield knowledge." This is the inevitable consequence of this corrupt doctrine, and those who carry it through most consistently are the furthest from knowledge and faith.
What is truly astonishing is that they refuse to rely on the reports of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), claiming that they do not yield knowledge, yet they rely on mental fancies and false doubts that they received from the people of philosophy, Jahmism, and Mu'tazilism, and claim that these are rational proofs.
Ibn al-Qayyim quoted Ibn Hazm: "The eleventh proof: Those who deny that the reports of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) yield knowledge bear decisive, categorical testimony concerning their imams and their doctrines and statements, affirming that they said such things. Were someone to say that these statements had not been authentically transmitted from them, they would reject that most vehemently and marvel at the ignorance of whoever said it. Yet it is well known that those doctrines were transmitted from them only by one, two, or three people, or the like; they were not transmitted from them by a number sufficient to establish mass transmission. This is known with certainty.
How, then, do they attain necessary knowledge, or knowledge approaching the necessary, that their imams and those whose religion they followed issued such-and-such a legal opinion and held such-and-such a view, while they do not attain it from what Abu Bakr al-Siddiq, Umar ibn al-Khattab, and all the Companions reported from the Messenger of Allah"


[Page 194]

May Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor by what the Successors narrated from them, which became widespread and well known in the community, with its chains of transmission multiplying and varying, while his concern for it was far greater than the concern of those people for the sayings of the authorities they follow? This is truly astonishing beyond measure."

From what has preceded, it is clear that solitary reports (ahad) yield knowledge.

(Evidence that one is not deemed an unbeliever on account of interpretation or ignorance, provided one has general faith and does not falsely attribute anything to Allah, whether by affirming or denying it)

By this heading I mean that whoever, following evidence that led him to a mistaken action or belief, does so while seeking nothing but Allah's ruling on the matter, and does not lie about Allah or deny anything of Him merely on the basis of conjecture without proof, is excused. Likewise, whoever is ignorant of something in the Sharia that he is not required to know, while submitting and holding general faith, and without falsely attributing anything to Allah by affirming or denying it, is also excused. A number of legal proofs indicate that a Muslim is excused for ignorance in matters of creed. They are:

First: The legal texts that show that one who errs is excused, such as the words of Allah, Exalted is He: {Our Lord, do not take us to task if we forget or make a mistake} (al-Baqara 2:286).

Allah, Exalted is He, said, "I have done so," as in Sahih Muslim (126).

And His words, Exalted is He: {There is no blame upon you for what you do by mistake, but only for what your hearts intend. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful} (al-Ahzab 33:5).


[Page 195]

And his saying (peace and blessings be upon him): "Allah has pardoned my community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and what they are compelled to do." Narrated by Ibn Majah (2043); al-Albani graded it hasan.
These texts indicate that anyone who contravenes what he was charged with, out of forgetfulness or ignorance, is pardoned. The term "one who errs" includes the ignorant, since one who errs is anyone who departs from the truth unintentionally, provided that he has not been negligent in seeking knowledge or fabricated lies about Allah without proof, as will come, if Allah wills. Second: the texts indicating that Allah's proof against His servants is established only after they have received knowledge:
Such as His saying, Exalted is He: {We would never punish until We had sent a messenger} (al-Isra 17:15).
And His saying: {Messengers bearing good news and warning, so that people would have no argument against Allah after the messengers. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise} (al-Nisa 4:165).
And His saying: {Allah would not lead a people astray after He had guided them until He had made clear to them what they should avoid} (al-Tawba 9:115), along with other verses indicating that proof is established only after knowledge and clarification. So long as a person has not turned away from the truth, neglected seeking knowledge and exerting his utmost effort, and then interpreted the matter and erred, there is no sin upon him.
The story of Dhat Anwat.
Abu Waqid al-Laythi said: "We went out with the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) toward Hunayn. We passed by a lote tree and said, 'O Prophet of Allah, make this a Dhat Anwat for us, just as the disbelievers have a Dhat Anwat.' The disbelievers used to hang their weapons on a lote tree and gather around it."


[Page 196]

The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Allah is the Greatest! This is just like what the Children of Israel said to Moses: {Make for us a god, just as they have gods.} You will follow the ways of those who came before you." Narrated by al-Tirmidhi (2180), who graded it authentic; also narrated by Imam Ahmad (21900), and this is his wording. Shaykh al-Albani graded it authentic.
They asked the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) to do something that constitutes major shirk: to prescribe for them attachment to a tree, as the polytheists used to do. For this reason, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) likened what they said to what the Children of Israel said to Moses.
Muhammad Rashid Rida said: "Those who said to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) what has been mentioned had only recently left polytheism, and so they thought that what the Prophet might prescribe for them in this regard would be lawful and would not contradict Islam."
The saying of the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him): "If a judge exercises independent reasoning and reaches the correct conclusion, he receives two rewards; if he errs, he receives one reward."
Ibn Hazm says:
If the ruling of the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) reached him and was established as authentic in his view, but he opposed it, exercising independent reasoning in a matter whose correct course had not been made clear to him, then he is mistaken, excused, and rewarded once, as he (peace and blessings be upon him) said: "If a judge exercises independent reasoning and reaches the correct conclusion, he receives two rewards; if he errs, he receives one reward." Everyone who holds a belief, makes a statement, or performs an act is a judge with respect to that matter. If he opposes it through his actions, knowingly resisting the truth and believing contrary to what he has done, then he is a sinful believer. If he opposes it, knowingly resisting the truth, in his words or in his heart, then he is a disbeliever and a polytheist, whether ...


[Page 197]

That applies to matters of belief and to legal opinions concerning the texts we have cited. This is the view of Ishaq ibn Rahawayh and others, and it is what we hold. And Allah, Exalted is He, is the source of success." This is the meaning of "to judge" in the language: anyone who affirms something of a thing, or denies it of that thing, has judged concerning it. Thus, whoever says that ablution is obligatory has affirmed that ablution is obligatory; and whoever says that Allah will be seen in the Hereafter has judged on this matter with that ruling.
(Very, very important note)
Some have cited the preceding proofs to argue that proof is not established against anyone until knowledge reaches him, even if he says what he says, believes what he believes, or is negligent in seeking knowledge, so long as the proof has not reached him. I consider this view mistaken for several reasons. First, a person who is negligent in seeking knowledge is not called one who has erred in the sense required for the preceding verses and hadiths to apply to him. If someone were to blindfold himself and drive his car at top speed, killing whoever he killed, would he be called one who had made a mistake? Likewise, such a person is turning away from seeking the truth; he is not even called a mujtahid, so as to fall under the hadith, "When the judge exercises independent judgment..." Moreover, after his general belief in Allah and His Messenger, an ignorant person can only be one of two types. The first is a man who is asked about a religious matter and says, "I do not know. I believe in everything the Messenger brought, may Allah bless him and grant him peace." Such a person is excused, because he has neither fabricated a lie against Allah, nor committed shirk, nor attributed to Allah something He has not affirmed of Himself, nor denied of Him something He has affirmed of Himself. Accordingly, you will find that most of the hadiths cited in this matter are understood in this way. The people of Dhat Anwat did not commit shirk; rather, they asked for a religious prescription resembling one of the prescriptions in our Sharia, from among its forms of


[Page 198]

The Messenger is the Lawgiver himself, and forms of shirk are not a matter of reason that can be identified by reason in many cases. Prostration was not a form of shirk in the law of those before us, but it became so. The upshot is that they sought legislation of a ruling; they did not fabricate a lie against Allah by adopting this tree, nor did they attribute that legislation to Allah, so no shirk occurred on their part at all.
Likewise, the hadith about the man who reduced himself to ashes: he did not say that Allah was unable to resurrect him. Rather, he said, "If He should decree," and in another narration, "Perhaps I will elude Allah." In that narration is Hammad ibn Salama, who became confused in his later years. Both narrations indicate that the man was ignorant of this divine attribute; neither contains any indication that he affirmed its opposite or fabricated a lie about it. Likewise, the words of Aisha, "Whatever people conceal, Allah knows it"; the words of Imran ibn Husayn, "Will the people of Paradise know the people of Hell?"; and Aisha's words, "Does Allah not say, 'Then he will be reckoned with an easy reckoning'?"—and other examples that will come, if Allah wills—show only that they had general faith but were ignorant of particular matters, so they asked about them. They did not fabricate lies against Allah or attribute to Him what He had not attributed to Himself, except insofar as they inferred its affirmation from other evidence or presumed the original permissibility. For example, in the story of Muadh, prostrating in greeting kings and leaders was well known, just as it had been prescribed in the law of those before us. Muadh presumed this original rule applied to customary matters of social dealings, and acted accordingly with the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him). Thus, he had grounds for interpretation here, which excused him. As for the second man, he was ignorant of Allah's ruling on the matter, did not seek it in the proper way, and began speaking about Allah without knowledge. Such a person is not called mistaken, since


[Page 199]

Along with his ignorance, he deliberately did something else: he spoke about Allah without knowledge or proof, and thereby fell into shirk. One who speaks about Allah without knowledge is certainly not mistaken; rather, he is in error, so he does not fall under the preceding verses. But whoever is ignorant of something, exerts his utmost effort, and says something incorrect about Allah, erring through interpretation, is excused so long as he believes in Allah and His Messenger in a general sense. This is the distinction between these three categories, and Allah knows best. God willing, in the discussion of how the proof is established, there will be an explanation of why the heedless, ignorant person who speaks about Allah without knowledge is not excused.
(Among the proofs for excusing someone on the basis of interpretation) is the Companions' statement that Amir's deeds had been nullified. This is in Sahih al-Bukhari (5/131):
"When the two groups drew up in battle, Amir's sword was short. He struck at the leg of a Jew with it, but the sword's tip turned back and struck Amir in the hollow of his knee, and he died from it. He said: When they returned, Salama said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) saw me holding his hand and said, 'What is the matter?' I said to him, 'May my father and mother be sacrificed for you! They claim that Amir's deeds have been nullified.' The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said, 'Whoever said that is lying. He will have two rewards'—and he joined his fingers together—'for he is a striving warrior. Few Arabs have walked with a man like him.' Qutayba narrated to us; Hatim narrated to us, saying: 'He grew up with it.'"
‌‌_________


[Page 200]

("The tip of his sword") means its sharp edge. ("His deeds were rendered void") means that Amir's deeds were nullified because he killed himself with his sword. ("He would strive") means that he would exert himself in obedience. ("A fighter") means one who fights in the path of Allah, Exalted is He. ("By it") means by this praiseworthy quality, namely, striving in jihad with exertion. ("He grew up") means he matured and reached adulthood.

In this hadith, the Companions ruled that Amir's deeds had been nullified, based on their interpretation. This was a matter of belief, yet the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did not declare them unbelievers. Perhaps this was because they could have inferred it from general texts such as: "Whoever kills himself with an iron weapon will have that weapon in his hand, stabbing himself in the belly with it, abiding therein forever." They thus inferred, on the basis of their interpretation, that one who kills himself is an unbeliever and that his deeds are nullified, and applied this ruling to Amir.

Their saying, "We are doomed, by the Lord of the Kaaba: hypocrisy, hypocrisy," in the hadith

Imam Abu Ya'la, may Allah have mercy on him, said (vol. 6, p. 58): Abd al-Wahid narrated to us; Ghassan ibn Barzin, meaning al-Tahawi, narrated to us; Thabit al-Bunani narrated to us, from Anas ibn Malik, who said: One day, the Companions of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) came in the morning and said, "Messenger of Allah, we are doomed, by the Lord of the Kaaba!" He said, "What is the matter?" They said, "Hypocrisy, hypocrisy." He said, "Do you not testify that there is no god but Allah alone, with no partner, and that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger?" They said, "Yes." He said, "That is not hypocrisy." He said: Then they came back a second time and said, "Messenger of Allah, we are doomed, by the Lord of the Kaaba!" He said, "What is the matter?" They said, "Hypocrisy, hypocrisy." He said, "Do you not testify that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger?" [p. 81] They said, "Yes." He said, "That is not hypocrisy." He said: Then they came back a third time and said, "Messenger of Allah, we are doomed, by the Lord of the Kaaba!"


[Page 201]

He said, "What is that?" They said, "Hypocrisy, hypocrisy." He said, "Do you not testify that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger?" They said, "Indeed, we do." He said, "That is not hypocrisy." They said, "When we are with you, we are in one state, but when we leave you, the world and our families occupy our thoughts." He said, "If, when you left me, you remained in the state you are in with me, the angels would shake your hands in the streets of the city."
This is a sound hadith.
Here, the Companions thought that they had become unbelievers and fallen into major hypocrisy, as indicated by the Prophet's response to them that this was not hypocrisy, so long as they testified that there is no god but Allah. This would not be a valid interpretation if they had meant minor hypocrisy, since that can coexist with the two testimonies of faith. Perhaps these Companions understood this from the common linguistic meaning of hypocrisy: a person outwardly displaying something contrary to what is within. They therefore thought this was hypocrisy in its legal sense, which takes one out of the religion and leads to ruin. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) excused them on account of this interpretation, applying the linguistic meaning to the legal meaning. This hadith shows that one who declares another person an unbeliever on the basis of an interpretation is excused.
Umar declared Hatib an unbeliever on the basis of an interpretation, and Usayd ibn Hudayr declared Sa'd ibn Ubadah an unbeliever, saying, "You are a hypocrite, arguing on behalf of the hypocrites." Likewise, Usama ibn Zayd declared the man he had killed an unbeliever, thinking that the two testimonies of faith would not benefit him so long as he had uttered them under the sword. The hadith is in al-Bukhari. Here, Usama interpreted the two testimonies as not, by themselves, protecting a person's life and property or establishing the ruling of Islam when uttered under threat of arms. This is a matter of belief, yet the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) did not declare Usama an unbeliever on account of this interpretation, and


[Page 202]

He did not declare this person an unbeliever, judging him by the presumption that his status of unbelief remained. This is evidence that one is not declared an unbeliever on account of an erroneous interpretation.

Moses' words to al-Khidr, "You have certainly done a terrible thing" and "a grave thing," show that Moses interpreted it as impermissible to kill the boy, since he had killed no one. He judged al-Khidr's act to be a grave wrong, whereas in reality it was obedience to Allah. Thus Moses, on the basis of his interpretation, prohibited something recommended or obligatory, because the established principle for him was that it was impermissible to take a life except in retaliation for another life. Yet Allah did not declare him an unbeliever, nor did al-Khidr; rather, they excused him, even though he was a prophet. How much more so should those who are lesser than them in knowledge and understanding be excused?

It is related in Sahih al-Bukhari: Yahya ibn Yahya narrated to us; Hammad ibn Zayd informed us, from Yazid al-Duba'i; Mutarrif narrated to us, from Imran ibn Husayn, who said: It was asked, "Messenger of Allah, are the people of Paradise known from among the people of the Fire?" He replied, "Yes." It was asked, "Then what is the point of the deeds performed by those who act?" He said, "Everyone is facilitated toward that for which he was created."

This hadith clearly indicates that Imran was unaware of Allah's knowledge of what would come to pass, since his question was phrased in the passive voice, thus encompassing the possibility that the knowledge belonged to anyone whatsoever. The basic purpose of a question is to seek knowledge. Yet here the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) did not declare him an unbeliever for not knowing these two grave matters: first, Allah's knowledge of what deeds His servants would perform and of their ultimate fate; and second, the matter of predestination, which is one of the foundations of faith. But since Imran was a believer in faith


[Page 203]

The general meaning of what the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) brought, while being unaware of some of the details, and he excused him on that account and did not declare him an unbeliever. No one should say here that Imran only intended to ask whether the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) had taught them or not, since it is apparent that the Prophet did not teach them. How, then, could he answer "Yes"? This is supported by the hadith about those who are driven away from his Pool: he says, "My Companions," and it is said to him, "You do not know what they introduced after you." The apparent meaning of this is that he does not know who among them are the people of Paradise and who are the people of the Fire.

As for the Companions' saying to Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas, "We can only think that you have become an unbeliever," Imam al-Nasa'i, may Allah have mercy on him, said (vol. 7, p. 7): Abu Dawud informed us; he said: al-Hasan ibn Muhammad narrated to us; he said: Zuhayr narrated to us; he said: Abu Ishaq narrated to us, from Mus'ab ibn Sa'd, from his father, who said: "We were discussing something, and I had only recently left the Age of Ignorance, so I swore by al-Lat and al-Uzza. The Companions of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) said to me, 'What a bad thing you have said! Go to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family) and tell him, for we can only think that you have become an unbeliever.' So I went to him and told him. He said to me, 'Say, "There is no god but Allah alone, with no partner," three times; seek refuge in Allah from Satan three times; spit lightly to your left three times; and do not do it again.'"

Here, then, the Companions interpreted Sa'd as being an unbeliever destined to remain in the Fire because of the words he had said. All of this is interpretation concerning creed: first, that swearing by other than Allah is unbelief; and likewise, belief in what follows from this unbelief, namely, that its perpetrator remains in the Fire. All of this concerns matters of


[Page 204]

belief, yet it has not been transmitted that he (peace and blessings be upon him) declared his Companions unbelievers on that account. Sa'd reported this of them using a plural form with the definite article, which conveys generality.
Likewise, the hadith about the man who scattered his own ashes. Abu Muhammad said: "We say to anyone who declares a person an unbeliever merely for holding the same opinion, without the proof having been established against him and his having defied the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), and without finding any unease in himself over what he brought: Tell us, did the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) leave anything of Islam—belief in which is required, and denial of which makes one an unbeliever—without making it clear and calling all people to it? The answer must be, 'No.' Whoever denies this is an unbeliever, without disagreement. Once he acknowledges that, he is asked: Has it ever been reported of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) that he refused to accept the faith of the people of a village or a locality, or of any person who came to him, whether free or enslaved, man or woman, unless that person affirmed that ability precedes the act or accompanies it; or that the Quran is uncreated; or that Allah, Exalted is He, is seen or is not seen; or that He has hearing, sight, life, or any of the other superfluous matters of the theologians, which Satan introduced among them in order to sow enmity and hatred between them? If he claims that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) never invited anyone to embrace Islam without first explaining these matters to them, then he has lied, in contradiction to the consensus of the Muslims throughout the world, and has said what he knows to be false. He has also claimed that all the Companions (may Allah be pleased with them) colluded to conceal that this was something the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did. This is inherently impossible; moreover, it implies that they were unbelievers, since they concealed something without which no one's Islam is complete. If he says that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) never called anyone to any of these things, but that they are contained in the Quran and in his words, then he is told: You are right. It is thereby established that ignorance of any of all these matters would have been unbelief.


[Page 205]

When the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) failed to explain this to the free woman and the slave woman, the free man and the female slave, anyone who permits this has said that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) did not convey the message as he was commanded. This is outright disbelief on the part of anyone who permits it. It is therefore necessarily the case that ignorance of all this does no harm at all. Discussion of these matters is only required when people engage in them; then it becomes necessary to explain the truth from the Quran and the Sunnah, in accordance with the words of Allah, Mighty and Majestic: {Be steadfast for Allah, bearing witness with justice}, and the words of Allah, Mighty and Majestic: {You shall surely make it clear to the people and not conceal it}. Whoever turns away after the truth has been explained is therefore a disbeliever, because he has neither accepted the judgment of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) nor submitted to what he decreed. It is authentically reported from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) that a man who had never done any good deed said to his family when death came upon him, "When I die, burn me, then scatter my ashes on a windy day, half in the sea and half on land. For by Allah, if Allah, Exalted is He, has power over me, He will punish me with a punishment unlike any He has inflicted on anyone of His creation." Allah, Mighty and Majestic, gathered his ashes and brought him back to life, then asked him, "What made you do that?" He replied, "Fear of You, my Lord." And Allah, Exalted is He, forgave him for this statement.
Abu Muhammad said: This was a man who remained ignorant until he died that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, is able to gather his ashes and bring him back to life. Yet He forgave him because of his affirmation, his fear, and his ignorance. Some who distort words from their proper meanings have said that "if Allah has power over me" means only "if Allah constricts me," as in His words, Exalted is He: {But when He tests him and restricts his provision}. Abu Muhammad said: This is a false interpretation, which is impossible, because its meaning would then be, "If Allah constricts me, He will surely constrict me." Moreover, if that were the meaning, there would have been no point in his commanding them to burn him.


[Page 206]

And scatter his ashes. The meaning is that there is no doubt that he only instructed him to do this so that he might escape the punishment of Allah, Exalted is He.
Abu Muhammad said: What makes this clearer than anything else in this matter is the statement of Allah, Exalted is He: {When the disciples said, "O Jesus, son of Mary, can your Lord send down to us a table spread with food from heaven?"} up to His statement: {and know that you have told us the truth}. These disciples, whom Allah, Mighty and Majestic is He, praised, spoke out of ignorance when they said to Jesus (peace be upon him), "Can your Lord send down to us a table spread with food from heaven?" Yet this did not invalidate their faith. There is no way around this. They would only have disbelieved had they said this after the proof had been established and made clear to them.
Abu Muhammad said: There is a necessary proof about which there is no disagreement: the entire community is in agreement, with no dissent from anyone, that whoever deliberately alters a verse of the Quran, knowing that it appears differently in the written copies, or deliberately omits a word, or deliberately adds a word to it, is an unbeliever by the consensus of the entire community. Yet a person may make a mistake in recitation, adding one word and omitting another, and altering his speech in ignorance, thinking that he is correct; he may insist on this and argue about it before the truth has become clear to him. In the view of no one in the community is he thereby an unbeliever, a transgressor, or a sinner. But once he has consulted the written copies, or someone whose report constitutes proof has informed him of this, if he persists in his error, then in the view of the entire community he is undoubtedly an unbeliever on that account. This is the ruling that applies throughout the whole religion."
Some may say that the hadith (the hadith about the man who scattered himself) contains only disbelief, and that disbelief may be forgiven, because what we have been taught is that He does not forgive


[Page 207]

It is shirk, not disbelief. The answer to that is: "How can we accept this hadith when its apparent meaning contradicts what is necessarily known of the religion? For this established fact is explicitly stated in the Noble Quran, when the Lord of the worlds said: {Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating anything with Him, but He forgives anything less than that for whomever He wills} [al-Nisa: 48]. This verse is explicit in its indication; that is, in the terminology of the scholars of legal theory (usul al-fiqh), it is definitive in transmission and definitive in meaning. Its meaning is that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, may forgive any sin, however grave, except associating partners with Allah, Blessed and Exalted is He, for Allah does not forgive it: {Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating anything with Him, but He forgives anything less than that for whomever He wills} [al-Nisa: 48]." Someone might say, by way of reconciling this definitive Quranic text with the hadith: "There are two ways to reconcile them. The first is that the verse says Allah does not forgive shirk, whereas disbelief is not shirk.
In other words, what is mentioned in this hadith is not shirk, but disbelief, because this man believed in Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and his belief in Allah led him to make this unjust bequest: he felt that he deserved this punishment, so, to escape Allah's punishment, Mighty and Majestic is He, he made this bequest. He feared Allah; he did not merely believe in Him, but also feared Him. His fear of Allah led him to make this bequest.
Thus, he believed in Allah and did not associate anyone with Him. The way to reconcile the hadith with the verse is to leave the verse with its apparent meaning: {Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating anything with Him} [al-Nisa: 48]. As for disbelief that is not shirk, one could say that Allah may forgive it, and the evidence for that is this hadith.


[Page 208]

Someone might say this, and at first glance this reconciliation may seem acceptable and reasonable. But that is not the case, because there is a legal truth of the Sharia that we must know: first, because it is a law that must be understood as it is and in its true sense.
Second: Because this understanding helps us reconcile many texts that appear to contradict and conflict with one another. What is this legal truth? It is that every act of disbelief is polytheism. It is known to everyone, or at least to jurists and students of knowledge, that every act of polytheism is disbelief. But they do not know the converse: they know that every act of polytheism is disbelief, but the fact that every act of disbelief is polytheism is not known to the vast majority of people, even though it is as true as the words you speak: every act of disbelief is polytheism, just as every act of polytheism is disbelief. There is absolutely no difference between them.
Among the proofs for this is the dialogue that Allah, Exalted is He, mentions in Surat al-Kahf between a believer and a polytheist. Allah, Exalted is He, says: {Set forth for them the example of two men: We gave one of them two gardens of grapevines, surrounded them with date palms, and placed crops between them. Both gardens yielded their produce and withheld nothing from it, and We caused a river to flow through them. He had other produce, and he said to his companion} [al-Kahf: 32–34]. The owner of the two gardens, who was an unbelieving polytheist, as you will hear, said to his believing companion: {He said to his companion, as he was conversing with him, "I have more wealth than you and greater numbers." He entered his garden, wronging himself, and said, "I do not think this will ever perish. Nor do I think the Hour will come to pass"} [al-Kahf: 34–36]. In general usage, or according to common convention, is this polytheism or unbelief? This is unbelief, because he denied resurrection and the rising of the dead: {He said, "I do not think this will ever perish. Nor do I think the Hour will come to pass. And if ...}


[Page 209]

"If I am returned to my Lord, I will surely find a better place of return than this." His companion said to him, as he was conversing with him, "Have you disbelieved in the One who created you from dust, then from a drop of fluid, then fashioned you into a man?"} [al-Kahf: 35–37] His believing companion said: {But as for me, He is Allah, my Lord, and I associate none with my Lord.} [al-Kahf: 38] He concluded his admonition and conversation with his companion by saying that he did not associate partners with Allah as his companion did: {and I associate none with my Lord.} [al-Kahf: 38] This shows that the man who owned the two gardens—that is, the two orchards—when he doubted the resurrection and the raising of the dead, associated partners with Allah, Mighty and Majestic. That is why his believing companion said to him: "You have disbelieved and associated partners with Allah, but I associate none with my Lord." The conclusion of the story also confirms this, for at the end of the verses it says: {And his fruits were encompassed, so he began wringing his hands over what he had spent on it, while it lay fallen upon its trellises, and he said, "Would that I had not associated anyone with my Lord!"} [al-Kahf: 42] What was it that he associated with Allah? Apparently, he denied the resurrection and the raising of the dead. So where was the association of partners? Here lies the point: association with Allah means that every disbeliever, whatever the reason for his disbelief, has taken his own desire as his god. Thus, association with Allah applies to every kind of disbelief into which a person falls. This is why we said that there is a religiously established meaning: every disbelief is association with Allah. The context we have mentioned to you is the clearest proof of this.
Accordingly, it is not valid to reconcile the previously mentioned verse, {Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him} [al-Nisa: 48], with this hadith in the manner we related earlier. For, after this explanation, the meaning of the verse is: Allah does not forgive disbelief in Him—that is, whether the disbelief is linguistically termed association with Allah or not. Allah, Mighty and Majestic, does not forgive disbelief at all, whether it is association with Him or not. This explanation


[Page 210]

As I have said to you, this opens the way for us to resolve many problems. Once we know that shirk in the language of the Sharia means disbelief, and disbelief means shirk, the verse then clearly indicates that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, does not forgive shirk in any of its forms—unless it is practical shirk (minor shirk), not shirk of the heart. Thus, all the hadiths we cited as evidence that one who commits an act of disbelief on the basis of a mistaken interpretation may be excused also apply to one who commits shirk on the basis of a mistaken interpretation, so long as he has not been negligent in seeking knowledge. Likewise, Allah's saying, "Then those who disbelieve in their Lord equate others with Him"—that is, they regard others as equal to Him; this is shirk, for he has taken his own desire as his god.
The hadith of the white and black thread, as related in Sahih al-Bukhari (6/26):
Musa ibn Ismail narrated to us: Abu Awana narrated to us, from Husayn, from al-Sha'bi, from Adi, who said: Adi took a white cord and a black cord.
