This is similar to what they said about one who prostrates to an idol and the like, even though there is no explicit denial in such an act, after they had defined unbelief as denial. If they argue that consensus has been established on deeming the perpetrator of such an act an unbeliever, we say: These reports concerning these people likewise entail that they are unbelievers, even if they did not believe with certainty that those whom they declared unbelievers were exonerated. Nor does their general belief in Islam and their performance of the obligatory acts save them from being judged unbelievers, just as it does not save one who prostrates to an idol. I say: Among those who inclined toward some of this discussion was al-Tabari in his Tahdhib. After citing the hadiths of this chapter, he said: "In this is a refutation of the view of one who says that no one among the people of the qibla leaves Islam, once its ruling applies to him, except by consciously intending to leave it. This view is invalidated by the hadith's statement: 'They speak the truth, recite the Quran, and pass out of Islam, yet it does not go beyond their throats.' It is well known that they only deemed the blood and property of Muslims lawful because they erred in their interpretation of Quranic verses, understanding them otherwise than intended." He then transmitted with an authentic chain from Ibn Abbas, and mentioned the Kharijites and what they encounter when the Quran is recited. Ibn Abbas said: "They believe in its clear verses and perish over its ambiguous ones." The command to kill them also supports the aforementioned view, along with the previously cited hadith of Ibn Masud: "It is not lawful to kill a Muslim except for one of three reasons," including "one who abandons his religion and separates from the community." Al-Qurtubi said in al-Mufhim: "The analogy mentioned in the hadith of Abu Sa'id supports the view that they are unbelievers"—that is, the hadith to be cited in the following chapter—"for …"


[Page 254]

The apparent meaning intended is that they left Islam and retained no connection to it, just as an arrow passes through its target, because of its speed and the strength of the archer, so that it retains nothing of the target. He alluded to this by saying, "It passes beyond the dung and the blood." The author of al-Shifa said concerning this: "Likewise, we decisively declare an unbeliever anyone who makes a statement by which he seeks to lead the community astray or to declare the Companions unbelievers." The author of al-Rawda cited this from him in the chapter on apostasy and endorsed it. Most Sunni scholars of legal theory held that the Kharijites are sinners, and that the rulings of Islam apply to them, since they profess the two testimonies of faith and regularly observe the pillars of Islam. They were deemed sinners only because they declared Muslims unbelievers, relying on a corrupt interpretation; this led them to deem the blood and property of those who opposed them lawful, and to testify that they were unbelievers and polytheists. Al-Khattabi said: "Muslim scholars agree that the Kharijites, despite their misguidance, are a sect among the Muslim sects. They permitted marrying them and eating their slaughtered animals, and held that they are not declared unbelievers so long as they adhere to the foundation of Islam." Iyad said: "This question nearly became more perplexing to theologians than any other, to the point that the jurist Abd al-Haqq asked Imam Abu al-Maali about it, and he excused himself, saying that admitting an unbeliever into the faith community and expelling a Muslim from it is a grave matter in religion." He said: "Before him, Qadi Abu Bakr al-Baqillani had refrained from giving an answer, saying: 'The people did not explicitly declare unbelief; rather, they made statements that lead to unbelief.'" Al-Ghazali said in The Distinction between Faith and Heresy: "What is proper is to avoid declaring someone an unbeliever whenever there is any way to do so. For deeming it lawful to shed the blood of those who pray and affirm the oneness of God is an error, and the error of leaving a thousand unbelievers alive is less grave than the error of shedding the blood of even one Muslim." Among the evidence cited by those who did not declare them unbelievers is the Prophet's statement in the third hadith of this chapter, after describing them as passing out of the religion as an arrow passes through its target: "The archer looks at his arrow."


[Page 255]

Until he said: "Then he doubts whether anything has stuck to the arrow notch." Ibn Battal said: The majority of scholars held that
The scholars held that the Kharijites are not outside the community of Muslims, based on the words, "he is uncertain about the arrowhead," since uncertainty entails doubt. And when doubt arises in this matter, they cannot be definitively judged to have left Islam, for whoever is certainly established in the covenant of Islam does not leave it except with certainty. He said: Ali was asked about the people of al-Nahrawan, "Have they disbelieved?" He replied, "They fled from disbelief." I say: If this is authentically reported from Ali, it may be understood to mean that he had not learned of the belief they held which led those who declared them disbelievers to do so. There is, however, a question about his use of the words, "he is uncertain about the arrowhead," for in some versions of the hadith mentioned—as previously indicated and as will be discussed later—"nothing of it clung to it," while in others, "it passed through the dung and blood." The way to reconcile these is that he was initially unsure whether there was anything on the arrowhead or not, then he established that nothing from the shot had clung to the arrow or any part of it. It is also possible to attribute the difference to different individuals among them, and that the words "he is uncertain" indicate that some of them may retain something of Islam. Al-Qurtubi says in al-Mufhim: "The view that they are disbelievers is more apparent from the hadith." He said: "According to the view that they are disbelievers, they are fought and killed, and their property is taken as spoils. This is the view of a group of hadith scholars regarding the property of the Kharijites. According to the view that they are not disbelievers, they are treated as rebels when they break allegiance and take up arms. As for one of them who conceals an innovation, if he is discovered, whether he is to be killed after being asked to repent, or not killed but instead efforts are made to refute his innovation, scholars have differed, in accordance with their disagreement over whether they are disbelievers." End quote.
Although I am inclined to regard the Kharijites as unbelievers, I have cited this to show that the early generations differed over whether one who interprets the texts erroneously is to be declared an unbeliever, and whether this interpretation is acceptable grounds for excusing its proponent or not. For this reason, he did not declare him an unbeliever.


[Page 256]

Whoever excuses the Kharijites' disbelief. It will be shown that among those who did not declare them disbelievers were Ibn Abbas and Ali. They did not tell them, "You have lied about Allah and His Messenger, misunderstood the true nature of Islam, and contradicted consensus," because those who excused them had a doubt concerning their disbelief. And since you say that we follow the early generations, the question for you is: Why did you not follow them here as well and excuse the one who excuses the Asharis, when their innovations are but a drop in the ocean of the Kharijites' innovations, despite the explicit texts stating that they leave the religion?
(Another contradiction in their rejection of an infinite regress)
Abu Ubayda al-Jazairi said, "Whoever does not declare a person whom Allah has explicitly declared a disbeliever to be a disbeliever—such as Iblis—is himself a disbeliever, as is whoever excuses him, and whoever excuses that person, and so on ..." Then he said, "But an infinite regress is not addressed in matters other than these, where there may be some doubts concerning the excuse given by the one who excuses, such as the Jahmiyya, the issue of the createdness of the Quran, and the like."
I do not know what he means by saying that it is "not addressed." Does he mean that he does not affirm an infinite regress in this case, that an infinite regress is invalid, and that the one who excuses the excuser is therefore excused? That is what his words appear to mean. Or does he mean that he suspends judgment on the matter? If he means that he does not affirm an infinite regress and that the one who excuses the excuser is not declared a disbeliever, then the pressing question here is: Why is the one who excuses the excuser not declared a disbeliever?
Let us give an example. They declare Ibn Taymiyya a disbeliever because he does not declare the Asharis disbelievers and excuses them. If you ask them, they answer with the reasons I mentioned earlier: denying Allah and His Messenger, ignorance of the true nature of Islam, and violating consensus.
So Ibn Taymiyya is a disbeliever who interprets the matter differently and is not excused for his interpretation, just as the Asharis are disbelievers who interpret the matter differently and are not excused for their interpretation. If someone then says, "Ibn Taymiyya is not a disbeliever, and he is excused," it seems, as I understand them, that they do not declare him a disbeliever.


[Page 257]

The pressing question here, then, is: why? If the very same reason for which you declared Ibn Taymiyya an unbeliever—namely, that "he excused people who had no valid interpretation, while himself interpreting"—is also present in this man who excused Ibn Taymiyya, since "he excused a man who had no valid interpretation, while himself interpreting," then this is a contradiction. You would therefore have to declare everyone an unbeliever and accept an infinite regress, or excuse everyone because the existence of a doubt removes the ruling of unbelief. The latter seems more likely to me, and Allah knows best.

(Are the Ash'aris and Maturidis Jahmis?)
It is noticeable that most of the texts they cite from people below the level of the Companions—for they have not cited anything from the Companions at all—that declare certain deeds to be unbelief occur in discussions of the createdness of the Quran or of the Jahmiyya. The question now is: are the Ash'aris, upon whom they apply these texts, Jahmis, or do they believe that the Quran is created, such that their use of the statements of those they cite is valid? As we have already shown, most of these statements are weak, and most come from people outside the first three generations, who fall outside the meaning of the Salaf.

Shaykh al-Islam Ibn Taymiyya, may Allah have mercy on him, said in al-Tis'iniyya (1/265–271): "The Jahmiyya are of three degrees:
1. The worst of them are the extremists, who deny Allah's names and attributes. If they call Him by any of His most beautiful names, they say that it is metaphorical; in reality, according to them, He is neither living, knowing, powerful, hearing, seeing, nor speaking, and He does not speak. The scholars have likewise described the true meaning of their position, as Imam Ahmad did in what he included in al-Radd ala al-Zanadiqa wa al-Jahmiyya, and as Abu al-Hasan al-Ash'ari did in al-Ibana and Maqalat al-Islamiyyin.
This position, which is the position of the extremists who truly deny the names, is that of the esoteric Qarmatians and those who preceded them among their brethren, the Sabean philosophers.
2. The second degree of Jahmism is the Jahmism of the Mu'tazila and those like them. They generally affirm Allah's most beautiful names, but deny His attributes. They also do not affirm all of Allah's most beautiful names on the basis of ..."


[Page 258]

The truth; rather, they make many of them metaphorical. These are the well-known Jahmiyya.
3. As for the third category, they are the affirmers of the divine attributes who oppose the Jahmiyya, but who have a degree of Jahmism. They are those who affirm Allah's names and attributes in general, but reject some of His names and attributes that are transmitted in reports, or otherwise, and interpret them, just as the earlier group interpreted all of His attributes.
Among them are those who affirm His attributes transmitted in the Quran, but not those transmitted in hadith, as is the case with many theologians and jurists, and a group of hadith scholars.
Among them, too, are those who affirm, in general, the attributes transmitted in reports as well, but deny and negate some of what is established by textual evidence and reason. This is the case with Abu Muhammad ibn Kullab and his followers.
Abu al-Hasan al-Ashari and groups of jurists, theologians, hadith scholars, and Sufis fall into this category. They are closer to the pure Ahl al-Sunna than they are to the Jahmiyya, Rafidites, Kharijites, and Qadariyya. However, a group affiliated with them is closer to the Jahmiyya than to the pure Ahl al-Sunna: these people dispute with the Mu'tazila very strongly over the attributes they affirm, more strongly than they dispute with the rest of those who affirm the attributes over what they deny.
As for later scholars, they have increasingly allied themselves with and drawn close to the Mu'tazila, and have preferred them over the Ahl al-Sunna and those who affirm the attributes. They have opposed their predecessors. Some of them come close to one another in what they deny and affirm, and most people say that these groups contradict themselves in combining denial and affirmation.
It is clear from the preceding passage that the Asharis fall under the third category, of whom Ibn Taymiyya said that they are closer to the pure Ahl al-Sunna than to the Jahmiyya. This confirms that they are not Jahmiyya. He also described them as "those who oppose the Jahmiyya," which likewise confirms that they are not the Jahmiyya whom some of the Successors are reported to have declared disbelievers without qualification.
Some have tried to use the following transmitted report as evidence that the Asharis are Jahmiyya. They said:
"The Imam al-Tirmidhi said in al-Jami al-Kabir, commenting on hadith number 662, as follows: 'This is a hasan sahih hadith. A similar report has been narrated from Aisha, from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him). More than one of the people of knowledge has said concerning this hadith and reports like it that…"


[Page 259]

The attributes and the descent of the Lord, Blessed and Exalted is He, every night to the lowest heaven: they said, "The reports concerning this are established, and they are believed in without imagining how, and without asking, 'How is it?'" This is how it was related from Malik, Sufyan ibn Uyaynah, and Abdullah ibn al-Mubarak: they said regarding these hadiths, "Let them pass without asking how." This is also the position of the scholars among the people of the Sunnah and the community. As for the Jahmiyyah, they rejected these reports and said, "This is anthropomorphism." Allah, Mighty and Majestic is He, has mentioned the hand, hearing, and sight in more than one place in His Book. The Jahmiyyah interpreted these verses, explaining them in a way different from the explanation given by the scholars. They said that Allah did not create Adam with His hand, and said that "hand" here means power. Ishaq ibn Ibrahim said: "Anthropomorphism occurs only when someone says 'a hand like a hand,' 'a hand resembling a hand,' 'hearing like hearing,' or 'hearing resembling hearing.' If someone says 'hearing like hearing' or 'hearing resembling hearing,' that is anthropomorphism. But if someone says, as Allah, Exalted is He, has said, 'hand, hearing, and sight,' without saying how, or saying 'resembling hearing' or 'like hearing,' then this is not anthropomorphism. It is as Allah, Exalted is He, says in His Book: 'There is nothing like Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.'" End quote.))
There is nothing in al-Tirmidhi's words stating that the Asharis are Jahmiyyah. He only said that the Jahmiyyah interpret attributes of this kind, and this is true. The Asharis agreed with them on some of that, but this does not mean that they should be called Jahmiyyah, nor


[Page 260]

He says that he too is like that; not everyone who agrees with a sect on some issue thereby becomes one of them." End quote.

He said: The following are the reasons for declaring the Ash'aris unbelievers, without excusing them on the basis of interpretation:

"First: Denying Allah Most High's exaltedness, which is the most heinous of them.
The Salaf unanimously agreed that whoever denies Allah Most High's exaltedness is an unbeliever, and the Ash'aris affirm it, following the Jahmiyya.
Ibn Taymiyya said regarding Allah's exaltedness: 'For this reason, the Salaf were unanimous in declaring whoever denied this an unbeliever, since in their view it is known by necessity as part of the religion.' Al-Dar' 7/26." End quote.

Comment: It is astonishing that they cite Ibn Taymiyya here, since, as noted above, Ibn Taymiyya did not declare the Ash'aris individually to be unbelievers, nor did he transmit such a view from the Salaf, as they do when declaring individuals unbelievers. Rather, he transmitted a general, unrestricted ruling from the Salaf here. Ibn Taymiyya also said: "An unrestricted declaration of unbelief, like an unrestricted threat, does not entail declaring a particular person an unbeliever until the proof has been established against him, the rejection of which constitutes unbelief." And: "Ibn Taymiyya says: 'As for declaring someone an unbeliever, the correct view is that whoever among the community of Muhammad (peace and blessings be upon him) exercises independent judgment and seeks the truth, but errs, is not an unbeliever; rather, his error is forgiven.'" And this is his position from


[Page 261]

His position that individuals are not to be declared unbelievers is well known, as is his refusal to declare the Ash'aris unbelievers. For this reason, they declared him an unbeliever personally.
It is stated in Majmu al-Fatawa (20/31):
"In general, the reward is for following the truth according to one's own effort in reaching a judgment. Even if there were, in reality, a truth that contradicted his judgment, that truth would be more deserving of being followed, were he able to know it; but he was unable to do so. This is like those who strive to determine the direction of the Kaaba. The same applies to anyone who performs an act of worship that has been prohibited, without knowing of the prohibition, provided that it is of the same kind as what has been commanded. For example, someone who prays at times when prayer is prohibited, having received the general command to pray but not having learned of the prohibition; or someone who relies on a specific but weaker proof, such as the prayer of two rak'ahs after Asr performed by a group of the early generations, because the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) prayed them; or a prayer for which weak or fabricated hadiths have been narrated, such as the thousand-rak'ah prayer in the middle of Sha'ban, the prayer at the beginning of Rajab, and the tasbih prayer, as Ibn al-Mubarak permitted it, and other such prayers. If these fall under the general recommendation to pray, and he has not learned of anything that entails prohibition, he is rewarded for that, even if there is a prohibition concerning them in some respect, because he did not know that they were an innovation adopted as a regular practice and performed collectively every year. This is like someone instituting a sixth prayer. For this reason, if he wished to perform such a prayer without a hadith, he would have no right to do so; but when the hadith was narrated, he believed that"


[Page 262]

If it is sound, but he errs in it, his error is forgiven and he is rewarded for performing an act of a kind that is prescribed. The same applies to someone who fasts on the day of Eid without knowing that this is prohibited. This is unlike an act whose kind is not prescribed, such as associating partners with Allah: there is no reward for this, even though Allah does not punish its perpetrator until the message has been conveyed, as He, Exalted is He, says: {We would never punish until We had sent a messenger}. Yet although he is not punished, he is not rewarded either. Rather, this is as Allah, Exalted is He, says: {We shall turn to whatever deeds they did and make them scattered dust}. Ibn al-Mubarak said: "These are deeds done for other than Allah." Mujahid said: "These are deeds that were not accepted." And He, Exalted is He, says: {The deeds of those who disbelieve in their Lord are like ashes blown about by the wind} to the end of the verse. Their deeds are thus invalid, and there is no reward for them. If the Messenger forbids them from these deeds and they do not desist, they are punished; punishment for them is conditional upon the Messenger's message having been conveyed. As for their being invalid in themselves, that is because they are not commanded: every act of worship that is not commanded must be forbidden. Then, if someone knows that it is forbidden and does it, he deserves punishment. If he does not know, he does not deserve punishment. If he believes that it is commanded and it belongs to a kind of act that is prescribed, he is rewarded for it. But if it belongs to the kind of associating partners with Allah, nothing of that kind is commanded, although some people may think that certain forms of it are commanded. Such a person is not exercising independent legal reasoning (ijtihad), because one who exercises independent legal reasoning must follow a legal proof, and there can be no legal proof for these acts. But


[Page 263]

He may do so based on an exercise of independent reasoning like his own: by following the example of shaykhs and scholars who did it, and who did so because they thought it beneficial; or because they had heard a fabricated hadith. If proof establishing the prohibition has not been presented to such people, they will not be punished. As for reward, they may be rewarded in the sense that they are superior to others of their kind; but they will not be rewarded for drawing near to Allah through such acts.
Section:
The excusable error in independent reasoning occurs in both types of questions: those concerning transmitted reports and those concerning knowledge, as has been explained at length elsewhere. An example is someone who believes something to be established on the basis of a verse or hadith, while there is evidence that contradicts it and clarifies what is meant, but he does not know that evidence. This includes someone who believes that the one to be sacrificed was Isaac, on the basis of a hadith he believes to be authentic; or someone who believes that Allah cannot be seen, citing His words: {Eyes cannot perceive Him} and His words: {It is not for any human being that Allah should speak to him except by revelation or from behind a veil}. Aisha used these two verses as evidence that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did not see Allah. They indicate this only in a general sense. Likewise, it is reported that some of the Successors held that Allah cannot be seen, and interpreted His words: {Faces on that Day will be radiant} {looking toward their Lord} to mean that they would await the reward of their Lord, as is reported from Mujahid and Abu Salih. Or someone who believes that the dead are not punished for the living person's weeping, because he believes that Allah's words, {No bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another}, indicate this, and that this takes precedence over a narrator's report because the


[Page 264]

Hearing may be mistaken, as a group among the early and later scholars believed. Or someone may believe that the dead do not hear the speech of the living, because he thinks that His saying, {For you cannot make the dead hear}, indicates this. Or he may believe that Allah does not marvel, as Shurayh believed, because he thinks that marveling occurs only through ignorance of the cause, whereas Allah is exalted above ignorance. Or he may believe that Ali is superior to the Companions, because he thinks the hadith of the bird is authentic: that {the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said, "O Allah, bring to me the one dearest to You among Your creation, to eat this bird with me."} Or he may believe that whoever spies for the enemy and informs them of the Prophet's campaign
is a hypocrite, as Umar believed of Hatib, saying, "Let me strike off the head of this hypocrite." Or he may believe that whoever becomes angry on behalf of some hypocrites is a hypocrite, as Usayd ibn Hudayr believed of Sa'd ibn Ubadah, saying, "You are a hypocrite, arguing on behalf of the hypocrites." Or he may believe that certain words or verses are not part of the Quran because their transmission has not been established for him by a reliable report. It is related that more than one of the early scholars rejected certain Quranic expressions, such as some of them rejecting {And your Lord decreed}, saying, "Rather, it is 'And your Lord enjoined.'" Some rejected His saying, {And when Allah took the covenant of the prophets}, saying, "Rather, it is the covenant of the Children of Israel," and so it is in the reading of Abdullah. Some rejected {Have not those who believed despaired}, saying, "Rather, it is 'Have not ...'"


[Page 265]

Those who believe become clear. Just as Umar objected to Hisham ibn al-Hakam when he heard him reciting Surat al-Furqan differently from the way he had recited it. And just as a group of the early generations objected to some reciters over readings they did not recognize, until Uthman united them upon the master copy of the Quran. And just as a group of the early and later generations objected to the claim that Allah wills acts of disobedience, because they believed this meant that Allah loves and approves of them and commands them. Another group of the early and later generations objected to the claim that Allah wills acts of disobedience because they thought that "will" could only mean His willing their creation; yet they knew that Allah is the Creator of all things, that whatever He wills comes to be, and whatever He does not will does not come to be. The Quran uses the word "will" in both this sense and the other sense, but each group recognized one of the two meanings and rejected the other. And like the man who said to his family, "When I die, burn me, then scatter my ashes in the sea. For by Allah, if Allah has power over me, He will punish me with a punishment unlike that of anyone in all the worlds." And as a group of the early generations have mentioned regarding His saying: {Does he think that no one will have power over him?} and the disciples' saying: {Can your Lord send down to us a table spread with food from heaven?} And like the Companions who asked the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), "Will we see our Lord on the Day of Resurrection?" They did not know that they would see Him; and many people do not know this, either because the hadiths have not reached them or because they thought them false or mistaken.))


[Page 266]

(Ibn Taymiyya's discussion of declaring a specific person an unbeliever)

((After mentioning the disagreement among later scholars over whether the people of innovation are declared unbelievers in a way that takes them out of the religion or not, and whether they remain eternally in the Fire or not, Ibn Taymiyya said: "The reality of the matter is that, with regard to the general wording used by the imams, they fell into the same error as those before them fell into with regard to the general wording in the Lawgiver's texts. Whenever they saw the imams say, 'Whoever says such-and-such is an unbeliever,' the listener assumed that this wording applied to everyone who said it. They did not consider that declaring someone an unbeliever has conditions and impediments that may be absent or present in the case of a specific person, and that declaring someone an unbeliever in general does not entail declaring a specific person an unbeliever unless the conditions are met and the impediments are absent. This is made clear by the fact that Imam Ahmad and most of the imams who made such general statements did not declare most of those who said those very words to be unbelievers. For example, Imam Ahmad personally dealt with the Jahmiyya, who called on him to affirm that the Quran was created and to deny the divine attributes. They put him and the other scholars of his time to the test, and persecuted the believing men and women who did not agree with their Jahmism, beating and imprisoning them … He mentioned that most of those in authority declared everyone who was not a Jahmi and did not agree with them to be an unbeliever, and treated them as unbelievers … and so on," up to where he said: "It is well known that this is among the most extreme forms of Jahmism, for calling people to a doctrine is graver than merely holding it, and rewarding those who profess it and punishing those who reject it is graver than merely calling people to it …..

"Then Imam Ahmad prayed for the caliph and others who had beaten and imprisoned him, sought forgiveness for them, and absolved them of what they had done … Had they been apostates from Islam, it would not have been permissible to seek forgiveness for them, for seeking forgiveness for unbelievers is forbidden by the Book.


[Page 267]

The Sunnah and consensus. These statements and actions of his and of other imams make it clear that they did not declare specific individuals among the Jahmiyya to be unbelievers….
So consider, may Allah have mercy on you: despite the repugnance of the Jahmiyya's doctrine, he did not declare their specific adherents to be unbelievers; rather, he prayed for them. Nor did he support the people of polytheism and the Crusaders against them. Instead, he rejoiced at the conquest of Amorium and openly expressed his joy at it. He did not surrender to the Crusaders any land of Islam governed by the Sharia, on the pretext that the Jahmiyya and Mu'tazila declare us unbelievers."
Shaykh al-Islam Ibn Taymiyya says in his response to Ibn al-Bakri: "For this reason, the people of knowledge and the Sunnah do not declare those who oppose them to be unbelievers, even if those opponents declare them unbelievers, because declaring someone an unbeliever is a legal ruling, and a person may not retaliate in kind. If someone lies about you and commits adultery with your wife, you may not lie about him or commit adultery with his wife, because adultery and lying are forbidden as a right owed to Allah, Exalted is He. Likewise, declaring someone an unbeliever is a right owed to Allah, Exalted is He, so no one is declared an unbeliever except one whom Allah and His Messenger have declared an unbeliever." End quote, p. 257.

Those who declare the Ash'aris unbelievers cite among their reasons for doing so: "Second: denying the reality of Allah's speech and claiming that the Quran in the written copies is created and is not the speech of Allah.
The early generations declared an unbeliever anyone who denied Allah's speech and claimed that the Quran was created, and al-Lalaka'i and others transmitted a consensus on this. These people said that Allah has no verbal speech, only an inner meaning; Allah, Exalted is He, does not speak in words, letters, or a voice heard by Gabriel. The Quran is merely an expression of Allah's speech and a report of it; it is not Allah's speech. Rather, Gabriel's speech is an expression of what Allah intended, and the Quran in the written copies is created and is not Allah's speech." End quote. Here he attributed to al-Lalaka'i…


[Page 268]

The consensus was transmitted. But did al-Lalaka'i transmit a consensus in the sense they understood it—that the Salaf declared anyone who did this an unbeliever without qualification, even if acting on an interpretation? Let us examine his words. In Sharh Usul I'tiqad Ahl al-Sunna wa al-Jama'a (1/197), it says:
"We were informed by Muhammad ibn al-Muzaffar al-Muqri, who said: Al-Husayn ibn Muhammad ibn Habash al-Muqri narrated to us, who said: Abu Muhammad Abd al-Rahman ibn Abi Hatim narrated to us, who said: I asked my father and Abu Zur'a about the doctrines of the people of the Sunnah concerning the fundamentals of religion, what they had found the scholars in all the regions adhering to, and what they themselves believed regarding these matters. They replied: 'We found the scholars in all the regions—Hijaz, Iraq, Syria, and Yemen—adhering to the following doctrine: faith is speech and action; it increases and decreases. The Quran is the speech of Allah, uncreated in all its aspects. Both the good and the evil of divine decree are from Allah, Mighty and Majestic. The best of this community after its Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) is Abu Bakr al-Siddiq, then Umar ibn al-Khattab, then Uthman ibn Affan, then Ali ibn Abi Talib (peace be upon them). They are the rightly guided caliphs. The ten whom the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) named and testified would enter Paradise are as the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) testified, and his word is true. We pray for mercy upon all the Companions of Muhammad and refrain from discussing what occurred between them. Allah, Mighty and Majestic, is above His Throne, distinct from His creation, as He described Himself in His Book and through the words of His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), without asking how; His knowledge encompasses all things. {There is nothing like Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing} [al-Shura: 11]. And He, Blessed and Exalted, will be seen in the Hereafter: the people of Paradise will see Him with their eyes and hear His speech, as and however He wills. Paradise is true and the Fire is true; both are created and will never perish. Paradise is the reward for His devoted servants, and the Fire is the punishment for those who disobey Him, except those upon whom Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has mercy. The Bridge is true, and the Balance is true; it has two pans, on which the deeds of the servants will be weighed"


[Page 269]

Its good and evil are true. The Pool with which our Prophet has been honored is true. Intercession is true, and resurrection after death is true. Those who commit major sins are subject to the will of Allah, Mighty and Majestic. We do not declare the people of the qibla unbelievers on account of their sins, and we entrust their inner secrets to Allah, Mighty and Majestic. We uphold the obligation of jihad and pilgrimage alongside the leaders of the Muslims in every age and time. We do not consider rebellion against the leaders or fighting during civil strife permissible. We hear and obey whoever Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has placed in authority over us, and we do not withdraw our hand from obedience. We follow the Sunnah and the community, and avoid deviation, disagreement, and division. Jihad has continued from the time Allah, Mighty and Majestic, sent His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) until the Hour is established, under those in authority among the leaders of the Muslims; nothing can bring it to an end. Pilgrimage is likewise, as is the payment of alms from grazing livestock to those in authority among the leaders of the Muslims. People are treated as Muslims in rulings and inheritance, but we do not know their standing with Allah, Mighty and Majestic. Whoever says, "He is truly a believer," is an innovator; whoever says, "He is a believer in the sight of Allah," is among the liars; and whoever says, "He truly believes in Allah," is correct. The Murji'a and the innovators are astray, and the Qadari innovators are astray. Whoever among them denies that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, knows what has not yet come to be before it comes to be is an unbeliever. The Jahmiyya are unbelievers; the Rafida have rejected Islam; and the Kharijites have gone out of it. Whoever claims that the Quran is created is an unbeliever in Allah, the Almighty, with unbelief that takes him out of the religion. Whoever doubts the unbelief of such a person, if he understands the matter, is an unbeliever. Whoever doubts the speech of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and remains uncertain about it, saying, "I do not know whether it is created or uncreated," is a Jahmi. Whoever suspends judgment about the Quran out of ignorance is to be taught and deemed an innovator, but not declared an unbeliever." Here we have several points to make that turn his use of this statement against him.
First: al-Lalaka'i's statement: "Whoever claims that the Quran is created is an unbeliever in Allah, the Almighty, with unbelief that takes him out of the religion. Whoever doubts the unbelief of such a person, if he understands the matter, is an unbeliever."


[Page 270]

A disbeliever.")) This statement is evidence against them, since they declare anyone who excuses another to be a disbeliever without qualification, whereas here he has stipulated a condition: "that the one excusing him understand." If that is so, their argument turns against them, for if this was the consensus of the early generations, then there is consensus here that the one who excuses another is not declared a disbeliever until he understands the proof, as is apparent from al-Lalaka'i's words.
Second: Al-Lalaka'i's statement: "Whoever suspends judgment concerning the Quran out of ignorance is to be taught and declared an innovator, but not declared an unbeliever." This demolishes their claim that ignorance regarding the attribute is no excuse. And if this is a consensus, then the early generations also reached consensus on it, so Al-Lalaka'i's statement applies to them here as well.
Third: They say that one who is ignorant of an attribute established by innate disposition or reason is an unbeliever and is not excused, and must affirm those attributes that reason or innate disposition affirms, even if no revealed text has reached him. But what is al-Lalaka'i's position, which he attributes here to Ahl al-Sunna wa al-Jama'a? Al-Lalaka'i's position, which he attributes to Ahl al-Sunna wa al-Jama'a, is that belief in Allah's attributes is established only through revealed texts, and is obligatory through revealed texts, not through reason. It says in I'tiqad Ahl al-Sunna, by al-Lalaka'i (2/193):
Chapter on the Unity of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and His Attributes and Names,
and that He is Living, Powerful, Knowing, Hearing, Seeing, Speaking, Willing, Everlasting
An account of what is indicated by the Book of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and what has been narrated from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), showing that
The obligation to know Allah, Exalted is He, and His attributes through revelation, not reason. Allah, Exalted is He, says, addressing His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) in wording that is specific while the intended meaning is general: "So know that there is no god but Allah."


[Page 271]

And the Blessed and Exalted said: "Follow what has been revealed to you from your Lord. There is no god but Him, and turn away from the polytheists."
And the Blessed and Exalted said: "We sent no messenger before you without revealing to him: 'There is no god but Me, so worship Me.'"
In this verse, Allah informed His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) that the prophets before him came to know of monotheism through hearing and revelation.
And He said: "Say: If I go astray, I go astray only to my own detriment; and if I am guided, it is by what my Lord reveals to me. He is All-Hearing, Near."
Ibrahim inferred His oneness from His precise and perfect acts: the rising and setting of the sun, the appearance and disappearance of the moon, and the appearance and setting of the stars. Then he said: "If my Lord does not guide me, I will surely be among the people who have gone astray." Thus he knew that guidance came through hearing, and likewise that knowledge of the messengers is obligatory through hearing.
Allah, the Blessed and Exalted, said: "Say: O people, I am the Messenger of Allah to you all, to Him belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth. There is no god but Him; He gives life and causes death. So believe in Allah and His Messenger, the unlettered Prophet, who believes in Allah and His words, and follow him so that you may be guided."
And He said: "We would never punish until We had sent a messenger."
And the Blessed and Exalted said: "So that people would have no argument against Allah after the messengers."
And the Blessed and Exalted said: "You were not on the western side when We decreed the matter to Moses, nor were you among the witnesses. But We brought forth generations, and a long time passed over them. You did not dwell among the people of Midian, reciting Our signs to them, but We were sending messengers. And you were not


[Page 272]

by the side of the Mount when We called out, but as a mercy from your Lord, so that you may warn a people to whom no warner came before you, that they may take heed. And lest, when a calamity strikes them for what their hands have sent forth, they should say: Our Lord, why did You not send us a messenger, so that we might follow Your signs and be among the believers?)
(And they said: Why does he not bring us a sign from his Lord? Has there not come to them clear evidence of what was in the former scriptures? And had We destroyed them with a punishment before him, they would have said: Our Lord, why did You not send us a messenger, so that we might follow Your signs before we were humiliated and disgraced?
This shows that knowledge of Allah and of the messengers comes through revelation, as Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has told us.
This is the doctrine of Ahl al-Sunna wa al-Jama'a.)) I do not know what they say about this text, given how clearly it shows that the obligation to know the oneness of Allah and His attributes comes only through revelation, and that he attributes this to Ahl al-Sunna wa al-Jama'a. He said: ((Chapter gathering the oneness of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, His attributes and His names,
and that He is Living, Powerful, Knowing, Hearing, Seeing, Speaking, Willing, Everlasting:

the obligation to know Allah, Exalted is He, and His attributes comes through revelation, not through reason. Then, after setting out the evidence, he said: This shows that knowledge of Allah and of the messengers comes through revelation, as Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has told us.

[Page 273]

Is this based on this, and do they follow the doctrine of Ahl al-Sunna here??? Al-Lalaka'i's words are therefore also proof against them here.
(A Pause to Consider the Excuse for Choosing the Lesser of Two Evils)
The matter here must be precisely defined so that no one is confused. The discussion concerns a man who will not rule by anything other than what Allah has revealed, enact any legislation other than what Allah has revealed, or submit to the judgment of any law other than Allah's law. The only issue is that there are two candidates, one of whom must assume the office: one is close to Islam, while the other is hostile to it and wages war against it. If this hostile candidate will wreak havoc among the Muslims, killing and capturing them, and will put them in a state of necessity, then the previously discussed question of necessity arises. But if the other candidate will not bring the Muslims to a state of necessity and coercion, yet choosing the lesser of two evils would bring benefits to the Muslims and ease their situation, is it permissible in this case to nominate—only nominate—the lesser of two evils? There is no doubt that the person who wishes to choose the lesser of two evils does not intend for this ruler to rule by anything other than what Allah has revealed. His sole intention is to reduce disbelief and injustice. Whether or not he makes this choice, a ruler who does not rule by what Allah has revealed will most likely be installed. This voter has not ruled by anything other than what Allah has revealed, has not directly carried it out, and has not approved of it.
In some countries, there are two candidates for a certain office. One of them would cause severe harm to the Muslims if he assumed the office, while the other would cause less harm and would be conciliatory toward the Muslims, despite committing disbelief by ruling by other than what Allah has revealed. Is it permissible, in such circumstances, for the Muslims to nominate the one who would cause less harm—for example, someone like al-Najashi at the beginning of his rule, before he embraced Islam? Some view this issue from the perspective that whoever does this would, by doing so, be assisting in ruling by other than what Allah has revealed, which is major disbelief. Thus, the person who voted for him would also be a disbeliever for assisting in disbelief, and they would not excuse him on the grounds of ignorance, since he could have acquired knowledge.
But there is another point of view here: the elected representative did not intend, by his action, to help the disbeliever in his disbelief. Rather, he sought the disbeliever's leniency toward Islam and its people, and the reduction of disbelief. I would like to give an example here to clarify this matter. Suppose there are two pans of a balance: on one is a sheet of paper bearing a verse from the Quran, and on the other is a complete copy of the Quran, with impure water beneath the balance. We find two groups of people: one group is clinging to the pan with the complete Quran, trying to lower it so that it sinks into the muddy, impure water; the other group is clinging to the other pan, trying to lower it into the muddy, contaminated water. There is no doubt that both actions are disbelief. But by helping the people on the side of the verse, we will save the complete Quran from falling into the impure mud, because to the extent that we help lower the pan with the verse, the pan with the Quran rises. So there is no doubt that whoever helps lower the pan with the verse into the impure mud with the intention of saving the Quran has absolutely no intention of defiling that verse. Rather, when two prohibited matters conflict, he chooses the lesser prohibition. The researcher who considers something like this permissible does so for several reasons. Among them is that you may help a disbeliever for some legitimate reason, and your purpose in providing this kind of assistance is a legitimate one. Even if this assistance leads to the disbeliever being kept alive and remaining in existence for a longer period, and consequently to the continuation of his acts of disbelief, you do not here intend to preserve disbelief, bring it into existence, or perpetuate it. Your assistance is intended only for a legitimate purpose, even if it leads to a disbeliever being kept alive and continuing to exist, and consequently to his disbelief continuing. But since that is not what you intend, you are not blameworthy for it. Indeed, this may be something that is obligatory to do. I will give examples of this: giving food to a disbeliever—for it is authentically reported in al-Bukhari that the Companions were instructed to gather food for the polytheist woman who owned the waterskin; and his statement to his disbelieving relatives is established: "You have ties of kinship with me, and I shall maintain them as they should be maintained"; and it is established that the Companions disliked giving money to their relatives among the polytheists, so Allah revealed: {You are not responsible for their guidance, but Allah guides whom He wills}. Likewise, the People of the Book are allowed to remain in exchange for the jizya, and thus their disbelief is allowed to remain; and it is also permissible to release a polytheist as a favor, as Allah, Exalted is He, says: {Then either a favor afterward}; and the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) released Thumama ibn Uthal, who was a leader among Banu Hanifa. There is no doubt that he ruled them by other than the Sharia, and with this


[Page 274]

Perhaps this ruling by other than what Allah has revealed would have continued. Likewise, it is established in al-Bukhari that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said regarding the captives of Badr: "If al-Mut'im ibn Adi were alive and then spoke to me about these foul men, I would release them to him." Here, the Prophet would have released some of the leading men of Quraysh, and there is no doubt that this could have led to their disbelief continuing. Likewise, giving to those whose hearts are to be reconciled, according to the view that polytheists who are hoped to enter Islam may be given from the zakat; and likewise, the Prophet's supplication to Allah that He lift the drought from Quraysh, when they were polytheists at war with him, as related in al-Bukhari. The drought was lifted, and he had sought Allah's help in this matter.
There is no doubt that they continued in their disbelief thereafter, and in their rule by disbelief and their barring people from the way of Allah, until Allah granted the conquest of Mecca afterward. Thus, retaining these people meant retaining their disbelief. But this was certainly not what the Messenger of Allah intended by his supplication for them. Likewise, his statement, peace be upon him, that we would make a truce with the Romans, and his approval of that; there is no doubt that this truce meant the Romans remained in power and their disbelief continued. Likewise, the Treaty of Hudaybiyyah undoubtedly entailed the disbelievers' rule in Mecca being retained and their not being confronted in battle. But since there was a greater benefit, the truce was given precedence. Likewise, the Muslims rejoiced at the Romans' victory over the Persians. There is no doubt that this strengthened the Romans' rule based on disbelief. But since this rule was less harmful to Islam than that of others, they rejoiced in it. No one would say that they rejoiced in disbelief; rather, they rejoiced that the Persians' disbelief would be diminished as a result of their defeat.
(The question of a Muslim assisting one disbeliever against another in combat) I will address here an issue: a Muslim assisting a disbeliever in fighting another disbeliever. In this issue, the Muslim fights alongside a disbeliever against another disbeliever in order to achieve…


[Page 275]

for some interest or to avert some necessity. The issue under discussion is more serious than the issue we are examining, because in the matter of fighting, the Muslim exposes himself to death, the loss of a limb, or severe hardship. People fight only so that their rule will prevail and their word will be supreme. There is no doubt that if a Muslim assists a disbeliever in fighting, he thereby helps him consolidate his rule and, consequently, govern by other than what Allah has revealed.
But the Muslim does not, of course, intend that. Rather, he may have interests in doing so, such as financial gain, inflicting a blow on a more wicked enemy, or warding off harm from himself—for example, if he is a prisoner among them and fights alongside them so that they will release him. I am not here seeking to favor one opinion on this issue; I cite it only to show that even regarding the lowest degree of assistance to a disbeliever—such as a man helping one disbeliever against another solely for financial gain—none of the scholars held that this act constitutes disbelief, even though its consequence is to empower a disbelieving ruler and his rule by other than what Allah has revealed. But since the issue concerns warding off disbelief by means of disbelief, one of which will inevitably occur, Allah willing, and since the Muslim never intends that the victorious disbeliever should govern by other than what Allah has revealed, because in either case the ... I have not found any scholar who declared him a disbeliever. Rather, some of them permitted him to do so merely in hope of financial gain, despite the fact that in this matter the Muslim faces the risk of exposing himself to danger to a degree not present when voting for the lesser of two harms in order to avert the greater harm. This risk to oneself was the sole reason that led a group of jurists to prohibit assisting them in return for money alone, rather than out of necessity. There is undoubtedly no such risk to oneself in voting for the lesser harm, which makes applying their opinion to the issue of voting mean that there is nothing wrong with it if it is done to secure an interest and ward off harm from the Muslims.


[Page 276]

I shall now present these views, drawing on a writer named Abd al-Aziz al-Tamimi.
First, that the upper hand belongs to the unbelievers, with the Muslim subordinate to them. Is it permissible for a Muslim to fight alongside them against other unbelievers for the sake of money or the like?
Defining the point of disagreement.
The scholars differed over whether it is permissible to join their army and fight alongside them against other unbelievers for worldly gains, such as seeking money and the like. They held four different views on this matter:
The first view: Fighting alongside them is prohibited; this is the position of Malik.
It is stated in al-Mudawwana (1/518):
"I said: What is your view if a group of Muslims are captives in the land of polytheism, or are merchants, and the ruler of that land seeks their help against a group of polytheists who have opposed him, whether they are from among the people of his realm or from outside it? Do you think they should fight alongside him, or not?
He said: I heard Malik say concerning captives who are in the lands of the polytheists, and whom the king calls upon to help him fight his enemy, and who are brought to the lands of the Muslims?
He said: Malik said: "I do not think they should fight over this, nor is it lawful for them to shed their blood over anything like that. Malik said: People are fought only so that they may be brought from polytheism into Islam. As for fighting the unbelievers to bring them from one form of unbelief to another and shedding their blood for that, this is not something that should be done, and it is not fitting for a Muslim to shed his blood over this."
It appears in "Al-Nawadir wa al-Ziyadat ala ma fi al-Mudawwana min ghayriha min al-Ummahat" (14/551):
"If the polytheists sought their help in fighting an enemy of theirs, they should not assist them, unless some of the people of polytheism attack them and take them captive, and the Muslims fear that this will happen to them. In that case, there is no harm in fighting alongside those among whom they are." Here it is worth noting that the Maliki school held it permissible to fight alongside one unbeliever against another if they feared being taken captive. It is also worth noting that, in the matter of voting for the lesser of two evils, Muslims often resort to it out of fear of capture and imprisonment; this case is therefore included here all the more so.


[Page 277]

The second view: Fighting in their ranks is disliked. This is the position of the Hanafis, the Shafi'is, and others.
Muhammad ibn al-Hasan said: "If the rule of the people of polytheism prevails, Muslims should not fight alongside the people of polytheism, unless they fear for their own lives, in which case they defend themselves."(42) Al-Siyar al-Saghir
It appears in "Ikhtilaf al-Ulama by al-Tahawi, abridged by al-Jassas" (3/428): "On seeking assistance from the polytheists"
Our companions said: There is no harm in seeking the assistance of polytheists to fight polytheists if the rule of Islam prevails and is applied to them. Seeking their assistance is only disliked when the rule of polytheism is manifest. This is the view of al-Shafi'i.
Al-Awza'i said: "I do not disapprove of seeking their help."
Malik said: "I do not think they should seek their help unless they are servants."
Abu Ja'far said: Allah, Glorified is He, said: {Do not take as confidants anyone other than yourselves}
And He said: {And I would not take the misguided as helpers.} Malik ibn Anas narrated from al-Fudayl ibn Abi Abd Allah, from Abd Allah ibn Niyar al-Aslami, from Urwa ibn al-Zubayr, from Aisha, who said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) set out before Badr. When he was at Harrat al-Wabara, a man came to him and said, "I have come to follow you and fight alongside you." The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, "Do you believe in Allah and His Messenger?" He replied, "No." He said, "Then go back, for we will not seek help from a polytheist." The man came back to him three times, and the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said the same to him each time. The man said that he went back, then caught up with him at al-Bayda. The Prophet said to him what he had said the first time: "Do you believe in Allah and His Messenger?" He replied, "Yes." The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, "Then set out."
Abu Ja'far said: This indicates that he was one of the idol worshippers, because he called him to believe in Allah, whereas the People of the Book believe in Allah.
It is narrated from Abu Humayd al-Saidi that he said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) went out on the day of Uhud and saw a heavily armed troop. He asked, "Who are these?" They replied, "The Banu Qaynuqa, who are the clan of Abdullah ibn


[Page 278]

Salam said, "Become Muslims," but they refused. He said to them, "Then go back, for I do not seek the help of polytheists against polytheists."
This indicates that the People of the Book and the worshippers of idols are alike in that their assistance may not be sought.
It has been narrated that when the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) learned that Abu Sufyan had assembled men to march against him on the day of Uhud, he went to Banu al-Nadir, who were Jews, and said to them, "Either fight alongside us or lend us weapons."
Abu Ja'far said: It is possible that this was due to a necessity that had arisen.
It has been narrated that Safwan ibn Umayya attended Hunayn with the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) while he was a polytheist.
Malik said, "This was not by the Prophet's command."))
[44] It appears in al-Umm, "Al-Umm by al-Shafi'i" (4/256):
"If a group of Muslims were captured and the polytheists sought their help against other polytheists like themselves, so that they might fight them, it has been said that they should fight them. It has also been said that al-Zubayr and some of his companions fought in Abyssinia as polytheists against polytheists. Those who hold this view say: What is forbidden about fighting alongside them, when the blood and property of those they fight are lawful on account of their polytheism? If someone were to say that fighting alongside them is forbidden for several reasons, one of them would be that when Muslims prevail over polytheists and take spoils, one fifth is due to those entitled to the fifth, who are dispersed across different lands. In this case, there is no way for the fifth from the spoils to reach those entitled to it, so that it can be delivered to the imam for distribution. Also, if they fight the People of the Book and they pay the jizya, they are obliged to spare their lives. If they pay the jizya, he would be unable to prevent them from sparing their lives. This would be a valid position. If they are not compelled to fight them, it would be preferable to me that they not fight. We do not know that the report about al-Zubayr is established; and even if it were established, the Negus was a Muslim: he had believed in the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), and the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) prayed [over him]." Here al-Shafi'i is observed to have transmitted the disagreement in cases where there is no necessity to fight alongside a disbeliever against another disbeliever. Those who held that it is permissible said that the basic rule is that fighting these polytheists is permissible, and that their property is lawful; therefore, whoever fights alongside a polytheist against another polytheist has done something permissible. Those who held that it is impermissible argued that the Muslims would be unable to administer the war, distribute the spoils according to the rules of the Sharia, or refrain from fighting those who paid the jizya, among other things, because they would be under the domination of the disbelievers. Then he concluded.


[Page 279]

Al-Shafi'i held that fighting is disliked except in a case of coercion, in which it is permissible. Therefore, the issue of voting for the lesser harm in cases of necessity falls under this ruling all the more so.

The third position: suspension of judgment. This is the position of Ahmad ibn Hanbal. In Masa'il al-Imam Ahmad, narrated by Abu Dawud, in al-Jami' li-Ulum al-Imam Ahmad: al-Fiqh (8/569):

"The captive is asked to fight on the enemy's side in return."

Abu Dawud said: I said to Ahmad: If an enemy were to descend upon the people of Constantinople, and the king said to the captives, 'Go out and fight, and I will give you such-and-such'?

He said: If he tells them, 'I will set you free,' then there is no harm, in the hope that he will escape.

I said: What if he says, 'I will give you something and treat you well'?

He said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, "Whoever fights so that the word of Allah may be supreme" (1). I do not know.

Masa'il Abi Dawud (1590)

Nu'aym ibn Na'im narrated from him: I asked Ahmad about a captive in the hands of the enemy, when an enemy of theirs came and fought alongside them. Should he fight with them?

He said: If he fears for his life, or they tell him, 'If you fight with us, we will set you free,' then he should fight with them.

I said: What if he does not fear for his life, and they have not told him, 'We will set you free'?

He said: I have reservations about it.

Tabaqat al-Hanabila, 2/496–497

It is noteworthy that Imam Ahmad permitted him to fight alongside them if they would set him free, despite the fact that fighting alongside them would generally entail enabling the rule of disbelief, and would also involve shedding Muslim blood. But since there was a benefit for him in securing his release from captivity, and this was also a case of coercion, while fighting and killing an original non-Muslim combatant is in principle permissible, he permitted him to do so. The question here is


[Page 280]

Is it not less harmful to help a disbeliever by voting for him, in order to free Muslim prisoners or prevent them from being killed and imprisoned? But in this matter, people must be made aware that voting for him is not so that he may rule by what Allah has revealed. Rather, the reason for voting is to reduce harm to Muslims, free their prisoners, and stop them from being killed. This is so that people do not think that such voting is inherently permissible under Islamic law. If it is said that someone might see it and think the act is permissible, I say: the same danger exists in the case of someone who fought in the army of the polytheists, yet most scholars permitted him to ransom himself in this way. They likewise permitted uttering explicit words of disbelief under coercion. Therefore, people must be alerted that this vote is only to remove a state of necessity, while also explaining Allah's ruling on these matters. And Allah knows best.
Abu Talib transmitted from him, concerning a captive for whom they had not stipulated release and who did not fear them: he must not fight alongside them without him.
"Al-Furu'" 6/206
(1) Narrated by Imam Ahmad 4/392, al-Bukhari (123), and Muslim (1904), from the hadith of Abu Musa al-Ash'ari.
Ibn Muflih said: "We prohibit assisting them against their enemy, except out of fear. Ahmad suspended judgment regarding a captive for whom they had not stipulated release and whom he did not fear. Abu Talib transmitted: 'He must not fight alongside them without him.'"(46) Al-Furu (10/248).
.
.
The fourth opinion: It is permissible. It is stated in "The Differences of the Scholars by al-Tahawi—abridged by al-Jassas" (3/454):
"On a Muslim who has been granted safe-conduct fighting alongside the polytheists

Our companions said: He should not fight alongside the people of polytheism, because the ruling of polytheism is the one that prevails. This is the view of Malik.


[Page 281]

Al-Thawri said, "They may fight alongside them" (note here that al-Thawri permitted this without qualification).
Al-Awzai said, "They may fight only if they stipulate that, should they prevail, they will return them to the Abode of Islam."
Al-Shafi'i has two opinions.
Abu Ja'far said: "The fighting to which their allegiance has led them is fighting under the banner of unbelief. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said, 'I disavow every Muslim who is with a polytheist, such that their two fires are not visible to one another.'
It is also narrated that a polytheist man came to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) to pledge allegiance to him and fight alongside him while still a polytheist. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said to him, 'Go back, for we will not seek help from a polytheist.'" 2
"Furthermore, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) used to prescribe conditions for fighting. One of them was calling them to Islam. Then he said, 'If they refuse, call on them to pay the jizya. If they agree, accept it from them; but if they refuse, fight them.'
If it is said that it is narrated that al-Zubayr fought in Abyssinia alongside the Negus against another enemy,
the response is that the Negus was a Muslim. Moreover, no fighting is mentioned in that report; rather, he went to learn their news, then informed the Emigrants."
The evidence of al-Tahawi's statement that aiding an unbeliever against another unbeliever falls under the hadith, "I disavow every Muslim who resides among polytheists," has also been discussed as follows:
First point: This hadith is weak, and the leading scholars deem it defective on account of its being mursal, including Ahmad, al-Bukhari [51], Abu Hatim [52], al-Daraqutni [53], al-Bayhaqi [54], and others. However, the researcher considers this hadith authentic,
and the leading scholars have continued to cite it and similar reports as evidence for the prohibition on residing among unbelievers.
Second point: Fighting alongside them does not entail residing among them. Armies are for the most part on the move, and someone who travels to them and fights alongside them against other unbelievers is not thereby a resident. Scholars have disagreed over the length of time that, if spent there or intended to be spent there, makes a person a resident: is it four days, fifteen days, twenty days, or is it determined by custom? And there is no…


[Page 282]

No one is declared an unbeliever when there is a possibility [of another interpretation]. The general view is that a traveler who has not resolved to settle somewhere continues to shorten his prayers indefinitely and is therefore not considered a resident. Thus, many cases are not covered by the prohibition in the hadith.
The third argument: The hadith concerns someone who chooses to reside among the polytheists; it does not apply to someone who goes to them out of fear of oppression or the like, or to someone unable to leave because of citizenship issues or similar circumstances. Someone excused for residing there is not covered by the prohibition on fighting alongside the polytheists in such a situation; that is, he is permitted to reside among them for the reasons we have mentioned. If, after that, he assists them, joins their army, and fights alongside them for the sake of money, the hadith prohibiting this does not apply to him!
The fourth argument: The early generations—of whom no innovation is known—did not disagree that choosing to reside among the polytheists is not disbelief. This is demonstrated by their consensus that inheritance passed between the Emigrant and the non-Emigrant. It is well known that an unbeliever does not inherit from a Muslim. Al-Harith al-Muhasibi(56) said: "They agreed that the first verse was abrogated, and that Allah—glorious is His praise—established inheritance among the believers on the basis of kinship, not emigration."(57) He then said: "They used to inherit from one another on the basis of Islam and emigration: a man would embrace Islam but not emigrate, and so he would not inherit from his brother. This was abrogated by: 'And those related by blood are closer to one another in the Book of Allah than the believers and the emigrants.'"(58) Allah made it obligatory to support those who had not emigrated if they sought our help in religion. He, Exalted is He, said: "And those who have believed but have not emigrated—you have no duty of protection toward them until they emigrate. But if they seek your help in religion, then you must help them, except against a people with whom you have a treaty. And Allah sees all that you do."
.
The fifth argument: Scholars have explained this hadith in several ways that do not entail that someone who resides among the polytheists is an unbeliever. These are as follows:
The first interpretation: It means that he is absolved of liability for his blood-money and of retaliation against the person who killed him. Al-Shafi'i said: "If this is established, I think the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) gave what he gave to them voluntarily, and informed them that he was absolved of responsibility for every Muslim among the polytheists—Allah knows best—in the land of polytheism, so that they would know that no blood-money or retaliation was due to them."(59) Ibn Hazm said: "By doing so, he has wronged himself. If someone who does not know that he is a Muslim kills him, there is no retaliation and no blood-money; only expiation is due, according to the explicit text of the Quran."(60)
The second interpretation: It is possible that the hadith means disavowal of their guardianship, which concerns inheritance. This is of the same kind as the meaning of His saying, Exalted is He: "And those who have believed but have not emigrated—you have no duty of protection toward them until they emigrate." The scholars unanimously agreed that this refers to inheritance, but the ruling was abrogated.(61)


[Page 283]

The third interpretation: He is absolved of protecting the blood of any Muslim who chooses to live among the polytheists, because that person has brought harm upon himself, or because rulings follow the territory, as is the position of the Hanafis. Al-Jassas said: "His blood has no value, like that of the people of war who have no covenant of protection."(62)
This is open to objection: Allah made it obligatory to support them if they seek help in matters of religion. He, Exalted is He, says: {Those who believe but have not emigrated—you have no duty of protection toward them until they emigrate. But if they seek your help in religion, then you must help them, except against a people with whom you have a treaty. And Allah sees all that you do.} Supporting them presupposes that their Islam is outwardly established!
The fourth interpretation: It refers to disavowal of his residence and conduct, not disavowal of the person who resides there himself. This is like the saying, "Whoever bears arms against us is not one of us," and "Whoever cheats us is not one of us," and similar sayings.
The fifth interpretation: This applied when emigration was obligatory, but it was abrogated after the conquest of Mecca. This was the view chosen by al-Kiya al-Harrasi,(63) al-Razi,(64) and others.
The sixth interpretation: It refers to someone who lives in the territory of the polytheists and is unable to manifest any aspect of his religion, or fears that he will be tempted away from it there. He is absolved of remaining among them, and must emigrate. If he is safe, then residing in their territory is disliked. Al-Mawardi alluded to this,(65) and al-Shafi'i said: "The Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) indicates that the obligation to emigrate applies to those able to do so only when they are being persecuted for their religion in the land from which emigration will save them. For the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) permitted some people in Mecca to remain there after accepting Islam, among them al-Abbas ibn Abd al-Muttalib and others, provided they did not fear persecution. He would instruct his armies to tell those who accepted Islam: 'If you emigrate, you will have what the emigrants have; but if you remain, you will be like the Bedouin Muslims.' He offered them this choice only in matters that were permissible for them."(66)
The seventh interpretation: He disavowed those who joined the unbelievers to fight against the Muslims. Ibn Hazm said: "Whoever voluntarily joins the territory of unbelief and war, fighting against the Muslims in his vicinity, has by this act become an apostate and is subject to all the rulings applicable to an apostate: he must be killed when he can be apprehended, his property may be taken, his marriage is dissolved, and so on. For the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) did not disavow a Muslim."(67)
Given these various possibilities, it cannot be said that someone who assists a polytheist against another polytheist in fighting is covered by this hadith. Consequently, neither is the voter for the lesser harm covered by it, since such a person is generally unable to emigrate. And if he is able, where is he to emigrate, when rule by other than what Allah has revealed has become widespread throughout the world, except in a few places? And Allah knows best.


[Page 284]

It appears in al-Shafi'i's al-Umm (4/256). It appears in Sharh al-Siyar al-Kabir (p. 1422):
[CHAPTER ON SEEKING THE AID OF POLYTHEISTS AND POLYTHEISTS SEEKING THE AID OF MUSLIMS]
.
2754 - Then he mentioned the hadith of al-Zubayr (may Allah be pleased with him), when he was with the Negus and an enemy attacked him. On that day, al-Zubayr fought well alongside the Negus, and thereby attained a good standing with him. On the apparent meaning of this hadith, those who permit Muslims to fight alongside polytheists under their banner rely as evidence. But, in our view, this can be interpreted in two ways. First, the Negus was Muslim at that time, as has been reported, and so al-Zubayr considered it permissible to fight alongside him.
Second, the Muslims had no refuge at that time other than him, according to what has been reported from Umm Salama (may Allah be pleased with her). She said: "When we had settled in the land of Abyssinia, we were in the best of homes, with the best of neighbors, worshipping our Lord, until an enemy of the Negus marched against him. No great misfortune from him had ever befallen us. We said, 'If he prevails over the Negus, he will not recognize our rights as the Negus did.' So we devoted ourselves to supplication, asking Allah to grant the Negus victory. Then we said, 'Who will go and find out what is happening with the people?' Al-Zubayr ibn al-Awwam said, 'I will.' He inflated a waterskin, mounted it, and crossed the river. He met the people, and al-Zubayr joined them. We continued to devote ourselves to supplication until al-Zubayr appeared on the Nile, waving his garment and calling, 'Rejoice! Allah, Exalted is He, has granted the Negus victory, established him in the land, and destroyed his enemy.'" She said, "So we remained with the best of neighbors." This hadith makes clear the soundness of the interpretation we have given. And Allah knows best.

.
In sum, after researching and examining the statements of the early generations, I have found no views other than these four. None of them declares an unbeliever anyone who joined their army and fought alongside them against other unbelievers for money or the like;


[Page 285]

The third issue: When the upper hand belongs to the unbelievers and the Muslim is subject to them, is it permissible for a Muslim prisoner—who does not fear that he will be led astray while in captivity—to fight alongside them against other unbelievers in order to secure his release?
The scholars differed on this issue, holding two views:
The first view: It is forbidden to fight alongside them. This is the position of Abu Hanifa and his companions, the position of Malik, and the apparent position of al-Shafi'i's school.
Muhammad ibn al-Hasan said: "If the authority of the people of polytheism is dominant, Muslims should not fight alongside the people of polytheism, unless they fear for their own lives, in which case they may defend themselves."(86) Al-Siyar al-Saghir (249). In Al-Siyar al-Saghir, ed. Khadduri (p. 249), it says:
"I asked him about Muslims seeking the help of the people of polytheism against the people of war: Do you see any harm in that, and do they have a share of the spoils? He said: 'There is no harm in seeking their help if authority belongs to the people of Islam and they are the dominant and prevailing power. In that case, there is no harm in seeking the help of the people of polytheism. But if the authority of the people of polytheism is dominant, Muslims should not fight alongside the people of polytheism, unless they fear for their own lives, in which case they may defend themselves.' He said: 'The people of polytheism have no share, but they may be given a discretionary portion.'"
It says in Sharh al-Siyar al-Kabir: "If the people of war say to prisoners in their custody, 'Fight alongside us against our enemy, the polytheists,' and the prisoners do not fear for their lives if they refuse, they should not fight alongside them.
"For this fighting involves manifesting polytheism, and the fighter puts his life at risk. There is no dispensation for this except with the intention of strengthening the religion or defending himself. If they fear those others for their lives, there is no harm in their fighting them, because they are now warding off the danger of being killed.
"For they are safe from those who hold them in their custody, but they would not be safe from the others if they fell into their hands. It is therefore permissible for them to fight in self-defense.
"2973. If they say, 'Fight alongside us against our enemy, the polytheists, or we will kill you,' there is no harm in their fighting to ward them off."


[Page 286]

Because they are now warding off the worst kind of killing from themselves, and killing those polytheists is lawful for them. There is nothing wrong with undertaking something lawful when necessity is established because of coercion; indeed, it may even be obligatory, as in eating carrion and drinking wine ... If they were to say to the prisoners, "Fight with us against our enemy from among another people at war, on condition that we release you when our war is over," and they believed that they were sincere, there would be nothing wrong with their fighting alongside them.
Because by doing so they are warding off captivity from themselves. This is no less permissible than when they fear for their lives at the hands of those polytheists: just as they may undertake it in that case, so too may they do so here.
