If it is asked: How can this be permissible for them when it strengthens them against the Muslims? For if they defeat their enemy and feel secure from them, they will turn to fighting the Muslims. They may also take their horses and weapons and use them to strengthen themselves against the Muslims.
We say: That is speculative, whereas the deliverance they gain now from captivity at the hands of the polytheists by fighting alongside them is certain, so this consideration takes precedence.
Do you not see that if they asked the Muslim leader to ransom them in exchange for an equal number of polytheists, or for horses and weapons, it would be permissible for him to do so in order to free them from captivity, even if those they received in exchange would use what they took to strengthen themselves against the Muslims? If they were to say, "Fight with us against our enemy from among the polytheists, on condition that we let you remain in our land and do not allow you to return to your families," then they should not fight alongside them ... For if they are safe from harm to their lives or limbs at their hands, there is no difference between their being detained in their land and their being held in their prisons. In either case, they suffer the distress of being cut off from their families and from their fellow believers. So they should not fight to uphold the rule of polytheism when they gain no evident benefit from doing so.
- If they are in distress and affliction, fearing for their lives, there is nothing wrong with their fighting alongside the polytheists if they say, "We will get you out of this."
For they have a valid purpose in this fighting: to ward off the affliction and distress that have befallen them." So consider, may Allah have mercy on you: despite the Hanafi school's strictness in prohibiting this, they nevertheless permitted supporting a disbeliever against another disbeliever, even at the cost of blood and limbs, in order to ward off affliction and harm from themselves. How many afflictions and hardships have befallen Muslims at the hands of unjust rulers, such that they needed only to vote for the lesser harm, without fighting. And Allah knows best.


[Page 287]

The second view: Fighting in their ranks is permissible in order to free oneself from captivity. This is the view of al-Awzai, al-Thawri, and Ahmad ibn Hanbal.
Al-Awzai said: "They may fight only if they stipulate that, should they prevail, they will return them to the land of Islam."(95) Mukhtasar Ikhtilaf al-Ulama (3/454)
Al-Thawri said: "They may fight alongside them."(96) Mukhtasar Ikhtilaf al-Ulama (3/454)
Abu Dawud said: "I said to Ahmad: If an enemy were to attack the people of Constantinople, and the king said to the captives, 'Go out and fight, and I will give you such-and-such,' what then? He replied: If he tells them, 'I will set you free,' then there is no harm in it, in the hope that they will escape."(97) Masa'il al-Imam Ahmad, narrated by Abu Dawud al-Sijistani (332)
.

Preferred view: It appears to the researcher that the second view is stronger, and that there is no evidence prohibiting fighting alongside them in order to secure one's release from captivity. Accordingly, fighting alongside them in this case does not constitute allegiance to them. And Allah knows best.

It says in al-Muhalla bi al-Athar (11/356):
If we are able to set the unbelievers among the people of war against one another, so that they fight each other, and Muslims enter among them and use them as a means to harm others, that is good.
The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Allah supports this religion through people who have no share [in the Hereafter]," as was narrated to us by Abd Allah ibn Rabi, who said: Muhammad ibn Muawiya narrated to us, who said: Ahmad ibn Shuaib informed me, who said: Imran ibn Bakkar ibn Rashid narrated to us, who said: Abu al-Yaman narrated to us, who said: Shuaib—namely, Ibn Abi Hamza—narrated to us, from al-Zuhri, who said: Said ibn al-Musayyib informed me, who said: Abu Hurayra narrated to us that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Allah indeed supports this religion through a wicked man."

It is noteworthy that Ibn Hazm's view is that it is permissible to enter the ranks of the polytheists in order to inflict harm on another polytheist, and this is very similar to our present issue.
(Mention of some of the evidence for this issue)


[Page 288]

It is related in the Musnad of Ahmad: Muhammad ibn Mus'ab—namely, al-Qarqasani—narrated to us; he said: al-Awza'i narrated to us, from Hassan ibn Atiyya, from Khalid ibn Ma'dan, from Jubayr ibn Nufayr, from Dhu Mikhmar, from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), who said: "You will make a secure peace with the Romans, and you and they will campaign against an enemy behind them. You will return safely and take spoils. Then you will encamp at a meadow with hillocks, and a man (1) from among the Romans will stand up, raise the cross, and say, 'Behold, the cross has prevailed!' A man from among the Muslims will stand up and kill him. At that point the Romans will break their treaty, and the great battles will begin. They will gather (2) against you, and come to you with eighty banners, each banner accompanied by ten (3)
There is no doubt that making peace with the Romans strengthens their unbelieving rule, and fighting their enemy likewise empowers them. But all these harms are merely hypothetical and are not intended by the Muslims; rather, what they intend is to ward off the harm of this other, greater enemy. This is very close to the issue before us.
Al-Sindi said: His words, "secure," mean "possessing security"; the form is one of attribution, or "secure" is used in a figurative attribution.
His statement, "Then you will fight them": that is, you and they, as in the following narration.
His statement, "an enemy," in the accusative; that is, you will unite to fight the enemy, on account of the peace treaty.
His statement, "and you will be safe," means safety.
His statement, "in a pasture": the place where animals graze.
His statement, "mounds," with two dammas and a light l: the plural of "mound," with a fatha, meaning anything gathered on the ground, whether soil or sand.
His statement, "the cross prevailed," means the religion of the Christians, with the aim of nullifying the truce, or merely out of pride and to provoke the Muslims to anger. And Allah, Exalted is He, knows best.
In (M): "A man then stands up to him," with the addition "to him."
(Another proof) is that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said regarding the captives of Badr: "If al-Mut'im ibn Adi were alive and spoke to me about these foul men, I would release them to him." Narrated by al-Bukhari. There is no doubt that the Prophet's releasing seventy polytheist captives strengthened them, but this was certainly not what the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) intended. What seems apparent—and Allah knows best—is that he (peace and blessings be upon him) wanted to repay al-Mut'im ibn Adi for his kindness.
(Another proof)
Ibn Mas'ud reported: Quraysh were slow to embrace Islam. "The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) prayed against them, and they were struck by a drought that lasted until they perished in it, eating carrion and bones." Abu Sufyan came to him and said, "Muhammad, you came commanding the maintenance of kinship ties, while your people have perished. Pray to Allah." He recited: {So watch for the Day when there comes


[Page 289]

The sky with a manifest smoke} [al-Dukhan: 10]. Then they returned to their disbelief. This is what Allah, Exalted is He, says: {On the day We seize with the greatest seizure, We will indeed take vengeance} [al-Dukhan: 16]—the day of Badr. Abu Abd Allah said: Asbat added, from Mansur: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) prayed, and they were given rain. It poured over them for seven days, and the people complained of the excessive rain. He said, "O Allah, let it rain around us, not upon us." The cloud then moved away from above him, and the people around them received rain." There is no doubt that the lifting of drought from the unbelievers strengthened their position, but this was not what the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) intended. Thus, not every act that results in strengthening the position of an unbeliever is itself disbelief, especially when its purpose is to lessen another form of disbelief and remove harm from the Muslims.

(Another proof)
Imam al-Tirmidhi, may Allah have mercy on him, said (vol. 9, p. 51): Al-Husayn ibn Hurayth narrated to us; Muawiya ibn Amr informed us, from Abu Ishaq al-Fazari, from Sufyan, from Habib ibn Abi Amra, from Said ibn Jubayr, from Ibn Abbas, concerning the words of Allah, Exalted is He: {Alif Lam Mim. The Romans have been defeated in the nearest land} (1). He said: They were defeated, and then they would be victorious. The polytheists wanted the Persians to prevail over the Romans, because both they and the Persians worshipped idols, while the Muslims wanted the Romans to prevail over the Persians, because the Romans were People of the Book. They mentioned this to Abu Bakr, and Abu Bakr mentioned it to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him and his family). He said, "Indeed, they will be victorious." Abu Bakr then told them, and they said, "Set a term between us and you: if we prevail, we will have such-and-such, and if you prevail, you will have such-and-such." So he set a term of five years, but they did not prevail. They mentioned this to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him and his family), and


[Page 290]

...and blessings be upon him), and said, "Would that you had set it at less than"—I think he said—"ten." Sa'id said, "A few means less than ten." He then said, "After that, the Romans prevailed." This is what Allah, Exalted is He, says: {Alif Lam Mim. The Romans have been defeated} up to His words {And on that day the believers will rejoice in Allah's victory}. Sufyan said: I heard that they prevailed over them on the day of Badr.
This is a good, rare hadith. We know it only through the hadith of Sufyan al-Thawri, from Habib ibn Abi Amra.)) The apparent indication of this hadith is that rejoicing at the victory of a disbeliever is not disbelief. There is no doubt that his victory will lead to the establishment of his unbelieving rule on earth. But since the Romans were less unbelieving than the Persians, the Muslims rejoiced at the reduction of disbelief in the world, and wished for the Romans to prevail, not because of their disbelief, but because their disbelief was less than that of the Persians. Here arises the question of weighing good deeds against bad deeds. Allah knows best.
(Some matters in which the person involved has an interpretation excusing him from being declared a disbeliever)
Drawn from al-Jami and al-Thalathiniyya. My purpose in presenting these matters is only to show that the person involved has an interpretation that prevents him from being declared a disbeliever, even if, in my view, some of them involve sin. I quote here the statements of those who hold these views.
(Declaring someone a disbeliever on account of what his words necessarily entail and lead to)
The author of al-Thalathiniyya says: "Ibn Hazm, may Allah have mercy on him, says: 'As for declaring people disbelievers on account of what their statements lead to, that is an error, because it attributes a lie to one's opponent and makes him say what he did not say. Even if it necessarily follows from his position, nothing has been established beyond contradiction, and contradiction is not disbelief. Indeed, he has done well, since he has avoided disbelief ...'" He continued: "Thus it is established that he is not declared a disbeliever.


[Page 291]

anyone except by his own words and the explicit statement of his belief. It does no one any good to express his belief in words that make its ugliness seem attractive; rather, what is judged is only what his words entail." End quote. Al-Fasl (3/294).
Shaykh al-Islam Ibn Taymiyya said in Majmu al-Fatawa (29/25–26):
"A person's words have two kinds of implications:
The first is the implication of his words that is true. He must adhere to this, for what truth entails is itself true, and it may be attributed to him if it is known that he would not refuse to adhere to it once it became clear to him.
The second is the implication of his words that is not (1) true. He is not required to adhere to this, since at most it means that he has contradicted himself. It is established that contradiction occurs in every scholar other than the Prophets. Then, if it is known that he would adhere to it once it became clear to him, it may be attributed to him; otherwise, it is not permissible to attribute to him a statement that he would not adhere to if its error became clear to him, since he has said what entails it without realizing that the statement is erroneous, and it does not (2) bind him.
This distinction regarding people's disagreement over the implications of a school of thought—whether they are part of that school or not—is better than stating either position without qualification. Whatever implications the speaker accepts once they have been made clear to him are his words; whatever he does not accept is not his words, even if he is inconsistent." End quote.
As for a Muslim's merely seeking, in times of necessity, the protection or safe-conduct of a disbeliever, or asking him to redress a wrong or support him against another disbeliever, or to repel an aggressive wrongdoer whom nothing deters or frightens,


[Page 292]

Except for that, in the absence of authority and power for the law of Allah, it is not, in the first place, a matter of seeking judgment from anything.

We have already presented the Messenger of Allah's praise for the Alliance of al-Fudul, even though it was an alliance formed in the pre-Islamic era, established by unbelievers. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) witnessed it as a boy and said, "I would not like to have red camels in exchange for breaking it." According to another narration, "If I were invited to it in Islam, I would respond."

Its form was that of a tribal gathering in the pre-Islamic era, whose members pledged to aid the distressed, support the wronged, and restore people's rights to them through their collective strength and power. This was at a time when Islam had neither authority nor a state, before the Prophet's mission, and amid the benighted pre-Islamic age. It was therefore an institution of the pre-Islamic era. The Prophet's praise for that alliance thus entails that there is no blame on a vulnerable person who turns to it, or to something like it among the unbelievers who are able to ward off injustice from him, or protect his honor or property, or secure some of his rights through their strength, power, and authority, against other oppressive unbelievers, in the absence of authority for the Sharia. Were there even the slightest blame in this, let alone prohibition or unbelief, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) would have made it clear when praising the alliance. For he (peace and blessings be upon him) was the most God-fearing and scrupulous of the community, and never left any evil unaddressed without warning them against it.


[Page 293]

For this reason, Abraham (peace be upon him), who defied his people and did not fear their blazing fire at one time, despite being alone and without equal, was in another situation considered to be, along with his wife, vulnerable and compelled to comply with the request of the unbeliever when he summoned Sarah. Indeed, al-Bukhari included this in the Book of Coercion. I mean by this the hadith of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him): "Abraham migrated with Sarah. He entered a village in which there was a king—or one of the tyrants—and he sent for him, saying, 'Send her to me.' So he sent her. She stood before him, then began to perform ablution and pray, saying, 'O Allah, if I have believed in You and in Your Messenger, then do not give the unbeliever power over me.' Then he was seized and kicked with his leg." Al-Bukhari gave it the chapter heading in the Book of Coercion: "Chapter: If a woman is coerced into fornication, there is no prescribed punishment upon her."

He thus regarded this as coercion, and there was no blame on Abraham or his wife for being alone with that unbeliever, since she had been coerced, as the hadith scholar Ibn Hajar related among the statements of the scholars. They were not blamed or reproached for not fleeing, nor did anyone require them to do so. They knew best their circumstances and situation in that place.

Shaykh Muhammad ibn Ibrahim Al al-Shaykh said: "As for matters settled by tribal judges, if this was done by way of reconciliation and the reconciliation did not entail permitting something forbidden or forbidding something lawful, then the reconciliation is valid. But if it was done by way of judgment, then it is invalid, because what is known of tribal shaykhs is their ignorance and lack of knowledge of the rulings of the Sharia. Resorting to them for judgment is therefore a form of resorting to the judgment of the ...


[Page 294]

(the taghut) End quote. From the legal opinions and letters of Shaykh Muhammad ibn Ibrahim (12/292).
He said regarding defending oneself in courts that rule by other than what Allah has revealed: "Among the things that may properly be cited as evidence in this context is the account of Ja'far and his fellow emigrants to Abyssinia appearing before the Negus before he embraced Islam, and defending themselves as best they could when the two envoys of Quraysh came seeking them. None of them refused to appear or felt uneasy about doing so, and the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did not condemn it. Al-Hafiz Ibn Kathir related the account in al-Bidaya wa al-Nihaya, on the authority of Ibn Ishaq; Abu Nu'aym in al-Dala'il; al-Bayhaqi in al-Dala'il as well; and others, with chains of transmission, some of which he deemed good, and others he authenticated and strengthened. See it from (3/69) onward. It includes the account of the Negus granting them protection and supporting them, and refusing to hand them over to Quraysh...
Also suitable in this regard is citing the words of Joseph (peace be upon him), when the wife of al-Aziz accused him, saying: 'What is the recompense for one who intends evil against your family but that he be imprisoned or suffer a painful punishment?'
'He said, "It was she who sought to seduce me." And a witness from her family testified ...' [the verses]. This includes his defending himself among an unbelieving people, and some of them testifying, defending him, and declaring him innocent.
Even clearer than this is what he did in prison, when 'he said to the one of the two whom he thought would be saved, "Mention me to your master ..."' The disbelief of the king of Egypt at that time did not prevent him from doing so.


[Page 295]

And the fact that he had a law and religion of his own, contrary to the religion of Allah, did not prevent him from sending to him to inform him that he had been wronged and cast into prison without committing any sin, in the hope that he might release him, remove the injustice against him, and clear him of the accusation for which he had been imprisoned ... Nor did it prevent him from defending himself and seeking to establish his innocence when the king summoned him later. He said to the messenger: "Return to your master and ask him about the women who cut their hands. Indeed, my Lord knows their scheming." Thus he complains of the injustice done to him—or, rather, mentions it before the unbelieving king in order to establish his innocence,

and all of this was the action of an infallible prophet. Safeguarding the principle of monotheism and its purity was the very message with which all the prophets were sent, and to which all their calls were in agreement, as is well known from the call of the prophets and messengers. It was not for the Prophet of Allah, Joseph, to contravene or contradict that, or to depart even by a hair's breadth from the religion of his forefathers Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob ... How could he, when Allah, Exalted is He, declared him innocent and protected him from what was lesser than that, saying: "Thus We turned away from him evil and indecency"; then He said: "Indeed, he was one of Our sincere servants."
All of this shows that there is no objection whatsoever to a monotheist doing something like this.

Even if some sins and violations of the Sharia are present in those administrative systems, such as requiring employees to wear a particular garment resembling the clothing of unbelievers, requiring them to shave their beards, imposing financial penalties and unjust or oppressive discretionary punishments, or if those arrangements involve injustices, discrimination, and giving precedence to the wealthy and influential over


[Page 296]

the poor, or because they are influenced by some pre-Islamic, tribal, regional, or similar forms of partisanship, which are forbidden and which it is not permissible to impose on people or adopt as regulations.

All of this, despite the falsehood and sins it may involve, has nothing to do with the idolatrous, unbelieving legislation whose author, supporter, administrator, and anyone who willingly seeks its judgment are deemed unbelievers ... The fact that such things are written down and codified does not change their nature at all. Commanding someone to commit a sin in writing is like commanding it verbally; merely writing it down does not make it idolatrous legislation.

The point is that administrative regulations which are permissible and involve neither sin nor violation are matters on which the Companions unanimously agreed and acted accordingly ... This includes what some scholars call in works of jurisprudence and legal theory "unrestricted interests" (al-masalih al-mursala). If such regulations involve violations that do not amount to deeming something forbidden lawful, then although they are regulations that contravene the Sharia, they are not of the idolatrous legislative kind.
For not every contravention of the Sharia is unbelief; some are sins, while others are unbelief ...

Failure to distinguish between these two kinds has led some astray, prompting them to declare an unbeliever anyone who engages in lease contracts and similar sales, or who abides by transport fares


[Page 297]

or sales, since they claimed that price-fixing is idolatrous, unbelieving legislation that it is impermissible to implement.

He said regarding the issue of elections:

"Some people are brought to see those dazzling advertisements bearing, in bold letters, 'Islam is the solution,' and similar slogans with which the idolatrous legislators deceive ordinary Muslims. They vote for them and choose them out of love for Islam and loyalty to its Sharia, without knowing or intending the blocked, idolatrous method those representatives follow to enforce some of the limits of the Sharia, as they claim. All of this must be taken into consideration, along with the fact that the voter has not personally engaged in legislation, sworn to respect unbelieving laws, submitted to them for judgment, or committed any of the other statements or actions of unbelief that the representatives engage in. It is well known that the voter does not do or personally engage in all of this (*); rather, he votes and appoints as his representative the person he chooses.

If he intends to authorize or appoint someone to act on his behalf who commits these explicit acts of unbelief, then his ruling is the same as that of the person he appointed, for the ruling on one who provides support is the ruling on the one who acts directly ... So long as he supports him with this intention, his ruling is the same ..."


[Page 298]

However, if there is much obfuscation surrounding a matter that is not known or clear to everyone—I mean the true nature of the work of representatives and the acts of disbelief they engage in—and a person is among those to whom this may be unclear and who do not know about it, but elects a representative and appoints him to act on his behalf, intending that he provide some services for him, his clan, or his town or village, then he did not intend to appoint him to engage in acts of disbelief, nor did he choose him for that purpose. He is mistaken, but neither deliberate nor intending to support the acts of disbelief committed by those legislators when he chose them.
Therefore, it is not permissible to hasten to declare someone like him an unbeliever until the proof has been established against him and he has been informed of the true nature of the actions of the legislative representatives and of the acts of unbelief they commit, which contradict the religion of Islam and the oneness of the Lord of all worlds. If he nevertheless persists in electing them, he is an unbeliever. (I say:
It is therefore necessary to distinguish among those who vote: between those who intend to choose the legislator and those who do not intend that, but instead intend to choose something other than the legislator. The latter is not declared an unbeliever until proof has been established against him. For although, outwardly, he may have committed an act of unbelief in the eyes of someone who does not know his intention, the confusion and intermingling of circumstances, and the fact that democracy and parliament are innovations and foreign names and terms whose true nature is unknown to many people, have led some to undertake an act without knowing its real meaning. Such a person is like one who utters a word or says something whose meaning is unknown. The scholars hold that he is not held accountable for it until its meaning has been explained to him and proof has been established against him. In Qawa'id al-Ahkam fi Masalih al-Anam (2/102), in the chapter "On one who utters a word whose meaning he does not know: he is not held accountable for what it entails," al-Izz ibn Abd al-Salam (may Allah have mercy on him) said:


[Page 299]

(If a non-Arab utters a word of disbelief, faith, divorce, emancipation, sale, purchase, settlement, or release from a claim, he is not held accountable for any of that, because he has not undertaken its implications and did not intend it. Likewise, if an Arab utters a foreign word that indicates one of these meanings, but does not know what it means, he is not held accountable for any of that, because he did not intend it; for intention can only be directed toward something known or supposed. If an Arab intends, by uttering any of these words, to convey their meanings, knowing what they mean, then that takes effect from him. But if he does not know their meanings—for example, if an Arab says to his wife, "You are divorced according to the Sunnah" or "according to innovation," while she is pregnant (1), without knowing what the two expressions mean, or if he utters the word for khulʿ (wife-initiated divorce), or another word, or the word for taking back a divorced wife, marriage, or emancipation, without knowing what it means, despite being an Arab, he is not held accountable for any of that, since he has no awareness of what it denotes and therefore cannot intend the expression that signifies it.) End quote.
I say: In our time, this applies to someone who does not know the meaning of the word "democracy" and what it denotes, and so praises it, supposing that it means only—as many ordinary people believe—what stands in opposition to oppression, enslavement, and the confiscation of freedoms and rights, and the like ... Such a person is not declared an unbeliever until he is made aware that it fundamentally means the people legislating for the people, or the sovereignty of the masses rather than the sovereignty of Allah alone ... Until he knows this, he has no awareness of its meaning of disbelief and cannot intend it.
For the Sharia does not recognize any distinction between two things that are alike and equivalent ... "Are your disbelievers better than those, or have you been granted immunity in the scriptures?"


[Page 300]

The author of al-Jami said, concerning working in the governments of unbelievers: "If it is said that Joseph (peace be upon him), by working for the unbelieving king, was undoubtedly assisting him, the answer is that this is not prohibited unless it is assistance to an unbeliever in something forbidden, in which case it is forbidden, or assistance to an unbeliever in unbelief or against the Muslims, in which case the assistance constitutes allegiance that renders one an unbeliever. The author of al-Risala al-Limaniyya confused assistance with allegiance and made every kind of assistance allegiance. Our Sharia, as well as the laws of all the prophets, forbade allegiance to unbelievers, while permitting dealings with them through buying and selling, partnership, and leasing. I mentioned this earlier among things that might be thought to constitute allegiance but do not. All of this involves a kind of assistance to them, but it is assistance in worldly matters, not assistance in unbelief or against the Muslims. An example is that the unbelievers of Mecca used to bring wheat from al-Yamama. When Thumama ibn
Athal, who was one of the leading men of Yamama, withheld wheat from them until the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) gave permission for it (1). In his commentary, Ibn Hajar said that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did give permission for it (2). This was assistance indeed—but in a worldly matter.
If we consider the question of a Muslim working for a non-Muslim, has our Sharia given guidance on whether this is permissible or impermissible? The answer is: Yes, it has given guidance permitting it, as indicated by what al-Bukhari, may Allah have mercy on him, narrated in the Book of Hiring in his Sahih, in the chapter, "May a man hire himself out to a polytheist in enemy territory?" He cited in it the hadith of


[Page 301]

Khabbab ibn al-Aratt (may Allah be pleased with him) said: "I was a blacksmith, and I worked for al-As ibn Wa'il. A debt accrued to me with him, so I went to him to demand payment. He said, 'No, by Allah, I will not pay you until you disbelieve in Muhammad.' I said, 'By Allah, no—not even if you die and are then resurrected.' He said, 'Will I really die and then be resurrected?' I said, 'Yes.' He said, 'Then I will have wealth and children there, and I will pay you.' So Allah, Exalted is He, revealed: {Have you seen the one who disbelieved in Our signs and said, "I will surely be given wealth and children"} (Maryam: 77) (7). A qayin is a blacksmith, and the term is used for any craftsman.
Ibn Hajar, may Allah have mercy on him, said in his commentary: His words, "Chapter: May a man hire himself out to a polytheist in enemy territory?" He included in it the hadith of Khabbab—who was a Muslim at the time—about his working for al-As ibn Wa'il, who was a polytheist. This took place in Mecca, which was then enemy territory. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) learned of this and approved it. The author did not state the ruling definitively, since it may be that permissibility was restricted to cases of necessity, or that it was permitted before permission was given to fight the polytheists and oppose them, and before the command not to abase oneself as a believer. Al-Muhallab said: "The scholars disliked this, except in cases of necessity, subject to two conditions: first, that his work be something a Muslim is permitted to do; and second, that he not assist them in anything that would bring harm upon the Muslims." Ibn al-Munir said: "The schools of law have settled on the view that craftsmen in their workshops may work for the People of the Covenant, and this is not considered"
That is a form of humiliation, unlike serving him in his home as his subordinate. And Allah knows best. [Joseph's work for the unbelieving king fulfilled these conditions.


[Page 302]

the three conditions: his work was undertaken to secure a benefit, it was permissible, and he did not assist the unbeliever against the Muslims or harm a Muslim.
If it is said: You have listed collecting money for an unbeliever among prohibited acts, yet this was the work of Joseph, peace be upon him. How, then, was his work permissible? The answer is: What is prohibited is collecting money according to the unbeliever's judgment and in accordance with his man-made laws, which are rife with injustice. But if the collection is carried out according to the judgment of the Muslim entrusted with collecting it, then it is justice and reform. This was the situation of Joseph, peace be upon him: he had been given authority to conduct affairs according to his own judgment. This is indicated by the king's words to him: {Indeed, today you are established in position and trusted in our presence} (Yusuf: 54). Allah, Exalted is He, also says: {And thus We established Joseph in the land, to settle wherever he wished} (Yusuf: 56). And Allah, Exalted is He, says, relating Joseph's words: {My Lord, You have indeed granted me authority and taught me the interpretation of events} (Yusuf: 101). What greater authority could there be than this? Al-Shawkani said: "His saying, Exalted is He, {to settle wherever he wished} (Yusuf: 56), means that he could reside wherever he wanted ... This expression indicates the completeness of his power" (1). Another indication of his authority is that he applied his own law in enslaving the thief, as stated in Allah's words: {The one in whose possession we found our property} (Yusuf: 79); that was his law (2). In sum, although Joseph, peace be upon him, took charge of the funds, collecting and disbursing them, he did so on the basis that he would exercise his own judgment in managing them, not according to the unbeliever's choice. This is permissible today: if an unbelieving king appoints a Muslim to oversee finances, judge cases, or hold another office, on the condition that the Muslim exercise his own judgment in it and not the unbeliever's, that is permissible (3).


[Page 303]

He said: The person who abandons the obligatory migration despite being able to undertake it, while unable to manifest his religion in the land of unbelief, may have abandoned migration out of allegiance that constitutes unbelief. If he abandoned it for some worldly benefit in the land of unbelief, then he is sinful, unless his inability to manifest his religion reaches the point of unbelief, such as when he is unable to perform the prayer. But if he abandoned migration out of love for his unbelieving people and preferring to live among them, this is allegiance that constitutes unbelief. The legal characterization of abandoning migration, in the case of this category, is therefore that it is an act that may indicate allegiance; it should not be said that it is manifest allegiance. It is well established that the call is directed only to the leaders of those who refuse, not to each of their individuals by name. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) sent invitations to Islam to Khosrow, Caesar, al-Muqawqis, and the Negus, and his letter to them was one and the same. Whoever among them turned away would bear only the sin of the Arysiyyun, who were his followers. These people—and similarly, Ibn al-Qayyim (may Allah have mercy on him) said.))
(How Unbelief Is Established and the Question of Blind Following in Declaring Others Unbelievers)
It is noticeable that some movements go to extremes in declaring other movements working for Islam to be unbelievers, on the grounds of what they call allegiance to another group. That other group's unbelief may not be apparent to the group that did not declare it unbelieving, because of an interpretive doubt. One should not go to extremes in such matters, for the conditions for establishing unbelief are conditions that we have explained in this study.
The author of al-Jami' said ((


[Page 304]

If a person utters or commits an act of disbelief, and some people become aware of it, but only one man, a child, or a woman testifies against him, then this act of disbelief is not established against him, because the required number of witnesses to prove apostasy has not been met in his case. In outward legal judgment he is a Muslim, though in reality he is a disbeliever. ......This third scenario was the usual state of the hypocrites in the time of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him). They would speak words of disbelief among themselves, but none of them would testify against another, as Ibn Taymiyya said: "He would be a hypocrite inwardly, but would be unable to manifest apostasy; rather, he would speak hypocritically with those close to him" (3). Sometimes a Muslim would hear them and testify to what he had heard, but this was not sufficient to establish it. Thus Zayd ibn Arqam testified against Abdullah ibn Ubayy that he had said, "If we return to Medina, the mightier will surely expel the humbler," as is established in the Sahih. Although revelation confirmed that Zayd was truthful, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did not hold them accountable on the basis of revelation, but rather according to the legally prescribed means of proof. Moreover, much of what the hypocrites said was ambiguous in meaning, not explicit, as Allah, Exalted is He, says: {And you will surely recognize them by the tone of their speech} Muhammad: 30. The tone of speech is what is understood from its meaning without being stated explicitly; this was mentioned by al-Qurtubi.
This is how the scholars answered the question: Why did the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) not kill the hypocrites? Ibn Taymiyya, may Allah have mercy on him, said: "Most of them did not say things amounting to disbelief that could be established against them by evidence; rather, they would outwardly profess Islam, and their hypocrisy would sometimes be recognized by a word that someone heard"


[Page 305]

The believing man would convey it to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), and they would swear by Allah that they had not said it—or they would not swear. At other times, their hypocrisy was evident from their lagging behind in prayer and jihad, their finding the payment of zakat burdensome, and the aversion they showed toward many of Allah's rulings. Most of them could be recognized by their manner of speech—until he said: All these hypocrites outwardly professed Islam and swore that they were Muslims, and they had taken their oaths as a shield. Given their condition, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did not impose the prescribed punishments on the basis of his own knowledge, a report from a single narrator, revelation alone, or circumstantial evidence and indications, until the grounds for the punishment had been established by legally admissible testimony or confession—up to where he said: He refrained from killing them, despite their being disbelievers, because their disbelief had not been established by legally valid proof. This is further indicated by the fact that he did not call them individually to repent. It is well known that the best course regarding someone whose hypocrisy and heresy have been established is to call him to repent, as one would an apostate: if he repents, then well and good; otherwise, he is killed. We have no report that he called any one of them individually to repent. It is therefore known that disbelief and apostasy were not established against any one of them individually in a manner that required him to be killed as an apostate. For this reason, their outward professions were accepted, and their inner realities were left to Allah. If this was the case with someone whose hypocrisy had become apparent without legally admissible testimony, then what of someone whose hypocrisy had not become apparent? [(1). (A useful point here): Is someone who knows that a man is guilty of disbelief entitled to regard him as a disbeliever—as in the case described above—even if it is not possible to establish his disbelief through the legally prescribed means of proof?
The answer is yes; indeed, he is obliged to judge him a disbeliever, but on two conditions:
First: The person must be qualified to make the judgment himself, or to seek a legal opinion from someone else, so that he can distinguish disbelief from other things and consider any impediments to making the judgment.


[Page 306]

The other is that he should not be punished with the penalties that are Allah Most High's right, such as permitting his blood to be shed or his property to be seized, lest he be held accountable for this without complete legal proof. Were this permissible, it would lead to chaos, with blood and property being deemed lawful on the basis of mere accusation. Rather, he may be punished with lesser measures, such as shunning him, refraining from marrying him or arranging a marriage for him, and refraining from performing the funeral prayer over him when he dies, and the like (1). Ibn Taymiyya said concerning the hypocrites: "At first, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) would perform the funeral prayer over them and seek forgiveness for them, until Allah forbade him to do so, saying: {And never pray over any of them who dies, nor stand at his grave} (al-Tawba: 84), and saying: {Whether you ask forgiveness for them or do not ask forgiveness for them, even if you ask forgiveness for them seventy times, Allah will never forgive them} (al-Tawba: 80). So he would neither perform the funeral prayer over them nor seek forgiveness for them. Yet their blood and property remained inviolable; he did not deem lawful against them what he deemed lawful against the unbelievers who do not claim to be believers, but instead openly profess unbelief and not faith" [(2).
Evidence that an individual may judge another to be an unbeliever when he knows that the person has committed unbelief is Allah Most High's saying: {Set forth for them the parable of two men: We gave one of them two gardens of grapevines, surrounded them with date palms, and placed crops between them. Both gardens yielded their produce and fell short of nothing; and We caused a river to flow through them. He had wealth, and he said to his companion, as he conversed with him, "I have more wealth than you and a stronger following." He entered his garden, wronging himself, and said, "I do not think this will ever perish. Nor do I think the Hour will come; and even if I am returned to my Lord, I will surely find a better place of return than this." His companion said to him, as he conversed with him, "Do you disbelieve in the One who created you?"} (al-Kahf: 32–37). The first man disbelieved by doubting the Resurrection: {Nor do I think the Hour will come}.


[Page 307]

Al-Kahf: 36. The other then declared him an unbeliever on that account. They were only two men, as Allah Most High says. There are many examples of this among the early generations, including al-Shafi'i's declaring Hafs al-Fard an unbeliever during a debate (3). Ibn Taymiyya held that al-Shafi'i did not declare this Hafs an unbeliever, but rather applied the term unbelief to his statement. However, the established account of the two of them is contrary to what the Shaykh of Islam said (4).
It is not permissible to require other Muslims to follow the judgment of someone who has declared another an unbeliever, so long as what was established for him has not been established for them, and so long as this person's unbelief has not been established by sound legal proof. .......
Is it permissible for someone who knows that a man is an unbeliever to make this known among the people, even if the unbeliever conceals his unbelief? The answer is: Yes; indeed, it is obligatory when harm from him is feared, especially if this unbeliever is one of the callers to innovations, someone from whom knowledge is taken, or someone seeking to marry a Muslim woman, or the like. For religion is sincere counsel. On this, Qadi Iyad (may Allah have mercy on him) said: "If the person who says this is someone from whom people seek to acquire knowledge or transmit hadith, or whose judgment, testimony, or legal opinion concerning people's rights is relied upon, then whoever hears him must make what he heard known, warn people against him, and testify against him regarding what he said. Any Muslim leaders to whom this reaches must likewise denounce it and explain his unbelief and the corruption of his statement, in order to avert his harm from the Muslims and uphold the right of the Master of the Messengers. The same applies if he is someone who preaches to the general public or teaches children, for a person with such an inner state cannot be trusted not to instill this in their hearts. Thus, the obligation is all the more emphatic in their case, in defense of the Prophet's right and the right of his Sharia [(2).


[Page 308]

This concerns legal establishment, that is, correctly establishing that the cause of disbelief has in fact been committed by the person who committed it.))
Therefore, members of movements are not obliged to obey their leaders in declaring another group unbelievers until this has been correctly established according to the Sharia. It is well known that declaring someone an unbeliever is a legal ruling, like all other legal rulings, and disagreement over declaring people unbelievers on certain grounds is a matter of independent legal reasoning (ijtihad), like other matters. Thus, a caliph in an Islamic state may not compel people to adopt one view on this matter, nor require them to declare an unbeliever anyone whom he himself declares one, if an individual believes that this declaration is unjustified and contrary to the Quran and Sunnah. A state governed by Islam should therefore accommodate differences arising from independent legal reasoning, even on the question of declaring people unbelievers; not all students of knowledge understand matters in the same way. The caliphs likewise did not compel anyone to follow their school of thought when another interpretation was possible.
It is stated in Majmu al-Fatawa (27/296):
"The twelfth point: When scholars disagree over a matter, no judge may settle the dispute by issuing a ruling. No judge may say, 'I have ruled that this opinion is correct and that the other opinion is rejected, along with the person who holds it.' Rather, in matters over which Muslim scholars disagree, or on which they have reached consensus, the ruler's statement is like that of any individual scholar, if he is a scholar; if he is merely an imitator, he is like the ordinary people who imitate others. Holding office and authority do not make someone who is not a learned jurist capable of independent reasoning into one. If authority and office were what qualified someone to speak about knowledge and religion, then the caliph and the sultan would be the most entitled to speak about knowledge and religion, and people would seek their legal opinions and refer to them when matters were unclear.


[Page 309]

in knowledge and religion. If the caliph and ruler do not claim this for themselves, nor impose their ruling on the subjects in this matter in favor of one opinion over another, except by the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger, then anyone whose authority is less than that of the ruler has even greater reason not to exceed his bounds or set himself up in a position he is not entitled to occupy. Abu Bakr, Umar, Uthman, and Ali—the Rightly Guided Caliphs—were all the more entitled to do so than anyone beneath them. For they, may Allah be pleased with them, only required people to follow the Book of their Lord and the Sunnah of their Prophet. Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, used to say, "I have only sent my officials—that is, my deputies—to you to teach you the Book of your Lord and the Sunnah of your Prophet, and to distribute your spoils among you." Rather, this is a matter on which Muslim scholars who know what the legal proofs—the Book and the Sunnah—indicate may speak. Whoever has greater knowledge of the Book and the Sunnah is more entitled than another to speak on it, even if he is not a ruler. A ruler has no say in it merely by virtue of being a ruler; rather, if he has knowledge, he may speak on it like any other scholar. These people have ruled on a matter over which, by consensus, they have no authority to rule. This is a form of ruling that is invalid by consensus.
Thirteenth: The general rulings that Muslims share—whether agreed upon or disputed—are not for judges to rule on. Rather, a learned ruler, like any other scholar, states what he knows; a judge only rules on specific cases. As for whether this act is obligatory, recommended, or forbidden, this is one of the general rulings over which no one has authority to rule except Allah and His Messenger. And the scholars of


[Page 310]

Muslims infer the ruling of Allah and His Messenger from the relevant proofs. These people have ruled on general rulings, and their ruling in this matter is invalid by consensus."

It is stated in Majmu al-Fatawa (35/378):
"Allah has made it obligatory upon the Muslim rulers to follow the Sharia, which is the Book and the Sunnah. If some Muslims disagree over any matter of religion, even if the disputant is one of the ordinary students of knowledge, the rulers may not compel him to follow a judge's ruling. Rather, they must make the truth clear to him, just as a learner makes the truth clear to an ignorant person. If the truth with which Allah sent His Messenger becomes clear to him and is manifest, yet he then opposes it out of obstinacy, he deserves punishment. As for someone who says, 'What I have said is my own view, or the view of a group of Muslim scholars, and I have said it by way of independent reasoning or following another,' then, by the agreement of the Muslims, he may not be punished, even if he has made an error that contradicts the Book and the Sunnah. Were such a person punished, all Muslims would be punished, for there is not one among them who does not hold views based on his own reasoning or on following another, in which he is mistaken. Were Allah to punish those who err, He would punish all creation."

Imam al-Shafii said: "People are agreed that if the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) has become clear to someone, he may not abandon it for anyone else's opinion." I'lam al-Muwaqqiin


[Page 311]

This means that individuals within the abode of Islam and belonging to it must pursue the truth to the extent of their ability, even if that truth differs from the caliph's legal judgment. Likewise, the caliph must not compel people to follow his school of thought. Rather, both are commanded to follow what they each find sound in the Book and the Sunnah; otherwise, they fall into the sin of obedience, taking rabbis and monks as lords besides Allah. May Allah protect us from that. I have discussed this in detail elsewhere. For further benefit, consult the chapter on invalidating blind following in Ibn Hazm's book al-Ihkam, and al-Shawkani's book The Beneficial Discourse on the Invalidity of Blind Following, since this is not the purpose of this discussion. And Allah knows best. There is no objection to a ruler considering a particular group to be unbelievers on the basis of an interpretation he holds, while one of those engaged in religious learning holds otherwise because evidence from the Book or the Sunnah has become clear to him that rules this out. Both should counsel one another by presenting the evidence they have, while not withdrawing their hand from obedience. I have previously mentioned the Companions' disagreement with the greatest of the Rightly Guided Caliphs when evidence from the Book or the Sunnah appeared to indicate otherwise, so there is no need to repeat it here. There is no doubt that those below them have even greater reason to do so.

Is the one who declares the person who excuses someone to be an unbeliever a Kharijite?


[Page 312]

It is noticeable that many who belong to the Islamic movement accuse anyone who declares the excuser to be an unbeliever of being a Kharijite. Here we find them falling into what they tried to avoid: they condemn the person who declares the excuser to be an unbeliever because the excuser is acting on a mistaken interpretation.
So how can someone who interprets the matter and errs be declared an unbeliever, when the evidence from the Book and the Sunnah rules that out? Yet they also find that the person who declares the excuser to be an unbeliever is acting on a mistaken interpretation in this matter, but they do not excuse him on account of his interpretation. Instead, they hasten to call him a Kharijite. You know what this term entails in the way of condemnations and attributes described by the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him): that they are "the worst of creation," and his command to kill them, "Wherever you find them, kill them," and his calling them the dogs of Hell. If they accuse someone of being a Kharijite, they must therefore apply this severe threat to him. Hence the need to examine who the Kharijites are.
(Who are the Kharijites?)
Some scholars, such as Ibn Hazm, defined the Kharijites. In al-Fasl fi al-Milal wa al-Ahwa wa al-Nihal (2/90), he wrote:
"Whoever agrees with the Kharijites in rejecting arbitration, declaring those who commit major sins to be unbelievers, advocating revolt against unjust rulers, holding that those who commit major sins will remain in the Fire forever, and holding that the imamate may belong to someone other than Quraysh, is a Kharijite. But if he disagrees with them on these matters, while agreeing with them on other matters over which the Muslims differ, then he is not a Kharijite."
There is no doubt that if we wish to apply a ruling to someone, we must first determine the effective cause of that ruling—that is, what is the cause whose presence in a person leads us to judge him to be a Kharijite?


[Page 313]

Second: Determining the effective cause of this ruling and its meaning means verifying that the effective cause we have identified is present in a person, so that we may judge him to be one of the Kharijites.
Let us begin by identifying the effective cause. The first question that comes to mind here is: What characteristics of the Kharijites did the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) mention? First, we list the descriptions that could constitute the effective cause of the ruling. Then we refine the effective cause by excluding incidental descriptions (those that do not affect the ruling). What remains are the descriptions that may serve as the effective cause of the ruling, upon which the designation and, in turn, the punishment follow.
The characteristics mentioned by the Messenger of Allah are that they are young in age (that is, young people), and foolish in understanding (that is, ignorant, with foolish minds). They speak with the words of the best of creation (that is, they recite the Quran or quote hadiths). In the hadith it says, "They recite the Quran, but it does not go beyond their throats." It has been said that this means they do not act upon it, and it has also been said that their recitation is not accepted. Then they kill the people of Islam and leave the idolaters alone. Then they leave the religion as an arrow passes through its prey (that is, they leave it entirely and become unbelievers, or they leave it through innovation or misguidance without becoming unbelievers, as Ibn Battal related from the majority). Then, "they will not return to it until the arrow returns to its notch." "People will emerge from the direction of the East." He said: "Their distinguishing mark is shaving"—or he said, "removing the hair" (and removing the hair means shaving it off). Then: "Their sign is a black man, one of whose upper arms is like a woman's breast, or like a piece of flesh that wobbles. They will emerge at a time when the people are divided." Abu Sa'id said: "I testify that I heard this hadith from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), and I testify that Ali ibn Abi Talib ..."


[Page 314]

"Fight them, and I am with him." He then ordered that man to be sought out, and he was brought to him, until I saw him matching the description the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) had given of him: "Among them is a black man, one of whose upper arms has no bone in it; it is like a woman's breast, quivering like a woman's breast." Then, when Ali saw this man killed, he said, "Indeed, my close friend told me that they are three brothers from among the jinn. This is the eldest of them; the second has a large following, and the third is weak." Shu'ayb deemed its chain of transmission good, and Ahmad Shakir authenticated it. Thus, if we consider this hadith authentic, one of the leaders of these jinn who took human form was among them. Then: "the worst of creation and creatures"—al-Albani authenticated it (and it is clear that they are unbelievers). Then: "The worst of those killed beneath the sky are they, three times over; and the best of those killed beneath the sky is the one they killed." Shu'ayb authenticated it. Then he considers insignificant...
"one of you [would consider] his prayer alongside their prayer, and his fasting alongside their fasting." Then: "fierce and stern"—that is, harsh and strong.
By tracing the hadiths and the characteristics reported concerning the Kharijites, we find that, alongside the characteristics mentioned, they include rulings. These rulings are that they are "the worst of creation" and "wherever you find them, kill them." Abu Umama, one of the Companions who narrated the hadith, said that they "disbelieved after their faith." These are three rulings concerning this world; then, in the Hereafter, they are "the dogs of Hell." We then ask whether these hadiths concern specific people—that is, people whom the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) knew through revelation would deliberately leave the religion, following their desires without interpretation, and whom Allah informed him about by their characteristics. If we find the characteristic, we recognize them and apply the ruling to them, namely that they...


[Page 315]

The Kharijites are among the worst of creation, the dogs of Hellfire. They must be fought without the need to establish proof against them on account of a form of interpretation. This is like the Dajjal: the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) described him by physical characteristics and actions, so when we find them, we know that he is the Dajjal—for example, that he is one-eyed, has tightly curled hair, and has a wiped-out left eye. The apparent meaning is the first possibility, and Allah knows best, for several reasons.
First: He (peace and blessings be upon him) informed us that they must be killed. It is known that if killing is on account of disbelief, it can only take place after proof has been established. Likewise, if it is on account of armed rebellion, it can only take place after it is known that the one who rebels against the imam has no valid interpretation. Since the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) ordered that they be killed—and this includes any one of them who can be apprehended—we know that proof was certainly established against them. The apparent meaning of the hadiths is that they are disbelievers, for several reasons.
First: He informed us that they "leave the religion and then do not return to it." They deliberately opposed revelation, and the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) knew this through revelation. If you say that they may have been ignorant people acting on an interpretation, you fall into the very position you sought to avoid: if they were acting on an interpretation, then they were ignorant in a compounded way. The fact that the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) judged that they would leave the religion and that they were the worst of creation, while, according to your view, they were ignorant, indicates that ignorance is no excuse, and that whoever falls into disbelief through interpretation or ignorance is a disbeliever. Al-Tabari used this as evidence for declaring those who act on interpretations to be disbelievers, and the contemporary group that declares such people disbelievers and does not accept ignorance as an excuse followed him in this. You certainly do not hold that view. It is therefore clear that they were deliberate opponents, because one who errs is excused, in light of the words of the Exalted: "There is no blame upon you for what you do by mistake, but only for what your hearts intend." And he (peace and blessings be upon him) knew this through revelation.


[Page 316]

Second: what shows that they are unbelievers is his saying "the worst of creation"
and his saying "the dogs of the people of the Fire", which shows that their standing is lower than that of the people of the Fire. This is also the evident view of Abu Umama, in his saying "These were Muslims and then became unbelievers". As for what Ibn Battal reported in his words: ((The majority of scholars hold that, in this rebellion of theirs, they did not leave the body of the believers, because of his saying (peace and blessings be upon him): "and he has doubts about the notch of the arrow", for having doubts is uncertainty, and when there is uncertainty about this, it cannot be ruled with certainty that they have left Islam entirely, since one whose bond of Islam is established with certainty is not ruled to have left it except with certainty. And it is narrated from Ali ibn Abi Talib by several routes that he was asked about the people of Nahrawan: "Are they unbelievers?" He said: "It is from unbelief that they fled." It was said: "Then are they hypocrites?" He said: "The hypocrites remember Allah only a little." It was said: "Then what are they?" He said: "They are a people whose effort went astray and who were blind to the truth; they rebelled against us, so we fought them."))
The reply to this is found in Fath al-Bari by Ibn Hajar (12/299):
"It has been cited as evidence by those who hold that the Kharijites are unbelievers, and this is what al-Bukhari's arrangement implies, since he placed them alongside the heretics and gave those who interpret a separate chapter heading. Judge Abu Bakr ibn al-Arabi stated this plainly in his commentary on al-Tirmidhi, saying: The correct view is that they are unbelievers, because of his saying (peace and blessings be upon him) 'they pass out of Islam', and his saying 'I will kill them as 'Ad were killed', and in another wording 'Thamud', and each of these perished only through unbelief; and because of his saying 'they are the worst of creation', and only unbelievers are described in this way; and because of his saying 'they are the most hateful of creation to Allah, Exalted is He'; and because they judge everyone who differs from their creed to be an unbeliever who will abide forever in the Fire, so they are more deserving of the name than those they judge. Among the later imams who inclined to this view is Shaykh Taqi al-Din al-Subki."


[Page 317]

He said in his fatwas: "Those who declared the Kharijites and the extremist Rafidites unbelievers argued that their declaring the leading Companions unbelievers entailed rejecting the Prophet's testimony that they were destined for Paradise (peace and blessings be upon him)." He said: "In my view, this is a sound argument." He said: "Those who did not declare them unbelievers argued that judging them to be unbelievers would require that they had prior, definitive knowledge of the testimony mentioned." This is open to question, for we know with certainty that those whom they declared unbelievers were deemed upright until the time of their deaths, and that is sufficient for us to hold that those who declared them unbelievers are themselves unbelievers. This is supported by the hadith, "If a man says to his brother, 'You are an unbeliever,' then one of the two has incurred it." In Muslim's wording: "Whoever accuses a Muslim of unbelief, or says, 'Enemy of Allah,' it will rebound upon him." He said: "It has been established that these people accuse a group of unbelief, people whose faith we know with certainty. Therefore, in accordance with the implication of the Lawgiver's report, they must be judged unbelievers. This is similar to what they have said about one who prostrates to an idol and the like, even though there is no explicit denial in such an act, after they had defined unbelief as denial. If they argue that there is consensus that whoever does that is an unbeliever, we say: These reports concerning these people likewise entail that they are unbelievers, even if they did not hold with certainty that those whom they declared unbelievers were deemed upright. Nor will their general belief in Islam and their performance of the obligatory acts save them from being judged unbelievers, just as that would not save one who prostrates to an idol." I say: Among those who inclined toward some aspects of this discussion was al-Tabari in his Tahdhib. After citing the hadiths in this chapter, he said: "It refutes the view of those who say that no one among the people of the qibla leaves Islam, after becoming subject to its rulings, except by knowingly intending to leave it. This view is invalidated by the hadith's words: 'They speak the truth and recite the Quran, yet they pass out of Islam and do not cling to any part of it.' It is well known that they only deemed the blood and property of Muslims lawful because they erred in their interpretation of Quranic verses, understanding them in a sense other than what was intended." He then transmitted with a sound chain from Ibn Abbas, and mentioned the Kharijites to him and what they experience when reciting the Quran. He said:


[Page 318]

They believe in its clear verses and perish at its ambiguous ones. The command to kill them supports the view mentioned, along with the previously cited hadith of Ibn Masud: "It is not lawful to kill a Muslim except for one of three reasons," including "one who abandons his religion and separates from the community." Al-Qurtubi says in al-Mufhim: "The example mentioned in the hadith of Abu Sa'id"—that is, the hadith to be cited in the following chapter—"supports the view that they are unbelievers. For its apparent intent is that they left Islam without retaining any part of it, just as an arrow passes through its prey because of its speed and the strength of the archer, without catching on anything from the prey. He alluded to this with his words, 'It passed beyond the dung and the blood.'" The author of al-Shifa' says: "We likewise decisively judge as an unbeliever anyone who makes a statement that leads to declaring the community misguided or the Companions unbelievers." The author of al-Rawda cited this from him in the chapter on apostasy and endorsed it. Most Sunni scholars of legal theory held that the Kharijites are sinners and that the rulings of Islam apply to them, because they profess the two testimonies of faith and persist in observing the pillars of Islam. They were deemed sinners only because they declared Muslims unbelievers, relying on a corrupt interpretation; this led them to deem the blood and property of those who opposed them lawful, and to testify that they were unbelievers and polytheists. Al-Khattabi said: "Muslim scholars unanimously agreed that the Kharijites, despite their misguidance, are a sect among the Muslim sects. They permitted marrying them and eating their slaughtered animals, and held that they are not declared unbelievers so long as they adhere to the foundations of Islam." Iyad said: "This question nearly became more difficult for theologians than any other, to the point that the jurist Abd al-Haqq asked Imam Abu al-Maali about it, and he excused himself, saying that admitting an unbeliever into the faith and expelling a Muslim from it is a grave matter in religion." He said: "Before him, Qadi Abu Bakr al-Baqillani had refrained from giving a ruling, saying, 'The people did not explicitly declare unbelief; rather, they made statements that lead to unbelief.'" Al-Ghazali said in The Distinction Between Faith and Heresy: "What is proper is to avoid declaring anyone an unbeliever whenever there is a way to do so, for indeed...


[Page 319]

Deeming it lawful to shed the blood of worshippers who affirm the oneness of Allah is an error, and the error of leaving a thousand unbelievers alive is less grave than the error of shedding the blood of a single Muslim. Among the proofs cited by those who did not declare them unbelievers is his statement in the third hadith of the chapter, after describing them as passing out of the religion as an arrow passes through its target: "The archer looks at his arrow," until he said, "Then he examines the notch to see whether anything has caught on it." Ibn Battal said: The majority held that
The scholars held that the Kharijites were not outside the body of the Muslims, based on the words, "he doubts about the arrowhead," since doubt is a form of uncertainty. And since uncertainty arose in this matter, they could not be definitively judged to have left Islam, for whoever has entered Islam with certainty does not leave it except with certainty. He said: Ali was asked about the people of al-Nahrawan, "Have they disbelieved?" He replied, "They fled from disbelief." I say: If this report from Ali is authentic, it may be understood to mean that he had not learned of the belief they held which led those who declared them unbelievers to do so. There is room to question his use of the words, "he doubts about the arrowhead," for in some versions of the hadith mentioned—as previously indicated and as will be discussed later—nothing of it clung to the arrow, while in others it passed through the dung and blood. The way to reconcile the two is that he was uncertain whether anything was on the arrowhead, then became certain that nothing had clung to the arrow, nor to any part of it, from what had been shot. It is also possible to attribute the difference to differences among individuals within their ranks, and to understand the words, "he doubts," as indicating that some of them may have retained something of Islam. Al-Qurtubi said in al-Mufhim: "The view that they are unbelievers is more apparent from the hadith." He said: "According to the view that they are unbelievers, they are fought and killed, and their property is taken as spoils. This is the view of a group of hadith scholars regarding the property of the Kharijites. According to the view that they are not unbelievers, they are treated as rebels when they break allegiance and take up arms. As for one who conceals himself..."


[Page 320]

"...among them an innovation. If he is apprehended, is he to be killed after being asked to repent, or not killed, but rather efforts made to refute his innovation? There is disagreement on this, corresponding to the disagreement over whether they are to be declared unbelievers." End quote.
From the foregoing, the following becomes clear: Some scholars held that they were unbelievers, for the reasons mentioned above, while most held that they were not to be declared unbelievers, for several reasons. First, they said that they were acting on an interpretation. Yet the hadiths do not mention that they were acting on an interpretation; rather, what is apparent is that these particular people, unlike others, were not acting on an interpretation. But how do we know that they were not? We say: We know this from the Messenger of Allah's ruling that they had left the religion, that they were the worst of creation, and that they must be killed—and nothing else—while also ruling that there is no blame on one who errs unintentionally. If it is said, "If others fall into some of the same things as these people, do you declare them unbelievers without qualification?" The answer is no, because we do not know from the report of the infallible that these people were unbelievers, as we know from the report of the infallible concerning the particular people mentioned, who were described precisely—unless they disbelieve in something necessarily known to be part of the religion and their interpretation is untenable. Ibn Hazm (may Allah have mercy on him) held this view, saying that these hadiths were revealed concerning a particular group, namely the Azariqa. It says in Lawami al-Anwar al-Bahiyya (1/87):
The Azariqa were the followers of Nafi ibn Abd Allah al-Azraq, the accursed Kharijite. A group from Basra, al-Ahwaz, and other towns in Persia and elsewhere went out with him. Their power grew, and they took control of the provinces. He had views and doctrines that they adopted along with him. Among these was his declaring Ali (may Allah be pleased with him) an unbeliever because of the arbitration. He claimed that the words of the Exalted, "And among the people is one whose speech pleases you" [al-Baqara: 204], and the rest of the verse, were revealed concerning him. He also claimed that the words "And among the people is one who sells himself, seeking Allah's pleasure" were revealed concerning Abd al-Rahman ibn Muljam (may Allah curse him).


[Page 321]

[Al-Baqara: 207], and among their doctrines was that they declared anyone who did not accept their opinion an unbeliever and deemed his blood lawful; declared those who stayed behind from fighting unbelievers and disavowed anyone who stayed behind; held that whoever committed a major sin left Islam and would remain forever in the Fire alongside all the other unbelievers; prohibited dissimulation; permitted killing the children and women of those who opposed them; and said: "There is no prescribed punishment for slander or for fornication."))
))The hadith of Abu Umama has already been mentioned. In a narration by Ahmad from Abu Ghalib, he said: When the heads of the Azariqa were brought and set up on the steps of Damascus, Abu Umama (may Allah be pleased with him) came. When he saw them, his eyes filled with tears, and he said: "Dogs of the Fire" (three times). "These are the worst of those killed beneath the sky, and the best of those killed beneath the sky are those whom these people killed." He said: I asked, "Why are your eyes filled with tears?" He replied, "Out of compassion for them; they were among the people of Islam." We asked, "Did you say that these are dogs of the Fire based on your own opinion, or is it something you heard from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him)?" He said, "I would be bold indeed!" Rather, I heard it from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) more than once, twice, or three times." He said: Then he counted them many times.))
Here, the Companions Abdullah ibn Abi Awfa and Abu Umama report that these particular people, the Azariqa, are dogs of the people of the Fire. It is authentically reported concerning the Khawarij who came before those fought by Ali that they would leave the religion. These hadiths, then, concerned specific people. For this reason, Ibn Hazm said in al-Muhalla bi al-Athar (12/173):
"As for the Azariqa, they are not among the sects of Islam, because they are the ones about whom the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) reported that they would leave the religion, just as


[Page 322]

"The arrow passes through the prey." Despite his statement in Al-Fasl fi al-Milal wa al-Ahwa wa al-Nihal (3/144):
"Whoever among the people of Islam exercises interpretation and errs: if the proof has not been established against him and the truth has not been made clear to him, then he is excused and rewarded with one reward for seeking the truth and intending to reach it, and his error is forgiven, since he did not deliberately adopt it, in accordance with the words of Allah, Exalted is He: {There is no blame upon you for what you have done by mistake, but only for what your hearts have intended.} If he is correct, he has two rewards: one for being correct and another for seeking it. But if the proof has been established against him and the truth made clear to him, and he turns away from the truth without opposing Allah, Exalted is He, or His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), then he is a transgressor, for his audacity toward Allah, Exalted is He, in persisting in what is forbidden. If he turns away from the truth in opposition to Allah, Exalted is He, and His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), then he is an unbeliever, an apostate, whose blood and property are lawful. In these rulings, there is no difference between erring in belief concerning any matter of the Sharia and erring in a legal opinion concerning any matter, as we explained earlier."
Abu Muhammad said: "Here, Allah willing, we shall be brief, and clarify everything on which we have elaborated. Allah, Exalted is He, said: {We would never punish until We had sent a messenger.} And He, Exalted is He, said: {So that I may warn you by it, and whoever it reaches.} And He, Exalted is He, said: {But no, by your Lord, they will not believe until they make you judge concerning what they dispute among themselves, then find no resistance within themselves to what you have decided, and submit wholeheartedly.} These verses clarify this entire subject. It is therefore established that no one is declared an unbeliever until the command of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) reaches him. If it reaches him and he does not believe in it, then he is an unbeliever. But if he believes in him, then adopts whatever belief Allah wills him to adopt, or gives whatever legal opinion or performs whatever act Allah, Exalted is He, wills him to, without the Prophet's ruling to the contrary reaching him in that matter, then he bears no blame at all for what he believed or did until it reaches him. If it reaches him..."


[Page 323]

And it was sound in his view. If someone disagrees with him, exercising independent reasoning about something whose true basis has not been made clear to him, then he is mistaken, excused, and rewarded once, as he (peace and blessings be upon him) said: "When a judge exercises independent reasoning and is correct, he receives two rewards; if he is mistaken, he receives one reward." Anyone who holds a belief, makes a statement, or performs an act is a judge with respect to that matter. If he disagrees with him through his actions, while knowingly opposing the truth and believing otherwise than what he acts upon, then he is a sinful believer. If he knowingly opposes him in his words or in his heart, then he is a disbeliever and an idolater. This applies equally to matters of belief and to legal opinions, in light of the texts we have cited. This is the view of Ishaq ibn Rahawayh and others, and it is what we hold. Success is granted by Allah, Exalted is He." End quote. However, Ibn Hazm stipulated that an interpretation must have some basis in language or the Sharia, must not contradict what is necessarily known of the religion—which is what consensus means in his view—and must not be a stubborn rejection of the proof after it has been established against the person.
He said in al-Muhalla bi al-Athar (11/343):
