"Whoever adopts an interpretation in which he is mistaken, but does not thereby contradict consensus, does not intend to oppose the Quran and the ruling of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), deliberately opposing them, nor stubbornly reject them after proof has been established against him; or whoever sets out seeking worldly dominance, but does not waylay travelers, shed blood wantonly, or take property unjustly—such a person is the rebel, between whom and those against whom he has rebelled reconciliation should be made, in accordance with the verse concerning the rebels and with what he (peace and blessings be upon him) said about the Khawarij emerging between two groups of his community: one of them is rebellious, and it is the group that kills Ammar; the other is closer to the truth. He (peace and blessings be upon him) praised those who brought reconciliation between them.
As we have narrated through al-Bukhari: Sadqah related to us; Ibn Uyaynah related to us; Abu Musa related to us, from al-Hasan, who heard Abu Bakrah say: 'I heard the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him)...'


[Page 324]

(peace and blessings be upon him) on the pulpit, with al-Hasan beside him, looking once at the people and once at him, and saying, "This son of mine is a leader, and perhaps Allah will bring reconciliation through him between two groups of Muslims." If matters escalate to the point that they terrorize the roads and seize the Muslims' property by force, without any interpretation, or shed blood in the same way, then they are waging war, and the rulings on those who wage war apply to them. If matters escalate further, to the point that they violate the consensus, then they are apostates: all their property is then taken as spoils, one-fifth is set aside, and the remainder is distributed. And success is from Allah, Exalted is He.)) For Ibn Hazm, consensus meant matters known by necessity. He explained how the Kharijites violated consensus, saying in al-Muhalla bil-Athar (11/334):
Thus it is established that interpretation varies. Any group that, in pursuing its aim, interpreted the texts in a way that erased some part of the Sunnah—such as those who adopted the Kharijite position in order to remove authority from Quraysh; to return people to the view that stoning is invalid; to declare sinners unbelievers; to demand loans from the Muslims; to kill children and women; to proclaim that divine decree is invalid; to deny the vision of Allah; to claim that Allah, Exalted is He, knows nothing until it comes to be; to disavow some of the Companions; to deny intercession; to invalidate acting upon established Sunnahs from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him); and to call for recourse to anyone other than the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him); or to refuse to pay zakat, or to withhold any right owed to a Muslim or any right owed to Allah, Exalted is He—such people are not excused on the basis of their corrupt interpretation, for it is utter ignorance.
Ibn Taymiyya says: "As for killing an individual from among the Kharijites who has been captured, such as the Haruriyya, the Rafida, and the like, the jurists hold two opinions on this, which are also two reports from Imam Ahmad. The correct view is that it is permissible to kill an individual among them, such as one who calls others to his doctrine, or someone similar whose presence entails corruption. For the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:


[Page 325]

Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said: {Wherever you find them, kill them.} And he said: {If I live to see them, I will kill them as the people of Ad were killed.} Umar said to Sabigh ibn Asal, "If I found you with your head shaved, I would strike the part of you that contains your eyes." Ali ibn Abi Talib sought to have Abdullah ibn Saba, the first of the Rafidah, killed, until he fled from him. These people are among the greatest corrupters on earth. If their corruption can be stopped only by killing them, then they are to be killed. But it is not obligatory to kill every one of them if this view has not become manifest, or if killing him would cause a greater harm. For this reason, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) initially refrained from killing that Kharijite, lest people say, "Muhammad kills his companions." At that time, he was not causing widespread corruption. For this reason, Ali refrained from killing them when they first appeared, because they were numerous, and outwardly they remained within obedience and the community; they had not fought the people of the community, and it was not clear to him that they were the ones in question.
As for declaring them unbelievers and judging them to remain in Hell forever, the scholars likewise have two well-known positions on this, which are two reports from Ahmad. These two positions concern the Kharijites and those who have seceded, including the Haruriyya, the Rafidah, and others like them. The correct view is that the statements they make, which are known to contradict what the Messenger brought, constitute unbelief; likewise, their actions that are of the same kind as the actions of unbelievers against Muslims also constitute unbelief. I have set out the evidence for this elsewhere. However, declaring any particular individual among them an unbeliever and judging that he will remain in Hell forever depends on the conditions for declaring someone an unbeliever being established and the impediments to doing so being absent. We state the general rulings found in the texts concerning promise, threat, declaring someone an unbeliever, and declaring someone a sinner, but we do not judge that a particular individual falls under that general ruling until the cause for it is established in his case, with no countervailing factor. I have explained this principle at length in "The Principle of Declaring Someone an Unbeliever." End of quotation.


[Page 326]

Consider how he said that the reason Ali did not fight them and kill them was that it had not become clear to him that they were the people intended by those hadiths. It is evident from the words of these scholars that these hadiths refer to specific people against whom proof had been established, as it must be. Whoever agrees with them in any of their doctrines, and whose ignorance could be excused and whose interpretation could be considered, has proof established against him. But if his ignorance cannot be excused, because the matter is necessarily known to be part of the religion, he is judged to be an unbeliever. Al-Tabari judged them to be unbelievers even though, in his view, they were interpreting the texts, because his school of thought does not excuse ignorance in matters of creed, as will come, Allah willing. However, as stated earlier, it has not been established that these people were interpreting the texts, so there is a basis for al-Tabari's statement.
Second: Those who held that they were not unbelievers did so on the basis of the Prophet's statement, "And he doubts about the arrowhead"—that is, he is uncertain ("he doubts about the arrowhead"): the archer wonders whether there is any trace of the prey in the notch of the arrow or not. This means that one is uncertain whether these Kharijites retain any remnant of religion or not.
However, as stated earlier, this uncertainty on the part of the observer is countered by the Prophet's explicit statement that they are the worst of creation and the dogs of the people of Hell, and other such statements. This statement (that is, the one who wonders whether any trace of the prey remains in the arrowhead or not) concerns the observer of their condition, not the reality of their case. What the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said did indeed come to pass: some people doubted whether they were unbelievers and did not judge them to be so, as is clear from the words of Ibn Hajar cited earlier.
Third: They cited Ali's statement, "They fled from unbelief." It is found in Abd al-Razzaq's work, and its chain includes someone who is unnamed. As stated earlier, it is possible that this was Ali's personal reasoning before he came to know their true nature, as indicated by the discussion cited earlier from Ibn Hajar.


[Page 327]

And if we wish to return to refining the effective cause (tanqih al-manat), we find that there are incidental attributes whose possessor is not judged sinful or declared an unbeliever. Examples include shaving the head, reciting the Quran, being fierce, and other such attributes that are not sins. Mentioning such attributes and dwelling on them extensively is evidence that the punishments mentioned apply to specific people. This is like the descriptions of the Messiah as one-eyed, with curly hair, and so on: these are identifying signs by which a particular person is specified, not grounds for imposing the ruling on everyone who happens to possess these attributes.
Then we find attributes that are sins, whether or not they reach the level of unbelief. The first is Dhu al-Khuwaysira's statement about the distribution, "It was not just." And his statement, "He will have followers," after which the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) described their attributes. What is apparent here is that these people will be followers and supporters of this man, and that they will share his position of diminishing the Prophet's standing (peace and blessings be upon him), since that is the only thing the man had manifested. This is the first attribute, and it is an attribute that constitutes unbelief, without doubt. The second is: "They delve deeply into religion until they leave it," and, "They leave the religion and do not return to it until the arrow returns to its bow." These two descriptions indicate that these people committed unbelief and departed from the religion. This is an effective cause that permits the Prophet's statement, "Wherever you encounter them, kill them," for leaving the religion entails being killed. The third is: "They call to the Book of Allah, yet have nothing to do with it." This also indicates that these people are unbelievers, since having nothing to do with it necessarily includes having no faith in it. They are therefore unbelievers. In Iqtida al-Sirat al-Mustaqim li-Mukhalafat Ashab al-Jahim (1/176), it says:
Allah, Exalted is He, said to His Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him): {You have nothing to do with them} [al-An'am: 159]. This entails that he disavows them in every respect.


[Page 328]

Whoever follows another in some of his affairs is of him in that matter. For when someone says, "I am of this, and this is of me," he means, "I am of his kind, and he is of my kind," since two people can be one only in kind, as in the words of the Exalted: {Some of you are from others} [Al Imran: 195] (2) (3), and his words (peace and blessings be upon him) to Ali: "You are of me, and I am of you" (4). Thus, when someone says, "I have nothing to do with this," he means, "I share nothing with him; rather, I disavow all his affairs."))
It is stated in al-Muhalla bi al-Athar (12/125):
We know that whoever leaves the Abode of Islam for the Abode of War has deserted Allah, Exalted is He, and the imam of the Muslims and their community. This is made clear by his hadith (peace and blessings be upon him): "He is free of responsibility for every Muslim who resides among the polytheists." He (peace be upon him) only disavows himself of an unbeliever. Allah, Exalted is He, says: {The believing men and believing women are allies of one another} [Al-Tawbah: 71].))
Fourth: His words (peace be upon him), "Their faith does not pass beyond their throats." It is stated in Fath al-Bari by Ibn Hajar (12/293):
This is like his other statement about them: "Their faith does not pass beyond their throats," meaning that they utter the two testimonies of faith but do not know them in their hearts.)) It is also stated in Fath al-Bari by Ibn Hajar (12/288):
What is meant is that they believe through speech, not through the heart.)) This too is a basis for the obligation to kill, and a basis for declaring someone an unbeliever. It is the same basis as before: the Prophet informed us that these people's faith does not pass beyond their throats. One who is an unbeliever in his heart is an unbeliever, but no one can know this; therefore, it is clear that he knew it only through revelation.


[Page 329]

Fifth: The statement of Abu Umama: "These people had been Muslims, but became disbelievers" and "They had been among the people of Islam." Sixth: There are grounds that do not constitute disbelief, but are sins: they kill the people of Islam. Killing the people of Islam is not disbelief, and they agree with us on this. But it makes killing them permissible and constitutes armed banditry if it is not based on a valid interpretation. Thus, it too is a valid ground, in light of his statement (peace and blessings be upon him), "Wherever you encounter them, kill them," on account of their armed banditry.
Seventh: "And they invoke the people of idols." This is a basis for a ruling, but it does not constitute disbelief; rather, it is a sin and does not permit killing. The apparent conclusion is that this is not the basis here for the obligation to kill these people, unless it is said that they invoke them as an act of religious devotion and hold that fighting them is forbidden, as some have maintained, as will be discussed.
If these people, in all the cases mentioned above, are a group who have committed disbelief, combined with killing the people of Islam and leaving the idolaters alone.
And now let us examine: what is the disbelief they committed? And what are the characteristics of these people that the scholars mentioned in their books?
First: It is stated in al-Muhalla (al-Muhalla bi al-Athar, 11/334):
It is thus established that interpretation varies. Any group whose interpretation in pursuit of its aims seeks to erase some part of the Sunnah—such as one who adopts the view of the Kharijites in order to take authority away from Quraysh; or to lead people back to the view that stoning is invalid, that people who commit sins are unbelievers, that Muslims may be borrowed from, or that children and women may be killed; or to proclaim the view that divine decree is invalid, or that the vision of Allah is impossible, or that Allah, Exalted is He, knows nothing until it comes to be; or to disavow some of the Companions; or to deny intercession; or to reject acting upon the established Sunnahs of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), and calls people to refer instead to someone below the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) ...


[Page 330]

and blessings be upon him—or to refusing to pay zakat, or to withholding a Muslim's due, or a due owed to Allah, Exalted is He: such people are not excused on account of a corrupt interpretation, because this is complete ignorance. As for one who calls people to an interpretation that does not invalidate any Sunnah, but rather to something like Muawiya's interpretation that he should exact retribution from Uthman's killers before pledging allegiance to Ali, he is excused, because this involves no rejection of any part of the religion; it is merely a particular error concerning a specific incident, which does not extend beyond it.
As for one who rose up solely for worldly gain, as Yazid ibn Muawiya, Marwan ibn al-Hakam, and Abd al-Malik ibn Marwan did when they rose against Ibn al-Zubayr; as Marwan ibn Muhammad did when he rose against Yazid ibn al-Walid; and as those who also rose in support of Marwan did, such people are not excused, because they have no interpretation at all: it is sheer transgression.
As for one who calls people to enjoin what is right, forbid what is wrong, uphold the Quran and the Sunnah, and rule with justice, he is not a rebel. Rather, the rebel is the one who opposes him. And Allah, Exalted is He, is the source of success.)) Here Ibn Hazm mentioned the matters for which the Kharijites declared others unbelievers and by which they contradicted the established consensus; among them he mentioned the abrogation of stoning.
It is stated in al-Tanbih wa al-Radd ala Ahl al-Ahwa wa al-Bida (p. 185):
Hassan ibn Farukh said: Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz (may Allah be pleased with him) asked me what the Azariqa say. I told him. He said, "What do they say about stoning?" I replied, "They declare it unbelief." He said, "Allah is greatest! They have disbelieved in Allah and His Messenger.")) And among the matters Ibn Hazm mentioned were declaring someone an unbeliever for a sin, deeming the killing of women and children lawful, denying divine decree, denying the vision of Allah, believing that Allah knows nothing until it comes to be, disavowing some or most of the Companions and declaring them unbelievers. The basis for unbelief here is denying the verses that praise the Companions.


[Page 331]

by promising them Paradise ("And to all Allah has promised the best reward"), or those that report that Allah is pleased with them; denying intercession; rejecting the practice of solitary-report hadiths, since the Kharijites reject solitary reports; and mentioning other grounds that do not constitute unbelief.
Therefore, the researcher holds that the two sects referred to in the hadiths are the Muhakkima and then the Azariqa. In al-Farq bayn al-Firaq (p. 62), it says:
"The Azariqa were a people who held the view of the first Muhakkima." Thus, it is clear here that the two sects held the same view. We mention here some of the characteristics of these two sects:
In al-Farq bayn al-Firaq (p. 174), it says:
Among the views of the Azariqa was that Allah, Exalted is He, would punish the child of a polytheist for the deed of his father.
In al-Fasl fi al-Milal wa al-Ahwa wa al-Nihal (4/144), it says:
The rest of the Azariqa, who were the followers of Nafi ibn al-Azraq, held that stoning an adulterer who was married was invalid. They cut off a thief's hand at the shoulder, and required a menstruating woman to pray and fast during her menstruation. Some of them said, "No; rather, she must make up the prayers when she becomes pure, just as she makes up the fasts." They deemed the blood of children lawful if they were not in their army, and also killed women who were not in their army. The Azariqa disavowed anyone who stayed behind from going out to fight, whether due to weakness or for any other reason. They declared anyone who opposed this view an unbeliever after the death of the first among them to hold it, but did not declare as an unbeliever anyone who opposed it during his lifetime. They said that they should test everyone they encountered who was not from their army, and kill him if he said, "I am a Muslim." They prohibited killing anyone who belonged to...


[Page 332]

the Jews, the Christians, or the Magians. In this way, the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) testified that they would depart from the religion as an arrow passes through its prey, when he said (peace be upon him) that they would kill the people of Islam and leave the idolaters alone. This is one of the signs of his prophethood (peace and blessings be upon him), for he foretold this, and it is one of the particulars of the unseen; it came to pass exactly as he said." End quote.
Here the meaning of his words (peace and blessings be upon him), "and they will leave the idolaters alone," becomes clear. They used to deem it forbidden to kill the People of the Book and the polytheists, as Ibn Hazm said. Thus, this rationale is also one that entails disbelief: their apparent leaving them alone indicates that they do so as an act of religious devotion, and this too is a rationale that entails disbelief.
It is stated in al-Milal wa al-Nihal (1/120):
The Azariqa had eight innovations:
The first was that he declared Ali (may Allah be pleased with him) an unbeliever, and said that Allah revealed concerning him: {And among the people is one whose speech about the life of this world pleases you, and who calls Allah to witness what is in his heart, yet he is the fiercest of opponents.} 1 He declared Abd al-Rahman ibn Muljam—may Allah curse him—correct in his action, and said that Allah, Exalted is He, revealed concerning him: {And among the people is one who sells himself, seeking Allah's pleasure.} 2
Imran ibn Hittan, the Kharijite jurist, ascetic, and greatest poet, said of Ibn Muljam's blow3—may Allah curse him—to Ali (may Allah be pleased with him):
"O blow from one who sought nearness, intending by it ... only to attain the pleasure of the Lord of the Throne.
Whenever I remember him, I deem him ... the weightiest of all creation in Allah's sight."
The Azariqa followed this innovation, and went further by declaring Uthman, Talha, al-Zubayr, Aisha, and Abd Allah ibn Abbas (may Allah be pleased with them) unbelievers,


[Page 333]

And the rest of the Muslims with them, and their being consigned to the Fire forever, all of them, ... The eighth: The Azariqa agreed that whoever commits one of the major sins becomes an unbeliever in the sense of apostasy, thereby leaving Islam altogether, and will remain forever in the Fire with the rest of the unbelievers." End quote.
Al-Fakhr al-Razi said: "In his discussion of the Kharijite sects:
The other sects among them agree that a servant becomes an unbeliever through sin. They declare Uthman and Ali, may Allah be pleased with them, as well as Talha, al-Zubayr, and Aisha, unbelievers, while holding Abu Bakr and Umar in high regard." End quote.
Al-Ashari said in Maqalat al-Islamiyyin, ed. Zarzur (1/84):
"The general position of the Kharijites:
The Kharijites unanimously agreed that Ali ibn Abi Talib, may Allah be pleased with him, was an unbeliever if he ruled; but they differed over whether his unbelief constituted associating partners with Allah.
They unanimously agreed that every major sin is unbelief, except the Najdat, who do not hold this view.
They unanimously agreed that Allah, Exalted is He, punishes those who commit major sins with everlasting punishment, except the Najdat, the followers of Najda."
"It is stated in Fath al-Bari that Shaykh Taqi al-Din al-Subki said in his fatwas: 'Those who declared the Kharijites and the extremist Rafidites unbelievers argued that they did so because they declared the leading Companions unbelievers, which entails accusing the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) of falsehood in his testimony that they would enter Paradise. He said: In my view, this is a sound argument.'"


[Page 334]

Among what was mentioned earlier were the criteria found in the hadiths, as well as the descriptions of this group. It became clear from these that many of the criteria are grounds for declaring them unbelievers, making them highly deserving of the command to kill them and of being called the dogs of the people of Hell. Those actions mentioned are sufficient to rule that they have left the religion.
After that came two very strange and vile opinions, based on two false premises. The first opinion is that everyone who rebels against the imam is a Kharijite, and therefore all the hadiths about the Kharijites apply to him.
The second opinion is that everyone who declares a Muslim an unbeliever is a Kharijite, and therefore the hadiths about the Kharijites apply to him.
First: Refuting those who hold the first opinion:
None of the criteria mentioned in the hadiths refers to rebelling against the imam. The criteria mentioned by the scholars likewise contain no reference to rebelling against the ruler. Indeed, in the hadiths, every expression meaning "leaving" refers to leaving the religion, as in the words, "They delve deeply into religion until they leave it," and "They pass out of the religion." Al-Shawkani said: (Nayl al-Awtar, 7/187)
"His words: 'Chapter on fighting the Kharijites': They are the plural of kharija, meaning a group. They were so called because they left the religion and introduced innovations, or because they left the best of the Muslims." End quote.
Their leaving "the best of the Muslims" means that they separated from them; they were not part of their company. It does not mean that they rebelled against them, since "from" is not "against." The apparent meaning, then, is that they separated from the community through the unbelieving practices they introduced.


[Page 335]

Some restricted them to the group that rebelled against Imam Ali (may Allah be pleased with him). Al-Ashari said: "The reason they were called Kharijites was their rebellion against Ali ibn Abi Talib." (1) .. However, some cited the following statement by al-Shahrastani as evidence that everyone who rebels against the imam is called a Kharijite, and that all the preceding hadiths therefore apply to him.
Ali)
From the scholars' statements, three reasons for the name can be identified. First, they departed from the religion; this is what is mentioned in the hadiths. Second, they were called this because they rebelled against Ali. Third, they were called this because they rebelled against the rightful imams. The latter two definitions have no support in the hadiths. But some people have confused the reason for the name with the criterion for the ruling, making the reason for the name the criterion for the ruling. This is manifest ignorance and clear misguidance. We will give examples to make the distinction clear. Among the names of the Kharijites are: "The Kharijites," because they rebelled against Ali ibn Abi Talib; "the Arbitrators," because their slogan was "There is no judgment except Allah's"; "the Haruriyya," because they settled at Harura at the beginning of their affair; "the Sellers," because they said, "We have sold ourselves in obedience to Allah," meaning that they had sold them in exchange for Paradise; and "the Apostates," because they departed from the religion just as an arrow passes through its target—as the Messenger described them. Another name for them is "the Doubters."
That was because, when they rejected arbitration, they said to Ali, "You doubted your own position and appointed your enemy to judge you." Thus they were called the Shakkakiyya. These are some of the names and some of the reasons for their designation. There is no doubt that not everyone who says, "There is no judgment except Allah's," is necessarily subject to the rulings concerning the Kharijites, even if, linguistically, he is called a Muhakkim for Allah. Likewise, not everyone who settles in Harura is subject to those rulings, even if he is called a Haruri; nor is every...


[Page 336]

Whoever says, "I sell myself, seeking Allah's pleasure," is subject to the rulings, even though he is called a seller, and its plural is "sellers." Likewise, not everyone who rises up against a ruler is subject to the hadiths, because the criteria mentioned by the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), and stated in the hadiths, for classifying someone as a Kharijite do not apply to him.
Otherwise, Muawiya and those with him rose up against Ali, yet no one said that these people were Kharijites in the technical sense. Al-Husayn and those with him, among the most virtuous of the Companions, rose up against Yazid; and Abdullah ibn al-Zubayr and those with him, among the best of the Companions, also rose up against him. Zayd ibn Ali, who was one of the virtuous, rose up against Hisham ibn Abd al-Malik with the support and funding of Imam Abu Hanifa. Yet none of the scholars said that these men are called Kharijites in the technical sense. This issue has already been discussed above, but in al-Shahrastani's words there are two qualifications that the compromisers deliberately overlook. The first is his phrase, "the rightful imam." Are these tyrants rightful imams? Is their appointment as imams valid?
The second is his phrase, "whom the people have agreed upon." There is no doubt that by "the people" he means the believers, not the polytheists who call for secularism, socialism, or democracy, and associate other tyrants with Allah in His rule. So have the monotheistic believers, who affirm Allah's sole authority to rule, agreed to choose these tyrants to govern them by the laws of disbelief? These are two questions that need to be answered before judging everyone who rises up against a tyrant to be a Kharijite. If you cannot answer them, then know that you are following your own desires. And who is more astray than one who follows his own desire without guidance from Allah? Distinguish between a specific label and the criterion for a ruling; otherwise, you would have to call everyone who supported the Messenger an Ansari and rule that Allah is pleased with him. And everyone who settled


[Page 337]

A town is Christian, yet you rule that its people will abide forever in the Fire. Everyone who turns to Allah and repents to Him, you declare to be a Jew, and rule that he deserves to be cursed. Everyone who separates from a group, you declare to be a Mu'tazilite; everyone who supports Ali, you declare to be a Shi'i. Wake up, may Allah have mercy on you.
As for the second group, they held that the basis for judging these people to be Kharijites is their declaring Muslims unbelievers and consequently deeming it lawful to kill them.
In response to them, we say that declaring someone an unbeliever takes several forms and types.
First: declaring someone an unbeliever for something that, by the consensus of Ahl al-Sunna, does not in itself constitute unbelief: namely, committing major sins. This is what the scholars have said about the Kharijites, but it is not mentioned in the hadiths, unless it can be inferred by implication from the Prophet's words (peace and blessings be upon him): "They kill the people of Islam." If so, one may say that this is among the grounds for the ruling. But, as noted earlier, since this occurred among a group other than the one mentioned, proof must be established before ruling that they are unbelievers, because the grounds for the ruling in the hadith are mixed together: their leaving the religion by rejecting the Sunnah, or denying something necessarily known to be part of the religion, such as solitary reports, and their deeming it unlawful to kill the people of idolatry, among other things mentioned earlier. Therefore, proof must be established.
But do you not see that the Mu'tazilites held that a person who commits a major sin will abide forever in the Fire, even though they did not declare him an unbeliever? Nevertheless, the imams deemed many of their leading figures upright, and some of them were caliphs, such as al-Mu'tasim. Likewise, the Mu'tazilites do not excuse people on the grounds of ignorance. They say that reason is sufficient to determine what is good and bad, even if a person has not received the scriptural text. Yet I know of no one who said that they were Kharijites and applied the Prophet's hadiths about the Kharijites to them, even though


[Page 338]

Their presence in the generations of the early Muslims, among the Successors, shows that, in their view, the absence of an excuse on grounds of ignorance or interpretation when declaring someone a disbeliever was not what made that person an extremist.
Second: Declaring someone an unbeliever on account of a sin concerning which there is disagreement as to whether it constitutes unbelief. There is no doubt among any of the imams that if someone declares another person an unbeliever over a matter concerning which the scholars disagree as to whether it constitutes unbelief, the one who declares him an unbeliever is not called a Kharijite, nor do the hadiths about the Kharijites apply to him.
And mention here some of the individuals whom the Sunnis differed over whether to declare unbelievers. It appears in al-I'tisam by al-Shatibi, edited by al-Shuqayr, al-Humayd, and al-Sini (1/223)
This is because the scholars among the earliest generations and others differed over declaring many of their sects unbelievers, such as the Kharijites, the Qadariyya, and others (7)(8)(9).
It is stated in
((Al-Sawa'iq al-Muhriqa 'ala Ahl al-Rafd wa al-Dalal wa al-Zandaqa (2/630):
Know that the Sunnis differed over whether to declare Yazid ibn Mu'awiya and his successor after him unbelievers. One group said that he was an unbeliever, citing the well-known report from Sibt ibn al-Jawzi and others: when the head of al-Husayn (may Allah be pleased with him) was brought, he gathered the people of Syria, began prodding the head with a cane, and recited verses by Ibn al-Ziba'ra.))
(Mirqat al-Mafatih Sharh Mishkat al-Masabih, 4/1641)


[Page 339]

They differed over whether one who is ignorant of one of the attributes of Allah, Exalted is He, is to be declared an unbeliever. Qadi Iyad said: Among those who declared him an unbeliever was Ibn Jarir al-Tabari. Abu al-Hasan al-Ashari initially held this view as well. Others said that he is not declared an unbeliever for this, unlike one who denies the attribute. Abu al-Hasan later returned to this position, and it became the settled view of his school. He said: "This is because he holds that belief with certainty that he is correct, and regards it as a religious doctrine prescribed by the Sharia. Only one who believes that his own doctrine is true is declared an unbeliever."
It is stated in
"Al-Irf al-Shadhi: Commentary on Sunan al-Tirmidhi (1/81):
They differed over whether the Rafidites are to be declared unbelievers. The Hanafis have two opinions: one says that they are unbelievers, and the other says they are not. The preferred view is that they are unbelievers, for whoever declares the majority of the Companions unbelievers is an unbeliever, and the Rafidites restrict Islam to nine or seven Companions."
It is stated in
"Sharh al-Tahawiyya by Yusuf al-Ghufays (19/18, according to the automatic numbering of al-Maktaba al-Shamila):
Disagreement over declaring one who abandons fasting and pilgrimage an unbeliever
As for fasting and pilgrimage, a group of the early generations held that abandoning the fast is unbelief, and a group of the early generations held that abandoning pilgrimage is unbelief. The vast majority of the early and later generations hold that abandoning fasting alone, or abandoning pilgrimage alone, is not unbelief."


[Page 340]

Ibn Hubayra said: "They differed concerning one who learns magic and practices it. Abu Hanifa, Malik, and Ahmad said that he thereby becomes an unbeliever. Some of Abu Hanifa's companions, however, made a distinction: if he learns it in order to guard against it or avoid it, he does not become an unbeliever; but if he learns it believing it to be permissible, or believing that it will benefit him, then he becomes an unbeliever. They did not regard the ruling as absolute. If he believes that the devils do whatever they wish, then he is an unbeliever. Al-Shafi'i said: 'If someone learns magic, we say to him: Describe your magic. If he describes something that entails unbelief, such as the belief of the people of Babylon that one draws near to the seven planets and that they do whatever is asked of them, then he is an unbeliever. But if it does not entail unbelief, then if he believes it to be permissible, he is an unbeliever.'"
The disagreement over declaring one who abandons prayer an unbeliever is well known.
Likewise, at the beginning of this study, we mentioned the disagreement among the early scholars over declaring those who interpret the texts figuratively to be unbelievers, and cited those who transmitted this disagreement, such as Qadi Iyad. We established that this was the position of Ibn Jarir al-Tabari. I know of no one who said that these early scholars were Kharijites, nor did anyone accuse Ibn Jarir al-Tabari of being a Kharijite. The position of this contemporary group, which declares those who interpret the texts figuratively to be unbelievers and does not excuse them on grounds of ignorance, is very close to Ibn Jarir al-Tabari's position on this issue. Whoever says that they are Kharijites must say the same of Ibn Jarir al-Tabari, without distinction; otherwise, he is contradicting himself.
In all these matters, scholars differed over the individuals who fell into them: according to some, they are unbelievers, abiding forever in the Fire, while according to others, they are believers. The group that declared them unbelievers did not say that the other group were Kharijites, because an error in declaring someone an unbeliever is not something for which one is held accountable. There is much evidence for this, including what is reported in the…


[Page 341]

1 Musnad al-Bazzar = al-Bahr al-Zakhkhar (1/309)
Umar said: "So I drew my sword and said, 'Messenger of Allah, let me deal with Hatib, for he has disbelieved; let me strike off his head.' The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said: 'Ibn al-Khattab, how do you know? Perhaps Allah looked upon this company of the people of Badr and said, "Do as you wish, for I have forgiven you."' This hadith concerning the story of Hatib has been narrated from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) through more than one chain of transmission" [p. 310]. The hadith has been authenticated by scholars.
In this hadith, Umar declared Hatib a disbeliever, and the Messenger of Allah excused him; he did not say that he was one of the Kharijites.
2 It is stated in Sahih al-Bukhari (3/175):
The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) stood up that day and sought redress concerning Abdullah ibn Ubayy ibn Salul. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said: "Who will excuse me concerning a man whose harm to my family has been reported to me? By Allah, I know nothing but good of my family, and they have mentioned a man of whom I know nothing but good; he never entered my home except in my company." Sa'd ibn Mu'adh stood up and said, "Messenger of Allah, by Allah, I will deal with him for you. If he is from the Aws, we will strike off his head; and if he is from our brothers, the Khazraj, command us, and we will carry out your order concerning him." Sa'd ibn Ubadah then stood up—he was the chief of the Khazraj, and had been a righteous man before that, but zeal overcame him—and said, "By the life of Allah, you lie! You will not kill him, nor are you able to do so." Usayd ibn Hudayr stood up and said, "By the life of Allah, you lie! By Allah, we will surely kill him, for you are a hypocrite arguing on behalf of the hypocrites." The two clans, the Aws and the Khazraj, erupted into dispute until they were on the verge of fighting, while the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) was on the pulpit. He came down and calmed them until they fell silent, and he too fell silent.


[Page 342]

Here, Usayd ibn Hudayr accused Sa'd ibn Mu'adh of disbelief, and he excused him (peace and blessings be upon him).
And he did not say that he was a Kharijite.
3. It is related in Sahih Muslim (1/96):
On the authority of Usama ibn Zayd—and this is the hadith of Ibn Abi Shayba—who said: "The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) sent us on an expedition. We attacked the Hurqat of Juhayna at dawn, and I caught up with a man who said, 'There is no god but Allah.' I stabbed him, but I felt troubled by what I had done. I mentioned it to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), and the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, 'Did he say, "There is no god but Allah," and you killed him?' I said, 'Messenger of Allah, he only said it out of fear of the weapon.' He said, 'Did you split open his heart so that you could know whether he said it or not?' He kept repeating this to me until I wished that I had only become Muslim that day." Here, Usama thought the man was an unbeliever, and the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) excused him and did not punish him.
4 It is stated in
Sahih al-Bukhari (5/160)
From Salim, from his father, who said: The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) sent Khalid ibn al-Walid to Banu Jadhimah and called them to Islam. They could not properly say, "We have embraced Islam," so they kept saying, "We have become Sabi'ah, we have become Sabi'ah." Khalid began killing some of them and taking others captive, and he gave each of us a captive. Then, one day, Khalid ordered each of us to kill his captive [p. 161]. I said, "By Allah, I will not kill my captive, and none of my companions will kill his captive," until we came to the Prophet.


[Page 343]

(peace and blessings be upon him), and we mentioned him. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) raised his hands and said, "O Allah, I disavow before You what Khalid has done," twice.
Here, Khalid assumed that these people remained in disbelief, yet the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did not punish him or dismiss him, nor did he say that he was one of the Kharijites.
Ibn Taymiyyah says (Majmu al-Fatawa, vol. 3): "If a Muslim acts on the basis of an interpretation in fighting or declaring someone an unbeliever, he does not become an unbeliever on that account. This is like when Umar ibn al-Khattab said to Hatib ibn Abi Balta'a, 'Messenger of Allah, let me strike the neck of this hypocrite.' The Prophet said, 'He was present at Badr. How do you know? Perhaps Allah looked upon the people of Badr and said, "Do as you wish, for I have forgiven you."' This is in the two Sahihs. Also in them, in the hadith of the Slander, Usayd ibn Hudayr said to Sa'd ibn Ubadah, 'You are a hypocrite, arguing on behalf of the hypocrites.' The two groups disputed, and the Prophet reconciled them. Among these people of Badr, one said to another, 'You are a hypocrite,' yet the Prophet declared neither of them an unbeliever; rather, he testified that they would all enter Paradise."
What preceded were proofs cited by those who say that one is not held accountable for an interpretation in declaring someone an unbeliever.
Its perpetrator is not called a Kharijite; otherwise, we would be accusing half the scholars of the Muslim community of Kharijism. This invalidates the claim of those who say that anyone who interprets matters in declaring someone an unbeliever is one of the Kharijites.
The fourth type of declaring someone an unbeliever is when an act is unanimously agreed to constitute disbelief, but the person who pronounces the ruling of disbelief applies it to specific individuals without considering the impediment of ignorance; that is, he does not excuse the person who committed the act, because in his view ignorance is no excuse at all.
So, is someone who does not excuse ignorance on the basis of an interpretation considered one of the Kharijites?


[Page 344]

The question of being excused on account of ignorance is one on which the scholars differ. It should be noted that this type of declaring someone an unbeliever is less severe than the previous type, since the previous type involves declaring someone an unbeliever who has not committed any act of unbelief at all, but whose act some people mistakenly thought was unbelief. Here, however, the person has committed an act of unbelief on which there is consensus, but is not excused on account of ignorance. Thus, all the evidence cited above is even more applicable here, and Allah knows best. The researcher's view is that a person who interprets matters incorrectly should be excused on account of ignorance if he has not been negligent in seeking knowledge and has not fabricated lies against Allah. But the question under discussion here is whether someone who is not excused, despite being an interpreter, is a Kharijite. Let us first review
the views of the scholars who did not excuse people on account of ignorance, either because they did not consider ignorance an excuse, or because they considered it an excuse but held that knowledge had become widespread and that the legally responsible person had been negligent in seeking knowledge, and so had no excuse:
Ibn Hazm reported the disagreement on the question of being excused on account of ignorance. He said in al-Fasl fi al-Milal wa al-Ahwa wa al-Nihal (3/137):
Discussion of those who are declared unbelievers and those who are not
Abu Muhammad said: People have differed on this subject. One group held that anyone who disagrees with them on any matter of belief or any matter of legal opinion is an unbeliever. Another group held that such a person is an unbeliever in some matters and a sinner, but not an unbeliever, in others, according to what their intellects and conjectures led them to conclude. Another group held that anyone who disagrees with them on matters of belief is an unbeliever, but that anyone who disagrees with them on matters of rulings and acts of worship is neither an unbeliever nor a sinner, but rather a qualified interpreter who is excused if he errs and rewarded for his intention. A group said the same about those who disagreed with them on matters of worship, and said of those who disagreed with them on


[Page 345]

Issues of beliefs: If the disagreement concerns the attributes of Allah, Mighty and Exalted, then he is an unbeliever; if it concerns anything less than that, then he is a sinner. A group held that no Muslim becomes an unbeliever or a sinner because of a statement he makes concerning belief or a legal opinion, and that anyone who exercises independent reasoning on any such matter and adopts as his religion what he considers to be the truth is rewarded in every case: if he is right, he receives two rewards, and if he is wrong, one reward. This is the view of Ibn Abi Layla, Abu Hanifa, al-Shafi'i, Sufyan al-Thawri, and Dawud ibn Ali (may Allah be pleased with them all). It is also the view of everyone among the Companions (may Allah be pleased with them) whose position on this issue we know; we know of no disagreement among them on this at all, except for the disagreement we have mentioned concerning declaring an unbeliever one who deliberately abandons prayer until its time has passed, or fails to pay zakat, or abandons the pilgrimage, or fails to fast Ramadan, or drinks wine. Those who declared people unbelievers on account of disagreements in matters of belief argued from various points, which we shall present, if Allah, Mighty and Exalted, wills.
Abu Muhammad said: They have cited a hadith from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) that the Qadariyya and the Murji'a are the Magians of this community, and another hadith stating that this community will split into seventy-odd sects, all of them in the Fire except one, which will be in Paradise.
Abu Muhammad said: These are two hadiths that are not sound at all in terms of their chains of transmission. Anything of this kind is not proof even according to those who accept solitary reports, so how much less according to those who do not accept them? They cited as evidence the established report from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him): "Whoever says to his brother, 'O disbeliever,' one of them has incurred disbelief." The first of them
Abu Muhammad said: They have no proof in this, because its wording indicates that he incurs sin by accusing him of disbelief; the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did not say that he thereby becomes a disbeliever.


[Page 346]

Abu Muhammad said: The majority of those who use this report as evidence do not declare a person to be an unbeliever for saying to a Muslim, "You unbeliever," during an exchange of insults between them. In this, they have contradicted the report they cited as evidence.
Abu Muhammad said: The truth is that anyone whose status as a Muslim has been established does not cease to be one except by a text or consensus. Mere claims and false accusations do not suffice. Therefore, no one should be declared an unbeliever for something he has said unless he contradicts what he knows to have been said by Allah, Exalted is He, or by the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), and thus deems it permissible to oppose Allah, Exalted is He, and His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him). This applies equally whether the matter concerns a religious belief, a sect, or a legal opinion, and whether what is authentically reported from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) has been transmitted by mass transmission establishing consensus or by solitary reports. However, one who opposes a consensus whose authenticity is certain and definitive is more clearly subject to the severing of his proof and the obligation to declare him an unbeliever, because everyone agrees that the consensus is known and that one who opposes it is to be declared an unbeliever. The proof that our position is correct is the saying of Allah, Exalted is He: {Whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him, and follows a path other than that of the believers, We shall leave him to what he has chosen and admit him to Hell—and what an evil destination.}
Statements of those who say that ignorance is no excuse:
Ibn Jarir, may Allah have mercy on him, said in his commentary on the saying of Allah, Exalted is He, in Surat al-A'raf: "A group He guided, while another group deserved misguidance. They took the devils as allies instead of Allah, while thinking that they were rightly guided." He said: The group that deserved misguidance strayed from Allah's path and deviated from the straight way by taking the devils as supporters and allies instead of Allah, out of ignorance of the error in what they were doing. Rather, they did so while thinking that they were rightly guided and correct, and that what they had done and undertaken was right. And this


[Page 347]

Among the clearest indications of the error in the claim that Allah punishes no one for a sin he commits or a misguidance he believes in unless he commits it after knowing that it is correct, and does so in defiance of his Lord, is that, were this so, there would be no difference between the party of misguidance, who went astray while thinking that they were rightly guided, and the party of guidance. Yet Allah distinguished between them in their names and rulings in this verse." End quote.
And Aba Butayn quoted Ibn Jarir in his commentary on the words of the Exalted: {A group He guided, while another group deserved misguidance. They took the devils as allies instead of Allah, yet they think that they are rightly guided.}
) Ibn Jarir said: "This indicates that the ignorant person is not excused." End quote. Al-Durar, 10/392. Also see Ibn Jarir's discussion of verse 104 of Surat al-Kahf.
Al-Tabari's statement concerning the Kharijites, which Ibn Hajar quoted from him, has already been mentioned: "Among those who inclined toward some of this discussion was al-Tabari in his Tahdhib. After citing the hadiths in this chapter, he said: In them is a refutation of the view that no one among the people of the qibla leaves Islam, after becoming subject to its ruling, unless he knowingly intends to leave it. This view is invalidated by the hadith's words: 'They speak the truth and recite the Quran, yet they pass out of Islam and do not cling to anything of it.' It is well known that they deemed the blood and property of Muslims lawful only because of an error in their interpretation of Quranic verses, taking them to mean something other than what was intended. He then related, with an authentic chain of transmission, from Ibn Abbas, who was told about the Kharijites and what they encounter when reciting the Quran. He said: 'They believe in its clear verses, but perish over its ambiguous ones.' The view mentioned is further supported by the command to kill them, alongside the previously cited hadith of Ibn Masud: 'It is not lawful to kill a Muslim except for one of three reasons,' including 'one who abandons his religion and separates from the community.'"


[Page 348]

Ibn Mandah, may Allah have mercy on him, said in his book al-Tawhid, 1/314: "Chapter: Citing the evidence that one who exercises independent reasoning but errs in knowing Allah, Mighty and Majestic is He, and His oneness is like one who stubbornly resists." Then he said: "Allah Most High said, informing us of their misguidance and stubborn resistance: {Say, 'Shall We inform you of those who are the greatest losers in their deeds, those whose efforts in the life of this world have gone astray, while they think that they are doing good?'}"

Al-Qurtubi said in his Tafsir, 7/319, regarding the verse of the covenant, at its end: "There is no excuse for the imitator in matters of divine oneness." End quote.

Abu al-Wafa Ibn Aqil, may Allah have mercy on him, said that whoever calls upon the occupant of a shrine and slips written petitions into graves has committed major shirk. The imams of the da'wa have often quoted him on this matter approvingly. Shaykh Muhammad said in Tarikh Najd, p. 266: "Ibn Aqil stated that they are unbelievers on account of this act"—meaning calling upon the occupant of a shrine and slipping written petitions into graves. Shaykh Aba Butayn said: "Ibn Aqil's statement has already been cited, in which he unequivocally declared unbelievers those whom he described as ignorant of the excesses they committed regarding graves. Ibn al-Qayyim quoted him approvingly." Al-Durar, 10/386.

Ibn Farhun said in Tabsirat al-Hukkam, in the chapter on apostasy: "A question: Whoever worships the sun, the moon, a stone, or anything else is to be executed and not asked to repent."

The sons of Shaykh Muhammad ibn Abd al-Wahhab and Hamad ibn Nasir said in Al-Durar, 10/136–138, when asked: "If a person who believes in Allah and His Messenger says or does something that constitutes unbelief, out of ignorance of that fact, do you refrain from declaring him an unbeliever until the proof of the message has been established against him? And if someone in this state was killed before this da'wa appeared, is that an issue or not?"


[Page 349]

They replied, saying: "If someone engages in disbelief and associating partners with Allah out of ignorance, or because there is no one to alert him, we do not rule that he is an unbeliever until proof has been established against him. But we do not rule that he is a Muslim either. Rather, we say: This deed of his is disbelief that makes his property and blood lawful. Although we do not rule on this particular person because proof has not been established against him, it should not be said, 'If he is not an unbeliever, then he is a Muslim.' Rather, we say: His deed is the deed of unbelievers, and applying the ruling to this particular person depends on the prophetic proof reaching him. The scholars have mentioned that the people who lived during intervals without prophets will be tested on the Day of Resurrection in the gathering place, and they did not assign them the ruling of unbelievers or the ruling of the righteous." Al-Durar, 10/137.

Shaykh Abd al-Aziz, the judge of Diriyah, said in Al-Rasa'il wa al-Masa'il al-Najdiyya, 5/576, in response to a question about a believer in Allah and His Messenger who says or does something that constitutes disbelief, being unaware of that: "Do you not declare him an unbeliever until proof has been established against him?"
He replied: "If someone engages in disbelief and associating partners with Allah out of ignorance, and because there is no one to alert him, we do not rule that he is an unbeliever until proof has been established against him. But we do not rule that he is a Muslim either. Rather, we say: This deed of his is disbelief that makes his property and blood lawful. Although we do not rule on this particular person because proof has not been established against him, it should not be said, 'If he is not an unbeliever, then he is a Muslim.' Rather, we say: His deed is the deed of unbelievers, and applying the ruling to this particular person depends on the prophetic proof reaching him. The scholars have mentioned that the people who lived during intervals without prophets will be tested on the Day of Resurrection in the gathering place, and they did not assign them the ruling of unbelievers or the ruling of the righteous."


[Page 350]

Shaykh Abdullah ibn Muhammad ibn Abd al-Wahhab said in Al-Rasa'il wa al-Masa'il, Part One of Volume One (p. 79): "As for one who died while committing shirk out of ignorance, not obstinacy, we leave his affair to Allah, Exalted is He. One should not supplicate for him, ask for mercy for him, or seek forgiveness for him. This is because many scholars say that whoever has received the Qur'an has had the proof established against him, as Allah, Exalted is He, says: {That I may warn you thereby and whoever it reaches}."

Al-Qarafi, Al-Furuq li al-Qarafi = Anwar al-Buruq fi Anwa al-Furuq (2/150):
"Whoever proceeds despite ignorance has sinned, particularly in matters of belief. For the Lawgiver has imposed great strictness regarding the creeds of the foundations of religion, to the extent that if a person exerts all his effort and exhausts his capacity to dispel his ignorance concerning one of the attributes of Allah, Exalted is He, or concerning something that must be believed as part of the foundations of religion, yet that ignorance is not dispelled, then he is sinful and an unbeliever for abandoning that belief, which is part of faith, and will abide in the Fire forever, according to the well-known position."

It is clear from what has preceded that there is disagreement over the question of whether ignorance is an excuse in matters of creed. Therefore, one who does not accept it as an excuse should not be accused of being a Kharijite; otherwise, let them say this of the imams mentioned above, or else they will inevitably fall into contradiction.

Then, if the criterion for judging someone to be a Kharijite is that he declares someone an unbeliever who does not deserve to be declared one, even on the basis of an interpretation, and consequently the hadiths about the Kharijites apply to him and he must be killed, and some even declare him an unbeliever, then I do not know why they have not applied this to...


[Page 351]

These hadiths are directed against the Mu'tazilites, for they declare the people of the Sunnah unbelievers on the basis of an interpretation they hold. Ibn Taymiyyah said: "In Minhaj al-Sunnah (5/158): 'The Kharijites declare the people of the community unbelievers, and so do most of the Mu'tazilites when someone disagrees with them (10). The same is true of most of the Rafidites. Those who do not declare them unbelievers call them sinners. Likewise, most of the people of heretical views introduce an opinion and declare those who disagree with them unbelievers. The people of the Sunnah follow the truth from their Lord, which the Messenger brought; they do not declare those who disagree with them unbelievers. Rather, they know the truth better and are more merciful toward creation, as Allah described the Muslims when He said: {You are the best community brought forth for mankind}.'"
Likewise, some Ash'aris declare the people of the Sunnah who affirm Allah's exaltedness and attributes unbelievers, and call them Hashawiyya, anthropomorphists, and assimilators. I do not know of anyone among the people of the Sunnah who called these people Kharijites.
In a study written by Professor Ridwan Shakdani, it says: "Shaykh Abu Ishaq al-Shirazi, the leading Ash'ari of his time and author of al-Muhadhdhab, upon which Imam al-Nawawi (may Allah have mercy on him) wrote his commentary, said: 'Whoever believes anything other than what we have indicated of the belief of the people of truth affiliated with Imam Abu al-Hasan al-Ash'ari (may Allah be pleased with him) is an unbeliever. Whoever attributes anything else to them has declared them unbelievers, and is therefore an unbeliever for declaring them so, because it is reported from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) that he said: "No man declares another man an unbeliever without one of them incurring it..."' See his Sharh al-Luma' (1/111).
Al-Subki said in his "wooden" Sayf al-Saqil, commenting on the eminent Imam Ibn al-Qayyim: 'He is the heretic, may Allah curse him! How shameless he is, and how audacious! May Allah disgrace him!' Sayf al-Saqil, p. 37. Al-Kawthari edited it and disfigured it!
Al-Kawthari went further, hurling at Ibn al-Qayyim (may Allah Most High have mercy on him) words of unbelief, abuse, and vilification that decent people would recoil from hearing. He gathered them in his treatise entitled Tabdid al-Zalam al-Mukhayyim min Nuniyyat Ibn al-Qayyim.
Al-Kawthari also said: "Ibn Taymiyyah declared the judges of the four schools in Egypt unbelievers when he prohibited visiting the Prophet's grave." Takmilat al-Sayf al-Saqil, p. 155.
Ibn Nujaym al-Hanafi al-Maturidi (d. 970 AH) said in al-Bahr al-Ra'iq, "Chapter on the Rulings Concerning Apostates" (5/129): "A person becomes an unbeliever by affirming that Allah Most High has a place. If he says, 'Allah is in heaven,' then if he intends to relate what has come in the apparent wording of the reports, he does not become an unbeliever; but if he means a place, he becomes an unbeliever."
Abu al-Mu'in al-Nasafi al-Hanafi al-Maturidi (d. 508 AH) said in Tabsirat al-Adilla (1/169): "Allah Most High has denied any likeness between His essence and anything else. Thus, the claim that He has a place contradicts this definitive text—that is, His saying, {There is nothing like unto Him}—which admits no interpretation other than its apparent meaning. Whoever rejects the text is an unbeliever. May Allah protect us from that." End quote.
Second: Declaring the generality of Muslims who have not received their creed concerning Allah Most High through rational inquiry—that is, the arrangement of rational syllogisms—to be unbelievers. You find them stating in their books that rational inquiry is obligatory in order to establish Allah's existence, and they call anyone who does not rely on these rational methods an imitator, then they assert that


[Page 352]

That the faith of the imitator is invalid is the position of al-Ashari and the leading scholars among his followers, and the choice of Muhammad ibn Yusuf al-Sanusi (d. 895), author of Umm al-Barahin and other works on kalam relied upon by later scholars. He said in his book al-Manhaj al-Sadid (p. 48): "This is the position of the majority, and some report consensus on it."
According to these scholars, an imitator is someone who has not established proof for the articles of creed; and, in their view, the proofs are the proofs of kalam.
Al-Bajuri said in his commentary on Jawharat al-Tawhid: "The phrase 'some of the people' means: because of his confusion and hesitation, the scholars differed over whether his faith is valid or invalid. The disagreement amounts to several positions, including:
that the faith of the imitator is invalid, in which case the imitator is an unbeliever; this is the position of al-Sanusi."

If the criterion for accusing someone of Kharijism is simply declaring others unbelievers, then that criterion must apply to these people mentioned above. According to their own claim, these hadiths must be applied to them, since in their view the hadiths are not restricted to particular people. They must therefore declare each of them an unbeliever and kill any one of them whom they are able to reach. But they do not say this, and so they have fallen into contradiction, inevitably.
One of the strange things about these people is that if you ask them, "Why are you fighting these people?" they say, "Because they declare us unbelievers and fight us." They are then asked, "Do they implement Allah's law in their territories, or not?" If they answer, "Yes," they are told: "The Crusaders, whom you allied yourselves with and fought alongside, also declare Muslims unbelievers and fight them, and do not implement Allah's law in their territories. So which of the two groups is more deserving of having the hadiths about the Kharijites applied to them, and which group is more deserving of allegiance? Unless you say that those whom you have called Kharijites have apostatized through this act of theirs and become unbelievers, and that an apostate is more deserving of being fought than an unbeliever." They are then told: "You have fallen into the very thing you sought to escape: declaring others unbelievers and refusing to excuse them on grounds of ignorance or misinterpretation. You have declared a large group unbelievers and pronounced them apostates, without excusing them. The hadiths about the Kharijites therefore apply to you, since the relevant criterion is present in you. Another criterion is that you have left the idolaters among the Christians and the polytheists alone, and made an alliance with them.


[Page 353]

The reconciliations over fighting your brothers: you fought the people of Islam and left the people of idols. Indeed, you accepted democracy, which forbids interfering with religious freedoms and with the sovereignty of other states, even if those people are idolaters. Thus you truly left the people of idols alone and accepted a constitution and law that forbids fighting them. You therefore fell into the description, "They kill the people of Islam and leave the people of idols." This is the clear criterion mentioned in the hadiths, whereas you chose a criterion not mentioned in the hadith.
If they say, "Rather, we do not say that they are apostates," we say: Then come, let us see what the scholars have said about such a case:
Scholars' statements:
Ibn Taymiyya said, after mentioning the disagreement among later scholars over whether declaring the people of innovation unbelievers constitutes unbelief that takes one out of the religion or not, and whether they remain eternally in the Fire or not … He said: "The reality of the matter is that, with regard to the general wording of the imams' statements, they suffered the same problem as the early generations did with the general wording of the Lawgiver's texts. Whenever they saw the imams say, 'Whoever says such-and-such is an unbeliever,' the listener assumed that this wording applied to everyone who said it, without considering that declaring someone an unbeliever has conditions and impediments that may be absent in the case of a particular individual, and that declaring someone an unbeliever in general does not entail declaring a particular individual an unbeliever unless the conditions are present and the impediments absent. This is made clear by the fact that Imam Ahmad and most of the imams who made such general statements did not declare most of those who uttered those very words to be unbelievers. For example, Imam Ahmad personally confronted the Jahmiyya, who called upon him to affirm that the Quran was created and to deny the divine attributes. They put him and all the scholars of his time to the test, and persecuted the believing men and women who did not agree with their Jahmiyya doctrine by beating and imprisoning them … He mentioned that most of the foremost …


[Page 354]

They used to declare as unbelievers everyone who was not a Jahmi and in agreement with them, and to treat them as unbelievers … and so on, up to his statement: "It is well known that this is among the most extreme forms of Jahmism, for calling people to a doctrine is graver than merely holding it, and rewarding one who professes it and punishing one who abandons it is graver than merely calling people to it …"

Then Imam Ahmad prayed for the caliph and others who had beaten and imprisoned him, sought forgiveness for them, and absolved them of what they had done … Had they been apostates from Islam, it would not have been permissible to seek forgiveness for them, for seeking forgiveness for unbelievers is impermissible according to the Book, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus. These statements and actions of his and of other imams clearly show that they did not declare individual Jahmis to be unbelievers(( ….
So consider, may Allah have mercy on you: despite the repugnance of the Jahmis' doctrine, he did not declare their individuals to be unbelievers. Rather, he prayed for them, did not support the polytheists and Crusaders against them, but rejoiced at the conquest of Amorium and openly expressed his joy at it. Nor did he surrender Muslim lands governed by the Sharia to the Crusaders on the pretext that the Jahmis and Mu'tazilites declare us unbelievers.
Shaykh al-Islam Ibn Taymiyya says in his response to Ibn al-Bakri: "For this reason, the people of knowledge and the Sunnah do not declare those who oppose them to be unbelievers, even if those opponents declare them unbelievers. For unbelief is a legal ruling, and a person may not retaliate with the like of it. If someone lies about you and commits adultery with your wife, you may not lie about him or commit adultery with his wife, because adultery and lying are forbidden as a right owed to Allah, Exalted is He. Likewise, declaring someone an unbeliever is a right owed to Allah, Exalted is He, so no one is declared an unbeliever except one whom Allah and His Messenger have declared an unbeliever." End quote, p. (257).
He also mentioned that "among the faults of the people of innovation is that they declare one another unbelievers, whereas among the merits of the people of knowledge is that they make mistakes but do not declare others unbelievers." End quote (9).


[Page 355]

He said in Minhaj al-Sunna (5/158): "The Kharijites declare the people of the community to be unbelievers. Likewise, most of the Mu'tazilites declare those who oppose them to be unbelievers,(10) as do most of the Rafidites. Those who do not declare them unbelievers deem them sinful. Likewise, most of the people of heretical doctrines introduce an opinion and declare those who oppose them in it to be unbelievers. The people of the Sunna follow the truth from their Lord, which the Messenger brought, and do not declare those who oppose them in it to be unbelievers. Rather, they know the truth better and are more merciful toward creation, just as Allah described the Muslims when He said: {You are the best community brought forth for mankind}."

He also said: "Among the people of innovation are those who have faith inwardly and outwardly, but also have ignorance and injustice, so that they err in some of the Sunna. Such a person is neither an unbeliever nor a hypocrite. He may then commit aggression and injustice that make him sinful or disobedient; he may be mistaken in his interpretation, with his error forgiven; and, alongside that, he may possess such faith and piety as grant him a share of Allah's friendship, in proportion to his faith and piety." End quote. Majmu al-Fatawa (Dar Ibn Hazm edition), 3/220.

Ibn al-Qayyim (may Allah have mercy on him) said in Zad al-Ma'ad: "Among the principles is that if polytheists, people of innovation and immorality, rebels, or oppressors seek something that honors one of the inviolable matters of Allah, they should be granted it, given it, and assisted in it, even if they are denied other things. They are to be helped in what honors the inviolable matters of Allah, not in their unbelief or rebellion, and they are to be prevented from anything beyond that. Whoever seeks assistance in something beloved to Allah and pleasing to Him should be granted it, whoever he may be, so long as assisting him in that beloved thing does not result in something even more hateful to Allah."


[Page 356]

Ibn Taymiyyah said (28/212): "If it is impossible to establish the obligatory duties of knowledge, jihad, and the like except through someone who has an innovation, whose harm is less than the harm of abandoning that obligation, then securing the benefit of the obligation alongside an outweighed harm is better than the reverse." End quote.
I have cited these texts for those who, as they claim, give precedence to allegiance to the original disbelievers over those who have an innovation, even though the Crusaders possess all the qualities they condemn in innovators: they declare them disbelievers and kill Muslims. Moreover, they do not uphold the Sharia, and they do not let anyone uphold it; rather, they rain bombs down on him. So take heed, people of understanding: which of these is more deserving of allegiance?
(Conclusion) After researching this issue, namely being excused on account of ignorance, the researcher has concluded the following: A person who commits disbelief out of ignorance, after having uttered the two testimonies and embraced faith in general terms, is one of two kinds. Either he did so on the basis of an interpretation that has some basis in the Sharia or in language; such a person is excused. Or he was negligent in seeking knowledge and spoke about Allah without knowledge, either denying or affirming; such a person is not excused. Whoever does not excuse people on account of ignorance and declares those who interpret matters to be disbelievers is himself excused on account of his ignorance; he cannot be declared a disbeliever for that, nor accused of Kharijism. Likewise, whoever excuses someone on account of ignorance based on an interpretation cannot be declared a disbeliever or accused of Murji'ism. And Allah knows best. Any error in this research that contradicts the words of Allah and the words of His Messenger, I retract. May Allah have mercy on whoever points out an error to me. Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds.


[Page 357]

The fourth type of declaring someone an unbeliever is when the unbelief is unanimously agreed upon, and the one who declares him an unbeliever excuses him on account of ignorance, but believes that the person who committed the act of unbelief had knowledge, and therefore has no excuse of ignorance. This is like Umar's treatment of Hatib, according to some interpretations.
And likewise the action of Sa'd ibn Mu'adh toward Usayd ibn Hudayr. This is the mildest form of declaring someone an unbeliever, and exercising independent judgment on this matter is quite plausible and has considerable scope. If the first three issues admit of interpretation, is it not possible that this one does too?
